The reference uses the same actual prime pairs and their actual gaps.

Uniformly on the window, both actual and reference ratios are
$H+2+o(1)$, with positive denominators. This follows from
$R_r,\mathcal R_r=r/L+O(1/L)$ on compact rank bands and
$|L(y)-L(X)|\le1$ on $[X,2X]$ eventually.
Every chosen long step is consequently a strict descent for every
grid tilt. For the short step, $2\le g_{a_i}\le H$ puts both
ratio-minus-gap thresholds inside $[1,H+1]$.
Writing $\Delta_i=\Delta_{r_X}(p_{a_i}-1)$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:count-residual}
 C_H(X)-\overline C_H(X)
 =M_X\sum_{i=0}^{m_X-1}\Delta_i+e_X,\qquad |e_X|<m_X
\end{equation}
whenever $m_X>0$.

\begin{theorem}[Localized separated-reversal criteria]\label{thm:count-rh}
For the preceding fixed width and deterministic construction, RH is
equivalent to each of the following:
\begin{align}
 C_H(X)&\le\overline C_H(X)\quad\hbox{eventually},\label{eq:count-rh-sign}\\
 C_H(X)-\overline C_H(X)&\le C X^{1/10}
 \quad\hbox{eventually for some fixed }C>0.\label{eq:count-rh-power}
\end{align}
Under false RH the signed difference divided by $X^{1/10}$ is
unbounded above along integer $X$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Under RH, the uniform coefficient sign comparison applies to
$r_X$ at every selected prefix. Actual ascent tests are included
in reference tests, proving \eqref{eq:count-rh-sign} and hence
\eqref{eq:count-rh-power}.

If RH is false, Lemma~\ref{lem:persistence}, followed by the
uniform coefficient transfer, supplies arbitrarily large integer
$n$ such that
\[
 \Delta_r(y)>T n^{-1/2}\qquad
 (n\le y\le n+n^{7/10})
\]
for every prescribed fixed $T>0$ and every rank in a fixed positive
compact $r/L(y)$ band. Indeed its retained main term is
$\gg n^{-b}/L(y)$ for some $b<1/2$, and
$n^{1/2-b}/\log\log n\to\infty$.
All selected prefixes at $X=n$ lie in this interval, and the single
rank $r_n$ lies in the required band throughout.
Since $M_n\ge\sqrt n$, \eqref{eq:count-residual} gives
\[
 C_H(n)-\overline C_H(n)>m_n(T-1).
\]
Now $m_n\ge c_0n^{1/10}$ eventually, with fixed $c_0>0$.
Choosing $T>1+C/c_0$ contradicts any proposed eventual bound
\eqref{eq:count-rh-power}. This proves both converses.
\end{proof}

For each individual tilt, the selected witnesses are bounded by
$N_{\eta(t)}(r_X+1)$. The sum in the theorem ranges over a growing
finite grid, so it is not the maximal count for one fixed law.
Its reference also retains $\theta$ and the entire profile.
An independent proof of either eventual count inequality would
prove RH; the equivalence itself supplies no such estimate.

An ascent-only variant uses every consecutive gap at most the source
width in $[X+1,X+1+\lceil2X^{7/10}\rceil]$ and the same grid and rank.
Let its actual and reference counts be $A_H(X),B_H(X)$.
The source at $\delta=3/5$, applied to this longer window, supplies
$\gg X^{7/10}/(\log X)^m$ pairs. At the persistent false-RH peaks,
\eqref{eq:grid-rounding} then gives
\[
 A_H(X)-B_H(X)\gg
 \frac{X^{6/5-b}}{(\log X)^m\log\log X},\qquad b<1/2.
\]
Thus RH is also equivalent to eventual $A_H\le B_H$, and to
an eventual one-sided bound $A_H-B_H=O(X^{7/10})$.
The persistence interval may be taken with any fixed exponent
between $7/10$ and $3/4$, which absorbs the shift and rounding
in this ascent-only window.

\section{Growing families and signed bounds}\label{sec:growing-families}

A fixed-family RH criterion does not by itself justify varying the family
with the prime cutoff. We first give a transfer with explicit error
intervals, then two arithmetic calculations in which the complete
correction can be retained. Throughout this section, a finite-prime
limiting law is taken before the cutoff or polynomial degree varies.
We make no assertion uniform in a finite sampling box.

\subsection{Correction intervals}
\begin{theorem}[Transfer through a correction interval]\label{thm:correction-interval}
Let $E,\ell,u:\N\to\mathbb R$ and let $c>0$ be fixed. Suppose that
\[
 \ell(N)\le E(N)-cF(N)\le u(N)
 \quad\hbox{for all sufficiently large }N.
\]
Assume that, for every fixed $0<b<1/2$, there is a constant $C_b>0$
such that $u(N)-\ell(N)\le C_bN^{-b}$ eventually.
Then RH is equivalent to eventual $E(N)\le u(N)$, and also to
eventual $E(N)<u(N)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Under RH, $F(N)<0$ eventually, so the upper interval bound gives
$E(N)<u(N)$. If RH fails, the integer-cutoff Nicolas excursion theorem
gives a fixed $0<b<1/2$ and arbitrarily large $N$ with
$cF(N)>(C_b+1)N^{-b}$. At these $N$, the lower interval bound implies
$E(N)>u(N)$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}
The interval center need not be small or positive. What matters is the
width and its proved arithmetic relation to the same $E$ and $F$.
Defining an endpoint from the unknown error would not provide an
independent estimate. The maintained theorem has a fixed coefficient
$c$; a moving coefficient requires a separate uniform argument.

\subsection{An explicit polynomial-plus-prime family}
For a positive integer $d$, put
\[
 P_d(a)=\prod_{j=1}^d(a-j),\qquad V_d(a,q)=P_d(a)+q,
\]
where $a$ ranges over integers and $q$ over primes, with ordered
representations retained. For each fixed finite set of primes, uniform
integer residues, prime progression limits and the Chinese remainder
theorem give independent local divisibility laws. The polynomial has
exactly $\min(d,p)$ roots modulo $p$. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:root-local-law}
 \eta_d(p)=\frac{p-\min(d,p)}{p(p-1)}.
\end{equation}
In particular, every prime $p\le d$ is excluded. Use this exact law,
including those exclusions, to form $E_d(N)=E_{\eta_d}(N,1)$ as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:family-product}. Set
\[
 Q_2(N)=\sum_{p>N}\frac1{(p-1)^2},\qquad
 T(N,b)=\sum_{p>N}\log\left(1+\frac b{(p-1)^2}\right)
 \quad(b\ge0).
\]

\begin{theorem}[Exact correction and finite bounds]\label{thm:root-exact}
For $N\ge\max(d,3)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:root-error}
 E_d(N)=F(N)-T(N,d-1).
\end{equation}
For $N\ge1$ and $b\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:root-tail-bounds}
 \left(b-\frac{b^2}{2N^2}\right)Q_2(N)
 \le T(N,b)\le bQ_2(N),\qquad 0\le Q_2(N)\le\frac2N.
\end{equation}
Moreover $N\log N\,Q_2(N)\to1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $p>d$, direct substitution into the odds gives
\[
 (1+w_d(p))(1-1/p)
   =\left(1+\frac{d-1}{(p-1)^2}\right)^{-1}.
\]
The exact profile identity expresses $E_d-F$ as the sum of these
logarithms over $p>N$. All excluded primes already lie in the finite
prefix; their profile factors cancel exactly, proving
\eqref{eq:root-error}. For $t\ge0$,
$t-t^2/2\le\log(1+t)\le t$. Since $p>N$ implies $p-1\ge N$,
summing these inequalities proves \eqref{eq:root-tail-bounds}.
Comparison with $\sum_{n\ge N}n^{-2}\le2/N$ gives the elementary
upper bound. Partial summation of the prime number theorem gives
the stated asymptotic for $Q_2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Independent signs at logarithmically reduced degree]
\label{thm:log-root-sign}
For every fixed integer $j\ge1$, let
\[
 d_j(N)=1+\left\lfloor\frac{N}{(\log N)^j}\right\rfloor .
\]
Then, unconditionally,
\[
 (\log N)^{j+1}E_{d_j(N)}(N)\longrightarrow-1.
\]
In particular $E_{d_j(N)}(N)<0$ for every sufficiently large integer
$N$. The diagonal degree $d=N$ also satisfies
$E_N(N)\log N\to-1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For large $N$, $d_j(N)\le N$, and
$b_N=d_j(N)-1=N/(\log N)^j+O(1)$. The two bounds in
\eqref{eq:root-tail-bounds}, together with $b_N\le N$, imply
\[
 (\log N)^{j+1}T(N,b_N)\longrightarrow1.
\]
For every fixed positive integer $A$, the quantitative prime number
theorem gives the unconditional limit
\[
 F(N)(\log N)^A\longrightarrow0.
\]
Subtracting in \eqref{eq:root-error} proves the result.
For the diagonal, use $b=N-1$ in the same bounds to obtain
\[
 T(N,N-1)\log N\longrightarrow1. \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}
These are complete independent signed estimates, but the family degree
is $N^{1-o(1)}$. For every fixed $\alpha<1$ and fixed $j$,
$d_j(N)/N^\alpha\to\infty$. The theorem therefore supplies no
fixed-power zero-free conclusion. Neither its onset nor its estimates
are asserted to be uniform when $j$ grows with $N$.

\subsection{The effect of adding two primes}
Let a base residue family have zero probability $r_p\in[0,1]$ at
each prime $p$. Add two independent uniform nonzero residues.
Their exact zero probability is
\[
 \eta(p)=\frac{p-2+r_p}{(p-1)^2}.
\]
Indeed there are $p-1$ ordered nonzero pairs with sum zero and
$p-2$ with any prescribed nonzero sum. Thus the laws of $q_1+q_2+1$
and $q_1+q_2$ attain the two endpoints, independently of the
complexity of the base family.

\begin{theorem}[Attained two-prime bracket]\label{thm:two-prime-bracket}
For every such finite residue family, every $N\ge3$, and $z\ge0$,
\[
 E_{q_1+q_2+1}(N,z)\le E_{\mathrm{base}+q_1+q_2}(N,z)
       \le E_{q_1+q_2}(N,z).
\]
At $z=1$, define the convergent tails
\[
 T_3(N)=\sum_{p>N}\log\left(1+\frac1{(p-1)^3}\right),\qquad
 U_2(N)=\sum_{p>N}-\log\left(1-\frac1{(p-1)^2}\right).
\]
Then the exact bracket is
\[
 F(N)-T_3(N)\le E_{\mathrm{base}+q_1+q_2}(N,1)\le F(N)+U_2(N).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For tail primes $p>N\ge3$, all three probabilities are less than one.
Both $\eta/(1-\eta)$ and $\log(1+z\eta/(1-\eta))$ increase with
$\eta$. Compare the convergent tail corrections to $zF(N)$,
including their exact normalizations. Substitution of $r_p=0,1$
gives the two displayed tails at $z=1$.
\end{proof}
The favorable endpoint correction is asymptotic to
$1/(2N^2\log N)$ by partial summation. Thus adding arbitrary base
complexity cannot enlarge this correction within the stated
two-prime-addition class. This controls a correction, not the sign
of the complete expression containing $F(N)$. Additional independent
prime additions can be included in the base distribution; this
ordinary specialization does not assert uniform finite-box limits
or a result for changing nonunit coefficients.

\section{Power-sized degrees and zero-free regions}\label{sec:power-degree}
The distinction between a logarithmic saving and a fixed power saving
can be made precise for the actual polynomial family of
Section~\ref{sec:growing-families}. The result below is a conditional
implication. It does not assume a zero-free region, but it does assume
the eventual sign of the complete arithmetic profile.

\begin{theorem}[Power-degree sign criterion]\label{thm:power-degree}
Let $d:\N\to\N$ satisfy $d(N)\ge1$. Fix real numbers $C>0$ and
$1/2\le\alpha<1$. Suppose that, for every sufficiently large integer $N$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-degree-hypotheses}
 d(N)\le CN^\alpha,\qquad E_{d(N)}(N)\le0,
\end{equation}
where $E_d$ is the complete profile of the consecutive-root polynomial
$P_d(a)+q$, with all small-prime exceptions included.
Then
\[
 \zeta(\rho)\ne0\qquad
 \hbox{whenever }\alpha<\operatorname{Re}\rho<1.
\]
The degree bound and the signed inequality refer to the same chosen
sequence of families and both hold eventually, not merely along
possibly different unbounded subsequences.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Since $\alpha<1$, the degree bound implies $d(N)\le N$ eventually.
The exact identity \eqref{eq:root-error} and
\eqref{eq:root-tail-bounds} therefore give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:power-degree-F}
 F(N)\le T(N,d(N)-1)
       \le(d(N)-1)Q_2(N)
       \le2C N^{\alpha-1}
 \quad\hbox{eventually}.
\end{equation}
Suppose $\zeta(\rho)=0$ with $\beta=\operatorname{Re}\rho>\alpha$
and $\beta<1$. Choose
\[
 b=\frac{(1-\beta)+(1-\alpha)}2.
\]
Then $0<1-\beta<b<1-\alpha\le1/2$. The zero-dependent
integer Nicolas excursion argument from
Section~\ref{sec:excursions} gives arbitrarily large integers $N$
with $F(N)>2C N^{-b}$. But $\alpha-1<-b$, so for $N\ge1$,
\[
 F(N)\le2C N^{\alpha-1}\le2C N^{-b}
\]
by \eqref{eq:power-degree-F}, a contradiction.
This uses the already established integer excursions, including the
nonlinear logarithm correction. It neither differentiates an
asymptotic error nor assumes a new real-to-integer interpolation.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Square-root degree]\label{cor:sqrt-degree-RH}
If $d(N)\ge1$, $C>0$, and eventually
\[
 d(N)\le C\sqrt N,\qquad E_{d(N)}(N)\le0,
\]
then RH holds.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:power-degree} excludes every nontrivial zero to
the right of $1/2$. The functional equation then places every
nontrivial zero on the critical line.
\end{proof}

The new content here is the exact passage from this arithmetic
degree budget to the classical zero-dependent Nicolas obstruction;
the underlying oscillation mechanism is classical
\cite{Nicolas1983,Nicolas2012}. External priority for this
polynomial-family formulation is not established.

The complete sign in \eqref{eq:power-degree-hypotheses} remains
unproved independently for power-sized degrees. In contrast,
Theorem~\ref{thm:log-root-sign} proves that sign at
$d(N)=1+\lfloor N/(\log N)^j\rfloor$ for every fixed $j\ge1$.
Such a degree exceeds $CN^\alpha$ eventually for every fixed
$C>0$ and $\alpha<1$. Thus that unconditional theorem cannot be
substituted into \eqref{eq:power-degree-hypotheses}. Increasing
a fixed logarithmic saving does not reach any fixed power saving.

This criterion concerns the specified polynomial-plus-prime law.
No degree-only assertion is made for arbitrary changing
coefficients or sampling rules: those can introduce additional
excluded primes and a different correction. Nor does an arbitrary
sequence of individually qualifying fixed laws automatically
satisfy the uniform estimate used here.

\section{A generalized Robin critical window}
\label{sec:lcm-window}

The same signed prime-product calculation has a separate application
to generalized divisor sums. It gives actual counterexamples to a
proposed extension of a generalized Robin inequality. It does not
supply an independent RH criterion.

For a real parameter $\kappa>1$, define
\[
 S_\kappa(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(n/d)\sigma(d)^\kappa,\qquad
 \mathcal G_\kappa(n)=
 \frac{\zeta(\kappa)S_\kappa(n)}
 {(e^\gamma n\log\log n)^\kappa}\quad(n\ge3).
\]
Here $\kappa$ is a real divisor-sum exponent, not the prime-factor
rank $k$. These are the LCM-power functions of
Fan--Kobayashi--Molnar~\cite[Definition~1.2]{FKM}.
For an integer $N\ge8$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:modified-lcm}
 M_N=\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,N)
       \prod_{\substack{p\le N\\N<p^2\le2N}}p,\qquad
 K_N=\frac32+\frac{2}{5\log\log M_N}.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform critical window]\label{thm:lcm-window}
For every $X\in\N$, there is an integer $N>X$, $N\ge8$, such that
simultaneously for every real $1<\kappa\le K_N$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lcm-window-margin}
 \log\mathcal G_\kappa(M_N)>
       \frac1{100\sqrt N\log N}.
\end{equation}
Under RH, this holds for every sufficiently large integer $N$,
with a common cutoff for the entire parameter window.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:lcm-counterexamples}
For every fixed real $1<\kappa\le3/2$, there are arbitrarily large
integers $n$ for which $\mathcal G_\kappa(n)>1$.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{Exact arithmetic and the full signed comparison}
Let $a_p=v_p(M_N)$ for $p\le N$, and define
\begin{align*}
 u_p&=p^{-a_p-1},& U_N&=\sum_{p\le N}u_p,\\
 R_N&=4\sum_{p\le N}\frac{u_p}{p}
             +32\sum_{p\le N}u_p^2,&
 D_N&=\log\log\log M_N-\log\log\theta(N).
\end{align*}
All finite sums have the same prime support $p\le N$. Write
\[
 \mathcal T_\kappa(N)=
       \sum_{p>N}-\log(1-p^{-\kappa}).
\]
For $\kappa>1$ this converges and equals the logarithm of the
omitted part of the zeta Euler product.

The defining divisor sum is multiplicative, and at a prime power
\[
 S_\kappa(p^a)=\sigma(p^a)^\kappa-\sigma(p^{a-1})^\kappa
 =\left(\frac{p^a}{1-1/p}\right)^\kappa
       (1-p^{-\kappa})\,C_\kappa(p,a),
\]
where
\[
 C_\kappa(p,a)=
 \frac{(1-p^{-a-1})^\kappa-p^{-\kappa}(1-p^{-a})^\kappa}
      {1-p^{-\kappa}}>0.
\]
Therefore the exact normalized identity is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lcm-exact-log}
 \log\mathcal G_\kappa(M_N)=
 \mathcal T_\kappa(N)-\kappa\bigl(F(N)+D_N\bigr)
       +\sum_{p\le N}\log C_\kappa(p,a_p).
\end{equation}
This retains the actual integer height $\log M_N$.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform finite lower bound]\label{lem:lcm-finite}
For $N\ge8$ and $1<\kappa\le2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lcm-signed-bound}
 \log\mathcal G_\kappa(M_N)\ge
 \mathcal T_\kappa(N)-\kappa(F(N)+D_N+U_N)-R_N.
\end{equation}
If $1<\kappa\le K\le2$, its right side may be replaced by
\[
 \mathcal T_K(N)-K\max(F(N)+D_N+U_N,0)-R_N.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $u=p^{-a-1}$, $v=p^{-\kappa}$ and $t=p^{-a}$.
For $1\le\kappa\le2$, $u\le1/8$ and $v\le1/2$, Bernoulli's
inequality gives
\[
 C_\kappa(p,a)\ge1-\frac{\kappa u}{1-v}.
\]
The number $w=\kappa u/(1-v)$ satisfies $0\le w\le4u\le1/2$
and $w\le\kappa u+4uv$. The bound
$\log(1-w)\ge-w-2w^2$ yields
\[
 \log C_\kappa(p,a)\ge-\kappa u-4uv-32u^2
                  \ge-\kappa u-4u/p-32u^2.
\]
For the exponents of $M_N$, $p^{a_p+1}>N$; hence $u_p\le1/8$
when $N\ge8$. Sum the bound in \eqref{eq:lcm-exact-log}.
Finally $\mathcal T_\kappa\ge\mathcal T_K$, and for every real $A$,
$-\kappa A\ge-K\max(A,0)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The two arithmetic costs and the positive tail}
The ordinary prime number theorem gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lcm-cost-limits}
 \sqrt N\log N\,D_N\longrightarrow\sqrt2,\qquad
 \sqrt N\log N\,U_N\longrightarrow\sqrt2,\qquad
 \sqrt N\log N\,R_N\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Here is a direct explanation of all three limits. The exact height is
\[
 \log M_N=\psi(N)+\theta(\sqrt{2N})-\theta(\sqrt N).
\]
Since $\psi(N)-\theta(N)=\theta(\sqrt N)+O(N^{1/3}\log N)$,
we have $\log M_N-\theta(N)\sim\sqrt{2N}$.
The derivative of $\log\log t$ is $1/(t\log t)$, giving the first limit.

For $p>\sqrt{2N}$, $a_p=1$, so the upper-band contribution is
\[
 \sum_{\sqrt{2N}<p\le N}p^{-2}.
\]
Its normalized limit is $\sqrt2$. Below $\sqrt{2N}$, split at
$N^{1/3}$ to bound the remaining contribution by
\[
 N^{-1}\pi(N^{1/3})+\sum_{p>N^{1/3}}p^{-3}.
\]
Both terms are negligible on the displayed scale. Finally,
$u_p<1/N$ gives $R_N=O((1+\log N)/N)$ and proves the third limit.

We need only a finite lower comparison for the moving zeta tail.
For $q>1$, $m\in\N$, and $1<K\le2$, put
\begin{align}
 A_2(x)&=\sum_{p>x}p^{-2},\notag\\
 L_m(N,q,K)&=N^{2-K}A_2(N)\notag\\
 &\quad+\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}
 \bigl((Nq^{j+1})^{2-K}-(Nq^j)^{2-K}\bigr)A_2(Nq^{j+1}).
 \label{eq:lcm-finite-tail}
\end{align}
Then $\mathcal T_K(N)\ge L_m(N,q,K)$. To see this, first use
$-\log(1-p^{-K})\ge p^{-K}$, and then bound the increasing function
$p^{2-K}$ from below by its step approximation at the displayed
thresholds. Every increment is nonnegative, so summation preserves
the bound.

Fix $s>1$ and $m$ before taking $N\to\infty$, and put $q=s^2$.
Because $x\log x\,A_2(x)\to1$ and
$\log\log M_N/\log N\to1$, the finitely many terms give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lcm-finite-limit}
 \begin{aligned}
 \sqrt N\log N\,L_m\left(N,s^2,\frac32+
                        \frac c{\log\log M_N}\right)
     &\longrightarrow C_m(s)e^{-c},\\
 C_m(s)&=1+\frac1s-\frac1{s^{m+1}}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Only fixed-dilation prime-square-tail limits are used. In particular,
we have not assumed an asymptotic for a prime sum uniform in a
moving exponent.

Choose $s=1001/1000$ and $m=10^6$. Bernoulli's inequality gives
$C_m(s)>999/500$. With $\beta_0\le1/20$ from the classical
Nicolas constant, the limiting complete RH lower bound exceeds
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lcm-margin}
 C_m(s)e^{-2/5}-3(\sqrt2-1)-\frac32\beta_0>\frac7{600}.
\end{equation}
For example, $\log(3/2)>2/5$ and $\sqrt2<99/70$ show that the
left side is greater than
$333/250-87/70-3/40=99/7000>7/600$.
The finite choice $m=10^6$ is a symbolic parameter choice; no
million-term numerical verification is used.

\subsection{Proof in the two RH cases}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:lcm-window}]
Under RH, \eqref{eq:nicolas-wave} gives
\[
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}\sqrt N\log N\,F(N)\le\beta_0-2.
\]
Insert this and \eqref{eq:lcm-cost-limits}--\eqref{eq:lcm-finite-limit}
into Lemma~\ref{lem:lcm-finite}, with $K=K_N$.
For large $N$, $K_N<2$. The limiting lower bound for the common
scaled comparison is at least the quantity in \eqref{eq:lcm-margin}.
Since $1/100<7/600$, one cutoff works for every
$1<\kappa\le K_N$.

If RH fails, negative Nicolas excursions give arbitrarily large
integer $N$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lcm-negative-supply}
 F(N)\sqrt N<-1.
\end{equation}
For completeness, take real excursions $F(x)<-2x^{-b}$ with
$0<b<1/2$ and $x>3$, and let $N=\lfloor x\rfloor$.
The prime products and $\theta$ are constant on $[N,N+1)$, so
$F(N)=F(x)$. Also $x<N+1\le4N$. Thus
$F(N)<-2/\sqrt{x}\le-1/\sqrt N$. Only $F$ is rounded in
this argument; no floor invariance of $M_N$ or $W$ is used.

By \eqref{eq:lcm-cost-limits}, the positive arithmetic costs satisfy
$(D_N+U_N)\sqrt N\log N\to2\sqrt2$, whereas
\eqref{eq:lcm-negative-supply} gives
$F(N)\sqrt N\log N<-\log N$. Hence $F(N)+D_N+U_N<0$
at arbitrarily large integer cutoffs. At those cutoffs the maximum
in Lemma~\ref{lem:lcm-finite} vanishes. Equations
\eqref{eq:lcm-cost-limits} and \eqref{eq:lcm-finite-limit} then give
the stronger positive limiting lower bound $C_m(s)e^{-2/5}$.
Taking cutoffs beyond the common asymptotic thresholds proves
\eqref{eq:lcm-window-margin}. The same explicit integer family
therefore works in both logical cases.
\end{proof}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:lcm-counterexamples}]
We have $\log M_N\ge\theta(N)\to\infty$, so $M_N\to\infty$.
Also $K_N>3/2$. Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:lcm-window} beyond any
prescribed integer-height threshold and use positivity of
$\mathcal G_\kappa$ to exponentiate its positive logarithm.
\end{proof}

The theorem concerns integer cutoffs and supplies no effective onset
or frequency of the exceptional cutoffs without RH. It establishes
neither optimality of $2/5$ nor that $M_N$ is colossally abundant.
Every fixed $\kappa>3/2$ eventually lies outside the shrinking window,
so this result is consistent with the fixed-parameter upper theorem
of \cite{FKM}. In particular, the counterexamples do not violate
ordinary Robin's inequality and do not prove or disprove RH.

\section{Implications and scope}\label{sec:implications}
The spectrum gives a quantitative dictionary. Bounded-gap counts
yield reversal counts through the finite selection lemma; the
upper comparison shows that no other late ascents can improve
the rate. Finite changes in an admissible law preserve that rate.
Many different affine prime-pair expressions consequently share
a spectrum despite different forced primes and small ranks.

Location and frequency encode different information.
Corollary~\ref{cor:critical} depends on the least recurring gap.
The rate $\Lambda(\nu)$ depends on how frequently bounded gap
classes occur. Infinitude alone need not give $\gamma_H>0$:
a hypothetical count growing as a power of $\log X$ has
$\gamma_H=0$. This is why bare infinitude cannot replace
the input to Corollary~\ref{cor:power}.

For Goldbach-type expressions, the arithmetic theorem describes
factorization in the full representation-weighted ensemble.
It gives no lower bound for representations of each fixed even
integer. Fixed-modulus independence likewise does not supply
uniform estimates when a prime cutoff grows with input size.
Moving-tail distribution laws require additional uniformity.

Repeated raw reversals and centered moving-rank excursions have
different quantifiers. At each fixed rank there is a final decreasing
tail. The RH criterion lets the rank grow with the endpoint and
compares its ratio with the full reference profile.
It is the transfer in Theorem~\ref{thm:linearization} that relates
this second question to Nicolas's error. Theorem~\ref{thm:family-ratio}
extends the centered comparison to the qualifying local laws and the
actual affine families. An independently proved centered inequality
in any one such family would therefore imply RH. A coarse reversal
rate or a numerical prime-gap bound does not itself provide that sign.

The exact raw comparison can be written as
\[
 R_r(x)-(g+1)=
 \bigl(\mathcal R_r(L(x))-(g+1)\bigr)+\Delta_r(x).
\]
RH controls the second term. The first term and the occurrence of
the actual gap remain necessary for an ascent. In particular,
for fixed $1/5<b<c<1/3$ and all sufficiently large $k$, an ascent
at a prime with $bk\le\LL(p-1)\le ck$ occurs exactly when the next
gap is two. This follows directly from the uniform ratio margins.
Proving positive ascent supply in this window independently would
prove twins; the identity itself is not such a supply proof.

The Hardy--Littlewood input in Theorem~\ref{thm:hl-location}
provides actual twins approaching the eligibility cutoff.
RH instead refines that cutoff's reference error and gives
Theorem~\ref{thm:rh-capacity}. The global twin asymptotic allows
a distance $o(X_k)$ from the cutoff, which can exceed
$\sqrt{X_k}$; it cannot by itself transfer the RH square-root
clock correction to the location of the last actual ascent.
No implication from RH to twin-prime infinitude is established
by these results.

The relation to Robin and Lagarias is through their established
equivalences with RH \cite{Lagarias}. Thus the eventual inequality
in Theorem~\ref{thm:rh-ratio}, if proved independently, would imply
the corresponding universal divisor-sum inequalities. A valid
violation of either criterion would imply the false-RH coefficient
excursions. These are logical consequences of known equivalences,
not new bounds for colossally abundant numbers.
Indeed, for $n=\prod p^{a_p}$,
\[
 \frac{\sigma(n)}n=
 \prod_{p\mid n}\frac{1-p^{-(a_p+1)}}{1-p^{-1}},
\]
and optimizing the exponent-dependent numerator factors together
with $\log\log n$ remains a separate problem.

The classical inputs include Nicolas's criterion and spectral
formula, Landau oscillation, prime-factor generating products and
saddle-point estimates. The potentially new contributions are the
exact reversal-frequency spectrum, its affine realization and
the sign-preserving moving-coefficient transfer with its
applications. These attributions are provisional: proof
verification does not establish priority. Speculative RH and
independent small-gap-supply investigations are excluded from the
theorem claims of this paper.

\subsection{Finite smaller gaps and the complete spectrum}
Suppose, for a fixed even $h\ge2$, that all sufficiently late
consecutive gaps are at least $h$. Every fixed initial prime prefix
has finite support in the rank variable. Consequently, for all
sufficiently large ranks, none of its steps can be an ascent.
The RH argument in Theorem~\ref{thm:rh-capacity} then uses the
threshold $h+1$ throughout and gives
\[
 \theta(P_k-1)<T_{k-1,h}:=\EE(u_{k-1,h+1}-\gamma),\qquad
 N_k\le(1+\varepsilon)\frac{T_{k-1,h}}{\log T_{k-1,h}}
\]
eventually, and hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-gap-capacity}
 \log\log N_k\le\frac{k-1}{h+1}-\gamma
 -\frac{\mathcal H'(h+1)}{\mathcal H(h+1)}+\frac{C_h}{k-1}.
\end{equation}
Constants and onsets depend on the fixed $h$. There is no fixed-width
sign transition: $\mathcal H(s)>0$ at every positive $s$, and the RH
source multiplier $-(2+W)$ stays negative in every fixed positive
rank band.

Finite twins give $h=4$ as an eventual lower bound and
$\gamma_2=\gamma_3=0$. If additionally $\Lambda(1)=1/5$, the spectrum
forces $\gamma_4=1$: every $H\ge5$ contributes at most $1/6$, while
the only remaining possible maximizing term is $\gamma_4/5$.
It follows that
\[
 \Lambda(\nu)=\frac1{4+\nu}\qquad(\nu>0).
\]
Indeed the full-frequency width-four term dominates every larger
width, and smaller widths have zero exponent.
The RH ceiling \eqref{eq:general-gap-capacity} now has the same
leading coefficient $1/5$. The matching leading values yield no
opposing signed inequality. Nor does $\gamma_H=1$ specify an actual
density: both $G_H(X)\sim X^\delta$, with any fixed $\delta>0$,
and $G_H(X)\sim cX/\log^2X$ have that exponent.

\subsection{When local frequency reveals the RH displacement}
A stronger frequency hypothesis makes the reference displacement
visible in actual last-ascent locations.
Fix even $h\ge2$, assume smaller gaps occur only finitely often, and
assume every sufficiently late interval
\[
 [X,X+\ell(X)],\qquad \ell(X)=\frac{\sqrt X}{(\log X)^2},
\]
contains the lower member of a consecutive gap exactly $h$.
Let $V_k$ be the last supported prime eligible for a hypothetical
gap-$h$ ascent, and $P_k$ the last actual ascent prime. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:local-tracking}
 P_k/V_k\longrightarrow1,\qquad
 T_{k-1,h}-\mathcal C(P_k-1)=o(\sqrt{P_k}).
\end{equation}
Under RH this gives
\[
 \frac{T_{k-1,h}-\theta(P_k-1)}{\sqrt{P_k}}
 =2+W(P_k-1)+o(1).
\]

Here is the precision argument. Monotonicity of the supported
ordinary coefficient ratio makes eligible primes an initial segment.
Ordinary prime existence provides a supported seed below $V_k/2$.
Apply local coverage at $X=V_k-2\ell(V_k)$. For large $V_k$,
$X\ge V_k/2$ and $\ell(X)\le2\ell(V_k)$, so the supplied gap-$h$
pair has lower prime in $(V_k-2\ell(V_k),V_k]$. Support propagates
from the seed, and monotonicity makes that step an actual ascent.
Beyond $V_k$, the eventual lower gap bound $h$ makes any ascent
eligible for the hypothetical gap-$h$ test, contradicting the
definition of $V_k$. Thus all finite exceptions are accounted for and
$V_k-2\ell(V_k)<P_k\le V_k$.

At $x=V_k-1$ and $y=w-1$, with $w$ the next prime after $V_k$,
the finite-product comparison gives
$R_r(x)=\mathcal R_r(B(x))+O_h(1/(rx))$, and likewise at $y$.
Only the prime $V_k$ is added between these prefixes, so
$B(y)-B(x)=\log(V_k/(V_k-1))=O(1/V_k)$.
Ordinary prime existence and the eventual cutoff bound first place
$r/B(x)$, $r/B(y)$ and $r/u_{r,h+1}$ in a common fixed positive
compact interval. The uniform signed coefficient comparison then
applies on both connecting segments. The strict eligible sign at
$x$, the noneligible sign at $y$, and the displayed one-prime jump give
$|B(x)-u_{r,h+1}|=O_h(1/V_k)$.
No prior spatial closeness of the two clocks is assumed.

The quantitative PNT-strengthened Mertens estimate gives
$e^{-\gamma}G_x(1)-\log x=o(1)$, and hence $\mathcal C(x)/x\to1$.
The weaker relation $\log\mathcal C(x)\sim\log x$ would not suffice.
Exponentiating the proved clock difference now gives
$|\mathcal C(V_k-1)-T_{r,h}|=O_h(\log V_k)$.
Each prime-clock jump in the remaining interval is $O(\log V_k)$.
There are at most $2\ell(V_k)+1$ integer sites, so their total change
is $O(\sqrt{V_k}/\log V_k)=o(\sqrt{V_k})$.
This proves \eqref{eq:local-tracking}; add \eqref{eq:clock-wave}
at the same actual endpoint for the RH assertion.

A global pair-count asymptotic alone gives only proportional coverage.
For the exact-gap count $G_h^{=}(X)$, the maintained sufficient condition is
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 G_h^{=}(X)=c_h\operatorname{Li}_2(X)+E(X),\qquad c_h>0,\\
 E(X)=o(\sqrt X/\log^4X),\qquad
 \operatorname{Li}_2(X)=\int_2^X\frac{dt}{(\log t)^2}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
Subtract this estimate at $X$ and $X+\ell(X)$. The main increment is
of order $\sqrt X/\log^4X$ and dominates both endpoint errors.
The full integral main term is essential at this precision;
$c_hX/\log^2X$ cannot replace it. A square-root-plus-epsilon
global error is insufficient for this subtraction.
This is a sufficient route to local coverage, not its necessary strength.

Finally, on any of the finite supports in Section~\ref{sec:local-count},
the signed excess is exactly the number of grid thresholds in
$[U_i,R_i)$ minus the number in $[R_i,U_i)$, where
$U_i=\mathcal R_r(L(p_i-1))$.
A positive excess arbitrarily far out contradicts RH.
Neither finite twins nor a positive main term for another gap class
has been shown to force that signed excess. These frequency statements
therefore identify additional information needed by this route;
they establish neither RH nor twin-prime infinitude.

conditional inverse twin-prime transfer;

coefficient comparison and uniform sign transfer;

Theorems~\ref{thm:family-product} and~\ref{thm:family-ratio},

actual affine-family consumers, and the generalized reference constant;
\item \path{Helpers/FamilyIntervalTransfer.lean} and

\end{itemize}
Paths are relative to \texttt{PrimeFactorOscillations/}. The
\href{https://github.com/kimihiro64/prime-factor-oscillations/blob/main/paper/THEOREM_STATUS.md}{theorem ledger}
maps intermediate arguments to declarations. Ordinary prose
sometimes combines several compiled lemmas; these combinations
are distinguished from individually named declarations.

The analytic inputs are the reciprocal-prime estimate,
fixed-modulus prime progression limits and \eqref{eq:clusterinput}.
Narrow leaves of the pinned Erd\H{o}s 690 development supply
ordinary density and symmetric bounds; its conditional
classification is not an input. The width-$600$ count is
extracted from a separately pinned prime-cluster development.
The RH part additionally reuses the Robin1984 analytic development
and the verified explicit-formula and complex-analytic dependencies.
Section~\ref{sec:local-count} additionally uses the published
Alweiss--Luo all-short-interval input. This input is not a discharged
Lean dependency. The complete ordinary proof above must be distinguished
from formal count theorems conditional on explicit arithmetic sampling
hypotheses. Those hypotheses concern actual prime positions, gap widths,
separation and the number of cells, and contain no density sign or RH
conclusion. No project axiom asserts the source theorem.
Classical analytic results in the maintained closed development are
retained as proved dependencies, not
research-target axioms. The paper provides the coefficient-transfer
argument and its consumers, while citing the underlying classical
prime-distribution and Nicolas theorems.

The relative-location implication in

Its cutoff, order and last-ascent dependency closure has passed the

\section{Second moments, simplicity defects, and prime-profile rigidity}
\label{sec:moment-rigidity}

The statements in this section separate three kinds of information: a
conditional spectral identity, an unconditional inverse theorem for local
probabilities, and an unconditional limit on what the normalized profile
can identify. No improved zero-free boundary is asserted.

\subsection{The full second moment and a simplicity defect}

Assume RH. Let $\Gamma$ be the set of distinct ordinates of the nontrivial
zeros, including both signs, write $m_\gamma$ for their analytic
multiplicities, and put
\[
 c_\gamma=(1/4+\gamma^2)^{-1},\qquad
 C=\gamma_{\mathrm E}+2-\log(4\pi).
\]
The existing zero-wave formula is absolutely convergent and has the form
$W(x)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}m_\gamma c_\gamma x^{i\gamma}$.
Its reality follows from conjugate pairing, and $|W(x)|\le C$.

\begin{theorem}[Universal second moment and excess multiplicity]
For any two fixed qualifying quadratic prime laws $F,G$ and any fixed
positive rank parameters $\alpha,\alpha'$, let $W_F,W_G$ denote the
normalized moving-rank observables already compared to $W$.
Under RH,
\[
 \lim_{M\to\infty}\frac1{\log M}
       \sum_{n=2}^{M}\frac{W_F(n^4)W_G(n^4)}n
   =V:=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}m_\gamma^2c_\gamma^2.
\]
Define
\[
 B=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}m_\gamma c_\gamma^2,\qquad
 \mathcal D=V-B.
\]
Then
\[
 0\le\mathcal D
   =\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}m_\gamma(m_\gamma-1)c_\gamma^2
   \le C^2-B.
\]
In particular $\mathcal D=0$ if and only if all these zeros are simple.
For every finite set $S\subset\Gamma$,
\[
 \sum_{\gamma\in S}m_\gamma(m_\gamma-1)c_\gamma^2
       \le\mathcal D,\qquad
 \frac{m_\gamma(m_\gamma-1)}{(1/4+\gamma^2)^2}
       \le C^2-B.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For every real $u$,
$(\log M)^{-1}\sum_{n=2}^{M}n^{iu}/n$ tends to $1$ for $u=0$
and to $0$ otherwise. Absolute summability permits termwise extraction
from the product of two spectra. All pairs of frequencies whose sum is
zero remain; conjugate symmetry therefore gives
$\sum_\gamma m_\gamma^2c_\gamma^2$, with no simplicity assumption.
The established comparison gives
$W_F(n^4)-W(n^4)\to0$ and $W_G(n^4)-W(n^4)\to0$.
Since $W$ is bounded, their product minus $W(n^4)^2$ tends to zero.
The normalized harmonic average of that error tends to zero as well.
Thus the same limit holds for both probability observables.
The pointwise bound $W(n^4)^2\le C^2$ gives $V\le C^2$.
Subtracting $B$ produces the nonnegative series displayed above.
Every summand is zero precisely when its positive integer multiplicity
is one. Finite subsums and a single complete frequency fiber give the
remaining inequalities.
\end{proof}

Here $n^4$ is a divisor cutoff for each fixed family; it is not an
argument substituted into a two-variable polynomial family.
Finite exceptional primes and forced-rank shifts are included in the
exact reference used to define each normalized observable.
These bounds do not prove simplicity, RH, or a better unconditional
multiplicity estimate than those already available.

\clearpage
\subsection{What the entire Bernoulli profile determines}

For a law $\eta(p)\in[0,1]$ with $\sum_p\eta(p)^2<\infty$, define
\[
 \Phi_\eta(z)=\prod_p
 (1+(z-1)\eta(p))\exp(-(z-1)\eta(p)).
\]
This is a locally uniformly convergent entire product. Its zeros,
with multiplicity, are exactly $1-\eta(p)^{-1}$ for $\eta(p)>0$.
Thus the profile recovers the unordered positive probabilities.
This standard canonical-product observation alone does not label
them by primes.

The analytic input now has a maintained Lean proof in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/BernoulliProfileZeros.lean}
under the project prefix. For $w=\eta(p)(z-1)$,
\[
 (1+w)e^{-w}-1=-(e^w-1-w)e^{-w}.
\]
Consequently, on $|z-1|\le R$, each factor's defect from one is at most
$R^2e^{2R}\eta(p)^2$. Summing this bound gives locally uniform product
convergence and entire continuation. At a point where no linear factor
vanishes, the summable-defect nonvanishing theorem for infinite products
makes the product nonzero. Conversely a zero factor makes the product zero.
Thus the compiled zero-set statement is exactly
\[
 \Phi_\eta(z)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists p:\ \eta(p)>0,\quad z=1-\eta(p)^{-1}.
\]
The same module proves invariance under every bijective relabelling.
The companion maintained module
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/BernoulliProfileMultiplicity.lean}
also proves the full multiplicity identity
\[
 \operatorname{ord}_{z=1-1/c}\Phi_\eta
   = m_c,\qquad m_c=\#\{p:\eta(p)=c\},\quad c>0.
\]
Indeed square summability makes this fibre finite. Splitting off the
\emph{entire} fibre writes the profile as the $m_c$-th power of
$(1+c(z-1))e^{-c(z-1)}$ times the profile of the remaining coordinates.
The displayed factor has a simple zero at $1-1/c$; the remaining product
is entire and nonzero there by the proved zero-set theorem. Orders add,
giving the identity. It follows that equal profiles recover every positive
probability with its complete multiplicity. The case $c=1$, giving a zero
at $z=0$, is included. Zero probabilities contribute the unit factor and
are invisible to the profile itself.
The maintained companion
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/BernoulliProfileReconstruction.lean}
now proves the complete unlabelled inverse statement: two admissible,
square-summable arrays have equal entire profiles if and only if there is a
bijection between their positive coordinates preserving every probability
value. To construct it, match each finite positive-value fibre using the
proved multiplicity identity, then assemble these fibre bijections over all
positive values. Conversely, delete the zero coordinates, whose factors are
one, and reindex the remaining product by the value-preserving bijection.
No hypothesis about the Riemann hypothesis or prime distributions enters
this unlabelled reconstruction.
The next maintained module,
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/BernoulliProfileTailMatching.lean},
also proves the ordered-tail matching step. If $a_n,b_n$ have eventually
strictly decreasing positive tails, $a_n\to0$, and the same set of positive
values, then there are fixed indices $A,B$ such that
\[
 a_{A+n}=b_{B+n}\qquad(n\ge0).
\]
\begin{sloppypar}
To exclude finite exceptions, take a tail value $a_A$ smaller than every
positive exceptional value in either sequence. Its matching value $b_B$
lies in the ordered tail. Every positive value at most $a_A$ then lies
after $A$ and after $B$, respectively. These two tails have the same range.
Their mutual value-matching maps are strictly increasing maps of the natural
numbers and are inverse to one another; each strictly increasing self-map satisfies
$n\le f(n)$, so both maps must be the identity. The Lean equal-profile
corollary consumes the positive-coordinate bijection above.
The maintained assembly \path{Assembly/PrimeProfileInverse.lean} now proves
the prime-labelled tail conclusion as well: equal profiles and reciprocal
asymptotics $a_n^{-1}=p_n/\nu_1+b_1+o(1)$ and
$b_n^{-1}=p_n/\nu_2+b_2+o(1)$, with $\nu_i>0$, force
$\nu_1=\nu_2$, $b_1=b_2$, and $a_n=b_n$ eventually. Here $p_n$ is the
increasing enumeration of primes. Its proof derives tail positivity,
convergence to zero and strict ordering from the reciprocal remainder,
then consumes the compiled matching theorem. The existing unconditional
theta PNT and exact prime-prefix identity give $p_{n+b}/p_{n+a}\to1$
for every fixed $a,b$. This identifies the leading scales. The remaining
integer prime difference converges to $\nu(b_1-b_2)$, hence is eventually
constant. A positive constant would translate every sufficiently large
prime to a prime, contradicting the maintained prime-translation theorem.
These complete consumers are implemented in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfilePrimeRatio.lean},
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileInverseAsymptotics.lean}, and
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileShiftRigidity.lean}.
The finite-exception conclusion is now compiled as well.
The maintained modules
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/BernoulliProfileIdentity.lean}
and
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/BernoulliProfileFinitePermutation.lean}
split off the common tail and cancel it near $z=1$, where it has value one.
The analytic identity theorem makes the remaining finite profiles equal
everywhere. Their positive coordinates admit a value-preserving bijection;
the equal total number of coordinates then gives equally many zeros.
Joining the two bijections gives a permutation of the exceptional prefix,
extended by the identity on its entire complement. Probabilities equal to
one are included. The assembled theorem
\texttt{prime\_profile\_eq\_iff\_finite\_permutation} proves both directions
of this classification together with equality of the scale and intercept.
The general finite-accumulation version is also compiled, with equality
expressed as frequent agreement in a punctured neighborhood of the
accumulation point.
\end{sloppypar}

\begin{theorem}[Prime-coordinate rigidity]
Suppose two such laws satisfy, outside finite sets,
\[
 0<\eta_i(p)<1,\qquad
 \eta_i(p)^{-1}=p/\nu_i+b_i+o(1),\quad \nu_i>0.
\]
If $\Phi_{\eta_1}=\Phi_{\eta_2}$, then $\nu_1=\nu_2$, $b_1=b_2$,
and $\eta_1(p)=\eta_2(p)$ for every sufficiently large prime.
The complete laws differ only by a permutation of finitely many prime
coordinates. Conversely every such finite permutation preserves the profile.
Equality on a real interval, or on any set with a finite accumulation
point, suffices.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write the reciprocal remainders as $r_i(p)\to0$.
For sufficiently large consecutive primes the reciprocal increment is at
least $1/\nu_i-2\sup|r_i|>0$. Each tail is therefore strictly ordered.
Equality of entire functions gives equality of their zero multisets.
After removing finitely many exceptional probabilities, the two ordered
tails consequently match with one fixed prime-index shift:
$\eta_1(p_{j+a})=\eta_2(p_{j+b})$ for all sufficiently large $j$.
The prime number theorem gives $p_{j+a}/p_{j+b}\to1$, so comparison of
the leading reciprocal terms forces $\nu_1=\nu_2=\nu$.
Now
\[
 p_{j+b}-p_{j+a}=\nu(b_1-b_2)+o(1).
\]
An integer sequence with a real limit is eventually constant.
If this constant were nonzero, one direction of the matching would give
a fixed positive integer $c$ such that every sufficiently large prime
$p$ has $p+c$ prime. Iteration would then make $p+pc=p(1+c)$ prime,
a contradiction. Hence the constant and the shift are zero, and
$b_1=b_2$. The prime-labelled tails coincide.

Cancel the common tail product in a neighborhood of $z=1$.
The remaining finite products have the same positive-probability
multiset. Enlarging to one common finite prime set gives the same number
of zero probabilities too, hence a finite permutation.
This also retains probabilities equal to one, whose zeros lie at $z=0$.
The converse follows by reordering finitely many factors. The identity
theorem gives the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Sharp cubic camouflage outside the rigid class}

\begin{theorem}[Identical profiles with optimally small finite-cutoff defects]
There are continuum many admissible laws
$\eta(p)=p^{-1}+O(p^{-2})$, pairwise distinct on infinitely many primes,
whose entire profiles are identical. They can be chosen so that for
each fixed $T\ge0$, every $N\ge1200$ and $0\le t\le T$,
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 0\le L_\eta(N,t)-L_0(N,t)\le4800\,T^2N^{-3},\\
 L_\eta(N,t)=\sum_{p\le N}
       \{\log(1+t\eta(p))-t\eta(p)\}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
For every nontrivial construction with infinitely many swaps and every
fixed $t>0$, the difference is not $o(N^{-3})$.
These laws need not come from a fixed polynomial arithmetic family.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The established bounded-gap theorem supplies disjoint pairs
$p_j<q_j\le p_j+H$, where $H=600$, $p_j>2H$,
and $q_j<p_{j+1}$. Swap the probabilities $1/p_j,1/q_j$
on any chosen subset of these pairs. The resulting law obeys
$|\eta(p)-1/p|\le2H/p^2$.
The entire product is unchanged, because its locally uniform absolute
convergence permits permutation.

Every cutoff meets at most one incomplete pair.
Completed pairs cancel exactly. If $p\le N<q$ is an incomplete
selected pair, the complete signed defect is
\[
 d_t(p,q)=\log(1+t/q)-\log(1+t/p)+t(1/p-1/q).
\]
Applying $\log u\le u-1$ to both reciprocal ratios gives
\[
 \frac{t^2(q-p)}{pq(q+t)}
       \le d_t(p,q)\le
 \frac{t^2(q-p)}{pq(p+t)}
       \le \frac{t^2(q-p)}{p^3}.
\]
All denominators are positive. Since $p>N-H\ge N/2$, this proves
the asserted bound. At the selected cutoffs $N=p_j$ the two-sided
bounds imply
$p_j^3d_t(p_j,q_j)/(q_j-p_j)\to t^2$.
As $q_j-p_j\ge1$, the error cannot be $o(N^{-3})$.
Partition the pair indices into countably many infinite blocks and swap
all indices in a block according to one binary choice. This gives
continuum many laws, any two differing on infinitely many coordinates.
Finally their reciprocal deviations $\eta(p)^{-1}-p$ take positive
and negative integer values at infinitely many swapped pairs; they have
no limiting constant. Thus they lie outside the preceding rigidity
hypothesis, demonstrating the significance of its $o(1)$ remainder.
\end{proof}

The canonical product is classical mod-Poisson machinery; the
prime-coordinate rigidity and arithmetic swap comparisons are the
specific conclusions considered here, without a claim of literature
priority. The prime-coordinate inverse, an infinite swap construction
and its exact optimal constant are now Lean-formalized. The
continuum-cardinality assertion retains its ordinary proof.
Additional maintained owners for completed surrounding inputs, relative to
\path{PrimeFactorOscillations/}, are:
\begin{itemize}\small
\item \path{Helpers/XiWaveMoments.lean} and
      \path{Helpers/XiMultiplicityBudget.lean};
\item \path{Mathlib/Analysis/Fourier/LogarithmicBilinear.lean};
\item \path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/CompensatedReciprocal.lean};
\item \path{Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/Translation.lean};
\item \path{Assembly/PrimeProfileRigidity.lean}.
\end{itemize}
The latest promotion audit is separate from the older verification totals
elsewhere in this manuscript; those historical totals do not certify this
new batch or an improved analytic exponent.

\subsection{Universal cutoff rigidity and recurring prime gaps}

The swap construction admits a converse without a reciprocal-intercept
assumption. Write $p_0=2<p_1<\cdots$, and suppose
$0\le a_j\le1$, $\sum_j a_j^2<\infty$, and
$\Psi_a=\Psi_{(1/p_j)}$. Define the complete signed defect
\[
 \Delta_{a,n}(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}
 \left\{\log(1+ta_j)-ta_j-
          \log(1+t/p_j)+t/p_j\right\},\qquad t>0.
\]
The term $t/p_j-ta_j$ is part of the observable and is retained throughout.

\begin{theorem}[Universal adaptive cutoff inverse]
For every positive probe sequence $(t_n)$,
\[
 \frac{p_n^2(p_n+t_n)}{t_n^2}\Delta_{a,n}(t_n)\longrightarrow0
\]
if and only if $a_j=1/p_{\sigma(j)}$ for a permutation $\sigma$ fixing
every sufficiently large index. In particular, for each fixed $t>0$,
\[
 \Delta_{a,n}(t)=o(p_n^{-3})
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 a\text{ is a finite permutation of }(1/p_j).
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Multiplicity recovery shows that the positive coordinates of $a$ are
exactly the distinct numbers $1/p_j$. First suppose no coordinate is
zero, so $a_j=1/p_{\sigma(j)}$ for a permutation $\sigma$.
Put $H_t(j)=\log(1+t/p_j)-t/p_j$, which is strictly increasing and negative.

For $B_n=\{0,\ldots,n\}$ and $S_n=\sigma(B_n)$, cancel the common
coordinates in the signed sum. If
$m_n=|S_n\setminus B_n|=|B_n\setminus S_n|$, every entering weight is
at least $H_t(n+1)$ and every leaving weight is at most $H_t(n)$.
Consequently
\[
 \Delta_{a,n}(t)\ge
 m_n\{H_t(n+1)-H_t(n)\}\ge0.                 \tag{21.1}
\]
Our signed scalar comparison gives, with $p=p_n$ and $q=p_{n+1}$,
\[
 H_t(n+1)-H_t(n)\ge\frac{t^2(q-p)}{pq(q+t)}.
\]
Eventually $q\le2p$, by the unconditional adjacent-prime ratio limit.
Since $q-p\ge1$, uniformly for every $t>0$,
\[
 \frac{p^2(p+t)}{t^2}\{H_t(n+1)-H_t(n)\}
 \ge\frac{p(p+t)}{q(q+t)}\ge\frac14.         \tag{21.2}
\]
A vanishing normalized defect therefore forces $m_n=0$ at every
sufficiently large index. Two successive invariant prefixes imply
$\sigma(n)=n$: the image of $n$ belongs to $B_n$ but cannot belong to
the invariant prefix $B_{n-1}$. Conversely, a permutation fixing a
tail preserves every sufficiently large prefix, whose defect is exactly zero.

It remains to rule out a zero coordinate. After the first zero has
entered $B_n$, the positive probabilities in that prefix correspond
to a set $S_n$ of at most $n$ distinct prime indices. If
$r=|S_n\setminus B_n|$ and $s=|B_n\setminus S_n|$, then $s\ge r+1$.
The same common-coordinate cancellation, now using the boundary
weight $H_t(n)$ on both sides, yields
\[
 \Delta_{a,n}(t)\ge(r-s)H_t(n)\ge-H_t(n).
\]
For $y>0$,
\[
 y-\log(1+y)\ge\frac{y^2}{2(1+y)}.
\]
Indeed the difference vanishes at zero and its derivative is
$y^2/(2(1+y)^2)\ge0$. Taking $y=t/p_n$ gives
\[
 \frac{p_n^2(p_n+t)}{t^2}\Delta_{a,n}(t)
 \ge\frac{p_n}{2}\longrightarrow\infty,     \tag{21.3}
\]
uniformly over all positive probes. Thus a zero coordinate cannot
satisfy the proposed accuracy condition. For fixed $t$, the
normalizing factor is asymptotic to $p_n^3/t^2$, proving the final equivalence.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Optimal distortion records the least recurring prime gap]
Let $G$ be the least positive integer occurring infinitely often as
$p_{n+1}-p_n$. This integer exists by bounded-gap supply and satisfies
$2\le G\le600$. Every same-profile law $a$ that is not a finite
permutation satisfies, for every positive probe sequence $(t_n)$,
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 \frac{p_n^2(p_n+t_n)}{t_n^2}\Delta_{a,n}(t_n)\ge G.
\]
There is one infinitely supported permutation law attaining equality
for every such probe sequence. It satisfies
$a(p)=1/p+O(p^{-2})$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Every gap smaller than $G$ occurs only finitely often; there are only
finitely many such integer gaps. Hence eventually $q-p\ge G$.
An infinite permutation moves infinitely many prefixes, and at each
such sufficiently large prefix (21.1) gives normalized defect at least
\[
 G\frac{p(p+t)}{q(q+t)}
 \ge G\left(\frac pq\right)^2\longrightarrow G,
\]
uniformly in $t>0$. Zero coordinates instead give the divergent
lower bound (21.3).

For attainment, choose disjoint adjacent prime pairs $p<q=p+G$
at arbitrarily large heights, and swap each pair. At any cutoff at
most one pair is incomplete; at its lower endpoint the exact defect
is $d_t(p,q)$. At every other prime cutoff it is zero.
The two-sided signed comparison gives
\[
 G\left(\frac pq\right)^2
 \le \frac{p^2(p+t)}{t^2}d_t(p,q)
 \le G\frac pq\le G.
\]
Both endpoints tend to $G$ uniformly in $t>0$, proving the claimed
limsup for every probe sequence, including probes tending to zero or
growing without bound. The bounded-displacement estimate proves the
stated local-law error.
\end{proof}

Thus the optimal normalized cost among all infinite rearrangements is
exactly $G$. In particular, cost $2$ is equivalent to infinitely many
twin primes. This is an inverse characterization, not a new bound
on $G$ or a zero-free region.

\begin{corollary}[The same constant governs late ascents]
Let $C$ denote the optimal distortion constant in the preceding theorem.
The critical late-ascent scale for the ordinary and generic odd laws is
\[
 b_*=\frac{1}{1+C}=\frac{1}{1+G}.
\]
Every $0<b<b_*$ admits arbitrarily late simultaneous ascents in both
laws; every $b>b_*$ has eventual strict descent beyond the corresponding
moving-rank scale. The endpoint $b=b_*$ is left open.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply the maintained recurring-gap/late-ascent critical-scale theorem
to the actual least recurring gap $G$. The preceding optimality theorem
identifies $C=G$.
\end{proof}

\begin{sloppypar}
The full adaptive inverse is maintained in
\path{Assembly/PrimeProfileZeroHoles.lean}. The exact optimality theorem,
including the definition and bounds $2\le G\le600$, its universal lower
threshold for every law \emph{not a finite permutation}, and a single
witness for all positive probe sequences, is maintained in
\path{Assembly/PrimeProfileMinimax.lean}. The formal statements use
the equivalent threshold formulation: every witness defect is at most
$G$, and for every $\varepsilon>0$ and $N$ some $n\ge N$ has defect
at least $G-\varepsilon$. The witness also satisfies the explicit bound
$|a_n-1/p_n|\le2G/p_n^2$ whenever $p_n\ge2G$, retaining all small-prime
exceptions. Its signed construction is in
\path{Assembly/PrimeProfileAttainment.lean} and
\path{Mathlib/Combinatorics/AdjacentSwapPrefix.lean}.
The late-ascent corollary has its own compiled declaration. The stronger
zero-hole inequality (21.3) retains its ordinary proof; the formal
inverse uses the sufficient uniform bound $p_n/4$. Finite permutations
have eventual zero defect and are excluded from every positive
lower-distortion assertion. No literature priority is claimed.
\end{sloppypar}

\subsection{Two-coordinate cutoff transport}

For a prime-coordinate permutation $\sigma$, define the crossing width
\begin{align*}
 m_n&=\bigl|\sigma(\{0,\ldots,n\})\setminus\{0,\ldots,n\}\bigr|,\\
 D_n(t)&=\frac{p_n^2(p_n+t)}{t^2}\Delta_{a,n}(t),
 \qquad a_j=1/p_{\sigma(j)}.
\end{align*}
Put $g_j=p_{j+1}-p_j$. The full signed defect detects more than whether
a prefix moves: it distinguishes simultaneous crossings.

\begin{lemma}[Complete two-crossing cost]
For every $n\ge1$ with $m_n\ge2$, and every $t>0$,
\[
 D_n(t)\ge
 T_n\left(\frac{p_n}{p_{n+2}}\right)^2,
 \qquad
 T_n=g_{n-1}+2g_n+g_{n+1}.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $W_t(j)=\log(1+t/p_j)-t/p_j$, an increasing weight.
Cancel common coordinates from the two prefixes. There are $m_n$
entering and $m_n$ leaving coordinates. Every entering coordinate
has index at least $n+1$, and at least one has index at least $n+2$.
Every leaving coordinate has index at most $n$, and at least one
has index at most $n-1$. Consequently,
\begin{align*}
 \Delta_{a,n}(t)
 &\ge m_n(W_t(n+1)-W_t(n))\\
 &\quad+W_t(n+2)-W_t(n+1)+W_t(n)-W_t(n-1)\\
 &\ge W_t(n+1)+W_t(n+2)-W_t(n)-W_t(n-1).
\end{align*}
The remaining crossing contributions have been retained in this
inequality; no unsigned coefficient bound is used.

The maintained scalar comparison, with a middle reference denominator,
states that for $t>0$, $0<p\le q$ and $p\le r$,
\[
 \frac{r^2(r+t)}{t^2}
 \left\{\log(1+t/q)-t/q-\log(1+t/p)+t/p\right\}
 \ge(q-p)\left(\frac r q\right)^2.
\]
Apply it to $(p,q,r)=(p_{n-1},p_{n+1},p_n)$ and
$(p_n,p_{n+2},p_n)$. Replace the first ratio by the smaller common
ratio $p_n/p_{n+2}$ and add the two signed costs. Their denominator
displacements sum to $T_n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The second prime-geometry threshold]
For $n\ge3$, $T_n\ge12$. Equality holds exactly when
\[
 (g_{n-1},g_n,g_{n+1})=(2,4,2)\quad\hbox{or}\quad(4,2,4).
\]
The exceptional initial values are $T_1=7$ and $T_2=10$; a
double crossing is impossible at $n=0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The four primes start beyond $3$, so all three gaps are even and
at least $2$. Two consecutive gaps cannot both be $2$: among
$p,p+2,p+4$, one is divisible by $3$ and exceeds $3$.
If the central gap is at least $4$, the weighted sum is at least
$2+2\cdot4+2=12$, with equality only for $(2,4,2)$.
If the central gap is $2$, both neighbours are at least $4$,
giving $4+2\cdot2+4=12$, with equality only for $(4,2,4)$.
The initial values use $2,3,5,7,11$ directly.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Double-crossing rigidity]
Fix any positive probe sequence $(t_n)$. For a law that is
\emph{not a finite permutation} and has $m_n\ge2$ at arbitrarily large
indices,
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}D_n(t_n)\ge12.
\]
Equivalently, an upper limit strictly below $12$ forces $m_n\le1$
eventually. If the upper limit is strictly below $14$ and double
crossings persist, one of the two displayed four-prime patterns
occurs arbitrarily far out.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The fixed-shift prime ratio $p_n/p_{n+2}$ tends to $1$.
The first two lemmas therefore give the lower threshold uniformly
over every positive probe sequence. If the integer $T_n$ is not $12$
at a sufficiently large double crossing, its parity gives $T_n\ge14$.
Such crossings would force an upper limit at least $14$, contradicting
the stated ceiling. Thus every sufficiently late double crossing
has one of the two equality patterns. Persistent double crossings
give their recurrence.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A sharp probability-profile equivalence]
The following statements are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Arbitrarily late consecutive four-prime windows, starting beyond $3$,
have offsets $(0,2,6,8)$ or $(0,4,6,10)$.
\item There is one permutation $\sigma$ with $m_n\ge2$ at arbitrarily
large cutoffs and
\[
 \forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists N\
 \forall n\ge N\ \forall t>0,\qquad D_n(t)\le12+\varepsilon.
\]
\end{enumerate}
For the law $a_j=1/p_{\sigma(j)}$ in the construction, $0<a_j\le1$,
$\sum_j a_j^2<\infty$, and the entire canonical profile equals that of
$u_j=1/p_j$. It is \emph{not a finite permutation}. For every positive
probe sequence, chosen after the law,
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}D_n(t_n)=12.
\]
The cutoff in the upper estimate is independent of the positive probe.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose the windows recur. Greedily choose an unbounded set $A$ of
their starting indices with $a+4\le b$ whenever $a<b$ in $A$.
This is possible by selecting the next start beyond the previous start
plus four. Set $B=A\cup(A+1)$. No $j$ and $j+2$ both belong to $B$:
such a pair would put two selected starts at distance less than four.
Define
\[
 \sigma(j)=
 \begin{cases}
 j+2,&j\in B,\\
 j-2,&j\notin B,\ j\ge2,\ j-2\in B,\\
 j,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
The absence of two-step chains makes this an involution. In each
selected block it swaps the first with the third coordinate and the
second with the fourth. Both prime displacements equal six, for either
offset pattern. At cutoff $n=a+1$ the distinct exterior coordinates
$a+2,a+3$ enter, so double crossings recur.

For an arbitrary real weight $W$, completed pairs cancel exactly:
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{j=0}^{k+1}\bigl(W(\sigma(j))-W(j)\bigr)
 &=\mathbf1_B(k+1)\bigl(W(k+3)-W(k+1)\bigr)\\
 &\quad+\mathbf1_B(k)\bigl(W(k+2)-W(k)\bigr).
\end{align*}
This identity includes every finite-prefix term. It follows either
by pairing completed swaps or by induction, retaining the two pairs
that can cross the boundary.

We also need the upper comparison at a middle reference. Write
$\delta_t(p,q)=\log(1+t/q)-t/q-\log(1+t/p)+t/p$.
For $t>0$ and $0<p\le r\le q$,
\[
 \frac{r^2(r+t)}{t^2}\delta_t(p,q)
 \le(q-p)\left(\frac r p\right)^2.
\]
Indeed,
$\delta_t(p,q)=\int_p^q t^2/(x^2(x+t))\,dx
 \le t^2(q-p)/(pq(p+t))$.
After normalization, the desired comparison follows from the
nonnegative identity
\begin{align*}
 &\left\{(q-p)(r/p)^2-
   \frac{(q-p)r^2(r+t)}{pq(p+t)}\right\}p^2q(p+t)\\
 &\hspace{25mm}=(q-p)r^2\{p(q-r)+t(q-p)\}\ge0.
\end{align*}
All denominators are positive.

Apply the exact prefix identity to $W=W_t$ and this upper comparison
with reference $r=p_{k+1}$. Each present pair has displacement six.
The pair starting at $k+1$ costs at most $6$, and the pair starting
at $k$ costs at most $6(p_{k+1}/p_k)^2$. Absent pairs contribute zero.
Thus, for every $k\ge0$ and $t>0$,
\[
 D_{k+1}(t)\le6(p_{k+1}/p_k)^2+6.
\]
The right side tends to $12$, proving the upper estimate with a
cutoff uniform in $t$. The previous lower threshold at recurring
double crossings supplies arbitrarily late values at least
$12-\varepsilon$ for every positive probe sequence. This proves
the exact upper limit, including the rank shift $n=k+1$.

Reindexing preserves square summability and the canonical entire
product, so the constructed sequence has the asserted probability
properties and profile. A finite permutation fixes all sufficiently
late coordinates and preserves all later prefixes; it cannot have
recurring double crossings. The selected blocks start beyond $3$,
so the exceptional prime coordinates $2,3$ are fixed. The first
cutoff $n=0$ plays no role in the eventual estimate.

Conversely, use the constant probe $t=1$ and $\varepsilon=1$ in
statement~(ii). The eventual ceiling is $13<14$. The compiled inverse
criterion therefore forces an equality pattern at every sufficiently
late double crossing. Their recurrence gives statement~(i).
\end{proof}

The complete signed finite-set inequality is maintained in
\path{Mathlib/Combinatorics/TwoBoundaryTransport.lean}; the actual
prime-profile specialization and its middle-prime normalization are
maintained in \path{Helpers/PrimeProfileDoubleCrossing.lean}.
The prime-congruence classification is maintained in
\path{Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/WeightedQuadruplet.lean}.
The quantified uniform lower threshold and arbitrarily late witnesses
are compiled there, as is the eventual width bound under a ceiling
$C<12$. Their limsup formulations are mathematical consequences of
these quantified statements. The stronger inverse under an eventual
ceiling $C<14$ is maintained in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileQuadrupletInverse.lean}.
The exceptional initial values $7,10$ retain their elementary checks.

\begin{sloppypar}
The converse is maintained in
\path{Assembly/PrimeProfileQuadrupletAttainment.lean}.
It formally proves the actual probability-profile equivalence, with
one law before every positive probe, as well as positivity, the unit
bound, square summability, full profile equality, arbitrarily late
lower witnesses and exclusion of finite permutation representations.
Its compiled upper formulation has one cutoff for all positive probes.
The sparse block selection and the complete uniform upper estimate
are maintained in \path{Helpers/PrimeQuadrupletSupport.lean} and
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileTwoStepTransport.lean}; the distance-two
involution, exact signed prefix identity and double-crossing witness
are reusable owners in
\path{Mathlib/Combinatorics/TwoStepSwapPrefix.lean}.
The matching middle-reference upper comparison is maintained in
\path{Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/CompensatedReciprocalNormalization.lean}.
No infinitude of either four-prime pattern is asserted.
\end{sloppypar}

\subsection{Quantized distortion and probe independence}

The whole finite transport controls the distortion of every bounded
profile. For a permutation $\sigma$, put
\[
 S_n=\sum_{j=0}^{n}p_{\sigma(j)}-\sum_{j=0}^{n}p_j.
\]
This is the exact signed prime-denominator transport. It is nonnegative,
because the first $n+1$ primes have the least possible sum.

\begin{lemma}[Localization and a uniform transport error]
Let $r=p_n$, $C\ge0$, $C<r/4$, and suppose that $D_n(t)\le C$
for one $t>0$. Then every entering prime is at most $r+4C$,
and every leaving prime is at least $r-C$. Moreover,
\[
 0\le S_n\le4C,
 \qquad
 S_n\left(\frac r{r+4C}\right)^2
 \le D_n(v)\le
 S_n\left(\frac r{r-C}\right)^2
 \quad(v>0),
\]
and, uniformly over every new positive probe,
\[
 \left|D_n(v)-S_n\right|\le\frac{32C^2}{p_n}.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $E$ and $L$ be the finite sets of entering and leaving prime values,
after common coordinates cancel. They have equal cardinalities.
For $\delta_t(p,q)=\log(1+t/q)-t/q-\log(1+t/p)+t/p$ and
$N_r(t)=r^2(r+t)/t^2$, exact insertion of the common reference gives
\begin{align*}
 D_n(t)&=N_r(t)\left\{\sum_{q\in E}\delta_t(r,q)
                   +\sum_{p\in L}\delta_t(p,r)\right\},\\
 S_n&=\sum_{q\in E}(q-r)+\sum_{p\in L}(r-p).
\end{align*}
Every retained summand is nonnegative. Thus each reference cost is
at most the full cost $C$. The middle-reference lower comparison
gives $N_r(t)\delta_t(p,r)\ge r-p$ for a leaving prime.
If an entering prime satisfies $q\ge2r$, monotonicity in $q$ gives
\[
 N_r(t)\delta_t(r,q)\ge
 N_r(t)\delta_t(r,2r)\ge r/4,
\]
contrary to $C<r/4$. Hence $q<2r$, and the lower comparison gives
$N_r(t)\delta_t(r,q)\ge(q-r)(r/q)^2\ge(q-r)/4$.
This proves both localization bounds for every transported coordinate.

For any $v>0$ and $0<\ell\le p\le r\le q\le u$, the two scalar
comparisons already proved give
\[
 (q-p)(r/u)^2\le N_r(v)\delta_v(p,q)
               \le(q-p)(r/\ell)^2.
\]
Use $\ell=r-C>0$ and $u=r+4C$, and sum this inequality on the
\emph{same} two reference transports. This proves the displayed
two-sided comparison for $D_n(v)$ and $S_n$. Applying its lower
bound at the original probe $t$, with $r/(r+4C)>1/2$, gives $S_n\le4C$.

Write $\lambda=(r/(r+4C))^2$ and $\mu=(r/(r-C))^2$.
The following nonnegative identities justify the remaining constants:
\begin{align*}
 \{\lambda-(1-8C/r)\}\,r(r+4C)^2
   &=48rC^2+128C^3,\\
 \{1+4C/r-\mu\}\,r(r-C)^2
   &=C\{2r(r-4C)+rC+4C^2\}.
\end{align*}
Thus $1-8C/r\le\lambda$ and $\mu\le1+4C/r$.
Retaining the signed two-sided comparison and $0\le S_n\le4C$ gives
\begin{align*}
 S_n-D_n(v)&\le8CS_n/r\le32C^2/r,\\
 D_n(v)-S_n&\le4CS_n/r\le16C^2/r.
\end{align*}
The asserted absolute bound concerns this isolated error after the
complete signed transport has been compared.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A probe-independent even distortion level]
Fix a permutation $\sigma$. Suppose that one positive probe sequence
$(t_n)$ has eventually bounded normalized distortion:
$D_n(t_n)\le C$ for all sufficiently large $n$, with one fixed $C\ge0$.
Then there is an integer $K\ge0$, independent of subsequent probes,
such that
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}D_n(v_n)=2K
\]
for every positive sequence $(v_n)$. More strongly, for every
$\varepsilon>0$ one cutoff gives $D_n(v)\le2K+\varepsilon$
for all later $n$ and all $v>0$; arbitrarily late cutoffs satisfy
$D_n(v)\ge2K-\varepsilon$ for \emph{all} $v>0$ simultaneously.
The level is zero exactly for a finite permutation.
Consequently a law that is \emph{not a finite permutation} has a
positive even level whenever one probe has eventually bounded cost.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply the lemma at every sufficiently large cutoff using the original
probe $t_n$. Since $p_n\to\infty$, it gives a single eventual bound
$0\le S_n\le4C$ and an error tending to zero uniformly in every new
positive probe. This step only uses $p_n\ge n$, not a quantitative
prime number theorem.

Once $p_n-C>2$, every entering and leaving prime is odd.
The two transported sets have equal cardinalities, so their sums
have the same parity. Hence $S_n$ is a nonnegative even integer.
Only finitely many such values lie in $[0,4C]$. At least one occurs
arbitrarily late; let $2K$ be the largest value that does.
Every larger value then disappears after one cutoff, so $S_n\le2K$
eventually, whereas $S_n=2K$ at arbitrarily late cutoffs.
The error estimate in the lemma gives both uniform assertions,
with the same cutoffs for all positive probes, and therefore every
displayed limsup.

A finite permutation has zero defect at every sufficiently late
prefix, so its level is zero. Conversely, level zero and
nonnegativity give $D_n(1)\to0$. The maintained adaptive cutoff
inverse then forces the permutation to be finite.
\end{proof}

\begin{sloppypar}
The localization theorem is maintained in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileCostLocalization.lean}.
The complete cross-probe comparison, nonnegative transport, budget
$S_n\le4C$ and explicit error $32C^2/p_n$ are maintained in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileTransportComparison.lean}.
The even-lattice and uniform-tail owners are
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileTransportParity.lean} and
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileTransportTail.lean}.
The quantified uniform even-level theorem is maintained in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileQuantization.lean}.
Its single cutoff is selected before every new positive probe,
and its lower witnesses are shared by all positive probes.
The limsup wording and zero-level characterization above are
mathematical consequences of that compiled formulation and the
previously maintained cutoff inverse; they are not advertised as
separate named Lean declarations.
The generic finite reference transport and parity lemmas are maintained
in \path{Mathlib/Combinatorics/FinitePrefixDisplacement.lean};
the bounded natural-sequence recurrence lemma is maintained in
\path{Mathlib/Order/BoundedNatSequence.lean}.
No independent arithmetic bound on an RH detector is inferred from
this profile classification.
\end{sloppypar}

\begin{theorem}[Complete shared-profile distortion alternatives]
Let $a_j\in[0,1]$, $\sum_j a_j^2<\infty$, and suppose its entire
canonical profile equals the reciprocal-prime profile.
Write
\[
 D_{a,n}(t)=\frac{p_n^2(p_n+t)}{t^2}
 \sum_{j=0}^{n}
 \left\{\log(1+ta_j)-ta_j-\log(1+t/p_j)+t/p_j\right\}.
\]
Exactly one of the following alternatives holds:
\begin{enumerate}
\item There is an integer $K\ge0$ such that, for every $\varepsilon>0$,
one cutoff gives $D_{a,n}(t)\le2K+\varepsilon$ for all later $n$
and every $t>0$, while arbitrarily late cutoffs satisfy
$D_{a,n}(t)\ge2K-\varepsilon$ for every $t>0$ simultaneously.
\item For every real $C$ and every $N$, some $n\ge N$ satisfies
$D_{a,n}(t)>C$ for every $t>0$ simultaneously.
\end{enumerate}
Thus every positive adaptive probe sequence has the same upper limit,
belonging to $\{0,2,4,\ldots\}\cup\{+\infty\}$.
The upper limit is zero exactly when $a$ is a finite coordinate
permutation of the reciprocal-prime law.
If even one coordinate of $a$ is zero, the stronger conclusion
\[
 \forall C\in\mathbb R\ \exists M\
 \forall n\ge M\ \forall t>0:\quad D_{a,n}(t)>C
\]
holds.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
First suppose that all coordinates are positive. The maintained entire-profile
reconstruction gives one permutation $\sigma$ with
$a_j=1/p_{\sigma(j)}$. Substituting this equality into the complete signed
prefix sum gives $D_{a,n}(t)=D_n(t)$ exactly.

If the constant unit probe $D_n(1)$ is eventually bounded, the preceding
theorem gives the first alternative. Otherwise, fix $C\in\mathbb R$ and
$N$, and put $A=\max(C,0)$. There are arbitrarily late cutoffs with
$D_n(1)>12A$; choose one beyond $N$ with $p_n>4A$.
If any positive probe $v$ had $D_n(v)\le A$, the localization lemma,
applied with $v$ as the original probe, would give
\[
 D_n(1)\le S_n+\frac{32A^2}{p_n}
          \le4A+8A=12A,
\]
a contradiction. Thus every positive probe exceeds $A\ge C$ at this
same cutoff. This proves the second alternative without selecting
different cutoffs for different probes.

If $a_i=0$, the maintained zero-hole estimate instead gives
$D_{a,n}(t)\ge p_n/4$ for every $n\ge i$ and every $t>0$.
Since $p_n\ge n$, it gives the asserted eventual uniform divergence.
Nonnegativity of the coordinates makes these two cases exhaustive.

The alternatives are disjoint: in the first, use $\varepsilon=1$;
in the second, use $C=2K+2$ and a cutoff beyond that upper envelope.
Their quantified bounds give the stated common upper limit.
A finite permutation has eventual zero defect. Conversely, the zero
level is incompatible with a zero coordinate, so reconstruction reduces
it to the positive permutation case, where the preceding cutoff inverse
applies. In particular, a law that is \emph{not a finite permutation}
has a positive even level or infinite upper limit.
\end{proof}

\begin{flushleft}
The quantitative implication
$D_n(v)\le C\Rightarrow D_n(1)\le12C$ for $p_n>4C$, the shared
unbounded witnesses, and the permutation dichotomy are compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileUniformDichotomy.lean}.
The general-law dichotomy and eventual uniform zero-hole divergence are
compiled in \path{Assembly/PrimeProfileDistortionClassification.lean}.
The exact coordinate-substitution identity is maintained in
\path{Assembly/PrimeProfileCutoffInverse.lean}.
The formal alternatives use the displayed quantified upper and lower
envelopes; their upper-limit wording, exclusivity and zero-level
characterization are mathematical consequences, not additional named
Lean declarations. The prime $2$ is retained in every finite prefix;
evenness is used only after the localization window excludes it.
No new prime-pattern infinitude or improvement of the certified
$7/8$ RH boundary follows from this classification.
\end{flushleft}

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite-accuracy transport readout]
Fix a prime-coordinate permutation $\sigma$ and a cutoff with $r=p_n\ge3$.
Suppose $C\ge0$, $D_n(t)\le C$ for one $t>0$, and $32C^2<r$.
Then there is one integer $K\ge0$ such that
\[
 S_n=2K,\qquad |D_n(v)-2K|<1\quad\hbox{for every }v>0.
\]
For each positive probe $v$ separately, $K$ is the unique nonnegative
integer $j$ with $|D_n(v)-2j|<1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The precision hypothesis implies $C<r/4$: otherwise
$32C^2\ge2r^2\ge6r>r$. Hence $r-C>3r/4\ge9/4>2$.
Localization excludes the exceptional prime $2$ from the transported
sets, so their exact signed sum is $S_n=2K$ for some $K\ge0$.
The uniform error estimate gives
$|D_n(v)-2K|\le32C^2/r<1$ for every $v>0$.
Two distinct even integers have distance at least $2$; they cannot both
lie at distance strictly less than $1$ from the same observation.
This proves uniqueness for each probe, with the same recovered value
for every probe.
\end{proof}

The theorem has no global boundedness assumption.
For example, suppose one positive adaptive probe has eventual cost at
most $C_n\ge0$, where $C_n^2/p_n\to0$. Then, at every sufficiently late
cutoff, \emph{every} positive probe determines the exact moving transport
$S_n$ by its unique nearest even integer. Moreover the uniform readout
error is at most $32C_n^2/p_n\to0$.
The levels may grow with $n$; no constant distortion level is assumed.
The finite theorem, including per-probe uniqueness, is compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileTransportReadout.lean}. The growing-budget
statement is a mathematical consequence of that finite certificate and
the maintained uniform error estimate, not a separately named limit
declaration.

\begin{theorem}[Pairwise recovery and discrete separation]
Let $\sigma,\tau$ be two permutations of the prime coordinates. Suppose
one positive adaptive probe sequence $(t_n)$ and one $C\ge0$ satisfy
\[
 D_n^\sigma(t_n)\le C,\qquad D_n^\tau(t_n)\le C
\]
for all sufficiently large $n$.
Then
\[
 |D_n^\sigma(t_n)-D_n^\tau(t_n)|\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \sigma(n)=\tau(n)\ \hbox{for all sufficiently large }n.
\]
More quantitatively, if the two permutations do not agree eventually,
then for every $\varepsilon>0$ and every $N$ there is $n\ge N$ such that
\[
 |D_n^\sigma(v)-D_n^\tau(w)|\ge2-\varepsilon
 \qquad\hbox{for every }v,w>0
\]
simultaneously. The cutoff is shared by all pairs of probes.
The hypothesis of nonagreement excludes pairs differing only finitely;
in particular, at least one permutation is \emph{not a finite permutation}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At a cutoff with $p_n>4C$ and $p_n-C>2$, let
$S_n^\sigma=2K$ and $S_n^\tau=2L$ be the exact signed transports.
For any two positive probes, the two previously proved error bounds give
\[
 |S_n^\sigma-S_n^\tau|
 \le |D_n^\sigma(v)-D_n^\tau(w)|+\frac{64C^2}{p_n}.
\]
Consequently
\[
 |D_n^\sigma(v)-D_n^\tau(w)|+\frac{64C^2}{p_n}<2
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad S_n^\sigma=S_n^\tau,
\]
because distinct even integers have distance at least $2$.

Exact consecutive-prefix subtraction gives
\[
 S_{n+1}^\sigma-S_n^\sigma
   =p_{\sigma(n+1)}-p_{n+1},
\]
and the same identity holds for $\tau$.
Thus eventual equality of the signed transport sequences forces
eventual equality of the prime coordinates, hence of their indices.
If $\sigma$ and $\tau$ do not agree eventually, the transports therefore
differ at arbitrarily late cutoffs. Choose one beyond $N$, both cost
cutoffs, and a threshold where
\[
 p_n>\max(4C,\,2+C,\,64C^2/\varepsilon).
\]
Its two even transports differ by at least $2$. The displayed error
comparison then proves the claimed $2-\varepsilon$ separation for every
pair of positive probes at this one cutoff.

In particular, asymptotically equal observations are incompatible with
eventual nonagreement: use separation with $\varepsilon=1$ and the
original probes. Conversely, if the permutations agree past a cutoff,
their images of every later finite prefix are identical. Indeed those
images have the same complement, namely the common image of the
remaining tail. All weighted prefix sums, and therefore the normalized
defects at each common probe, then agree exactly.
\end{proof}

\begin{flushleft}
The finite comparison, shared separation witnesses, and pairwise inverse
are compiled in \path{Helpers/PrimeProfilePairwiseRigidity.lean}.
The formal inverse states convergence by its explicit
$\varepsilon$--cutoff condition. The reusable consecutive-defect identity,
two-prefix coordinate recovery, equal-tail prefix-image identity and
eventual exact-defect equivalence are compiled in
\path{Mathlib/Combinatorics/FinitePrefixDisplacement.lean}.
This recovery compares two possibly infinite rearrangements; it does not
assume either is the reference order. It identifies their tails even
though their entire canonical profiles are equal. The separation
constant $2$ is proved, with no claim here that it is attained by two
distinct classes.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{Two-moment compensation and a larger exact-readout range}

The preceding readout uses one logarithmic probe. Two finite probability
moments cancel its first nonlinear transport error. Write
$I_n=\{0,\ldots,n\}$, $A_n=\sigma(I_n)$, $r=p_n$, and
\[
 E_n=A_n\setminus I_n,\qquad L_n=I_n\setminus A_n.
\]
Both sets are finite and have equal cardinality. Define
\[
 \Delta_j=\sum_{i=0}^n\left(p_i^{-j}-p_{\sigma(i)}^{-j}\right),
 \qquad Q_n=3r^2\Delta_1-r^3\Delta_2.
\]
Thus $Q_n$ uses only the first two finite power sums of the actual
Bernoulli probabilities and their reference probabilities. These are
finite-prefix observations; equality of the entire canonical profiles
does not assert equality of these labelled finite sums.

\begin{theorem}[Cubic compensated transport and exact readout]
Suppose $D_n(t)\le C$ for one $t>0$, where $C\ge0$ and $C<r/4$.
Then the full signed transport satisfies
\[
 0\le S_n-Q_n\le\frac{64C^3}{r^2}.
\]
If instead one assumes $r\ge3$ and $32C^3<r^2$, the condition $C<r/4$
