\subsection{RH readout on the same prime-quadruplet transport}

The short-quadruplet theorem and the moving-coefficient RH criterion
apply to the same probability law. The bridge is a quantitative
bound on its actual local odds. It also clarifies which existential
statement includes an additional prime-pattern conjecture.

\begin{lemma}[Bounded prime reindexing preserves the RH law class]
Let $f$ send every prime $p$ to a prime $q=f(p)$, and suppose
$|q-p|\le H$ for every prime, where $H\ge0$ is fixed. Then
\[
 \left|\frac1{q-1}-\frac1{p-1}\right|
 \le \frac{H(H+1)}{(p-1)^2}.
\]
Thus the weights $w(p)=1/(f(p)-1)$ define a qualifying quadratic
prime law with $\nu=1$ and error constant $H(H+1)$.
Bijectivity is unnecessary for this qualification.
For each fixed such law and every fixed $\alpha>0$, its existing
moving-coefficient reference-sign criterion is equivalent to RH.
All primes, including $2$, belong to the stated domain.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $x=p-1\ge1$ and $y=q-1\ge1$. The displacement hypothesis gives
$|x-y|\le H$ and
\[
 x\le y+H\le(H+1)y.
\]
Consequently, keeping the exact reciprocal difference,
\[
 \begin{split}
 \left|\frac1y-\frac1x\right|x^2
   &=|x-y|\frac{x}{y}\\
   &\le H(H+1).
 \end{split}
\]
Division by the positive $x^2$ proves the estimate. The weights
are positive, $\nu=1>0$, and $H(H+1)\ge0$, verifying every field
of the maintained quadratic-law definition. The established
quadratic-law RH equivalence now applies to this fixed law.
No RH hypothesis is used to establish its qualification.
\end{proof}

To state the specialization precisely, let $B\subset\mathbb N$
satisfy
\[
 n\in B\ \Longrightarrow\ n+2\notin B,\qquad
 n\in B\ \Longrightarrow\ p_{n+2}=p_n+6.
\]
Let $e_B$ be the existing permutation exchanging $n$ with $n+2$
for each $n\in B$, fixing all other coordinates. Selected starts
may be adjacent; the first condition prevents conflicting edges.
The associated same-profile probability law is
\[
 a_i=\frac1{p_{e_B(i)}}.
\]
For a prime-labelled coordinate $p=p_i$ its actual local odds are
\[
 w_B(p_i)=\frac{a_i}{1-a_i}=\frac1{p_{e_B(i)}-1}.
\]
Write $\mathcal L_B$ for the quadratic law with these weights.
Its finite coefficients and ratios are
\[
 \begin{split}
 E_{B,N,r}&=[z^r]\prod_{p_i\le N}(1+w_B(p_i)z),\\
 R_{B,N,r}&=\frac{E_{B,N,r-1}}{E_{B,N,r}},\\
 L(N)&=\gamma+\log\log\vartheta(N),\qquad
 r_N=\lfloor\alpha L(N)\rfloor .
 \end{split}
\]
The reference ratio $\mathcal R_{B,r}(u)$ is the maintained
factorial-convolution ratio formed from the entire reference
profile of $\mathcal L_B$. The subscript records its defining law;
the following invariance theorem identifies it with one canonical
reference. The natural number $N$ is an inclusive prime-value
cutoff; $i$ remains the prime index. On the finite observed
probability prefix the earlier notation is $n+1$, with endpoint
$p_n$. No scalar is substituted into the permutation map.

\begin{theorem}[RH on a fixed constellation witness]
For every fixed $B$ satisfying the two conditions above,
$\mathcal L_B$ has $\nu=1$ and error constant $42$.
For every fixed $\alpha>0$,
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 R_{B,N,r_N}<\mathcal R_{B,r_N}(L(N))
       \quad\hbox{for every sufficiently large }N.
\]
The same fixed $B$ is used throughout the limit.
The coefficient denominators and moving ranks are positive
for all sufficiently large $N$, as provided by the existing
quadratic-law comparison.
Recurrence of the selected blocks is unnecessary for this
fixed-witness equivalence.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
A selected start $i\in B$ is sent to $i+2$, so its prime value
increases by exactly $6$. If $i$ is the upper end of a selected
edge, then $i\ge2$, $i-2\in B$, and its prime value decreases by
exactly $6$. Every other coordinate is fixed. Thus
\[
 |p_{e_B(i)}-p_i|\le6\qquad(i\ge0).
\]
This covers every coordinate, including the indices below two
in the backward-edge test. Apply the preceding lemma with $H=6$:
\[
 |w_B(p)-1/(p-1)|\le42/(p-1)^2.
\]
The odds identity is exact since all moved values are primes
at least $2$. The existing quadratic-law RH theorem, with
$\nu=1$, now gives precisely the displayed moving-rank
equivalence for $\mathcal L_B$.
\end{proof}

Here is how the established sign comparison preserves the zero
information. For this fixed $B,\alpha$, there is $C\ge0$ such
that eventually, with the actual Nicolas log oscillation $F(N)$,
\[
 \begin{array}{rcl}
 F(N)>C/N&\Longrightarrow&
       \mathcal R_{B,r_N}(L(N))<R_{B,N,r_N},\\
 F(N)<-C/N&\Longrightarrow&
       R_{B,N,r_N}<\mathcal R_{B,r_N}(L(N)).
 \end{array}
\]
Under RH the installed Nicolas estimate eventually gives the
second inequality for every fixed $C$. If RH fails, its installed
unbounded positive excursions give $N F(N)>C+1$ arbitrarily far
out, forcing the opposite coefficient sign. This contradicts an
eventual upper-reference sign. The proof uses an unconditional
comparison and the appropriate RH or non-RH Nicolas input in
each direction. The cutoff and the whole signed oscillation are
retained. No uniform cutoff over $B$ or $\alpha$ is asserted.

\begin{corollary}[The exact existential constellation statement]
Fix $\alpha>0$. The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item RH holds and short prime quadruplets recur arbitrarily far out.
\item There is a set $B$ satisfying the two support conditions above,
with arbitrarily large $k$ for which $k,k+1\in B$, and whose
actual law $\mathcal L_B$ has
$R_{B,N,r_N}<\mathcal R_{B,r_N}(L(N))$ eventually.
\end{enumerate}
The short quadruplets are the two already defined consecutive-prime
families
\[
 (p,p+2,p+6,p+8),\qquad (p,p+4,p+6,p+10),\qquad p>3.
\]
No disjointness assumption is imposed on their arithmetic
occurrences. The support construction selects separated
four-coordinate blocks when constructing its one permutation.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
If RH and recurrence hold, the maintained support-selection theorem
supplies $B$ with unbounded adjacent selected starts. The fixed-law
RH equivalence gives its eventual coefficient sign.

Conversely, the coefficient sign gives RH by that same equivalence.
An adjacent selected pair $k,k+1$ forces two coordinates across the
prefix cutoff $k+2$. These double crossings recur arbitrarily far
out. The maintained signed transport estimate gives, for every
$\varepsilon>0$, eventually and uniformly over all positive probes,
\[
 D_{k+1}(t;e_B)\le12+\varepsilon.
\]
Take $\varepsilon=1$ and the constant probe $t=1$. The resulting
eventual ceiling $13<14$, together with recurrent double crossings,
satisfies every hypothesis of the existing quadruplet inverse
theorem. It yields recurrent short quadruplets. In particular the
critical cost and the coefficient-sign readout concern the same
transport, rather than independently chosen probability laws.
\end{proof}

This establishes an RH connection for the constellation witness.
It does not identify RH with quadruplet recurrence alone: the
existential criterion above detects their conjunction. The cost
threshold $12$ records local four-prime geometry; the moving
coefficient sign records the Nicolas oscillation. The full
Bernoulli profile alone does not distinguish these transports,
since it is invariant under coordinate permutations.

\begin{flushleft}
The reciprocal displacement bound, complete quadratic-law adapter,
prime-index conversion and bounded-reindexing RH criterion are
compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileBoundedReindexing.lean}.
The complete distance-six verification, actual probability-odds
identity, explicit law constants, fixed-witness RH equivalence and
existential conjunction criterion are compiled in
\path{Assembly/PrimeProfileQuadrupletRH.lean}, exported through
\path{Assembly/ProbabilityProfiles.lean}.
The existing short-quadruplet attainment, support selection and
inverse theorem are consumed without a new prime-pattern supply
assumption. No external-priority claim is made. The certified
zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.
\end{flushleft}


\clearpage
\subsection{One canonical RH reference for every constellation transport}

The entire reference in the preceding criterion is independent of the
chosen block support. This follows from an exact infinite signed pairing,
using the quadratic bounds to justify the complete series.

\begin{theorem}[Involutive invariance of the entire reference]
Let $\mathcal A,\mathcal B$ be qualifying quadratic prime laws with
the same parameter $\nu$. Suppose $\sigma$ is an involutive permutation
of the primes and
\[
 w_{\mathcal A}(p)=w_{\mathcal B}(\sigma(p))
       \qquad\hbox{for every prime }p.
\]
Then their entire reference profiles are equal. Consequently all
reference Taylor coefficients are equal, including degree zero.
Their factorial-convolution reference ratios agree wherever the common
denominator is nonzero.
The two laws may have different quadratic error constants.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For a fixed real $t\ge0$, define the complete signed difference
\[
 \begin{split}
 g_t(p)
 &=\left[\log(1+w_{\mathcal A}(p)t)
           -\nu t\log(1+1/(p-1))\right]\\
 &\quad-\left[\log(1+w_{\mathcal B}(p)t)
           -\nu t\log(1+1/(p-1))\right]\\
 &=\log(1+w_{\mathcal B}(\sigma(p))t)
                  -\log(1+w_{\mathcal B}(p)t).
 \end{split}
\]
Each bracketed profile series is absolutely convergent by its proved
quadratic-law logarithmic bound. Their difference is therefore summable.
The normalizing terms have been canceled exactly. Involutivity gives
\[
 g_t(\sigma(p))=-g_t(p).
\]
Reindex only this summable difference:
\[
 \sum_p g_t(p)=\sum_p g_t(\sigma(p))
             =-\sum_p g_t(p).
\]
Its sum is zero. The two logarithmic reference profiles are thus equal
for every $t\ge0$. Exponentiating and applying the existing positive-real
product identity shows equality of their entire profiles on the
nonnegative real axis.

Both reference profiles are entire by the established quadratic-law
construction. Their coincidence set has $0$ as an accumulation point:
for every $\varepsilon>0$, the nonzero real point $\varepsilon/2$
lies within distance $\varepsilon$ of zero and has equal profile values.
The analytic identity principle therefore gives equality on the whole
complex plane. Every Taylor coefficient at zero is equal. Substitution
in the finite factorial convolutions proves the reference-ratio equality
on its stated nonzero-denominator domain.
\end{proof}

Only the signed difference is reindexed in this proof. In particular,
the argument does not rearrange either divergent positive sum
$\sum_p\log(1+w_{\mathcal A}(p)t)$ or
$\sum_p\log(1+w_{\mathcal B}(p)t)$ separately.

Let $\mathcal L_0$ be the fixed canonical law with
\[
 w_0(p)=\frac1{p-1},\qquad \nu=1,\qquad\hbox{quadratic error }0,
\]
and denote its reference ratio by $\mathcal R_{0,r}(u)$.

\begin{corollary}[A common canonical constellation RH criterion]
For every fixed support $B$ from the preceding subsection,
\[
 \Phi_{\mathcal L_B}=\Phi_{\mathcal L_0},\qquad
 \mathcal R_{B,r}(u)=\mathcal R_{0,r}(u)
\]
on the common nonzero-denominator domain of the ratios. The reference
clock is exactly the same at every cutoff. For each fixed $\alpha>0$,
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 R_{B,N,r_N}<\mathcal R_{0,r_N}(L(N))
       \quad\hbox{eventually in }N.
\]
Thus the reference profile, its coefficients, its clock and its ratio
are fixed independently of $B$. The actual finite coefficient ratio
$R_{B,N,r_N}$ still belongs to the chosen constellation transport.
The existential criterion of the preceding subsection remains valid
with $\mathcal R_0$ replacing every $\mathcal R_B$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The exact correspondence $i\mapsto p_i$ identifies the permutation
$e_B$ with an involutive permutation of the primes. Its inverse
coordinate is the number of primes strictly below the prime label;
the two inverse identities hold on the entire enumeration, beginning
at $p_0=2$. The actual odds satisfy
\[
 w_B(p_i)=w_0(p_{e_B(i)}).
\]
Both laws qualify: $\mathcal L_B$ has the already proved error bound
$42$, and $\mathcal L_0$ has error zero; both have $\nu=1$.
Apply the theorem to obtain equality of the entire profiles and every
reference coefficient and ratio. The clock identity follows directly
from their common $\nu=1$. Substitution into the already compiled
fixed-witness and existential RH equivalences gives the assertions.
Their eventual positive-denominator domain is retained.
\end{proof}

The infinite signed cancellation here removes the change in the
reference caused by reindexing. The finite-cutoff coefficient ratio,
the complete compensated cutoff cost, and the Nicolas clock oscillation
remain in their respective statements. In particular the RH sign
response is preserved; no zero signal is set to zero by this pairing.
The cost threshold continues to distinguish the local prime geometry
even though these entire references coincide.

\begin{flushleft}
The summable logarithmic pairing, entire-function equality, equality of
all reference coefficients and ratios, and exact prime-coordinate
conversion are compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileReferenceInvariance.lean}.
Their application to the actual quadruplet law, the canonical clock,
the fixed-reference RH criterion and the fixed-reference existential
criterion are compiled in
\path{Assembly/PrimeProfileQuadrupletCanonicalRH.lean},
exported through \path{Assembly/ProbabilityProfiles.lean}.
The preceding corollary concerns each fixed support and rank parameter.
The next theorem makes the cutoff uniform over all supports, and more
generally over every prime permutation of a fixed displacement radius.
No uniformity in a growing rank parameter is asserted.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{A uniform RH criterion over bounded prime permutations}

Write $P_N=\{p\text{ prime}:p\le N\}$ and $w_0(p)=1/(p-1)$.
For a permutation $\sigma$ of the primes put
\[
 S_{\sigma,N}=\sigma(P_N),\qquad
 E_{\sigma,N,r}=e_r\bigl((w_0(p))_{p\in S_{\sigma,N}}\bigr),\qquad
 R_{\sigma,N,r}=\frac{E_{\sigma,N,r-1}}{E_{\sigma,N,r}}.
\]
Thus these are exactly the coefficients obtained by assigning odds
$w_0(\sigma(p))$ to the sampled prime coordinate $p\le N$.
For the identity permutation write $E_{0,N,r}$ and $R_{0,N,r}$.
The benchmark remains the fixed canonical reference
$\mathcal R_{0,r}(u)$, rather than a reference chosen after $\sigma$.
Set
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 A(N)=\sum_{p\in P_N}\log(1+w_0(p)),\\
 L(N)=\gamma+\log\log\vartheta(N),\qquad
 r_N=\lfloor\alpha L(N)\rfloor .
 \end{gathered}
\]

\begin{theorem}[Uniform bounded-displacement RH criterion]
Fix $H\in\mathbb N$ and $\alpha>0$. Let $\mathcal S_H$ be the
class of all permutations of the primes satisfying
$|\sigma(p)-p|\le H$ at every prime. Then
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists N_0\ \forall N\ge N_0\ \forall\sigma\in\mathcal S_H:
 \quad R_{\sigma,N,r_N}<\mathcal R_{0,r_N}(L(N)).
\]
Under RH one may choose this same $N_0$ so that $r_N>0$,
all the displayed actual denominators, and the canonical reference
denominator are positive. The cutoff depends on $H$ and $\alpha$,
and is independent of $\sigma$. The theorem includes $H=0$ and
permutations with cycles of arbitrary length; no involution or prime-gap
recurrence premise is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We first establish two exact finite comparisons.

If $H\le N$, bounded displacement applied to $\sigma$ and its inverse
gives
\[
 P_{N-H}\subseteq S_{\sigma,N}\subseteq P_{N+H}.
\]
Indeed, $p\le N$ implies $\sigma(p)\le N+H$.
If $q\le N-H$ and $p=\sigma^{-1}(q)$, then $p\le q+H\le N$,
so $q\in S_{\sigma,N}$. This accounts for every crossing coordinate,
including those at the endpoints.

Adding a nonnegative coordinate to a finite nonnegative vector can
only decrease $e_{r-1}/e_r$ wherever its initial denominator is positive.
To see the sign, write $a\ge0$ for the new coordinate and use
$e_j^{\rm new}=e_j+a e_{j-1}$, with $e_{-1}=0$.
After multiplying the positive denominators, the assertion reduces to
\[
 a(e_{r-1}^2-e_{r-2}e_r)\ge0.
\]
The established elementary-symmetric log-concavity inequality gives
this for $r\ge2$; for $r=1$ the numerator is $e_0=1$ and the
denominator increases. Iteration over the finite complement proves
\[
 R_{0,N+H,r}\le R_{\sigma,N,r}\le R_{0,N-H,r}
 \tag{U1}
\]
whenever $r\ge1$ and $E_{0,N-H,r}>0$.
All intermediate and larger-prefix denominators remain positive.
The maintained proof first works over the nonnegative rational odds
and then transports the exact symmetric coefficients and inequalities
to $\mathbb R$.

Next, for every $M\ge1$ the complete clock correction satisfies
\[
 \begin{split}
 0\le A(M+H)-A(M)
 &=\sum_{\substack{p\text{ prime}\\M<p\le M+H}}
       \log\left(1+\frac1{p-1}\right)\\
 &\le \frac{H}{M}.
 \end{split}
 \tag{U2}
\]
There are at most $H$ integers in the interval, and each summand is
nonnegative and at most $1/(p-1)\le1/M$.
No prime, logarithmic term, or positive correction is discarded.
The argument includes $H=0$ and the prime $2$.
In particular, for fixed $H$ this increment tends to zero.

Apply the existing moving-clock coefficient-sign theorem to the single
canonical law, at prefix $M$, with arithmetic clock $A(M)$ and reference
clock $L(M+H)$. Its hypotheses hold: $L(M+H)\to\infty$, and
\[
 A(M)-L(M+H)
 =[A(M+H)-L(M+H)]-[A(M+H)-A(M)]\longrightarrow0.
\]
It supplies a constant $C\ge0$, depending only on $H,\alpha$ and the
canonical law, and an eventual domain with positive rank and
denominators, on which
\[
 L(M+H)-A(M)<-\frac C M
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 R_{0,M,r_{M+H}}<
   \mathcal R_{0,r_{M+H}}(L(M+H)).
 \tag{U3}
\]
Its finite factorial convolutions are the actual elementary symmetric
coefficients by the proved generating-product identity.

Assume RH. Write $F(N)=L(N)-A(N)$ on the eventual domain
$\vartheta(N)>1$. The established Nicolas consequence of RH gives
$F(N)<-K/N$ eventually for every fixed real $K$.
Choose $K=2(C+H+1)$ and take $M\ge\max(1,H)$, $N=M+H$.
Then $N\le2M$, and (U2) gives the fully signed estimate
\[
 \begin{split}
 L(N)-A(M)
 &= F(N)+[A(N)-A(M)]\\
 &<-\frac{2(C+H+1)}{N}+\frac H M\\
 &\le-\frac{C+H+1}{M}+\frac H M
   =-\frac C M-\frac1M<-\frac C M.
 \end{split}
\]
Thus (U3) applies. Combining it with the upper inequality in (U1)
proves the desired strict inequality for every $\sigma\in\mathcal S_H$.
Every threshold used here concerns only $M,H,\alpha$ and the canonical
law. Replacing $M$ by $N-H$ therefore yields one eventual cutoff for
the entire class, with all positivity conclusions retained.

Conversely, the identity permutation belongs to $\mathcal S_H$ for
every $H\ge0$. Selecting it in the asserted eventual inequality gives
the canonical moving-rank coefficient criterion, whose already proved
converse implies RH. This also proves nonvacuity at $H=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[One cutoff for all constellation supports]
For each fixed $\alpha>0$, RH is equivalent to the eventual inequality
\[
 R_{B,N,r_N}<\mathcal R_{0,r_N}(L(N))
\]
holding simultaneously for every support $B$ satisfying the separation
and distance-six hypotheses of the quadruplet construction.
Under RH the actual denominators are positive on this common domain.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The proved all-coordinate displacement theorem bounds the associated
prime permutation by $6$, in both swap directions and at the small
indices. Apply the uniform theorem with $H=6$; its actual finite odds
are exactly those of $\mathcal L_B$.
For the converse select the empty support, which satisfies both support
hypotheses and gives the canonical criterion. Equivalently, apply the
already proved fixed-support RH converse to that support.
\end{proof}

The quantifiers are stronger than
$\forall B\,\exists N_0(B)\,\forall N\ge N_0(B)$:
here $\exists N_0\,\forall N\ge N_0\,\forall B$.
Consequently the same sign test also permits an admissible support or
permutation chosen separately at each cutoff.
The theorem does not assert uniformity for $H=H(N)$ or
$\alpha=\alpha(N)$. It supplies no constellation occurrence by itself:
adding a recurrent-witness requirement retains the earlier conjunction
of RH with constellation recurrence. The canonical zero signal is
retained through the signed clock correction. No zero-free exponent
gain is claimed; the certified boundary remains $7/8$.

The finite rational and real ratio comparisons and the exact
prime-prefix sandwich are compiled in
\path{Assembly/PrimeProfilePrefixSandwich.lean}.
The $H/M$ clock bound and shifted-clock limits are compiled in
\path{Helpers/PrimeProfileClockShift.lean}.
The uniform RH equivalence, its positive-denominator forward theorem
and the simultaneous quadruplet specialization are compiled in
\path{Assembly/PrimeProfileUniformConstellationRH.lean},
exported through \path{Assembly/ProbabilityProfiles.lean}.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{Shared excursions and every prescribed constellation shape}

The uniform criterion has a stronger converse than selecting the identity.
A false-RH excursion is visible simultaneously through every bounded
prime permutation. This will supply an RH converse for a single
shape-specific transport, including cycles longer than two.

\begin{theorem}[Shared positive excursions]
Fix $H\in\mathbb N$ and $\alpha>0$. If RH is false, then
arbitrarily large integer cutoffs $N$ satisfy
\[
 \mathcal R_{0,r_N}(L(N))<R_{\sigma,N,r_N}
       \qquad\text{for every }\sigma\in\mathcal S_H
\]
simultaneously. At these cutoffs $r_N>0$ and all actual and reference
denominators are positive.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The past reference clock $L(M-H)$ tends to infinity. Moreover
\[
 A(M)-L(M-H)
 =[A(M-H)-L(M-H)]+[A(M)-A(M-H)]\longrightarrow0.
\]
For the second term apply (U2) at $M-H$, using
$(M-H)+H=M$ eventually. Both assertions include $H=0$.

Apply the canonical moving-clock sign theorem at prefix $M$ with
reference clock $L(M-H)$. It supplies $C\ge0$ and an eventual domain
on which a positive clock difference greater than $C/M$ forces the
actual ratio above the reference. Put $M=N+H$.
The fully retained signed difference is
\[
 L(N)-A(N+H)=F(N)-[A(N+H)-A(N)].
\]
Set $K=C+H+1$. If $N\ge1$ and $F(N)>K/N$, then
\[
 \begin{split}
 L(N)-A(N+H)
 &>\frac{C+H+1}{N}-\frac HN\\
 &=\frac CN+\frac1N
   >\frac C{N+H}.
 \end{split}
\]
Thus the canonical ratio at $N+H$, with rank $r_N$, exceeds the
reference. The lower inequality in (U1) transfers the same strict
inequality to every $\sigma\in\mathcal S_H$.

For completeness, the denominator required by (U1) is available
unconditionally: the canonical moving-clock theorem with prefix $N-H$
and reference clock $L(N)$ supplies positivity of
$E_{0,N-H,r_N}$ and the reference denominator eventually, by the
forward-shift clock limits already proved. Their finite superset
comparisons supply every actual denominator.

If RH is false, the proved Nicolas excursion theorem gives, beyond
every threshold, an integer $N$ with $NF(N)>K$.
Choose $N$ beyond all the preceding eventual domains.
Dividing by the positive $N$ gives the required hypothesis.
Neither $C$, $K$, nor the selected cutoff depends on $\sigma$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Every individual bounded permutation reads RH]
For any fixed $\sigma\in\mathcal S_H$ and $\alpha>0$,
\[
 \mathrm{RH}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 R_{\sigma,N,r_N}<\mathcal R_{0,r_N}(L(N))
                      \quad\text{eventually in }N.
\]
Even the nonstrict eventual upper inequality implies RH.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The uniform RH theorem gives the forward implication.
If RH were false, the shared excursions would violate the asserted
eventual upper inequality for this same $\sigma$.
The argument uses its actual coefficients against the canonical
reference, without assuming that $\sigma$ is an involution.
\end{proof}

We now retain a complete finite constellation shape.
Let $k\ge2$, let $h_0,\ldots,h_{k-1}$ be distinct nonnegative integers,
and suppose $h_j\le W$ for every $j$.
The usual normalized description is
$0=h_0<h_1<\cdots<h_{k-1}=W$; the formal theorem only needs
injectivity of the offset list and its bound by $W$.
Write $\operatorname{Rec}(\mathcal H)$ for
\[
 \forall X\in\mathbb N\ \exists q\ge X:
          \quad q+h_j\text{ is prime for every }j<k.
\]
A \emph{full-shape cycle at $q$} in a prime permutation $\sigma$
means that there are prime labels $a_0,\ldots,a_{k-1}$ satisfying
\[
 a_j=q+h_j,\qquad
 \sigma(a_j)=a_{j+1\bmod k}\qquad(0\le j<k).
\]
Distinctness of the offsets gives distinct labels.
This definition records the whole shape, including its cyclic order.

\begin{theorem}[Arbitrary-shape constellation and RH equivalence]
Fix the finite shape above and $\alpha>0$. Then the following are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item RH holds and $\operatorname{Rec}(\mathcal H)$ holds.
\item There is a permutation $\sigma$ of the primes with
$|\sigma(p)-p|\le W$ for every prime, with full-shape cycles at
arbitrarily large anchors $q$, such that
\[
 R_{\sigma,N,r_N}<\mathcal R_{0,r_N}(L(N))
                      \quad\text{eventually in }N.
\]
\end{enumerate}
The construction in the forward direction can use disjoint intervals
$[q,q+W]$ and fix every prime outside the selected copies.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Assume recurrence of the shape. Apply the proved separated-subset
selection theorem to its set of anchors, with separation $W+1$.
This yields an unbounded set $A$ of valid anchors such that
\[
 a<b,\ a,b\in A\quad\Longrightarrow\quad a+W<b.
\]
For $a\in A$ and $j<k$, define the prime label
$\iota(a,j)=a+h_j$.
This map from $A\times\{0,\ldots,k-1\}$ to the primes is injective.
Indeed, if $a<b$, then
$a+h_i\le a+W<b\le b+h_j$, excluding equality.
The reverse ordering is excluded in the same way.
Equal anchors then force equal offsets, and injectivity of the
offset list forces equal indices.

Rotate the second coordinate of this labelled set and extend the
resulting permutation along $\iota$, fixing its complement.
The established embedding-extension API gives a genuine permutation
of all primes, with
\[
 \sigma(\iota(a,j))=\iota(a,j+1\bmod k).
\]
On a selected copy the anchor cancels from the displacement:
\[
 |\sigma(\iota(a,j))-\iota(a,j)|
      =|h_{j+1\bmod k}-h_j|\le W.
\]
On the complement the displacement is zero.
Every selected anchor gives its full-shape cycle, and the anchors
are unbounded. Under RH, the preceding individual-permutation
criterion supplies its eventual canonical coefficient sign.

Conversely, a full-shape cycle supplies prime labels equal to every
$q+h_j$. Arbitrarily large cycle anchors therefore imply
$\operatorname{Rec}(\mathcal H)$, with every offset retained.
The eventual coefficient sign for this one bounded permutation implies
RH by the preceding corollary. This proves both required conclusions.
\end{proof}

The construction starts with all occurrences and selects disjoint copies;
it never assumes that the original occurrences do not overlap.
No consecutive-prime condition is imposed: intervening primes may exist.
If consecutive patterns are desired, their absence-of-extra-primes
condition is additional geometric information. The analytic sign cutoff,
when RH holds, depends only on $W$ and $\alpha$, and works for every
permitted shape and cycle transport inside that displacement class.
No uniformity for a growing $W$ or a growing $\alpha$ is claimed.

For a compact comparison, let $s_\sigma(N)$ denote the canonical
coefficient inequality at cutoff $N$, let $E_\sigma$ denote its eventual
validity, and let $C_{\mathcal H}(\sigma)$ denote recurrence of full-shape
cycles. For every fixed $\sigma\in\mathcal S_H$ we have
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ E_{\rm id}
              \ \Longleftrightarrow\ E_\sigma .
\]
The uniform and shape-specific statements are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathrm{RH}
 &\ \Longleftrightarrow\
       \exists N_0\ \forall N\ge N_0\ \forall\sigma\in\mathcal S_H:
          s_\sigma(N),\\
 \mathrm{RH}\land\operatorname{Rec}(\mathcal H)
 &\ \Longleftrightarrow\
       \exists\sigma\in\mathcal S_W:
           C_{\mathcal H}(\sigma)\land E_\sigma .
 \end{aligned}
\]
The smallest-recurring-gap location theorem records one coarse
geometric parameter. The full-shape cycle word records an arbitrary
finite geometric pattern. The centered sign tests the common global
Nicolas bias, which cannot be hidden by choosing a different bounded
transport: false RH produces shared positive excursions.
These are separate requirements in the equivalence; no logical
independence theorem is asserted. An independently proved eventual
sign for one such witness would imply RH. Shape recurrence alone
does not supply that sign, and RH alone does not supply shape recurrence.
The certified zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.

\begin{flushleft}
The past-clock limits are compiled in

\subsection{Prime progressions with unbounded additive diameter}

The fixed-shape theorem requires recurrence of one prescribed offset vector.
Prime arithmetic progressions offer a different, established supply: the
length is prescribed, while the common difference may vary. We retain this
distinction throughout.

Consider finite blocks of primes
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 B_i=\{p_{i,0}<\cdots<p_{i,k_i-1}\},\qquad k_i\ge2,\\
 p_{i,k_i-1}<p_{i+1,0},\qquad p_{i,k_i-1}\le2p_{i,0}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
Rotate each block in increasing order, sending its last element to its first,
and fix every other prime. This defines a permutation $\sigma$; the block
lengths and additive displacements may be unbounded. Put
\[
 P_{\sigma,N}(z)=\prod_{p\le N}\left(1+\frac{z}{\sigma(p)-1}\right),
 \qquad Q_{\sigma,N}(z)=P_{\sigma,N}(z)e^{-A(N)z},
\]
where $A(N)$ is the canonical arithmetic clock. Write $P_{0,N}$ and $Q_{0,N}$
for the identity permutation, and $\Phi_0$ for the canonical entire profile.

\begin{theorem}[One-boundary stability]\label{thm:ordered-cycle-boundary}
For every fixed $R\ge0$, the normalized products satisfy
\[
 \sup_{|z|\le R}|Q_{\sigma,N}(z)-\Phi_0(z)|
 \le \frac{C_R}{N}
\]
for all sufficiently large $N$. No upper bound on $k_i$ or on the additive
diameter is needed. Consequently, for each fixed $b\ge0$ there are
$r_0,N_0,K$ such that, for $r\ge r_0$, $N\ge N_0$, $u>0$ and $r/u\le b$,
the finite and reference factorial-convolution denominators are positive
and their adjacent coefficient ratios differ by at most $K/(rN)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
All complete blocks cancel exactly. If a block is incomplete at $N$, let
$p_0$ be its first prime and $p_m$ its first prime exceeding $N$.
The included primes form an initial segment, whose shifted product obeys
\[
 P_{\sigma,N}(z)(1+z/(p_0-1))
   =P_{0,N}(z)(1+z/(p_m-1)).
\]
This identity is valid at zeros of either full product. Define instead the
boundary multiplier
\[
 D_N(z)=\frac{1+z/(p_m-1)}{1+z/(p_0-1)}.
\]
If no block is incomplete, set $D_N=1$. In the incomplete case,
$p_0\le N<p_m\le2p_0$, so
\[
 0\le\frac1{p_m-1}\le\frac1{p_0-1}\le\frac4N.
\]
For $N\ge\max(1,8R)$ and $|z|\le R$, the boundary denominator has norm
at least $1/2$. Thus
\[
 |D_N(z)-1|
 =\left|\frac{(1/(p_m-1)-1/(p_0-1))z}{1+z/(p_0-1)}\right|
 \le\frac{8R}{N}.
\]
The cross-multiplied identity now gives
$Q_{\sigma,N}=Q_{0,N}D_N$ without dividing by $P_{0,N}$.
If $|Q_{0,N}|\le B_R$ and
$|Q_{0,N}-\Phi_0|\le C_{0,R}/N$, then
\[
 |Q_{\sigma,N}-\Phi_0|
 \le\frac{C_{0,R}+8RB_R}{N}.
\]
The canonical prefix bound and profile approximation supply these constants.

Cauchy's estimate on a disc of radius $b+1$ bounds every Taylor coefficient
error by $C/[N(b+1)^j]$. The geometric descending-factorial estimates give
$O(1/N)$ errors for both the normalized coefficient polynomial and its first
derivative. The positive canonical lower bound controls their denominators.
In the exact adjacent-ratio identity the common leading term cancels,
leaving $(r/u)^2/r$ times a difference of logarithmic derivatives.
This retains the bound $K/(rN)$, rather than $O(1/N)$.
The initial cutoff is allowed to depend on the chosen radius throughout.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Progression transports retain the RH criterion]
\label{cor:prime-progression-transport-rh}
Fix $\alpha>0$ and the above permutation $\sigma$. Let
$r_N=\lfloor\alpha L(N)\rfloor$, let $R_\sigma(N,r)$ denote the adjacent
coefficient ratio of $P_{\sigma,N}$, and use the canonical reference ratio
$\overline R_0$. Then
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 R_\sigma(N,r_N)<\overline R_0(r_N,L(N))
 \quad\hbox{for every sufficiently large }N.
\]
The ranks and both denominators are positive eventually.
There are such permutations whose blocks are prime arithmetic progressions
of lengths tending to infinity.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The preceding inverse-rank comparison and the canonical reference secant
preserve both clock signs whenever $|L(N)-A(N)|>C/N$.
Under RH the proved Nicolas estimate gives
$L(N)-A(N)<-C/N$ eventually, for every fixed $C$.
If RH fails, $N(L(N)-A(N))$ has arbitrarily late positive excursions
exceeding every fixed constant. These force the reverse strict coefficient
inequality and contradict even an eventual non-strict upper sign.
The factorial-convolution coefficients at $A(N)$ are the coefficients of
$P_{\sigma,N}$, since $P_{\sigma,N}=Q_{\sigma,N}e^{A(N)z}$.

For the progression supply, use Green--Tao's theorem on arbitrarily long
prime progressions beyond any prescribed threshold.\footnote{B.~Green and
T.~Tao, \emph{The primes contain arbitrarily long arithmetic progressions},
Annals of Mathematics 167 (2008), 481--547, Theorems~1.1 and~1.2,
\url{https://doi.org/10.4007/annals.2008.167.481}.}
From a progression of length $2k$, retain its latter $k$ terms.
If its first retained prime is $p_0=a+kd$, its last is
$a+(2k-1)d<2p_0$. Choose these blocks successively beyond the preceding
last prime and with $k$ tending to infinity. Their disjoint cycles give
the required permutation. This does not produce recurrence at a fixed
common difference.
\end{proof}

The same ordinary construction works inside a fixed reduced residue class:
its prime subset has positive relative density, so Green--Tao's
Theorem~1.2 applies there. Every prime outside the chosen blocks remains
fixed. The observable still uses all primes and the canonical RH reference.

Green--Tao as an explicit external supply input; its complete Lean transport
construction remains separate.
\end{flushleft}

\subsection{One residue class and the complete Dirichlet zero signal}

An intrinsic class-only observable has a different reference from the
full-prime transport above. There is a particularly useful choice: the
identity class $1\bmod q$, with a fixed integer $q\ge2$.
All Dirichlet characters take value one at that class.

\begin{theorem}[Positive character folding]\label{prop:identity-class-folding}
Initially for $\Re s>1$, put
\[
 G_q(s)=\sum_{\substack{p\ {\rm prime}\\p\equiv1\ (q)}}\frac{\log p}{p^s}.
\]
Then
\[
 G_q(s)=-\frac1{\varphi(q)}
       \sum_{\chi\bmod q}\frac{L'}{L}(s,\chi)-H_q(s),
 \qquad
 H_q(s)=\sum_{\substack{p\ {\rm prime},\ k\ge2\\p^k\equiv1\ (q)}}
                  \frac{\log p}{p^{ks}}.
\]
The remainder is holomorphic on $\Re s>1/2$. A zero $\rho$ with
$1/2<\Re\rho<1$ of any character in this family is a pole of $G_q$, with
\[
 \operatorname{Res}_{s=\rho}G_q(s)
   =-\frac1{\varphi(q)}\sum_{\chi\bmod q}m_\chi(\rho)\ne0.
\]
Here $m_\chi(\rho)=0$ when that character has no zero at $\rho$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In the absolutely convergent Euler region, expand
\[
 -\frac{L'}L(s,\chi)
  =\sum_{\substack{p\ {\rm prime}\\p\nmid q}}\sum_{k\ge1}
          \chi(p)^k\frac{\log p}{p^{ks}}.
\]
Character orthogonality gives
$\varphi(q)^{-1}\sum_\chi\chi(p)^k
 ={\bf1}_{p^k\equiv1\ (q)}$.
Separate $k=1$ to obtain the asserted identity. Primes dividing $q$
contribute to neither side. For every fixed $\sigma_0>1/2$ and
$\Re s\ge\sigma_0$,
\[
 |H_q(s)|
 \le \frac1{1-2^{-\sigma_0}}
           \sum_{n\ge2}\frac{\log n}{n^{2\sigma_0}}<\infty.
\]
This modulus-independent majorant gives normal convergence and holomorphy.
Continue by the finite sum of logarithmic derivatives. At a zero in the
stated strip, each contributing logarithmic derivative has residue
$m_\chi(\rho)$; no $L$-function has a pole there. All multiplicities have
the same sign in their sum, while $H_q$ is holomorphic. The residue is
therefore nonzero, including when several characters share the zero.
\end{proof}

This identity has a direct probability interpretation. For each prime
$p\equiv1\bmod q$, take a Bernoulli variable $B_p$ of success probability
$1/p$, independently over each finite prefix. Its odds are precisely
$1/(p-1)$, as in the coefficient law. The weighted mean is
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 M_q(x)=\mathbb E\!\sum_{\substack{p\le x\\p\equiv1\ (q)}}(\log p)B_p
       =\sum_{\substack{p\le x\\p\equiv1\ (q)}}\frac{\log p}{p},\\
 \int_{1^-}^{\infty}x^{-s}\,dM_q(x)=G_q(s+1)
 \qquad(\Re s>0).
 \end{gathered}
\]
Consequently the centered transform
\[
 \mathcal D_q(s)=G_q(s+1)-\frac1{\varphi(q)s}
\]
is holomorphic on $\Re s>-1/2$ if and only if GRH holds for the characters
modulo $q$ (equivalently, their associated primitive $L$-functions).
The subtraction removes the principal-character pole. Nonprincipal
characters are regular and nonzero at one; finite imprimitive Euler
factors introduce no zeros in $\Re s>1/2$.
The functional equations pair $\chi$ with $\overline\chi$, both present
in the family, so absence of right-half-strip zeros gives the full
critical-line assertion.

For an explicit sufficient arithmetic estimate, fix $1/2\le c<1$.
If there is a constant $C_q$ such that, for every $\varepsilon>0$,
\[
 M_q(x)-\frac{\log x}{\varphi(q)}-C_q
       =O_{q,\varepsilon}(x^{c-1+\varepsilon}),
\]
then every nontrivial zero in this Dirichlet family has real part at most
$c$. Indeed, integration by parts gives, initially for $\Re s>0$,
\[
 \mathcal D_q(s)
  =C_q+s\int_1^\infty
     \left(M_q(x)-\frac{\log x}{\varphi(q)}-C_q\right)x^{-s-1}\,dx.
\]
The assumed estimate continues this expression holomorphically to
$\Re s>c-1$, contradicting the nonzero residue of any zero with
$\Re\rho>c$.

\begin{corollary}[Cyclotomic polynomial realization]
\label{cor:cyclotomic-class-observable}
Fix $q\ge2$ and put $F_q(u)=\Phi_q(u)$, the $q$th cyclotomic polynomial.
For a divisor prime $p\nmid q$, its local root count and probability odds are
\[
 \rho_q(p)=\varphi(q){\bf1}_{p\equiv1\ (q)},\qquad
 \eta_q(p)=\frac{\rho_q(p)}{p-\rho_q(p)}.
\]
For each fixed cutoff $N$, sample $u$ uniformly modulo
$\prod_{p\le N,\ p\nmid q}p$. The weighted distinct-prime-divisor count has mean
\[
 \mathbb E\!\sum_{\substack{p\le N,\ p\nmid q\\p\mid F_q(u)}}\log p
    =\varphi(q)M_q(N).
\]
Its Mellin--Stieltjes transform has residue
$-\sum_{\chi\bmod q}m_\chi(\rho)$ at $s=\rho-1$ for every zero
$1/2<\Re\rho<1$ in the Dirichlet family. All characters contribute with
the same sign.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Since $p\nmid q$, the polynomial $X^q-1$ is separable over $\mathbb F_p$.
The roots of $\Phi_q$ are exactly the elements of multiplicative order $q$.
The cyclic group $\mathbb F_p^\times$ has $\varphi(q)$ such elements when
$q\mid p-1$, and none otherwise. In the nonzero case $p\ge q+1>\varphi(q)$,
so the local success probability $\rho_q(p)/p$ is strictly below one.
The Chinese remainder theorem makes these finite-prime divisibility
events independent under the stated sampling law. Their odds give the
displayed coefficient law, and linearity of expectation gives its weighted
mean. The preceding character identity supplies the Mellin residue after
multiplication by $\varphi(q)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite cyclotomic sampling]
\label{thm:cyclotomic-finite-sampling}
Fix $q\ge2$ and a finite set $S$ of primes. Sample $U$ uniformly modulo
$M_S=\prod_{p\in S}p$, and define
\[
 B_p(U)={\bf1}_{p\nmid q,\ p\mid\Phi_q(U)},\qquad
 \theta_{q,p}=
 \begin{cases}
  \varphi(q)/p,&p\nmid q,\ p\equiv1\pmod q,\\
  0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
For every prescribed pattern $\varepsilon:S\to\{0,1\}$,
\[
 \Pr(B_p(U)=\varepsilon_p\text{ for all }p\in S)
 =
 \prod_{\substack{p\in S\\\varepsilon_p=1}}\theta_{q,p}
 \prod_{\substack{p\in S\\\varepsilon_p=0}}(1-\theta_{q,p}).
\]
All factors satisfy $0\le\theta_{q,p}<1$. In particular, every $p\le q$
has $\theta_{q,p}=0$. The statement includes $S=\varnothing$, with
$M_S=1$ and both products equal to one.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Chinese remaindering bijects scalar residues modulo $M_S$ with tuples
$(u_p)_{p\in S}\in\prod_{p\in S}\mathbb F_p$. A success coordinate has
$\rho_q(p)$ permitted values, and a failure coordinate has
$p-\rho_q(p)$, where $\rho_q(p)=p\theta_{q,p}$ includes the depleted case.
The number of tuples giving the pattern is therefore exactly
\[
 \prod_{\substack{p\in S\\\varepsilon_p=1}}\rho_q(p)
 \prod_{\substack{p\in S\\\varepsilon_p=0}}(p-\rho_q(p)).
\]
Dividing by $M_S$ proves the formula. The preceding cyclotomic root
calculation gives the local probabilities. If a nondepleted success
is possible, $q\mid p-1$, so $q\le p-1$ and
$\varphi(q)\le q<p$. This also excludes every $p\le q$.
\end{proof}

This is an exact law for each fixed finite divisor support, without a
sampling-limit error. It asserts independence across divisor primes for
fixed $q$. Different cyclotomic families evaluated at the same scalar
need not be independent.

Thus polynomial inputs realize the intrinsic progression observable,
including quadratic and quartic examples:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \Phi_3(u)&=u^2+u+1,& \Phi_4(u)&=u^2+1,\\
 \Phi_5(u)&=u^4+u^3+u^2+u+1,\\
 \Phi_8(u)&=u^4+1,& \Phi_{12}(u)&=u^4-u^2+1.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The class-$1\bmod5$ example includes characters of order four, beyond the
quadratic characters modulo eight. Here $u$ is the sampled polynomial
input and $N$ is the divisor cutoff. Primes dividing $q$ are explicitly
removed; no assertion about their root counts is used. For fixed $q$ they
form a finite exceptional set. The rescaled remainder is
$\varphi(q)H_q$, so its bound carries this factor if $q$ varies.
The local first-order term is character dependent; this argument does not
silently qualify the law as a pointwise quadratic perturbation of the
canonical prime law.

The finite-field root counts, their prime-modulus specialization, strict
local probabilities, the actual \texttt{PrimeClockLaw}, its general
coefficient comparison, and the full finite sampling law are checked in
Lean. The maintained owners, under the project namespace, are
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Mathlib/FieldTheory/Finite/Cyclotomic.lean\\
Helpers/CyclotomicProbability.lean\\
Helpers/CyclotomicSampling.lean
\end{quote}
All nineteen declarations have only the standard foundational axioms
in their audits. The weighted expectation formula is an ordinary
consequence of the checked finite distribution and linearity of
expectation; it has no separately checked declaration here.
Character folding and the analytic continuation argument remain ordinary
written proofs, with no priority claim. They identify a concrete class-only
probability observable and an explicit remaining arithmetic estimate.
Existence of primes or progressions in the class does not itself provide
that estimate.

\subsection{An entire profile that remembers every prime}

Each proper nonzero fraction $\rho_p/p$ has a prime denominator in lowest
terms. This permits exact reconstruction at every prime, including inactive
primes. For the forward compensated Bernoulli product, see
\cite[Proposition~3.1]{KN}. Here the prime denominators supply an inverse
conclusion without a common leading asymptotic.

\begin{theorem}[Prime-labelled reconstruction from the zero set]
\label{thm:prime-denominator-profile-rigidity}
For each prime $p$, let $a_p,b_p$ be integers with
$0\le a_p<p$ and $0\le b_p<p$, and suppose
\[
 \sum_p(a_p/p)^2<\infty,\qquad \sum_p(b_p/p)^2<\infty.
\]
The canonical probability-centred profiles
\[
 \mathcal P_a(z)=
 \prod_p\bigl(1+(a_p/p)(z-1)\bigr)
           \exp\bigl(-(a_p/p)(z-1)\bigr),
\]
and $\mathcal P_b$ are entire. Their zero sets recover every coordinate:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal Z(\mathcal P_a)=\mathcal Z(\mathcal P_b)
 &\ \Longleftrightarrow\ a_p=b_p\quad\text{for every prime }p\\
 &\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathcal P_a=\mathcal P_b.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Zero-set inclusion already implies $a_p=b_p$ wherever $a_p>0$.
Every zero is negative and simple.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
On each compact set the compensated factor is
$1+O_R((a_p/p)^2)$, giving locally uniform convergence.
A sufficiently late tail has an absolutely convergent logarithm wherever
its factors are nonzero. Consequently the zeros are exactly
\[
 z=1-\frac{p}{a_p}\qquad(a_p>0).
\]
If a zero contributed at $p$ is also contributed at a prime $q$ by
$b_q>0$, then $p\,b_q=q\,a_p$. Hence $p\mid q\,a_p$.
Since $0<a_p<p$, we have $p\nmid a_p$, so $p\mid q$, whence $p=q$.
Cancellation gives $a_p=b_p$. This proves the inclusion assertion.

Apply this argument in both directions when the zero sets agree.
If $a_p=0<b_p$, reverse inclusion would force $b_p=a_p$.
Thus all coordinates agree, giving equality of the products as well.
Conversely, equality of the entire functions gives equality of their zeros.

No two positive coordinates of one profile can contribute the same zero.
At its unique vanishing factor, the remaining convergent product is
nonzero. The linear factor has nonzero derivative, so the zero is simple.
Finally $a_p<p$ implies $1-p/a_p<0$.
\end{proof}

The strict upper bound is essential: a deterministic success has factor
$z\exp(-(z-1))$, and moving it to another prime preserves the profile.
All probabilities may vanish, giving the constant profile one.
The theorem recovers local root counts; different polynomials may have
the same such counts.

\begin{corollary}[Complete cyclotomic local-law fingerprint]
\label{cor:cyclotomic-profile-fingerprint}
Fix $q\ge2$, use the depleted root counts $\rho_q(p)$ above, and put
$\theta_{q,p}=\rho_q(p)/p$. Then
\[
 \mathcal C_q(z)=
 \prod_p(1+\theta_{q,p}(z-1))
             \exp(-\theta_{q,p}(z-1))
\]
is entire, with simple zeros exactly at
\[
 \left\{\,1-\frac{p}{\varphi(q)}:
          p\text{ prime},\ p\equiv1\pmod q\,\right\}.
\]
For $q,r\ge2$, equality of these zero sets, equality of the entire
profiles, and $\rho_q(p)=\rho_r(p)$ at every prime are equivalent.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The explicit root count gives
\[
 0\le\theta_{q,p}\le\frac{\varphi(q)}p
       \le\frac{\varphi(q)}{p-1},\qquad \theta_{q,p}<1.
\]
The squared probabilities are therefore summable. Apply
Theorem~\ref{thm:prime-denominator-profile-rigidity}.
At an active prime the numerator is precisely $\varphi(q)$;
$q\mid p-1$ already implies $p\nmid q$, giving the displayed zero set.
\end{proof}

For the actual scalar sampling experiment, the exact finite law gives
\[
 \mathcal C_q(z)=\lim_{N\to\infty}
  \exp\!\left(-(z-1)\sum_{p\le N}\theta_{q,p}\right)
  \mathbb E_N z^{\sum_{p\le N}B_p(U_N)} .
\]
Each finite expectation uses its complete Chinese remainder period.
These profile zeros lie in the generating variable $z$. The Dirichlet
zeros in the preceding subsection occur in the Mellin continuation of
the corresponding weighted prime count.

A profile determines the local law rather than a unique modulus:
$q=3$ and $q=6$ give the same depleted counts, since the relevant primes
are odd. In contrast, the laws for $q=3$ and $q=4$ differ at $p=7$.
Both facts are consistent with exact every-prime reconstruction.

\begin{theorem}[Exact intersection of cyclotomic profile zero sets]
\label{thm:cyclotomic-profile-common-zeros}
Fix $q,r\ge2$, and let $L=\operatorname{lcm}(q,r)$.
If $\varphi(q)\ne\varphi(r)$, the zero sets of $\mathcal C_q$
and $\mathcal C_r$ are disjoint.
If $\varphi(q)=\varphi(r)=D$, their intersection is exactly
\[
 \mathcal Z(\mathcal C_q)\cap\mathcal Z(\mathcal C_r)
 =\left\{\,1-\frac pD:
                 p\text{ prime},\ p\equiv1\pmod L\,\right\}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For general proper prime-denominator laws, suppose a zero contributed
by $a_p>0$ is also contributed by $b_\ell>0$. Then
$p/a_p=\ell/b_\ell$, hence $p b_\ell=\ell a_p$.
As $p$ is prime and $0<a_p<p$, it follows that $p=\ell$ and
then $a_p=b_p$.
Conversely, an agreeing positive coordinate contributes the same zero
to both profiles. Thus a common zero is equivalent to an agreeing
positive count at the same prime.

At an active cyclotomic coordinate the counts are $\varphi(q)$ and
$\varphi(r)$. They must therefore be equal. When both equal $D$, a
prime contributes to both profiles exactly when
$q\mid p-1$ and $r\mid p-1$, or equivalently $L\mid p-1$.
Its zero is $1-p/D$. Every prime satisfying this condition contributes
to both, which proves the reverse inclusion as well.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact shared-support factorization]\label{thm:cyclotomic-shared-factor}
Let $q,r\ge2$, $L=\operatorname{lcm}(q,r)$ and $d_m=\varphi(m)$.
Define the residual entire function
\[
 R_{q\setminus r}(z)=
 \prod_{\substack{p\ {\rm prime}\\q\mid p-1,\ r\nmid p-1}}
 \left(1+\frac{d_q}{p}(z-1)\right)
       \exp\!\left(-\frac{d_q}{p}(z-1)\right).
\]
Then, with the displayed compensation retained exactly,
\[
 \mathcal C_q(z)=
 \mathcal C_L\!\left(1+\frac{d_q}{d_L}(z-1)\right)
 R_{q\setminus r}(z).
\]
The analogous identity holds with $q,r$ interchanged. Moreover,
\[
 R_{q\setminus r}(z)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists\,p\ {\rm prime}:\quad
 q\mid p-1,\quad r\nmid p-1,\quad z=1-\frac{p}{d_q}.
\]
The two residual functions have disjoint zero sets.
If $d_q=d_r=D$, the common entire factor is
\[
 A_{q,r}(z)=
 \mathcal C_L\!\left(1+\frac{D}{\varphi(L)}(z-1)\right),
\]
and $\mathcal C_q=A_{q,r}R_{q\setminus r}$,
$\mathcal C_r=A_{q,r}R_{r\setminus q}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The prime support $q\mid p-1$ splits into $L\mid p-1$ and
$q\mid p-1,\ r\nmid p-1$. On the first part,
\[
 \eta_q(p)=\frac{d_q}{p}
         =\frac{d_q}{d_L}\,\eta_L(p).
\]
For $B_a(z)=(1+a(z-1))\exp(-a(z-1))$ the exact identity
$B_{ca}(z)=B_a(1+c(z-1))$ includes both the linear factor and
the exponential. Squared local probabilities are summable, so the
products of $B_a$ converge locally uniformly. Equivalently,
$\sum_p|B_{\eta_q(p)}(z)-1|$ converges for every fixed $z$.
Splitting this convergent product over the two complementary supports
therefore proves the factorization, including at its zeros.

The residuals inherit square summability, take local probabilities
in $[0,1)$, and are entire. The zero theorem for compensated Bernoulli
products gives the displayed residual zero set. If the two residuals
shared a zero, their full profiles would share it as well.
The preceding common-zero theorem forces $d_q=d_r$.
Their residual prime labels $p,t$ would then satisfy
$1-p/d_q=1-t/d_q$, hence $p=t$. One residual requires
$r\nmid p-1$, whereas the other requires $r\mid p-1$,
a contradiction.
\end{proof}

The support-partition identity is proved for arbitrary square-summable
Bernoulli laws and arbitrary, possibly infinite supports. The scaling
identity itself needs no convergence hypothesis, since its local
factors agree pointwise. The actual cyclotomic factorization, residual
entireness and residual zero statements above are kernel checked;
no division by a profile at a zero is used.

The infinitude in these formulas is unconditional, by Dirichlet's
theorem in the identity class. More quantitatively, write
$\pi(X;m,1)$ for the number of primes at most $X$ that are $1$ modulo
$m$, and count profile zeros in $[-T,0]$. The exact formulas and the
prime number theorem in a fixed progression give
\[
 \begin{split}
 N_q(T)&=\pi(\varphi(q)(T+1);q,1)
                \sim \frac{T}{\log T},\\
 N_{q,r}(T)&=\pi(D(T+1);L,1)
                \sim \frac{D}{\varphi(L)}\,\frac{T}{\log T}
       \quad\bigl(\varphi(q)=\varphi(r)=D\bigr).
 \end{split}
\]
Here $q,r$ are fixed and $T\to\infty$; these prime-counting
consequences are ordinary deductions, not additional checked Lean
declarations. Every cyclotomic profile has the same leading zero
density, while the overlap records the intersection of progressions.
For example, $q=5$ and $r=8$ have $D=4$ and $L=40$, so their shared
zeros have relative density $1/4$. For $q=3,r=4$ it is $1/2$.
Different totients instead give no shared profile zeros.
These statements concern zeros in $z$, and introduce no new
Riemann or Dirichlet zero-free estimate.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform separation and stable prime-label recovery]
\label{thm:prime-profile-stable-recovery}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:prime-denominator-profile-rigidity},
suppose additionally that $a_p,b_p\le D$ for every prime, where
$D\ge1$ is an integer.
Any two distinct zeros $z,w$ drawn from the two profiles satisfy
\[
 |z-w|\ge \frac1{D^2}.
\]
More precisely, distinct proper prime/count labels $(p,r)$ and
$(\ell,s)$, with $1\le r<p$, $1\le s<\ell$ and $r,s\le D$,
satisfy
\[
 \left|\left(1-\frac p r\right)
           -\left(1-\frac\ell s\right)\right|
       \ge\frac1{rs}\ge\frac1{D^2}.
\]
Consequently, an observed complex location within strictly less than
$1/(2D^2)$ of a zero can match at most one such prime/count label.
The constants are independent of the height of the zero.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The prime-label argument shows that $ps-\ell r$ is a nonzero integer
for distinct admissible labels. Its absolute value is at least one.
Since $r,s>0$, clearing denominators gives
\[
 \left|\left(1-\frac p r\right)
           -\left(1-\frac\ell s\right)\right|
   =\frac{|ps-\ell r|}{rs}\ge\frac1{rs}\ge\frac1{D^2}.
\]
Every profile zero has one of these labels by the exact zero formula,
which proves separation between any two distinct zeros, including
zeros taken from different profiles.
If an observation were within the stated strict tolerance of two
different labels, the triangle inequality would put their locations
strictly less than $1/D^2$ apart, contradicting the bound.
\end{proof}

For the actual progression profiles, take
$D=\max(\varphi(q),\varphi(r))$ for fixed $q,r\ge2$.
Then every distinct pair of zeros from $\mathcal C_q$ and
$\mathcal C_r$ has distance at least $D^{-2}$.
This is a uniform lower bound, with no optimality claim.
It concerns accuracy of zero locations: a bound on the error in
function values alone still needs a separate analytic estimate to
control the displacement of zeros. The determinant inequality used
in the proof is elementary; the conclusion here is its application
to exact and approximate prime-labelled profile recovery.

The reconstruction, support-partition and actual cyclotomic results have
thirty-three checked declarations in the maintained owners
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Log/\\
\hspace*{1em}BernoulliProfilePartition.lean\\
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/BernoulliProfile.lean\\
Helpers/CyclotomicProfileRigidity.lean
\end{quote}
under the project namespace. Their audits contain only the standard
foundational axioms. The simple-zero assertion follows from the
zero-factor argument above and is not a separately named declaration in
this batch. All comparisons here use the displayed probability-centred
compensation.

\subsection{Special integers: reciprocal-divisor fingerprints}

Directly sampling a special sequence and sampling divisors of one of
its members are different experiments. The latter gives a single exact
probability model for every positive integer, without an infinitude
assumption on the class from which that integer is selected.
For $n\ge1$, choose $d\mid n$ with probability
\[
 {\mathbb P}_n(d)=
 \frac{d^{-1}}{\sum_{e\mid n}e^{-1}},\qquad
 P_n(z)={\mathbb E}_n z^{\omega(d)}.
\]
Here $\omega(d)$ counts distinct prime factors and $\omega(1)=0$.
The divisor involution $d\mapsto n/d$ gives
$\sum_{d\mid n}d^{-1}=\sigma(n)/n$, so $P_n(0)=n/\sigma(n)$.

\begin{theorem}[Complete prime-power recovery and unitary divisibility]
\label{thm:divisor-profile-unitary}
For positive integers $n,m$, the zero set of $P_n$ determines $n$.
More precisely, put
\[
 S_{p,a}=1+p+\cdots+p^{a-1},\qquad a\ge1.
\]
The zeros of $P_n$ are exactly the distinct simple numbers
\[
 -\frac{p^a}{S_{p,a}}\qquad(p^a\Vert n).
\]
Consequently,
\[
 \begin{split}
 Z(P_n)=Z(P_m)&\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad n=m,\\
 Z(P_n)\subseteq Z(P_m)&\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists\,k\ge1:\ m=nk,\ \gcd(n,k)=1.
 \end{split}
\]
For $n=1$ the zero set is empty. Both conclusions also hold for
the canonically compensated profiles
\[
 \mathcal D_n(z)=e^{-\mu_n(z-1)}P_n(z),\qquad
 \mu_n=\sum_{p^a\Vert n}\frac{S_{p,a}}{p^a+S_{p,a}}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under reciprocal divisor weight the exponent choices at distinct
primes are independent, since both the support and the weight factor:
\[
 P_n(z)=
 \prod_{p^a\Vert n}
 \frac{1+z\sum_{j=1}^{a}p^{-j}}
      {1+\sum_{j=1}^{a}p^{-j}}
 =
 \prod_{p^a\Vert n}
 \frac{p^a+S_{p,a}z}{p^a+S_{p,a}}.
\]
Each linear factor has the displayed zero.
Since $S_{p,a}\equiv1\pmod p$, the fraction $p^a/S_{p,a}$
is reduced. Its positive numerator therefore identifies the prime
$p$ and the exponent $a$. In particular, distinct prime powers from
different prime coordinates cannot contribute the same zero.
The product has exactly one factor for each positive prime valuation,
so all its zeros are simple. Its zero set recovers every positive
valuation and hence the integer.

Inclusion of the zero sets says that every prime dividing $n$
occurs in $m$ with exactly the same exponent. This is equivalent to
$m=nk$ with no prime common to $n$ and $k$. Conversely, such a
coprime factorization retains precisely those prime-power factors
of $P_n$ in $P_m$. Finally the exponential compensation never
vanishes, so it changes neither zero set.
\end{proof}

The profile itself is a finite compensated Bernoulli product with
success probabilities $S_{p,a}/(p^a+S_{p,a})$.
The maintained Lean results prove its exact zero formula, recover
arbitrary square-summable prime-indexed exponent functions, discharge
summability for every integer factorization, and prove the two integer
equivalences for $\mathcal D_n$, including unitary divisibility.
Their transfer to $P_n$ uses the ordinary product identity above.
There are fourteen checked declarations in
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/BernoulliDivisorProfile.lean\\
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/ReciprocalDivisorInteger.lean
\end{quote}
under the project namespace, with only standard foundational axioms.
The finite weighted-divisor sampling identity in the displayed product
is proved ordinarily here; it is not yet a separately named Lean
sampling adapter. No priority claim is made for this inverse statement.

This fingerprint retains substantially more information than
abundancy alone. For example, both $6$ and $28$ are perfect, so both
have $P_n(0)=1/2$, but their zero sets are respectively
$\{-2,-3\}$ and $\{-4/3,-7\}$.
Zero-set inclusion also differs from ordinary divisibility:
$2\mid4$, but the zero $-2$ of $P_2$ is absent from $P_4$,
whose zero is $-4/3$.

\paragraph{The classical RH criterion in the same model.}
Robin's criterion, together with its colossally abundant
counterexample reduction, gives
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 P_n(0)>\frac{e^{-\gamma}}{\log\log n}
 \quad\hbox{for every integer }n\ge5041.
\]
One may equivalently quantify over just the colossally abundant
integers $n\ge5041$, or over just the superabundant integers in that
range: a counterexample exists among the former if RH fails, and
they are contained in the latter. These are translations of the
classical criterion, not new independent estimates
\cite[Section~2 and equation~(1.2)]{Lagarias}.
The denominator is positive on the stated range. Compensation
must be undone before reading this probability:
$P_n(0)=e^{-\mu_n}\mathcal D_n(0)$, rather than
$\mathcal D_n(0)$ itself.

\paragraph{What the named classes contribute.}
The common divisor model above applies to every existing member of
each class. Its relation to direct sampling is as follows.
\begin{description}
\item[Sophie Germain primes.]
For primes $p$ with $2p+1$ prime, direct counts are
$\omega(p)=1$ and $\omega(p(2p+1))=2$.
They have no growing distinct-factor rank regime.
No infinitude assumption is made. The integer fingerprint is valid
for each such $p$ or product; conditioning a polynomial sieve family
on both outputs being prime needs its own sampling theorem.
\item[Fermat and Mersenne primes.]
Use the precise values $2^{2^j}+1$ with $j\ge0$, or $2^j-1$
with $j\ge2$, only when prime.
Their individual divisor profiles each have one zero, at the
negative prime itself. This neither assumes infinitely many such
primes nor transfers an index cutoff to a divisor cutoff.
Uniform residue sampling of a cyclotomic polynomial is a separate
experiment from evaluating it at the fixed base $2$.
\item[Even and odd perfect numbers.]
Every hypothetical or actual perfect integer has $P_n(0)=1/2$.
For even perfect numbers the Euclid--Euler form specifies the two
prime coordinates and their exponents; the full profile retains them.
For odd perfect numbers the statement is conditional on a witness,
without assuming one exists. At $n\ge5041$ the value $1/2$ already
satisfies Robin's probability inequality, so restricting that
inequality to perfect numbers supplies no stronger RH criterion.
\item[Superabundant and colossally abundant numbers.]
Under the strict record convention, superabundant integers are
exactly the strict lower records of $P_n(0)$.
For a fixed $\epsilon>0$, a colossally abundant optimizer minimizes
$n^\epsilon P_n(0)$; all maximizing ties in the usual non-strict
definition are retained. These classes lie in the actual extremal
regime of the RH criterion. Exact reconstruction does not estimate
their probability relative to the critical threshold.
\item[Fibonacci values.]
Take $F_0=0,F_1=1$ and values $F_j$ for $j\ge2$,
distinguishing all Fibonacci values from Fibonacci primes.
Each positive value has the exact divisor fingerprint.
Uniform index sampling instead gives local divisibility through
ranks of apparition; simultaneous events are controlled by the
least common multiple of those ranks, so independence requires
a separate justification. The index cutoff, the value $F_j$,
and a prime-divisor cutoff are distinct parameters.
\item[Primorials, factorials and highly composite numbers.]
They also fit the finite model. A primorial uses exponent one at
each supported prime, whereas factorials and the extremal classes
retain nontrivial exponents. Direct sampling of these sequences
can force every fixed small prime in the limit, so a finite rank
shift does not justify applying a positive-rank prime clock to
that direct measure.
\end{description}
The zero-set theorem is uniform over positive integers and requires
no class-specific existence theorem. An RH implication still needs
the appropriate independent inequality or a proved transfer of its
probability law; injective encoding alone supplies neither.

\subsection{Fibonacci carriers and the golden-ratio clock}
\label{sec:fibonacci-golden-carriers}

The Fibonacci sequence supplies an exact prime clock through its
\emph{indices}. Set $F_0=0,F_1=1$, and, for each odd prime $\ell$,
let $P_\ell$ be the least prime factor of $F_\ell$.
This exists because $F_\ell>1$ for $\ell\ge3$.
The sampled variable is the integer index $n$; the event is
$P_\ell\mid F_n$. A label cutoff $X$ means $\ell\le X$.
It is neither a bound on $P_\ell$ nor a bound on the value $F_n$.

\begin{theorem}[A prime-index carrier of a strongly divisible sequence]
\label{thm:strong-divisibility-carrier}
Suppose $a_1=1$ and
$\gcd(a_m,a_n)=a_{\gcd(m,n)}$ for a sequence of nonnegative integers.
If $\ell$ is prime and $c\ne1$ divides $a_\ell$, then
\[
 c\mid a_n\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\ell\mid n
 \qquad(n\ge0).
\]
In particular, for every odd prime $\ell$ and every prime divisor
$c$ of $F_\ell$ this equivalence holds with $a_n=F_n$.
The selected primes $P_\ell$ are pairwise distinct.
For every finite set $S$ of odd primes and arbitrary marks $z_\ell$,
\[
 \prod_{\ell\in S}
   z_\ell^{\boldsymbol{1}_{P_\ell\mid F_n}}
 =
 \prod_{\ell\in S}
   z_\ell^{\boldsymbol{1}_{\ell\mid n}}.
\]
The product convention means a factor $1$ at an unsuccessful event,
including when a mark is zero.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $\ell\nmid n$, primality gives $\gcd(\ell,n)=1$.
A common divisor $c$ of $a_\ell$ and $a_n$ would then divide
$a_1=1$, contradicting $c\ne1$. If $\ell\mid n$, the gcd identity
instead gives $\gcd(a_\ell,a_n)=a_\ell$, so
$a_\ell\mid a_n$ and hence $c\mid a_n$.
For Fibonacci numbers use their strong divisibility identity
$\gcd(F_m,F_n)=F_{\gcd(m,n)}$.
If $P_\ell=P_q$ at two prime labels, the equivalence at $n=q$
gives $\ell\mid q$, hence $\ell=q$.
Finally, each individual indicator in the two products is equal.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The real closed form and its alternating correction.}
Write
\[
 \varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2},\qquad
 \psi=\frac{1-\sqrt5}{2}=-\varphi^{-1}.
\]
Binet's formula and its exact relative error are
\begin{align*}
 F_n&=\frac{\varphi^n-\psi^n}{\sqrt5}
     =\frac{\varphi^n}{\sqrt5}
       \bigl(1-(-1)^n\varphi^{-2n}\bigr),\\
 F_n-\frac{\varphi^n}{\sqrt5}
     &=\frac{(-1)^{n+1}\varphi^{-n}}{\sqrt5}.
\end{align*}
Both powers satisfy the Fibonacci recurrence, and their difference
divided by $\varphi-\psi=\sqrt5$ has initial values $0,1$;
this proves the formula. Its absolute error is at most
$1/\sqrt5<1/2$, so $F_n$ is the nearest integer to
$\varphi^n/\sqrt5$ for every $n\ge0$.
The sign alternates with parity and the relative error is
$\varphi^{-2n}$. This real approximation explains the exponential
value scale. The divisibility clock uses the same quadratic equation
over finite fields, as follows.

\begin{theorem}[Golden-root order and Fibonacci divisibility]
\label{thm:fibonacci-golden-clock}
For roots $u,v$ of $T^2-T-1$ in any commutative ring,
\[
 (u-v)F_n=u^n-v^n.
\]
In a field, if $v=1-u$ and $u\ne v$, then
\[
 F_n=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \left(\frac{u}{v}\right)^n=1
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 (-u^2)^n=1.
\]
Here $F_n$ is interpreted by the natural-number map into the field.

For an odd prime $r\ne5$, choose
$u=(1+\sqrt5)/2$ in $\mathbb F_{r^2}$ and put
$\chi_5(r)=(5/r)$. The Fibonacci entry point
$\mathfrak z(r)=\min\{n\ge1:r\mid F_n\}$ satisfies
\[
 \mathfrak z(r)=\operatorname{ord}(-u^2),
 \qquad
 \mathfrak z(r)\mid r-\chi_5(r).
\]
The exceptional primes have the exact rules
\[
 2\mid F_n\Longleftrightarrow3\mid n,\qquad
 5\mid F_n\Longleftrightarrow5\mid n.
\]
Thus $\mathfrak z(2)=3$, $\mathfrak z(5)=5$,
$P_3=2$ and $P_5=5$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The ring identity follows from the initial values at $n=0,1$
and the recurrence: $u^2=u+1$ and $v^2=v+1$ ensure that both
sides satisfy the same second-order recursion.
For $v=1-u$, these identities give $uv=-1$, so $v\ne0$
and $u/v=-u^2$. With $u-v\ne0$, the ring identity can be
divided by $u-v$ and $v^n$, proving the power test.

For $r\ne2,5$, the discriminant $5$ is nonzero and the two roots
in $\mathbb F_{r^2}$ are distinct. If $\chi_5(r)=1$, both roots
belong to $\mathbb F_r$, so their nonzero quotient has order
dividing $r-1$. If $\chi_5(r)=-1$, the Frobenius automorphism
interchanges the two roots. For $t=u/v$ this gives
$t^r=v/u=t^{-1}$, hence $t^{r+1}=1$.
In either case the least positive exponent giving $t^n=1$
is exactly the least index with $r\mid F_n$.

Modulo $2$, the successive pairs in the Fibonacci recurrence
cycle through $(0,1),(1,1),(1,0)$, yielding precisely the indices
divisible by $3$. Modulo $5$ the roots coalesce at $3$; direct
substitution in the recurrence gives
$F_n=n\,3^{n-1}$ for $n\ge1$, with $n=0$ handled separately.
Since $3$ is invertible, vanishing is equivalent to $5\mid n$.
The selected carriers follow from $F_3=2$ and $F_5=5$.
\end{proof}

The use of finite fields is through the integral quadratic
identity, not a reduction map from the real field.
For the selected carrier and any odd prime label $\ell\ne5$,
the appropriate distinct-root field therefore gives
$\operatorname{ord}(-u^2)=\ell$.
At $P_3=2$ one may use a root of $T^2+T+1$ in $\mathbb F_4$;
its quotient has order $3$.
At $P_5=5$ the repeated-root formula supplies the clock instead.
This explicitly identifies the two exceptional labels.

For $\ell>5$ the carrier is odd and different from $5$.
The order bound consequently gives
\[
 \ell\mid P_\ell-\chi_5(P_\ell),\qquad
 2\ell-1\le P_\ell\le F_\ell<\varphi^\ell.
\]
Indeed, $P_\ell\pm1$ is even, so its positive multiple of the
odd prime $\ell$ is a multiple of $2\ell$.
The upper bound uses $P_\ell\mid F_\ell$ and Binet's formula.
These inequalities connect the real growth scale and the modular
clock while keeping label order and carrier magnitude distinct.

\begin{theorem}[The marked Fibonacci law and an inherited RH criterion]
\label{thm:fibonacci-marked-nicolas}
For a finite set $S$ of odd primes put $M_S=\prod_{\ell\in S}\ell$.
Uniform index sampling over $0\le n<M_S$ gives, for every binary
pattern $b:S\to\{0,1\}$,
\[
 \mathbb P\!\left(
   \boldsymbol{1}_{P_\ell\mid F_n}=b_\ell
   \text{ for all }\ell\in S\right)
 =
 \prod_{\ell\in S}
   \left(\frac1\ell\right)^{b_\ell}
   \left(1-\frac1\ell\right)^{1-b_\ell}.
\]
The empty support is allowed, with period $1$.
For $N\ge1$ and sampling over $1\le n\le N$, the probability of any fixed
pattern differs from this product by at most $M_S/N$.

For real $X\ge3$ let $S_X$ be the odd primes at most $X$,
$M_X=M_{S_X}$, and
\[
 A(X)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac1N
   \#\{1\le n\le N:
       P_\ell\nmid F_n\ \hbox{for every }\ell\in S_X\}.
\]
Then
\[
 A(X)=\prod_{\ell\in S_X}(1-1/\ell),
\]
and the classical Nicolas criterion gives the exact equivalence
\[
 \mathrm{RH}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 A(X)<\frac{2e^{-\gamma}}{\log\log(2M_X)}
 \quad\hbox{for all sufficiently large }X.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The carrier theorem replaces every Fibonacci event by $\ell\mid n$.
The Chinese remainder theorem identifies a uniform residue modulo
$M_S$ with independent uniform residues modulo the distinct labels.
There is one successful residue at each label and $\ell-1$
unsuccessful residues, proving every pattern probability.
For arbitrary $N=kM_S+t$, $0\le t<M_S$, there are $k$ complete
periods and one remainder. If $a$ residues in a full period give
the pattern, its total count is $ka+r$ with $0\le r\le t$.
The difference from $Na/M_S$ is $r-ta/M_S$, of absolute value
at most $t<M_S$. This proves the stated error and the fixed-cutoff
limit. Divisibility at $F_0=0$ is well-defined; this sampling
argument does not apply a reciprocal-divisor measure to zero.

For $X\ge3$, the full primorial is $2M_X$, so
$\theta(X)=\log(2M_X)>1$ and
\[
 f(X)=e^\gamma\log\theta(X)
          \prod_{p\le X}(1-1/p)
      =\frac{e^\gamma}{2}\log\log(2M_X)\,A(X).
\]
Every factor outside $A(X)$ is positive. Thus the displayed
probability inequality is exactly $f(X)<1$, or $F(X)<0$,
with $f,F$ as in \eqref{eq:analytic-objects}.
The Nicolas equivalence already used above is precisely
eventual $F(X)<0$ \cite{Nicolas1983,Nicolas2012}.
\end{proof}

The order of limits is essential: $S_X$ is fixed while
$N\to\infty$, and only afterward does $X\to\infty$.
The explicit sampling error $M_X/N$ gives no unproved uniform
growing-cutoff assertion. The finite generating polynomial is
\[
 G_X(z)=\prod_{\ell\in S_X}(1+(z-1)/\ell).
\]
After canonical compensation the profiles
\[
 \mathcal F_X(z)=
 \prod_{\ell\in S_X}
   (1+(z-1)/\ell)e^{-(z-1)/\ell}
\]
converge locally uniformly to an entire function, since
$\sum_\ell\ell^{-2}<\infty$. The existing Bernoulli profile
zero theorem gives zeros exactly $1-\ell$ at the odd prime labels.
These zeros encode index labels; the naturally ordered,
unmarked prime factors of $F_n$ form a different observable.

The maintained candidate owners
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/StrongDivisibilityCarrier.lean\\
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/FibonacciGoldenCarrier.lean
\end{quote}
check twenty-two declarations: the generic carrier and its exact
finite count/product transport, the ring Binet identity, the
field power tests, prime-index orders, and the two exceptional
integer clocks. Six more checked declarations in
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Helpers/FibonacciProbability.lean
\end{quote}
reuse the existing CRT equivalence to identify the actual marked
events and prove their full finite-period joint law.
All twenty-eight axiom audits contain only the standard
foundational axioms. The finite-field existence and Frobenius
classification, interval remainder estimate, and displayed
Nicolas equivalence are ordinary proofs here.
Entry-point arithmetic and finite-field torus descriptions are
classical; see Cubre--Rouse \cite{CubreRouse}.
The contribution here is their exact use as a marked probability
carrier and the explicit transfer to this existing RH criterion.
No infinitude of Fibonacci primes is assumed, and no improved
zero-free exponent follows without a new independent estimate.

\subsection{Actual Fermat primes: finite or infinite}
\label{sec:actual-fermat-prime-rh}

Here the prime family is exactly
\[
 T_j=2^{2^j}+1,\qquad
 J=\{j\ge0:T_j\text{ is prime}\},\qquad
 \mathcal P_F=\{T_j:j\in J\}.
\]
No assertion about the finiteness of $J$ is assumed. This family
differs from choosing one prime factor of every $T_j$: a composite
Fermat number contributes nothing to $\mathcal P_F$.
The distinction matters for the RH criterion. In the joint
Fibonacci--Fermat construction below, the RH information comes
from all odd prime labels in the Fibonacci block; the nested
Fermat block contributes a fixed vacancy factor $1/2$.
That construction is not an RH equivalence for actual Fermat
primes by themselves.

\begin{theorem}[Actual Fermat-prime avoidance and deficiency]
\label{thm:actual-fermat-avoidance}
Whether $J$ is finite or infinite, the product
\[
 b_F=\prod_{j\in J}(1-1/T_j)
\]
converges and is positive. It is the natural density of positive
integers divisible by no actual Fermat prime. More precisely,
\[
 \frac{2^{32}+1}{2^{33}}
 \ \le\ b_F\ \le\ \frac{2^{31}}{2^{32}-1},\qquad
 \frac{2^{31}}{2^{32}-1}-\frac{2^{32}+1}{2^{33}}
 =\frac1{2^{33}(2^{32}-1)}.
\]
Every positive integer $n$ whose prime divisors all belong to
$\mathcal P_F$ satisfies
\[
 \frac{\sigma(n)}n<2,\qquad P_n(0)>\frac12.
\]
Consequently restricting Robin's inequality to these integers
$n\ge5041$ gives a statement already true unconditionally,
rather than an RH equivalence.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The finite Fermat product telescopes:
\[
 \prod_{j<k}T_j=2^{2^k}-1,\qquad
 \prod_{j<k}(1-1/T_j)
 =\frac{2^{2^k-1}}{2^{2^k}-1},\qquad k\ge0.
\]
The empty product at $k=0$ is $1$. Thus the product over
\emph{all} Fermat numbers tends to $1/2$, and deleting factors
in $(0,1)$ can only increase it. Also
$\sum_jT_j^{-1}<\infty$, so the subproduct defining $b_F$
converges positively. The first five Fermat numbers are prime,
whereas $T_5=641\cdot6700417$ is composite. Retaining the first
five factors gives the upper bound. Retaining every factor
except $j=5$ gives the lower bound
$\tfrac12(1-1/T_5)^{-1}=(2^{32}+1)/2^{33}$.
Subtracting gives the displayed interval width. These elementary
facts about Fermat numbers are recalled in the introduction to
\cite{PomeranceCyclotomic}; no later primality decisions are used.

For any fixed finite prime cutoff, the Chinese remainder theorem
gives the avoidance density as its finite product. The upper
density of positive integers divisible by any omitted Fermat
prime is at most the reciprocal tail: for each $N\ge1$,
\[
 \frac1N\#\{1\le n\le N:\exists\,p\in\mathcal P_F,
                      \ p>X,\ p\mid n\}
 \le\sum_{\substack{p\in\mathcal P_F\\X<p\le N}}\frac1p
 \le\sum_{\substack{p\in\mathcal P_F\\p>X}}\frac1p.
\]
The final tail tends to zero, proving the full natural density
without exchanging two unproved sampling limits.

Finally write $n=\prod_{j\in S}T_j^{a_j}$, where $S$ is a
finite subset of $J$ and $a_j\ge1$. Choose $k$ with
$S\subseteq\{0,\ldots,k-1\}$. The divisor-sum product gives
\[
 \frac{\sigma(n)}n
 =\prod_{j\in S}
    \frac{1-T_j^{-(a_j+1)}}{1-T_j^{-1}}
 \le\prod_{j<k}(1-1/T_j)^{-1}
 =2-2^{1-2^k}<2.
\]
This also covers $n=1$ with $k=0$.
Since $P_n(0)=n/\sigma(n)$, its vacancy is greater than $1/2$.
On $n\ge5041$, $e^{-\gamma}/\log\log n<1/2$, as used in
the perfect-number discussion above, so Robin's probability
inequality follows with no RH hypothesis.
\end{proof}

This proves that even allowing arbitrary powers and arbitrarily
many actual Fermat primes does not reach the large-abundancy
regime responsible for Robin counterexamples. It does not prove
that $J$ is finite, infinite, or independent of RH.

\begin{theorem}[The Fermat Euler factor preserves the zeta zeros]
\label{thm:fermat-euler-zero-preservation}
For $\Re s>0$ define
\[
 H_F(s)=\prod_{p\in\mathcal P_F}(1-p^{-s}).
\]
This is holomorphic and nowhere zero on that half-plane, with
$H_F(1)=b_F$. The meromorphic function
\[
 \zeta_{\overline F}(s)=\zeta(s)H_F(s)
\]
has exactly the same zeros, with the same multiplicities, as
$\zeta$ in $\Re s>0$, and still has a simple pole at $1$.
In $\Re s>1$ it is the Euler product over primes that are not
Fermat primes. In particular,
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \zeta_{\overline F}(s)\ne0
 \quad(\Re s>1/2,\ s\ne1).
\]
This holds in both the finite and infinite cases for $J$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For every $\sigma>0$,
\[
 \sum_{j\in J}T_j^{-\sigma}
 \le\sum_{j\ge0}2^{-\sigma 2^j}
 \le\sum_{j\ge0}2^{-\sigma(j+1)}<\infty.
\]
On a compact subset of $\Re s>0$ choose a lower bound
$\Re s\ge\sigma>0$. The factors $p^{-s}$ have modulus at most
$p^{-\sigma}<1$, and the displayed sum gives locally uniform
absolute convergence of $\sum_p\log(1-p^{-s})$ using the branch
defined by its power series in the unit disk. Its exponential
is $H_F$, proving holomorphy and nonvanishing. This argument
does not distinguish a finite subproduct from an infinite one.

The ordinary Euler product of $\zeta$ in $\Re s>1$ gives the
asserted complementary product. Multiplication by a holomorphic
unit preserves every zero and its order, and $H_F(1)=b_F>0$
preserves the pole. The final equivalence is the usual zero-free
half-plane formulation of RH.
\end{proof}

Thus the Fermat-prime Euler product $H_F^{-1}$ itself is
already zero-free throughout $\Re s>0$. The displayed RH
equivalence retains the complementary primes; it is not a
criterion involving only the number or spacing of Fermat primes.

\begin{theorem}[An infinitude-free complementary probability criterion]
\label{thm:fermat-complement-nicolas}
For $X\ge3$ put
\[
 B_F(X)=\prod_{\substack{p\le X\\p\in\mathcal P_F}}(1-1/p),
 \quad
 A_{\overline F}(X)=
      \prod_{\substack{p\le X\\p\notin\mathcal P_F}}(1-1/p),
 \quad
 \theta_{\overline F}(X)=
      \sum_{\substack{p\le X\\p\notin\mathcal P_F}}\log p,
\]
where every $p$ in these finite products and sums is prime.
Then, without an assumption on the size of $J$,
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 A_{\overline F}(X)<
 \frac{e^{-\gamma}}{b_F\log\theta_{\overline F}(X)}
 \quad\hbox{for all sufficiently large }X.
\]
The product $A_{\overline F}(X)$ is the fixed-cutoff limiting
probability of avoiding all non-Fermat primes at most $X$ in
uniform positive-integer sampling. If $f(X)$ is the original
Nicolas function and
\[
 f_{\overline F}(X)=e^\gamma b_F
             \log\theta_{\overline F}(X)A_{\overline F}(X),
\]
then an absolute eventual error bound is
\[
 0\le\log f(X)-\log f_{\overline F}(X)\le\frac{19}{X}.
\]
The constant $19$ is explicit; the starting threshold here is
only asserted to exist, not numerically specified.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $m$ be the least label for which $T_m>X$. Telescoping the
full Fermat-number product gives
\[
 1-\frac1{T_m-1}
 =\prod_{j\ge m}(1-1/T_j)
 \le\frac{b_F}{B_F(X)}\le1.
\]
Consequently, for $X\ge3$,
\[
 0\le\log B_F(X)-\log b_F
 \le-\log(1-1/(X-1))\le\frac3X.
\]
For $r_F(X)=\theta(X)-\theta_{\overline F}(X)$, the other
finite product identity gives
\[
