 0\le r_F(X)\le\sum_{j<m}\log T_j
 =\log(T_m-2)<2\log T_{m-1}\le2\log X.
\]
Here $m\ge1$ because $X\ge3$.
The unconditional prime number theorem implies that, eventually,
$\theta(X)\ge X/2$, $2\log X\le X/4$, and
$\log(X/4)\ge\tfrac12\log X$. Therefore
$\theta_{\overline F}(X)\ge X/4>1$ and
\[
 0\le\log\log\theta(X)-\log\log\theta_{\overline F}(X)
 \le\frac{r_F(X)}{
      \theta_{\overline F}(X)\log\theta_{\overline F}(X)}
 \le\frac{16}{X}.
\]
All denominators used in the criterion are positive in this
eventual range. Since the full prime product is
$B_F(X)A_{\overline F}(X)$, exact subtraction gives
\[
 \log f-\log f_{\overline F}
 =\log B_F-\log b_F+
   \log\log\theta-\log\log\theta_{\overline F}.
\]
The preceding estimates prove the claimed signed error bound.

Under RH, Nicolas's criterion gives $\log f(X)<0$ eventually;
the nonnegative subtraction then gives
$\log f_{\overline F}(X)<0$. If RH fails, the same source
gives positive excursions
$\log f(X)\ge cX^{-b}$ at arbitrarily large $X$, for some
constants $c>0$ and $0<b<1/2$
\cite[equation~(1.10)]{Nicolas2012}.
For sufficiently large points on that excursion sequence,
$cX^{-b}>19/X$, hence $\log f_{\overline F}(X)>0$.
The eventual inequality is therefore false. This proves both
directions without assuming finitely or infinitely many Fermat
primes, and without using an RH-dependent reconstruction in
the non-RH direction.
\end{proof}

These are ordinary proofs in this section, not new Lean
declarations or claims of a new RH estimate. The last criterion
is an application of the existing Nicolas excursion theorem
with a proved $O(X^{-1})$ deletion error. If $J$ is finite,
$B_F(X)$ eventually equals the finite constant $b_F$; if $J$
is infinite it approaches the same well-defined positive
constant without stabilizing. Neither case changes the
equivalence. No implication from RH to finiteness or infinitude
of actual Fermat primes has been established here, and the
certified zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.

\subsection{Which prime classes may be removed?}
\label{sec:prime-class-deletion}

The preceding argument depends on the size of the removed prime
class, rather than its being a Fermat family. The following gives
a sufficient condition, not a classification of all possible
zero-preserving restrictions.

\begin{theorem}[Critical-exponent prime deletion]
\label{thm:critical-prime-deletion}
Let $S$ be a fixed set of primes. Suppose that for every
$\epsilon>0$ there are constants $C_\epsilon>0$ and
$X_\epsilon\ge2$ such that
\[
 \pi_S(X)=\#\{p\in S:p\le X\}
 \le C_\epsilon X^{1/2+\epsilon}
 \qquad(X\ge X_\epsilon).
\]
Then
\[
 H_S(s)=\prod_{p\in S}(1-p^{-s})
\]
is holomorphic and nowhere zero for $\Re s>1/2$.
Thus $\zeta(s)H_S(s)$ retains exactly the zeros of $\zeta$,
with their multiplicities, in that half-plane, as well as its
pole at $1$.

Let $C$ denote the complementary prime set, and define
\[
 b_S=\prod_{p\in S}(1-1/p)>0,\quad
 A_C(X)=\prod_{\substack{p\in C\\p\le X}}(1-1/p),\quad
 \theta_C(X)=\sum_{\substack{p\in C\\p\le X}}\log p.
\]
There is an exact probability version of the equivalence:
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 A_C(X)<\frac{e^{-\gamma}}{b_S\log\theta_C(X)}
 \quad\hbox{for all sufficiently large }X.
\]
Here $A_C(X)$ is the limiting avoidance probability for
uniform sampling of positive integers at each fixed cutoff $X$.
No existence or infinitude assumption on the class $S$ is needed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For every $\sigma>1/2$, choose $\epsilon>0$ with
$1/2+\epsilon<\min(\sigma,1)$. Partial summation gives
$\sum_{p\in S}p^{-\sigma}<\infty$. The logarithmic product
argument in Theorem~\ref{thm:fermat-euler-zero-preservation}
therefore gives the claimed holomorphic unit. In particular,
the product $b_S$ converges positively.

We record the complete signed error for the probability
criterion. Write
\begin{align*}
 B_S(X)&=\prod_{\substack{p\in S\\p\le X}}(1-1/p),\qquad
 r_S(X)=\sum_{\substack{p\in S\\p\le X}}\log p,\\
 f_C(X)&=e^\gamma b_S\log\theta_C(X)A_C(X).
\end{align*}
Whenever $\theta_C(X)>1$, the exact identity is
\begin{align*}
 \Delta_S(X)&=\log f(X)-\log f_C(X)\\
 &=\sum_{\substack{p\in S\\p>X}}-\log(1-1/p)
   +\log\log\theta(X)-\log\log(\theta(X)-r_S(X))\ge0.
\end{align*}
Both the reciprocal tail and the change of the theta normalizer
are retained. For a quantitative bound, suppose
$\pi_S(t)\le C_0t^\alpha$ on $t\ge X_0$, where
$C_0>0$ and $0\le\alpha<1$. For $X\ge\max(2,X_0)$,
\[
 \sum_{\substack{p\in S\\p>X}}-\log(1-1/p)
 \le2\sum_{\substack{p\in S\\p>X}}\frac1p
 \le\frac{2C_0}{1-\alpha}X^{\alpha-1},\qquad
 r_S(X)\le C_0X^\alpha\log X.
\]
Eventually the unconditional prime number theorem and
$r_S(X)=o(X)$ give $\theta_C(X)\ge X/4$ and
$\log\theta_C(X)\ge\tfrac12\log X$. Hence
\[
 0\le\Delta_S(X)
 \le C_0\left(8+\frac2{1-\alpha}\right)X^{\alpha-1}
 \quad\hbox{eventually}.
\]
Every denominator is positive in this range. The numerical
coefficient is explicit; the threshold depends on the stated
counting bound and is not specified numerically here.

Under RH, $\log f<0$ eventually and $\Delta_S\ge0$, so
$\log f_C<0$. If RH fails, the existing Nicolas theorem
supplies some $c>0$ and $0<b<1/2$ with
$\log f(X)\ge cX^{-b}$ at arbitrarily large $X$.
Choose $\epsilon$ with $0<\epsilon<1/2-b$ and apply the
last bound with $\alpha=1/2+\epsilon$. Then
$\Delta_S(X)=o(X^{-b})$, so $\log f_C(X)>0$ at arbitrarily
large points of the same sequence. This contradicts the
eventual inequality and proves the converse.
The CRT proves the fixed-cutoff probability interpretation.
\end{proof}

Finite unions of classes satisfying the hypothesis can be
deleted simultaneously. Examples include actual Fermat primes,
Mersenne primes and Fibonacci primes: their counting functions
are at most logarithmic, with no assumed infinitude.
For a fixed integer polynomial $P$ of degree $d\ge2$ and
positive leading coefficient, the primes among $P(n)$,
$n\ge0$, have counting function $O_P(X^{1/d})$ and can also
be deleted. These are prime \emph{values}, not all prime
divisors of those values. Infinite unions or varying polynomial
parameters require additional uniform estimates.
The theorem alone does not cover a positive-density residue
class, nor does it supply the needed bound for Sophie Germain
primes. Failure of this sufficient counting condition is not
an impossibility theorem for those classes.

\begin{theorem}[All Fermat factors form a deletable class]
\label{thm:all-fermat-factor-deletion}
Let
\[
 \mathcal D_F=\{p\text{ prime}:p\mid 2^{2^j}+1
                              \text{ for some }j\ge0\}.
\]
This is an infinite set. For every real $X\ge3$,
\[
 \pi_{\mathcal D_F}(X)
 \le(1+6\log2)\sqrt{\frac{X}{\log X}}.
\]
Consequently every prime in $\mathcal D_F$ can be deleted
simultaneously in Theorem~\ref{thm:critical-prime-deletion}.
The result concerns all prime factors of all Fermat numbers,
regardless of whether there are finitely or infinitely many
actual Fermat primes.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $m_j$ be the number of distinct prime factors of $T_j$,
and $W_k=\sum_{j<k}m_j$. The proved factor-count and cumulative
weight estimates give
\[
 (j+1)m_j\le2^j,\qquad (k+1)W_k\le3\cdot2^k.
\]
Their maintained providers are
\texttt{Nat.fermatFactorCount\_mul\_le\_pow} and
\texttt{Nat.dyadicPrefixWeight\_mul\_le\_three\_pow}.
Every factor has multiplicative order $2^{j+1}$ for base $2$.
Lagrange's theorem in the nonzero residue group therefore
gives $2^{j+1}\mid p-1$. In particular, all factors from
labels $j\ge k$ lie in the \emph{single} progression
$p\equiv1\pmod{2^{k+1}}$. Since $p>1$, there are at most
$X/2^{k+1}$ such primes up to $X$. Thus the finite covering
bound, including the low-label factors, is
\[
 \pi_{\mathcal D_F}(X)\le W_k+\frac{X}{2^{k+1}}
 \qquad(k\ge0).
\]
This bound does not sum overlapping progression counts.

Set $Y=\sqrt{X\log X}$ and choose $k\ge0$ with
$2^k\le Y<2^{k+1}$. Since $X\ge3$,
\[
 k+1>\frac{\log Y}{\log2}
       \ge\frac{\log X}{2\log2}.
\]
The low part is at most
$6\log2\sqrt{X/\log X}$, while the high part is at most
$X/Y=\sqrt{X/\log X}$. Adding proves the stated bound.
Every Fermat number has a prime factor, and distinct labels
have disjoint prime-factor supports, so $\mathcal D_F$ is
infinite. The counting bound implies the hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:critical-prime-deletion}.
\end{proof}

The finite covering and its optimized real bound are ordinary
consequences here of the named checked weight estimates and
the exact order theorem. They are not yet separately compiled
declarations. No improved zero-free exponent is inferred.

\begin{theorem}[Deletion by smooth multiplicative order]
\label{thm:smooth-order-deletion}
Fix an integer $a\ge2$ and a finite set $B$ of primes. Let
$S_{a,B}$ be the primes $p\nmid a$ for which every prime
factor of $\operatorname{ord}_p(a)$ belongs to $B$; order $1$
is allowed. For $0<\eta<1$ set
\[
 C_B(\eta)=\prod_{q\in B}(1-q^{-\eta})^{-1}.
\]
Then, for every $X\ge2$,
\[
 \pi_{S_{a,B}}(X)
 \le\left(1+\frac{\log a}{\log2}\right)
          C_B(\eta)X^{(1+\eta)/2}.
\]
In particular $S_{a,B}$ satisfies the critical-exponent deletion
criterion. This gives an RH equivalence after deleting all
primes whose multiplicative orders use any specified finite
set of prime factors.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $E_B$ be the positive integers all of whose prime factors
are in $B$, including $1$. Unique factorization and finite
geometric products give
\[
 \sum_{m\in E_B}m^{-\eta}=C_B(\eta).
\]
Partition the primes being counted by whether their actual
order $m$ is at most $Y\ge1$. Every low-order prime divides
\[
 \prod_{\substack{m\in E_B\\m\le Y}}(a^m-1).
\]
The distinct product of these primes is at least $2$ to the
power of their number and divides the displayed integer.
Consequently their number is at most
\[
 \frac{\log a}{\log2}
      \sum_{\substack{m\in E_B\\m\le Y}}m
 \le\frac{\log a}{\log2}C_B(\eta)Y^{1+\eta}.
\]
For the high-order primes, $m\mid p-1$ and $p\le X$ give
at most $X/m$ possibilities at any fixed $m$. Thus their
total number is at most
\[
 X\sum_{\substack{m\in E_B\\m>Y}}\frac1m
 \le X C_B(\eta)Y^{-1+\eta}.
\]
These are bounds on the two parts of the actual order
partition; they do not assume distinctness of the integers
$a^m-1$. Set $Y=\sqrt X$ and add the bounds.
Since $\eta$ can be arbitrarily small and positive, the
critical-exponent hypothesis follows. For $a=2$ and
$B=\{2\}$, a nontrivial power-of-two order is equivalent
to divisibility by a Fermat number, giving $\mathcal D_F$.
\end{proof}

The explicit factor $C_B(\eta)$ records the cost of enlarging
the removed order class. The theorem is uniform in $X$ for
fixed $a,B,\eta$, and does not exchange an unbounded $B$
with the cutoff limit. These deletions still leave a set of
primes of relative density $1$. To minimize the retained
\emph{proportion} requires a different argument; identifying
safe sparse deletions alone does not accomplish that.

\subsection{Sparse positive prime carriers for all Dirichlet zeros}
\label{sec:sparse-positive-prime-carriers}

Safe deletion of thin classes leaves almost all primes. Reducing
the retained proportion is a different problem. Fixed-rank
thinning, retaining every $k$th prime for a fixed $k$, already
occurs in partial-Euler-product RH criteria
\cite{JurzakPartialEuler}. The next construction instead
allows positive integer multiplicities and retains a single
set with only polynomially many primes in $\log X$.
The multiplicities are part of the data and cannot be erased.

\begin{theorem}[A simultaneous positive integer prime compression]
\label{thm:universal-prime-compression}
There are a fixed set $Q$ of ordinary primes and positive
integer weights $w_p$, $p\in Q$, with
\[
 \#\{p\in Q:p\le X\}
 \le(\lfloor\log_2X\rfloor+2)^6\qquad(X\ge2),
\]
such that the following holds for \emph{every} Dirichlet
character $\chi$, of any fixed modulus $q\ge1$.
The weighted Euler product
\[
 L_w(s,\chi)=\prod_{p\in Q}(1-\chi(p)p^{-s})^{-w_p},
 \qquad\Re s>1,
\]
converges absolutely and has a meromorphic continuation to
$\Re s>0$ of the form
\[
 L_w(s,\chi)=L(s,\chi)\exp G_\chi(s),
 \qquad G_\chi\text{ holomorphic on }\Re s>0.
\]
Consequently all zeros and their multiplicities in that
half-plane, and any principal-character pole at $1$, are
preserved exactly. In particular, with $\boldsymbol1$ the
character modulo $1$,
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 L_w(s,\boldsymbol1)\ne0
 \quad(\Re s>1/2,\ s\ne1).
\]
The weights can be chosen with $w_p\le2^j$ for
$2^j\le p<2^{j+1}$. The same $Q$ and the same weights work
for all characters; no uniform bound for their compensators
over unbounded conductors or heights is asserted.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $j\ge1$ let $P_j$ be the primes in $[2^j,2^{j+1})$,
and $N_j=\#P_j\le2^j$. We first choose nonnegative integer
weights $w_{j,p}$ on this finite set, preserving all moments
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prime-compression-moments}
 \sum_{\substack{p\in P_j\\p\equiv a\ ({\rm mod}\ m)}}
       w_{j,p}p^r
 =\sum_{\substack{p\in P_j\\p\equiv a\ ({\rm mod}\ m)}}p^r,
 \quad 1\le m\le j,\quad0\le a<m,\quad0\le r\le j.
\end{equation}
The all-one weights are feasible. The coordinate $m=1,r=0$
forces $\sum_p w_{j,p}=N_j$, so every feasible weight is
at most $N_j$ and the feasible collection is finite. Choose
a feasible weight vector with minimum positive support $S_j$.
For an empty block take the empty vector.

The map taking a subset $U\subseteq S_j$ to its vector of
\emph{unweighted} moment sums is injective. Otherwise two
distinct subsets with equal vectors give, after deleting their
intersection, disjoint nonempty subsets $A,B$ with equal
vectors. They are both nonempty because the zeroth moment
also gives equal cardinalities. Subtract
$t=\min_{p\in A}w_{j,p}\ge1$ from the weights on $A$ and add
$t$ to the weights on $B$. All weights remain nonnegative
integers, every moment is preserved, and at least one support
point disappears without adding a new one. This contradicts
minimality.

Write $K_j=\#S_j$. Each coordinate of a subset moment vector
at degree $r$ is an integer between $0$ and
$2^{j+(j+1)r}$. Its number of possible values is at most
$2^{(j+1)(r+1)}$. Counting all coordinates of the injective
map therefore gives
\[
 2^{K_j}\le
 2^{\sum_{m=1}^j\sum_{a=0}^{m-1}\sum_{r=0}^j
                         (j+1)(r+1)},\qquad
 K_j\le\frac{j(j+1)^3(j+2)}4\le(j+2)^5.
\]
Set $Q=\bigcup_{j\ge1}S_j$ and retain the positive weights
on each block. For $J=\lfloor\log_2X\rfloor\ge1$,
\[
 \#\{p\in Q:p\le X\}
 \le\sum_{j=1}^J K_j
 \le J(J+2)^5\le(J+2)^6.
\]

It remains to prove that these exact finite cancellations
preserve the full Euler factors, including all prime powers.
For $|c|\le1$, $x>1$ and $\Re s>0$ put
\[
 f_{s,c}(x)=-\log(1-cx^{-s})
           =\sum_{\ell\ge1}\frac{c^\ell x^{-\ell s}}\ell,
\]
using the power-series branch. Every finite block difference
\[
 D_{j,\chi}(s)=\sum_{p\in P_j}(w_{j,p}-1)f_{s,\chi(p)}(p)
\]
is holomorphic on $\Re s>0$. Fix a compact subset $K$ of
that half-plane, and bounds $\Re s\ge\sigma_0>0$ and
$|\Im s|\le T$ on $K$. Let $u=2^j$ and $c_j=3u/2$.
On the complex disk $|z-c_j|\le u$, we have
$\Re z\ge u/2$ and, for sufficiently large $j$,
\[
 |z^{-s}|\le2^{\sigma_0}e^{T\pi/2}u^{-\sigma_0}\le\frac12.
\]
Thus $f_{s,c}(z)$ is holomorphic there and is bounded uniformly
for $s\in K$ and $|c|\le1$ by $M_Ku^{-\sigma_0}$, where
one may take $M_K=2^{1+\sigma_0}e^{T\pi/2}$ in this
eventual range.

The Taylor polynomial of degree $j$ about $c_j$ has remainder
at most
\[
 M_Ku^{-\sigma_0}2^{-j}
 \quad\text{on }u\le x\le2u,
\]
by Cauchy's estimate and $|x-c_j|/u\le1/2$.
If $j\ge q$, apply this separately in each residue class
modulo $q$. The value $\chi(p)$ is constant on that class.
Equation~\eqref{eq:prime-compression-moments} cancels its
entire Taylor polynomial, including the constant term.
Only after this exact signed cancellation do we bound the
two remainder measures, whose total mass is $2N_j\le2u$.
It follows that
\[
 \sup_{s\in K}|D_{j,\chi}(s)|
 \le2M_K2^{-j\sigma_0}
 \qquad(j\ge\max(q,J_K)),
\]
where $J_K$ makes the preceding disk estimate valid. Hence
$G_\chi=\sum_{j\ge1}D_{j,\chi}$ converges locally uniformly
on $\Re s>0$ and is holomorphic there. More explicitly,
\[
 \sup_{s\in K}\left|\sum_{j\ge J}D_{j,\chi}(s)\right|
 \le\frac{2M_K2^{-J\sigma_0}}{1-2^{-\sigma_0}}
 \quad(J\ge\max(q,J_K)).
\]
This also makes the conductor and height restrictions on the
tail estimate explicit.

For $\Re s>1$, absolute convergence follows from
\[
 \sum_{p\in Q}w_pp^{-\sigma}
 \le\sum_{j\ge1}N_j2^{-j\sigma}
 \le\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-j(\sigma-1)}<\infty.
\]
The usual Dirichlet Euler product then gives, in that domain,
$\log L_w-\log L=G_\chi$. Exponentiation and analytic
continuation prove the displayed factorization. The multiplier
is a holomorphic unit, so it preserves zeros, multiplicities
and poles. Taking the character modulo $1$ proves the RH
equivalence.
\end{proof}

The finite support-reduction argument is the classical integer
moment-elimination principle, related to the integer-cone
bounds of Eisenbrand--Shmonin \cite{EisenbrandShmonin}.
Grosswald--Schnitzer \cite{GrosswaldSchnitzer} preserve zeta
and Dirichlet zeros by perturbing Euler bases. Jurzak's
fixed-rank thinning retains a positive proportion of the prime
list. The theorem above specifies a single actual prime
support of polynomial logarithmic size, positive integer
multiplicities, and simultaneous preservation for every fixed
Dirichlet character. Its proof is given here; priority for this
combination has not been established.

\paragraph{The associated positive probability law.}
The weights give a genuine probability model, without an
assumed prime-clock sampling theorem. For $\sigma>1$, expand
\[
 Z_w(s)=L_w(s,\boldsymbol1)
       =\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{a_w(n)}{n^s},\qquad
 a_w(p^r)=\binom{w_p+r-1}{r}\quad(p\in Q),
\]
with $a_w$ multiplicative and zero on integers having a prime
factor outside $Q$. All coefficients are nonnegative integers.
The distribution
\[
 \mathbb P_{w,\sigma}(n)=
       \frac{a_w(n)n^{-\sigma}}{Z_w(\sigma)}
\]
is normalized on positive integers. Its prime exponents are
independent negative-binomial variables with integer shape
$w_p$ and parameter $p^{-\sigma}$. For every Dirichlet
character and real $t$,
\[
 \mathbb E_{w,\sigma}[\chi(n)n^{-it}]
       =\frac{L_w(\sigma+it,\chi)}{Z_w(\sigma)}.
\]
The sampled variable is the integer $n$ under this explicitly
weighted law; $\sigma>1$ is fixed. This is not an assertion
about uniform integer sampling, nor a transfer of a
prime-clock estimate to a new family without an adapter.

\paragraph{What compression does and does not give.}
The main terms vanish through the exact moment identities;
the proof bounds only their retained analytic remainder.
It preserves the complete zero signal, rather than showing
that the signal cannot occur. The integer weights encode
moments of all primes in each block and can grow with the
prime size. A useful attack would need an independent estimate
on the resulting weighted arithmetic sums, with that growth
charged explicitly. No such zero-excluding estimate follows
from support size alone.

Indeed, deleting the multiplicities would destroy this
construction's signal: the unweighted Euler product on $Q$
is already holomorphic and nowhere zero on $\Re s>0$, since
its prime count is only polynomial in $\log X$.
The sparse weighted product need not admit a direct
pointwise Nicolas comparison at every cutoff: weights are
chosen on complete dyadic blocks, and no uniform small
error for partial blocks has been proved. Nor does
holomorphic continuation alone supply a square-root bound
for its signed inverse coefficients without the necessary
vertical growth estimates.

\begin{lemma}[Exact finite integer moment compression]
\label{lem:integer-moment-compression}
Let $I,K$ be finite sets and $v:I\times K\to\mathbb N$.
There are weights $w_i\in\mathbb N$ such that, writing
$S=\{i:w_i>0\}$ and $M_k=\sum_{i\in I}v(i,k)$,
\[
 \sum_{i\in I}w_iv(i,k)=M_k\quad(k\in K),
 \qquad 2^{|S|}\le\prod_{k\in K}(M_k+1).
\]
They can be chosen so that distinct subsets of $S$ have
distinct vectors of unweighted coordinate sums.
Empty index or coordinate sets are allowed.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The all-one vector is feasible. Minimize its support
cardinality among feasible natural weight vectors. If two
distinct subsets of the support have equal signatures,
delete their intersection and orient the resulting pair
so that the first subset is nonempty. Subtract its minimum
positive weight there and add it on the second subset.
Disjointness preserves nonnegativity and every coordinate
sum, while strictly decreasing support. This contradiction
proves injectivity, including when the second subset is empty.
For each $k$, every unweighted subset sum is an integer
between $0$ and $M_k$, because the support is contained in $I$
and $v$ is nonnegative. Counting signatures gives the bound.
No nonemptiness hypothesis was used.
\end{proof}

The finite lemma, including its injectivity and exact
support bound, is compiled in the maintained Lean candidate
\path{PrimeFactorOscillations/Mathlib/Combinatorics/IntegerMomentCompression.lean}.
Its quantitative declaration is
\texttt{Finset.exists\_nat\_weights\_card\_pow\_le}.
The axiom audit contains only \texttt{propext},
\texttt{Classical.choice} and \texttt{Quot.sound}.
The prime-class deletion results and the analytic continuation
and zero preservation in
Theorem~\ref{thm:universal-prime-compression} retain their
ordinary proofs; those analytic conclusions are not claimed
as compiled Lean declarations.
The certified $7/8$ zero-free boundary is unchanged.

\subsection{Controlled weights and a square-root prime carrier}
\label{sec:controlled-prime-carrier}

The preceding construction minimizes support aggressively, but
its large integer weights encode many moments. A different
choice gives a prescribed small weight cap and an explicit
height bound. It can simultaneously avoid every prime in a
fixed safely deletable class.

\begin{theorem}[A weight-controlled carrier at the RH threshold]
\label{thm:controlled-prime-carrier}
Let $S$ be a fixed prime set with
$\pi_S(X)\ll_\epsilon X^{1/2+\epsilon}$ for every $\epsilon>0$,
and put $C=\mathcal P\setminus S$.
There are a fixed set $Q\subseteq C$ and positive integer
weights $w_p\le\sqrt p$, $p\in Q$, such that, for
$J=\lfloor\log_2X\rfloor$,
\[
 \pi_Q(X)\le6\sqrt X+\frac{J(J+1)}2\qquad(X\ge2).
\]
The prime number theorem sharpens the order to
$\pi_Q(X)\asymp\sqrt X/\log X$ as $X\to\infty$.
For every fixed Dirichlet character $\chi$, the product
\[
 L_Q(s,\chi)=\prod_{p\in Q}(1-\chi(p)p^{-s})^{-w_p}
 \qquad(\Re s>1)
\]
has a meromorphic continuation to $\Re s>1/2$ of the form
\[
 L_Q(s,\chi)=L(s,\chi)H_{S,\chi}(s)\exp G_\chi(s),
 \qquad
 H_{S,\chi}(s)=\prod_{p\in S}(1-\chi(p)p^{-s}),
\]
where both multipliers are holomorphic and nowhere zero
there. Thus every zero and multiplicity in that half-plane
is preserved, as is the principal pole at $1$.
In particular, RH is equivalent to
$L_Q(s,\boldsymbol1)\ne0$ for $\Re s>1/2$, $s\ne1$.
The same $Q$ and weights work for all fixed characters.
For every fixed modulus $q$ and $\sigma_0>1/2$,
\[
 |G_\chi(s)|\le B_{q,\sigma_0}
                  +A_{\sigma_0}|s|
 \quad(\Re s\ge\sigma_0,\ \chi\bmod q),
\]
with constants specified in the proof.
There is no uncharged uniformity in a growing modulus.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $j\ge1$ let $C_j=C\cap[2^j,2^{j+1})$ and $N_j=|C_j|$.
Let $k_j$ be the largest integer $k\le j$ with $k!\le j$,
and set $M_j=k_j!$. Then $1\le M_j\le j$, and
$q\mid M_j$ whenever $j\ge q!$.
Set $h_j=2^{\lfloor j/2\rfloor}$.
Within each residue class modulo $M_j$, list the elements
of $C_j$ in increasing order and divide this list into
successive groups of length $h_j$, allowing a shorter last
group. Retain the largest prime of each nonempty group and
give it weight equal to the group length.

Writing $K_j=|Q\cap C_j|$, this gives the exact identities
and bounds
\[
 \sum_{\substack{p\in Q\cap C_j\\p\equiv a\ ({\rm mod}\ M_j)}}w_p
   =\#\{p\in C_j:p\equiv a\ ({\rm mod}\ M_j)\},\qquad
 \frac{N_j}{h_j}\le K_j\le\frac{N_j}{h_j}+M_j.
\]
All weights are positive integers at most $h_j\le\sqrt p$.
In particular,
\[
 \pi_Q(X)\le
 \sum_{j=1}^J2^{\lceil j/2\rceil}+\sum_{j=1}^Jj
 \le6\sqrt X+\frac{J(J+1)}2.
\]
For the first sum, pair $j=2r-1,2r$; the possible final
unpaired term is also covered by the constant $6$.
The deletion hypothesis implies $\pi_S(X)=o(X/\log X)$.
Consequently the prime number theorem gives
$N_j\asymp2^j/j$. The displayed two bounds on $K_j$, followed
by a geometric sum for the upper bound and the last complete
block for the lower bound, prove the sharper order for
$\pi_Q(X)$. Its comparison constants and starting point
are eventual; no numerical starting point is asserted.

Here is the signed cancellation preserving the full Euler
factors. For $u=2^j$, $u\le x\le2u$, and $0\le a<M_j$, put
\[
 E_{j,a}(x)=
 \sum_{\substack{p\in C_j,\ p\le x\\p\equiv a\ ({\rm mod}\ M_j)}}
       \bigl(w_p\mathbf1_Q(p)-1\bigr),
\]
where $w_p\mathbf1_Q(p)$ means zero off $Q$.
A completed group contributes zero. Inside an unfinished
group there are fewer than $h_j$ omitted primes.
The shorter final group also has total contribution zero.
Hence, including both endpoints,
\[
 -(h_j-1)\le E_{j,a}(x)\le0,\qquad E_{j,a}(2u)=0.
\]
For $|c|\le1$ and $\Re s>0$, retain the entire Euler function
\[
 f_{s,c}(x)=-\log(1-cx^{-s})
   =\sum_{\ell\ge1}\frac{c^\ell x^{-\ell s}}{\ell},
 \qquad
 f'_{s,c}(x)=-\frac{scx^{-s-1}}{1-cx^{-s}}.
\]
No prime powers have been discarded.
If $j\ge q!$, the character has a constant value $c_a$ on
each of these residue classes. Finite Abel summation gives
the exact coupled identity
\[
 D_{j,\chi}(s):=
 \sum_{p\in C_j}(w_p\mathbf1_Q(p)-1)f_{s,\chi(p)}(p)
 =-\sum_{a=0}^{M_j-1}\int_u^{2u}E_{j,a}(x)f'_{s,c_a}(x)\,dx.
\]
The endpoint term vanishes because each class has exactly
matched total mass. Only after this cancellation do we
estimate the retained derivative integral. For
$\sigma=\Re s>1/2$,
\[
 |D_{j,\chi}(s)|
 \le
 \frac{M_j(h_j-1)|s|\,2^{-j\sigma}(1-2^{-\sigma})}
      {\sigma(1-2^{-j\sigma})}.
\]
In particular, for $\sigma\ge\sigma_0>1/2$, put
$\rho=2^{-(\sigma_0-1/2)}<1$ and
$C_{\sigma_0}=1/[\sigma_0(1-2^{-\sigma_0})]$.
For every $R\ge q!$ this yields the explicit tail
\[
 \left|\sum_{j\ge R}D_{j,\chi}(s)\right|
 \le C_{\sigma_0}|s|\rho^R
       \left(\frac{R}{1-\rho}
                      +\frac{\rho}{(1-\rho)^2}\right).
\]
This proves normal convergence on $\Re s>1/2$ of
$G_\chi(s)=\sum_{j\ge1}D_{j,\chi}(s)$; the finitely many
blocks $j<q!$ are individually holomorphic on $\Re s>0$.
For the stated height bound one may take
\[
 B_{q,\sigma_0}=
 2\sum_{1\le j<q!}
       \frac{2^{j(1-\sigma_0)}}{1-2^{-j\sigma_0}},
 \qquad
 A_{\sigma_0}=
 C_{\sigma_0}\frac{\rho}{(1-\rho)^2}.
\]
Indeed, the absolute coefficient mass in an initial block
is at most $2N_j$, and
$|f_{s,c}(p)|\le p^{-\sigma_0}/(1-p^{-\sigma_0})$.
These are explicit constants, although the dependence on
$q!$ is too large to ignore in a growing-conductor application.

Exact mass preservation gives
$\sum_{p\in Q\cap C_j}w_p=N_j\le2^j$.
Thus the weighted Euler product converges absolutely on
$\Re s>1$. In that domain the full logarithms satisfy
\[
 \log L_Q(s,\chi)
 =\sum_{p\in C}f_{s,\chi(p)}(p)+G_\chi(s).
\]
The deletion hypothesis makes $H_{S,\chi}$ a holomorphic
unit on $\Re s>1/2$, by the same absolute-convergence proof
as Theorem~\ref{thm:critical-prime-deletion}.
Its power-series logarithm also satisfies, uniformly in $\chi$
and $\Re s\ge\sigma_0>1/2$,
\[
 |\log H_{S,\chi}(s)|
 \le B_{S,\sigma_0}:=
       \sum_{p\in S}\frac{p^{-\sigma_0}}{1-p^{-\sigma_0}}<\infty.
\]
Thus this deleted-prime factor has also been charged in the
full multiplier bound; its constant depends on $S$ and
$\sigma_0$.
Exponentiating proves the factorization and continuation.
The multiplier being a unit, all stated zeros, multiplicities
and poles are preserved. The ordinary RH criterion follows
by taking the character modulo $1$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{What the weight cap costs.}
The estimate $K_j\ge N_j/h_j$ is necessary for
\emph{any} nonnegative weight vector with weight cap $h_j$
and the same zeroth block moment: $N_j=\sum w_p\le h_jK_j$.
The construction is therefore within $M_j\le j$ support
points of the minimum permitted by this mass constraint.
This is an optimality statement for that specified constraint,
not a lower bound on the support of every possible RH detector.

The full signed identity also gives an untwisted real-axis
comparison. When $\chi=\boldsymbol1$ and $s=\sigma>1/2$ is
real, $E_{j,a}\le0$ and $f'_{\sigma,1}\le0$ show
$D_{j,\boldsymbol1}(\sigma)\le0$, so
$G_{\boldsymbol1}(\sigma)\le0$.
On $\sigma>1$, the weighted product is at most the Euler
product on $C$. This sign is not a zero-excluding inequality
at complex $s$.

More generally, the same construction with any integer
caps $h_j\ge1$ has the exact same signed identity. It works
on every half-plane where
$\sum_j jh_j2^{-j\sigma}$ converges, together with the
continuation domain of the deleted-prime multiplier.
For example, $h_j=(j+1)^r$, with fixed integer $r\ge1$
and $S=\varnothing$, gives weights bounded by
$(1+\log_2p)^r$, support
$\pi_Q(X)\asymp X/(\log X)^{r+1}$, and preservation of all
Dirichlet zeros on $\Re s>0$.
For every fixed $\sigma_0>0$ its compensator again has
a bound $B_{q,r,\sigma_0}+A_{r,\sigma_0}|s|$.
Thus the price of support reduction can be chosen explicitly:
polylogarithmic weights permit logarithmic thinning while
retaining the entire open positive half-plane; square-root
weights permit a square-root prime count while retaining
the open RH half-plane.

The positive negative-binomial probability law from the
preceding subsection applies to these integer weights as
well. It is still a weighted sampling law.
The finite rank-clock step is compiled in
\path{PrimeFactorOscillations/Mathlib/Combinatorics/IntegerBlockGrouping.lean}.
For $n\ge0$, $h\ge1$, define
\[
 P_{n,h}(m)=
 \begin{cases}h\lfloor m/h\rfloor,&m<n,\\n,&m\ge n,\end{cases}
 \qquad v_i=P_{n,h}(i+1)-P_{n,h}(i).
\]
The verified statements give $0\le v_i\le h$,
$\sum_{i<n}v_i=n$, and, for every $0\le m\le n$,
\[
 -(h-1)\le \sum_{i<m}v_i-m\le0.
\]
The signed declaration is
\texttt{Nat.groupedPrefix\_signed\_balance}; the exact mass
declaration is \texttt{Nat.groupedTerminalWeight\_total}.
The final shorter group is included in both statements.

The ordinary proof here does not establish a bound on its
signed inverse coefficients or improve the certified
$7/8$ boundary. The analytic Abel-summation and normal-convergence
steps are not yet formalized in Lean.
This controlled-weight construction is stated with its proof;
no established novelty priority is claimed.

\subsection{Power sparsity, weight caps and height growth}
\label{sec:prime-carrier-power-tradeoff}

The finite integer compression lemma can be used locally,
rather than on an entire dyadic block. This retains much
smaller weights and controls the full height dependence with
a fixed number of moments.

\begin{theorem}[A tunable prime-carrier tradeoff]
\label{thm:prime-carrier-power-tradeoff}
Fix an integer $k\ge2$ and put
\[
 C_k=k(k+1)\frac{2^{1/k}}{2^{1/k}-1}.
\]
There are a fixed prime set $Q_k$ and positive integer
weights $w_p\le p^{1-1/k}$ such that
\[
 \pi_{Q_k}(X)\le C_k J(J+1)X^{1/k},
 \qquad J=\lfloor\log_2X\rfloor,\quad X\ge2.
\]
For every fixed Dirichlet character $\chi$ the absolutely
convergent Euler product
\[
 \mathcal L_k(s,\chi)
 =\prod_{p\in Q_k}(1-\chi(p)p^{-s})^{-w_p},
 \qquad \Re s>1,
\]
continues meromorphically to $\Re s>0$ as
\[
 \mathcal L_k(s,\chi)=L(s,\chi)\exp G_{k,\chi}(s),
 \qquad G_{k,\chi}\text{ holomorphic on }\Re s>0.
\]
All zeros, multiplicities and poles in that half-plane are
therefore preserved. The same set and weights work for every
fixed character. In particular this gives an RH-equivalent
zero criterion on each $Q_k$.
For every fixed modulus $q$ and $\sigma_0>0$,
\[
 |G_{k,\chi}(s)|\le B_{q,\sigma_0}
       +\frac{2}{k}\prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(|s|+r)
                  \frac{2^{-\sigma_0}}{(1-2^{-\sigma_0})^{k+1}}
 \quad(\Re s\ge\sigma_0,\ \chi\bmod q),
\]
where, for every $\sigma_0>0$,
\[
 B_{q,\sigma_0}
   =2\sum_{1\le j<q!}\frac{2^{j(1-\sigma_0)}}{1-2^{-j\sigma_0}}.
\]
This is the same finite initial-block expression as before,
now used and justified throughout $\sigma_0>0$.
Thus it is the \emph{logarithm of the multiplier} that has
polynomial height growth of degree $k$.
All assertions keep $k$ fixed; no uniformity as $k\to\infty$
or the conductor grows is implicit.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the moduli $M_j\le j$ from the preceding construction,
so $q\mid M_j$ for $j\ge q!$.
For $u=2^j$, put
\[
 \ell_j=\left\lfloor\frac{(k-1)j}{k}\right\rfloor,\qquad
 L_j=2^{\ell_j},\qquad b_j=2^{j-\ell_j}.
\]
Divide $[u,2u)$ into its $b_j$ consecutive integer intervals
of length $L_j$. In each such interval, and each residue
class modulo $M_j$, let $I$ be the finite set of primes in
that cell and class. Apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:integer-moment-compression} to the natural
vectors $(1,p,\ldots,p^{k-1})$, $p\in I$.
It supplies nonnegative integer weights satisfying
\[
 \sum_{p\in I}w_pp^r=\sum_{p\in I}p^r\quad(0\le r<k),
 \qquad
 2^{|\{p:w_p>0\}|}\le
       \prod_{r=0}^{k-1}\left(1+\sum_{p\in I}p^r\right).
\]
This is exactly the already compiled finite compression
input, instantiated with coordinate type $\operatorname{Fin}(k)$.
The subsequent analytic argument is an ordinary proof.

Write $n=|I|\le L_j$. The zeroth moment forces
$\sum_{p\in I}w_p=n$, hence every weight is at most
$n\le L_j\le u^{1-1/k}\le p^{1-1/k}$.
Discard the zero weights. Empty cells contribute no primes.
For $0\le r<k$,
\[
 1+\sum_{p\in I}p^r
 \le2^{\ell_j+r(j+1)+1}.
\]
Consequently the retained support in one cell and class has
at most
\[
 k(\ell_j+1)+\frac{k(k-1)}2(j+1)
 \le\frac{k(k+1)}2(j+1)
\]
points. There are at most $j$ residue classes and
$b_j\le2\cdot2^{j/k}$ cells. The total block support is
therefore at most $k(k+1)j(j+1)2^{j/k}$.
Summing this bound for $1\le j\le J$, using
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^J2^{j/k}
 \le\frac{2^{1/k}}{2^{1/k}-1}\,2^{J/k},
\]
proves the claimed bound for all $X\ge2$.
The denominator is positive because $k\ge2$.

We now estimate the exact full Euler-block difference
\[
 D_{j,k,\chi}(s)
 =\sum_{\substack{p\text{ prime}\\u\le p<2u}}
        (w_p\mathbf1_{Q_k}(p)-1)f_{s,\chi(p)}(p),
 \qquad
 f_{s,c}(x)=\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{c^m x^{-ms}}m.
\]
The series retains every prime power.
If $j\ge q!$, the character value is constant on each
cell and residue class. The moment identities annihilate
the entire Taylor polynomial of degree $k-1$ of $f_{s,c}$
about the left endpoint of the cell. In particular,
\[
 D_{j,k,\chi}(s)
 =\sum_{\text{cells and classes}}\ \sum_{p\in I}
       (w_p-1)\bigl(f_{s,c}(p)-T_{k-1,s,c,I}(p)\bigr).
\]
This signed identity, with all moments removed exactly,
precedes any bound on the Taylor remainder.

For $\sigma=\Re s>0$, termwise differentiation gives
\[
 |f^{(k)}_{s,c}(x)|
 \le \prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(|s|+r)\,x^{-k}
          \sum_{m\ge1}m^{k-1}x^{-m\sigma}
 \le
 (k-1)!\prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(|s|+r)
       \frac{x^{-\sigma-k}}{(1-x^{-\sigma})^k}.
\]
For the last inequality, use
$m^{k-1}\le(k-1)!\binom{m+k-2}{k-1}$ and its geometric
generating series. The differentiation is justified
locally uniformly on $x>1$, $\Re s>0$, $|c|\le1$.
Taylor's integral remainder on an interval of length $L_j$
is bounded by $L_j^k/k!$ times this derivative bound.
The residual coefficient mass in a cell is at most $2n$.
Summing the remainders over cells and classes uses
$\sum n\le u$ and $L_j^k\le u^{k-1}$, and yields
\[
 |D_{j,k,\chi}(s)|
 \le\frac2k\prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(|s|+r)
       \frac{2^{-j\sigma}}{(1-2^{-j\sigma})^k}
 \qquad(j\ge q!).
\]
In particular, for $\Re s\ge\sigma_0>0$ and $R\ge q!$,
\[
 \left|\sum_{j\ge R}D_{j,k,\chi}(s)\right|
 \le\frac2k\prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(|s|+r)
       \frac{2^{-R\sigma_0}}{(1-2^{-\sigma_0})^{k+1}}.
\]
This proves normal convergence of
$G_{k,\chi}=\sum_{j\ge1}D_{j,k,\chi}$ on $\Re s>0$.
The initial blocks $j<q!$ are bounded by $B_{q,\sigma_0}$,
because they have total residual mass at most twice their
original prime count. Bounding the remaining tail by the
sum over all $j\ge1$ proves the stated height estimate.

Each block still has total weight equal to its original
prime count. Thus the Euler product is absolutely
convergent for $\Re s>1$, and the complete logarithms in
that domain differ by $G_{k,\chi}$.
Exponentiation gives
$\mathcal L_k=L(s,\chi)\exp G_{k,\chi}$ and hence the
meromorphic continuation. The exponential is a holomorphic
unit, so the full zero signal and multiplicities survive.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Deletion, probability and the unavoidable mass cost.}
The construction can instead be carried out on primes outside
any fixed class $S$ from
Theorem~\ref{thm:critical-prime-deletion}. All cell bounds
and the analytic estimate for $G_{k,\chi}$ remain valid.
The factorization becomes
\[
 \mathcal L_{k,S}(s,\chi)
   =L(s,\chi)H_{S,\chi}(s)\exp G_{k,S,\chi}(s).
\]
For a critical-thin class $S$ this preserves zeros on
$\Re s>1/2$, the domain where the deleted-prime factor is
proved to be a unit; it does not assert preservation on all
of $\Re s>0$ for that class.
The full logarithmic multiplier bound additionally includes
$B_{S,\sigma_0}$ from the preceding proof.
Thus all Fermat-number prime divisors can be excluded
while the support is further compressed to
$O_k(X^{1/k}(\log X)^2)$.
The positive integer weights again define the stated
negative-binomial probability law.

The mass cost is explicit. In a dyadic block, preserving
zeroth mass $N_j$ under the cap $w_p\le p^{1-1/k}$ requires
at least $N_j/(2u)^{1-1/k}$ support points.
The prime number theorem makes this
$\gg_k u^{1/k}/\log u$. Our construction is within
logarithmic factors of the smallest support power permitted
by that cap and mass constraint.
It is not a lower bound on all possible detectors.

For example, $k=2$ gives square-root weights and support
$O(\sqrt X(\log X)^2)$, with a quadratic height bound for
the logarithmic compensator and preservation on $\Re s>0$.
The preceding terminal-group construction has the sharper
support order $\sqrt X/\log X$, a linear height bound and
a real-axis sign, but only preserves $\Re s>1/2$.
These are distinct quantitative choices.
Taking larger fixed $k$ makes the prime support sparser,
increases the allowed weight exponent, and raises the degree
of the logarithmic height bound. It does not remove the
mass or height costs.

The finite integer moment theorem used here is compiled.
The short-interval prime construction, Taylor cancellation,
analytic continuation and resulting RH criterion have the
complete ordinary proof above; they are not claimed as
compiled Lean declarations. No independent bound on the
weighted signed inverse coefficients has been proved, and
the certified zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.
This explicit tradeoff is proved here without an established
novelty-priority claim.

\subsection{The exact scope of deletion by an Euler unit}
\label{sec:euler-unit-deletion-classification}

The sufficient deletion condition also has a converse for
the precise mechanism used here. Positivity prevents a
larger deleted prime class from having a holomorphic-unit
Euler factor throughout the same half-plane.

\begin{theorem}[Classification of pure Euler-unit deletions]
\label{thm:euler-unit-deletion-classification}
Let $S$ be any fixed prime set, and let $\alpha\ge0$.
The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item For every $\epsilon>0$,
$\pi_S(X)\ll_\epsilon X^{\alpha+\epsilon}$.
\item The Euler product
$E_S(s)=\prod_{p\in S}(1-p^{-s})^{-1}$, initially defined
on $\Re s>1$, has a holomorphic continuation to
$\Re s>\alpha$.
\item The product
$H_S(s)=\prod_{p\in S}(1-p^{-s})$, initially defined
on $\Re s>1$, continues to a holomorphic nowhere-zero
function on $\Re s>\alpha$.
\end{enumerate}
In these cases both Euler products actually converge
absolutely on $\Re s>\alpha$.
In particular, at $\alpha=1/2$, the critical-thin counting
condition is necessary and sufficient for deletion by an
Euler unit on the entire open RH half-plane.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Assume (1), and fix $\sigma>\alpha$.
Choose $0<\epsilon<\sigma-\alpha$.
Dyadic summation of the counting bound gives
$\sum_{p\in S}p^{-\sigma}<\infty$.
Since $\sigma>0$, the full logarithmic sums
\[
 \sum_{p\in S}\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{p^{-ms}}m
\]
converge normally on $\Re s>\alpha$.
Exponentiation and its reciprocal give absolutely convergent
Euler products that are holomorphic and nowhere zero there.
This proves (3), and (3) implies (2) by taking a reciprocal.

For (2) implies (1), write the initial Euler product as
\[
 E_S(s)=\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{a_S(n)}{n^s},\qquad
 a_S(n)=\mathbf1_{\{\text{every prime factor of }n
                            \text{ belongs to }S\}}.
\]
Here $a_S(1)=1$, and all coefficients are nonnegative and
at most $1$. The expansion and its differentiated series
are absolutely convergent on $\Re s>1$.

Fix a real $\sigma>\alpha$ and choose
$c>\max(1,\sigma)$.
The continued function is holomorphic on the disk
$|s-c|<c-\alpha$. Set $t=c-\sigma$; then
$0<t<c-\alpha$.
Termwise differentiation at $c$, followed by its convergent
Taylor expansion at $c-t$, gives
\[
 E_S(\sigma)
 =\sum_{r\ge0}\frac{t^r}{r!}
       \sum_{n\ge1}a_S(n)(\log n)^r n^{-c}.
\]
Every term is nonnegative. Tonelli's theorem therefore
allows the two sums to be interchanged, yielding
\[
 E_S(\sigma)
 =\sum_{n\ge1}a_S(n)n^{-c}
       \sum_{r\ge0}\frac{(t\log n)^r}{r!}
 =\sum_{n\ge1}a_S(n)n^{-\sigma}<\infty.
\]
This is the classical positive-Taylor argument behind
Landau's theorem, included here so that the continuation
hypothesis and the use of positivity are explicit.
In particular,
$\sum_{p\in S}p^{-\sigma}\le E_S(\sigma)<\infty$.
For $\sigma=\alpha+\epsilon$ and every $X\ge2$,
\[
 \pi_S(X)\le
 X^{\alpha+\epsilon}
       \sum_{p\in S}p^{-(\alpha+\epsilon)}.
\]
This convergent prime sum gives a finite bound constant,
depending on $S$ and $\epsilon$; no numerical value is asserted.
This proves (1), and the already proved implication from
(1) also establishes absolute convergence of both products.
\end{proof}

The classification applies to an Euler multiplier required
to be a unit at \emph{every} point of $\Re s>1/2$,
including $s=1$, as needed to preserve the original pole.
It is not a classification of every RH-equivalent observable.
At this threshold the deleted class has
$\pi_S(X)=o(\pi(X))$, so its unweighted complement retains
relative prime density $1$.
An entire positive proportion of primes therefore cannot
be removed by this exact unit mechanism.
Finite and many infinite special-prime classes both qualify;
infinitude itself is not the criterion.

The smaller weighted carriers proved above achieve
something different: they preserve zeroth mass in their
blocks and move that mass onto fewer locations.
Their support can have zero relative density because the
weights or normalization carry the missing information.
The classification explains precisely why the cost must be
tracked; it does not rule out a different compensated
observable or prove a zero-excluding estimate.
The classification has the complete ordinary proof above
and uses the classical Landau positivity principle.
No separate Lean compilation or novelty claim is made.
The weighted carriers retain the pole at $1$, so they do not
satisfy the holomorphy hypothesis in (2). Applying this
positivity argument after compensating that pole would
require a separately proved holomorphic function with
nonnegative Dirichlet coefficients; no such input is supplied
by compression alone.

\subsection{A reverse construction with capped prime capacities}
\label{sec:reverse-prime-capacity}

We now prescribe a favorable analytic model first and ask whether
actual primes can supply it. The construction separates an exact
normalization identity from the independent size estimate needed
to exclude zeros. All prime powers and overlaps between congruence
classes remain in the identity.

We use the following positive-series principle. Let $a_n\ge0$,
let $\sum_{n\ge1}a_n n^{-c}<\infty$, and suppose its sum to the
right of $c$ agrees with a real-analytic function on
$(\alpha,\infty)$. Then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{n\ge1}a_n n^{-\sigma}<\infty
 \qquad(\sigma>\alpha).
 \label{eq:positive-dirichlet-continuation-core}
\end{equation}
Indeed put $w_n=a_n n^{-c}$ and $x_n=\log n$. The finite positive
atomic measure $\sum_n w_n\delta_n$ has exponential transform
$\sum_n w_n e^{t x_n}$. Its analytic continuation to $t<c-\alpha$
forces exponential integrability there by the positive-measure
Landau frontier theorem. Taking $t=c-\sigma$ proves the claim.
This argument has now been compiled in
\texttt{PositiveDirichletContinuation.lean},
including the atomic measure identities and the final theorem
\texttt{summable\_}\allowbreak
\texttt{positiveDirichlet\_of\_}\allowbreak
\texttt{analyticContinuation}.
The five declarations use only the standard Lean axioms.
The Euler-product adapters and the reverse construction below
have ordinary proofs here; they are not yet Lean declarations.

\begin{lemma}[A positive continuous model with a prescribed pole]
\label{lem:continuous-prime-model}
For $x>1$, define the positive measure
\[
 d\nu(x)=
 \frac{1}{x\log x}
 \sum_{r\ge1}\frac{(\log x)^r}{r!\,\zeta(r+1)}\,dx.
\]
Its density is at most $1/\log x$, is bounded near $1$, and its
full Euler transform is
\begin{equation}
 M(s):=\exp\left(\int_1^\infty
                     -\log(1-x^{-s})\,d\nu(x)\right)
       =\frac{s}{s-1}\qquad(\Re s>1).
 \label{eq:continuous-prime-model}
\end{equation}
Thus its continuation is zero-free on $\Re s>0$, with one simple
pole at $1$. The logarithm on the initial half-plane is the full
series $\sum_{m\ge1}x^{-ms}/m$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $\zeta(r+1)>1$, the density is bounded above by
$(1-x^{-1})/\log x$, which has a finite limit at $1$.
For real $s>1$, substitute $x=e^t$ and use Tonelli:
\[
 P_0(s):=\int_1^\infty x^{-s}\,d\nu(x)
       =\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{1}{r\,\zeta(r+1)s^r}.
\]
Another nonnegative interchange gives
\begin{align*}
 \log M(s)&=\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{P_0(ms)}m\\
 &=\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{1}{r s^r\zeta(r+1)}
                      \sum_{m\ge1}m^{-r-1}\\
 &=\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{1}{r s^r}
 =\log\frac{s}{s-1}.
\end{align*}
The same sums and integrals converge absolutely and normally on
$\Re s>1$, so the identity holds there for complex $s$ as well.
The rational expression gives the stated continuation.
No assertion about the zeros of $\zeta$ was used.
\end{proof}

Fix a nonempty finite set $\mathcal Q$ of positive integer moduli.
Write $R=|\mathcal Q|$, $d_q=\varphi(q)$, and
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal Z_{\mathcal Q}(s)
   =\prod_{q\in\mathcal Q}\prod_{\chi\bmod q}L(s,\chi),
 \qquad
 A(p)=\sum_{\substack{q\in\mathcal Q\\p\equiv1\pmod q}}d_q.
 \label{eq:reverse-reference-capacity}
\end{equation}
These are fixed moduli throughout; no conductor-uniform assertion
is implicit. For $p\nmid q$, let $f_q(p)$ be its multiplicative
order modulo $q$. The local factor of the product over characters is
\begin{equation}
 \prod_{\chi\bmod q}(1-\chi(p)p^{-s})^{-1}
   =(1-p^{-f_q(p)s})^{-d_q/f_q(p)}.
 \label{eq:full-character-local-factor}
\end{equation}
For $p\mid q$ it is $1$. In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal Z_{\mathcal Q}(s)=
       \prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-A(p)}\,U_{\mathcal Q}(s),
 \label{eq:split-nonsplit-factorization}
\end{equation}
where $U_{\mathcal Q}$ retains every nonsplit local factor, with
$f_q(p)\ge2$. It is an absolutely convergent holomorphic unit on
$\Re s>1/2$. The order and the exponent are integers since
$f_q(p)\mid d_q$. Formula \eqref{eq:full-character-local-factor}
follows by multiplying over the characters of the finite unit group.
It includes the trivial groups for $q=1,2$. Overlapping split classes
contribute their summed capacity $A(p)$ exactly once.

The usual continuations of the Dirichlet $L$-functions show that
$\mathcal Z_{\mathcal Q}$ has a pole of order $R$ at $1$.
Every zero $\rho$ of $\zeta$ with $\Re\rho>1/2$ is a zero of this
reference of multiplicity at least $R\,m_\zeta(\rho)$:
each principal factor is $\zeta(s)\prod_{p\mid q}(1-p^{-s})$,
the finite product is a unit on $\Re s>0$, and all other factors
are holomorphic there. Additional nonprincipal zeros may increase
the multiplicity; they cannot cancel it.

Choose $0<\theta<1$. For $j\ge1$ put
\[
 u_j=2^j,\quad L_j=2^{\lfloor\theta j\rfloor},\quad
 B_j=2^{j-\lfloor\theta j\rfloor},\quad
 \tau=\max(\theta,1-\theta).
\]
Partition $[u_j,2u_j)$ into its $B_j$ half-open intervals $I$ of
integer length $L_j$, and denote their integer left endpoints by
$t_I$. Define the target, available capacity, and deficit by
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 H_I&=\lceil R\nu(I)\rceil,&
 C_I&=\sum_{\substack{p\in I\\p\ {\rm prime}}}A(p),\\
 \delta_I&=(H_I-C_I)_+,&
 \Delta_j&=\sum_{I\subset[u_j,2u_j)}\delta_I.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:reverse-capacity-deficit}
\end{equation}
All these sums are over the stated finite sets. Choose integer
weights on the actual primes of each interval satisfying
\begin{equation}
 0\le v_p\le A(p),\qquad
 \sum_{\substack{p\in I\\p\ {\rm prime}}}v_p
          =\min(H_I,C_I)=H_I-\delta_I.
 \label{eq:capped-prime-selection}
\end{equation}
They exist by filling the finite integer capacities in any fixed
order until the target is met or all capacities are exhausted.
This finite existence and exact-deficit assertion is compiled as
\texttt{exists\_capped\_selection} in
\texttt{CapacitySelection.lean}.
This constructs a set $Q=\{p:v_p>0\}$ without assuming that every
interval contains a prime. At fixed total weight, using larger
capacities first minimizes the number of selected primes in that
interval. The zero-capacity primes are never selected.

\begin{theorem}[Reverse model and retained-zero criterion]
\label{thm:reverse-prime-capacity}
For the construction above, set initially on $\Re s>1$
\[
 F(s)=\prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-v_p},\qquad
 D(s)=\sum_{j\ge1}\ \sum_{I\subset[u_j,2u_j)}
                  \delta_I[-\log(1-t_I^{-s})].
\]
There is a holomorphic $G$ on $\Re s>\tau$, defined independently
of any zero-free hypothesis, such that
\begin{equation}
             F(s)e^{D(s)}=M(s)^R e^{G(s)}
                    \qquad(\Re s>1).
 \label{eq:reverse-completed-model}
\end{equation}
Consequently the completed model has a zero-free meromorphic
continuation to $\Re s>\tau$, with a pole of order $R$ at $1$.

If $\tau\le\eta<1$ and, independently,
\begin{equation}
 \forall\epsilon>0\quad
       \Delta_j\ll_\epsilon 2^{j(\eta+\epsilon)}
                    \qquad(j\ge1),
 \label{eq:reverse-capacity-estimate}
\end{equation}
then every $L(s,\chi)$ for $\chi\bmod q$, $q\in\mathcal Q$,
and $\zeta(s)$ have no zeros on $\Re s>\eta$.
In particular, with $\theta=1/2$, a deficit bound of exponent
$3/4$ gives the requested boundary $3/4$, and exponent $1/2$
would imply RH as well as the corresponding assertions for
the included Dirichlet $L$-functions.

More quantitatively, put
\[
 \gamma=\limsup_{j\to\infty}
              \frac{\log(1+\Delta_j)}{j\log2}\in[0,1].
\]
Every zero $\rho$ of the reference with $\Re\rho>\tau$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
                         \Re\rho\le\gamma.
 \label{eq:deficit-zero-readout}
\end{equation}
Thus a hypothetical off-line zero has an unconditional nonzero
response in the capacity deficits. No estimate such as
\eqref{eq:reverse-capacity-estimate} is assumed in this last readout.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $f_s(x)=-\log(1-x^{-s})$, with its full power series.
The initial Euler products and logarithms converge absolutely on
$\Re s>1$, since
\[
 \sum_{I\subset[u,2u)}H_I
 \le R\nu([u,2u))+B_j\le R u/\log2+B_j\ll_R u.
\]
In particular this controls both $\sum v_p$ and $\Delta_j$.

The exact identity $\sum_{p\in I}v_p+\delta_I=H_I$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \log F(s)+D(s)-R\log M(s)=G_{\rm init}(s)+\sum_{j\ge1}G_j(s),
 \label{eq:reverse-exact-signed-log}
\end{equation}
where the initial interval, which is not part of the dyadic partition,
is retained explicitly:
\[
 G_{\rm init}(s)=-R\int_1^2 f_s(x)\,d\nu(x).
\]
The complete dyadic summand is
\begin{align*}
 G_j(s)={}&
 \sum_{I\subset[u,2u)}(H_I-R\nu(I))f_s(t_I)\\
 &+R\sum_{I\subset[u,2u)}
                  \int_I[f_s(t_I)-f_s(x)]\,d\nu(x)\\
 &+\sum_{I\subset[u,2u)}
             \sum_{\substack{p\in I\\p\ {\rm prime}}}
                    v_p[f_s(p)-f_s(t_I)] .
\end{align*}
This is an exact signed mass cancellation. In particular the
deficit term has not been discarded or included in an error norm.

For $\sigma=\Re s>0$ and $x\ge u$,
\[
 |f_s(x)|\le\frac{x^{-\sigma}}{1-x^{-\sigma}},\qquad
 f_s'(x)=\frac{-s x^{-s-1}}{1-x^{-s}},\qquad
 |f_s'(x)|\le\frac{|s|x^{-\sigma-1}}{1-x^{-\sigma}}.
\]
The rounding mass in each interval is less than $1$.
Moreover $L_j\le u^\theta$, $B_j\le2u^{1-\theta}$,
$\nu([u,2u))\le u/\log2$, and $B_j\le u$.
After the displayed mass cancellation, these inequalities give
the explicit remainder bound
\begin{equation}
 |G_j(s)|\le
 \frac{2u^{1-\theta-\sigma}
            +(1+2R/\log2)|s|u^{\theta-\sigma}}
      {1-u^{-\sigma}}.
 \label{eq:reverse-local-transport-bound}
\end{equation}
The first term is rounding; the other two transports together
have mass at most $(1+2R/\log2)u$ and displacement at most $L_j$.
The bound is summable, uniformly on compact subsets of
$\Re s>\tau$. The initial integral is holomorphic on $\Re s>0$:
its density is bounded near $1$, and on every closed subhalf-plane
$|f_s(x)|\le-\log(1-x^{-\sigma_0})$, an integrable logarithmic
singularity at $1$. This proves normal convergence of $G$ and
\eqref{eq:reverse-completed-model}. It also gives linear height
growth for the local error on each closed subhalf-plane.
The rational function $M^R$ has exactly the asserted pole and
no zeros on this domain.

Now suppose \eqref{eq:reverse-capacity-estimate}. If $\sigma>\eta$,
choose $\epsilon<\sigma-\eta$. Since $t_I\ge2^j$, the full deficit
logarithm is absolutely normally convergent on $\Re s>\eta$:
on closed subhalf-planes it is majorized by
\[
 \sum_{j\ge1}\frac{\Delta_j\,2^{-j\sigma}}{1-2^{-j\sigma}}<\infty.
\]
Thus $e^D$ is a holomorphic unit there and the continuation
\[
                       F=M^R e^{G-D}
\]
is zero-free with pole order $R$ at $1$.

The cap $v_p\le A(p)$ now supplies the independent positivity
input. On the initial half-plane the full quotient
\begin{equation}
 B(s):=\frac{\mathcal Z_{\mathcal Q}(s)}{F(s)}
 =\prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-(A(p)-v_p)}\,U_{\mathcal Q}(s)
 =\sum_{n\ge1}b_n n^{-s}
 \label{eq:positive-capped-quotient}
\end{equation}
has nonnegative integer coefficients and $b_1=1$.
All nonsplit prime powers and every overlap multiplicity are
still present. The meromorphic continuations of the numerator
and $F$ make $B$ holomorphic on $\Re s>\eta$:
$F$ has no zeros, and their equal pole orders cancel at $1$.
The positive-series principle above therefore forces actual
convergence of $\sum b_n n^{-\sigma}$ for every $\sigma>\eta$.

For completeness, this convergence gives a unit, not just a
positive value on the real axis. Every finite subproduct of the
positive Euler factors in \eqref{eq:positive-capped-quotient}
is bounded by $\sum b_n n^{-\sigma}$ for real $\sigma>\eta$.
Its nonnegative logarithm is therefore uniformly bounded.
Increasing the finite set of factors proves convergence of the
full logarithmic Euler sum at $\sigma$, and hence absolute normal
convergence at every complex point to its right. Varying $\sigma$
shows that $B$ is a holomorphic unit on $\Re s>\eta$.
Thus $\mathcal Z_{\mathcal Q}=FB$ has no zeros there.
Since none of its factors has a pole away from $1$, each factor
is zero-free there as well, including every principal factor
and hence $\zeta$. No RH-dependent recovery theorem is used.

Finally $\Delta_j\ll_R2^j$, so $0\le\gamma\le1$.
If a reference zero had $\Re\rho>\max(\tau,\gamma)$, choose
$\max(\tau,\gamma)<\eta<\Re\rho<1$. The definition of the
limsup implies \eqref{eq:reverse-capacity-estimate}, contradicting
the result just proved. The standard zero-free line $\Re s=1$
handles the endpoint if necessary. This proves
\eqref{eq:deficit-zero-readout}.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The size requirement is an Euler-capacity requirement.}
Under \eqref{eq:reverse-capacity-estimate}, positivity of the
prime coefficients in \eqref{eq:positive-capped-quotient} also gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \sum_p(A(p)-v_p)p^{-\sigma}<\infty\quad(\sigma>\eta),\\
 \sum_{p\le X}(A(p)-v_p)\le
       X^\sigma\sum_p(A(p)-v_p)p^{-\sigma}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:capacity-retention-size}
\end{equation}
In particular the number of eligible but unselected primes is
$O_\sigma(X^\sigma)$. For $\eta<\sigma<1$, the fixed-modulus
prime number theorem in progressions implies that $Q$ retains
relative density $1$ within the union of the eligible split
classes. With $\mathcal Q=\{1\}$ this means almost all primes;
with $\mathcal Q=\{q\}$ the ambient class is $p\equiv1\pmod q$,
whose primes have capacity $\varphi(q)$. Taking a large fixed
$q$ can make this class a small positive proportion of all
primes, but does not give a zero-density carrier.
Growing moduli would require new uniform estimates.
The lighter carriers of the preceding subsections use a different
weight budget and cannot inherit the positive quotient by assumption.

For $\sigma>1$ the selected weights also give an honest probability
law:
\[
 a_v(p^r)=\binom{v_p+r-1}{r}\quad(v_p>0),\qquad
 \mathbb P_\sigma(n)=\frac{a_v(n)n^{-\sigma}}{F(\sigma)}.
\]
Here $a_v$ is multiplicative and is zero on positive powers of
unselected primes. The quotient has its analogous positive
Dirichlet law. These are explicitly weighted integer laws, not an
unstated extension of a uniform prime-clock theorem. Their
positive-measure structure is exactly what the continuation
argument consumes.

\paragraph{Exact joint compensation between congruence classes.}
The advantage of combining moduli can be stated without discarding
signs. In one fixed interval $I$, let
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 h_I=\lceil\nu(I)\rceil,\qquad a_{q,I}=h_I-C_{q,I},\\
 C_{q,I}=d_q\,\#\{p\in I:p\ {\rm prime},\ p\equiv1\pmod q\}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
These last quantities are signed integers. Set
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 P_I=\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}(a_{q,I})_+,\qquad
 N_I=\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}(-a_{q,I})_+,\\
 r_I=R h_I-\lceil R\nu(I)\rceil\in\{0,\ldots,R-1\}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
Then the combined deficit obeys the exact identity
\begin{equation}
 \delta_I=(P_I-N_I-r_I)_+
         =P_I-\min(P_I,N_I+r_I).
 \label{eq:exact-joint-capacity-compensation}
\end{equation}
Indeed $\sum_q a_{q,I}=P_I-N_I$ and
$H_I-\sum_q C_{q,I}=\sum_q a_{q,I}-r_I$.
Thus capacity surplus from one split class cancels another
class's deficit in the same interval, including the precise
rounding correction. Overlaps are already counted correctly
in $C_I=\sum_q C_{q,I}$. This identity identifies an actual
joint saving; it is not an estimate for its size.
For an exponent improvement one must prove the full retained sum
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{I\subset[2^j,2^{j+1})}
       \bigl[P_I-\min(P_I,N_I+r_I)\bigr]
       \ll_\epsilon 2^{j(\eta+\epsilon)}.
 \label{eq:joint-capacity-analytic-target}
\end{equation}
A centered variance, a finite sample, or the existing $7/8$
boundary does not establish this inequality at a smaller $\eta$.

The reverse theorem supplies a sufficient arithmetic criterion,
not an equivalence between RH and these fine-interval deficit
bounds. RH alone has not been shown to imply their asserted
size, and including nonprincipal characters adds their own
zero-free requirements. Unconditionally the construction only
gives $\Delta_j\ll_R2^j$. The certified boundary remains $7/8$.
The new conclusion is the exact model, its noncancelling
zero readout, and the explicit capped-capacity estimate that
would change that boundary. No literature priority is asserted
for this construction.


\subsection{Capped moment transport and longer prime intervals}
\label{sec:capped-moment-transport}

Higher moment accuracy changes the interval scale in the reverse
construction. The following selection theorem supplies the missing
finite feasibility statement while preserving nonnegative weights
and the Euler-capacity cap. Its deficit is controlled by an explicit
one-sided interval discrepancy, rather than by an unsigned moment
matrix norm.

\begin{theorem}[Capped moment selection with positive defects]
\label{thm:capped-positive-moment-selection}
Let $I=[a,b]$ with $a<b$, let $\mu$ be a finite positive measure on
$I$ of mass $H$, and let
\[
 \alpha=\sum_{i\in S}A_i\delta_{x_i},\qquad
 x_i\in I,\quad A_i\ge0,
\]
where $S$ is finite. Repeated locations are allowed and their
capacities add. For an integer $k\ge1$ define
\begin{equation}
 d_I=\sup_{J\subseteq I}
                 \bigl(\mu(J)-\alpha(J)\bigr)_+,\qquad
 m_k=\lceil k/2\rceil.
 \label{eq:moment-one-sided-interval-deficit}
\end{equation}
Here $J$ ranges over all subintervals, with every choice of included
or excluded endpoints, and includes the empty interval.

There exist $0\le v_i\le A_i$ and a positive measure $\lambda$ on
$I$, supported on at most $k$ points, such that
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \sum_{i\in S}v_i x_i^r+\int_I x^r\,d\lambda(x)
      =\int_I x^r\,d\mu(x)\quad(0\le r<k),\\
 0\le\lambda(I)\le\min(H,m_k d_I).
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:capped-moment-defect}
\end{equation}
Thus the actual selected mass is at least $H-m_kd_I$.
If every capacity is at most $A_*>0$, its selected support has
cardinality at least $(H-m_kd_I)_+/A_*$.

Moreover the smallest possible defect mass $\delta_*$ satisfies
the exact finite-dimensional dual identity
\begin{equation}
 \delta_*=
 \sup_{\substack{P\in\mathbb R[x],\ \deg P<k\\
                         P(x)\le1\ (x\in I)}}
       \left(\int_I P\,d\mu
                    -\sum_{i\in S}A_i(P(x_i))_+\right).
 \label{eq:capped-moment-dual}
\end{equation}
The zero polynomial is allowed, so the supremum is nonnegative.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The case $H=0$ follows by taking all weights and $\lambda$ zero.
Write $z(x)=(1,x,\ldots,x^{k-1})\in\mathbb R^k$ and
$c=\int_I z\,d\mu$.
For $\delta\ge0$ the compact convex set
\[
 K_\delta=
 \left\{\sum_{i\in S}v_i z(x_i):0\le v_i\le A_i\right\}
       +\delta\,\operatorname{conv}(\{0\}\cup z(I))
\]
is exactly the set of moments obtainable with these capacities
and a positive defect measure of mass at most $\delta$.
Indeed a convex combination gives such a measure immediately.
Conversely the normalized moment vector of any positive measure
lies in the closed convex hull of the compact moment curve;
a separating linear functional would otherwise contradict its
own pointwise upper bound after integration. In finite dimensions
this hull is compact, so finite convex combinations suffice.

Suppose $c\notin K_\delta$. Strict separation of a point from a
compact convex set gives a polynomial $P$ of degree less than $k$
with
\begin{equation}
 \int_I P\,d\mu>
       \sum_{i\in S}A_i(P(x_i))_+
                  +\delta\max(0,\max_{x\in I}P(x)).
 \label{eq:capped-moment-separation}
\end{equation}
The maximum on the right is positive: otherwise $P\le0$ on $I$,
the left side is nonpositive, and the right side is zero.
Divide by this maximum. We obtain a polynomial $Q\le1$ on $I$
such that
\begin{equation}
 \int_I Q\,d\mu-\sum_i A_i(Q(x_i))_+>\delta.
 \label{eq:capped-moment-normalized-separation}
\end{equation}

For $0<t<1$, the relative open set $\{x\in I:Q(x)>t\}$ is a union
of at most $m_k$ intervals. To verify the count, a polynomial of
degree $d$ has at most $d$ distinct real roots unless it is
identically zero; the greatest possible number of positive
components on a compact interval is $\lfloor d/2\rfloor+1$.
Between successive positive components, either two sign-changing
roots or an even-multiplicity root are required; counting
multiplicities proves this bound. Here $d\le k-1$, which gives
$m_k=\lceil k/2\rceil$.
Endpoint conventions are covered by the definition of $d_I$.
The constant and identically-zero cases have at most one
component. Layer-cake integration now gives
\begin{align*}
 \int_I Q\,d\mu-\sum_i A_i(Q(x_i))_+
 &\le \int_I Q_+\,d(\mu-\alpha)\\
 &=\int_0^1
       [\mu(\{Q>t\})-\alpha(\{Q>t\})]\,dt
 \le m_k d_I .
\end{align*}
Take $\delta=m_kd_I$. This contradicts
\eqref{eq:capped-moment-normalized-separation}, so $c\in K_\delta$.
Also $c\in K_H$ by taking no actual atoms and the original
measure as defect. This proves the claimed bound with
$\min(H,m_kd_I)$.

A representation of the defect moments can be reduced to at most
$k$ atoms: whenever more than $k$ positive weights occur, their
vectors $z(x)$ are linearly dependent. Move the weights along a
nonzero dependence until one first reaches zero, choosing the
direction that decreases some weight. The first coordinate
preserves their total mass, and all coordinates preserve the
moments. Repeat finitely. This proves the support assertion.
The support lower bound for the actual atoms follows from the
zeroth moment and $v_i\le A_*$.

For the dual identity, any feasible choice with defect mass
$\delta$ and any polynomial $P\le1$ on $I$ obey
\[
 \int_I P\,d\mu-\sum_i A_i(P(x_i))_+
 =\sum_i[v_iP(x_i)-A_i(P(x_i))_+]+\int_I P\,d\lambda
 \le\delta.
\]
This proves that the dual supremum is at most $\delta_*$.
A feasible optimum exists: restrict to defect mass at most $H$;
the capped atom box and the compact truncated moment hull are
compact, and the zeroth moment expresses the defect mass
continuously as $H-\sum_i v_i$.
For any $0\le\delta<\delta_*$, strict separation gives the
normalized polynomial in
\eqref{eq:capped-moment-normalized-separation}, so the supremum
is greater than $\delta$. Letting $\delta$ increase to $\delta_*$
proves equality. If $\delta_*=0$, the zero polynomial and the
already proved upper inequality give the same conclusion.
\end{proof}

Apply this theorem in each half-open dyadic cell of the previous
construction, using its closed hull for the compact moment
problem. Take
\[
 \mu_I=R\,\nu|_I,\qquad
 \alpha_I=\sum_{\substack{p\in I\\p\ {\rm prime}}}A(p)\delta_p .
\]
Actual prime atoms remain assigned to exactly one half-open cell.
Only the synthetic defect measures may share an endpoint; their
masses add in the transform. There is no rounding:
$H_I=R\nu(I)$ is now real, and the selected weights may be real.
For $u=2^j$ and $L_j=2^{\lfloor\theta j\rfloor}$ define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal D_{j,k,\theta}
       =\sum_{I\subset[u,2u)} d_I,\qquad
 \widetilde\Delta_j
       =\sum_{I\subset[u,2u)}\lambda_I(I)
       \le m_k\mathcal D_{j,k,\theta}.
 \label{eq:moment-total-defect}
\end{equation}
Choose one of the feasible moment selections in every cell.
This defines the actual prime set and its capped weights
independently of a zero-free hypothesis.

\begin{theorem}[Moment accuracy extends the admissible interval length]
\label{thm:long-interval-reverse-capacity}
For the preceding construction and any fixed $k\ge1$, put
\[
 \tau_k=\max\bigl(1/2,\ 1-k(1-\theta)\bigr).
\]
If $\tau_k\le\eta<1$ and
\begin{equation}
 \forall\epsilon>0\qquad
       \mathcal D_{j,k,\theta}
             \ll_\epsilon 2^{j(\eta+\epsilon)}\quad(j\ge1),
 \label{eq:long-interval-capacity-target}
\end{equation}
then $\zeta$ and every included Dirichlet $L$-function have
no zeros on $\Re s>\eta$.
In particular, choosing
\begin{equation}
                   \theta=1-\frac{1}{2k}
 \label{eq:moment-optimal-cell-length}
\end{equation}
keeps $\tau_k=1/2$ while allowing intervals of length
$X^{1-1/(2k)}$.
The sufficient arithmetic exponent remains $3/4$ for boundary
$3/4$, or $1/2$ for RH; the intervals on which it must be
proved are longer as $k$ increases.

Unconditionally, a reference zero with $\beta=\Re\rho>\tau_k$
forces
\begin{equation}
 \limsup_{j\to\infty}
 \frac{\log(1+\mathcal D_{j,k,\theta})}{j\log2}\ge\beta.
 \label{eq:long-interval-zero-readout}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use all the full Euler factors, now setting
\[
 \widetilde D(s)=\sum_{j\ge1}\ \sum_{I\subset[u,2u)}
                           \int_I f_s(x)\,d\lambda_I(x).
\]
Initial absolute convergence on $\Re s>1$ follows because
the combined mass of actual and synthetic atoms in a block
is exactly $R\nu([u,2u))\ll_Ru$.
The complete identity is
\[
 \log F(s)+\widetilde D(s)-R\log M(s)
       =-R\int_1^2 f_s(x)\,d\nu(x)+\sum_{j\ge1}\widetilde G_j(s).
\]
For each cell, the degree less than $k$ Taylor polynomial of
$f_s$ at its left endpoint cancels exactly by
\eqref{eq:capped-moment-defect}.
For $x\ge u$ and $\sigma=\Re s>0$, the full-series derivative
bound from the earlier moment compression proof is
\[
 |f_s^{(k)}(x)|\le
 (k-1)!\prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(|s|+r)\,
       \frac{x^{-\sigma-k}}{(1-x^{-\sigma})^k}.
\]
It also follows directly by differentiating
$\sum_{m\ge1}x^{-ms}/m$: use
$|(ms)(ms+1)\cdots(ms+k-1)|
 \le m^k\prod_{r<k}(|s|+r)$ and
$\sum_{m\ge1}m^{k-1}z^m\le(k-1)!z/(1-z)^k$.

The two compared positive measures in a cell both have mass
$H_I$, so Taylor's remainder, after the exact moment cancellation,
gives
\begin{equation}
 |\widetilde G_j(s)|\le
 \frac{2R}{k\log2}\prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(|s|+r)\,
 \frac{u^{\,1-k(1-\theta)-\sigma}}{(1-u^{-\sigma})^k}.
 \label{eq:capped-high-moment-error}
\end{equation}
This is normally summable when
$\sigma>\max(0,1-k(1-\theta))$.
The initial integral has already been proved holomorphic
on $\Re s>0$. Thus the completed model is zero-free there,
apart from its prescribed pole, with polynomial height
cost of degree $k$. Both $k$ and the moduli are fixed.

By \eqref{eq:moment-total-defect} and
\eqref{eq:long-interval-capacity-target}, the synthetic
logarithm converges normally on $\Re s>\eta$, with majorant
\[
 \sum_{j\ge1}\frac{\widetilde\Delta_j\,2^{-j\sigma}}
                           {1-2^{-j\sigma}}.
\]
It follows that $F=M^R\exp(\widetilde G-\widetilde D)$
is zero-free there, with the exact same pole order $R$.
Although the $v_p$ are now real, the quotient is still positive:
for every real $a\ge0$,
\[
 (1-z)^{-a}=\sum_{r\ge0}
      \frac{a(a+1)\cdots(a+r-1)}{r!}\,z^r,\qquad |z|<1,
\]
where the constant coefficient is $1$.
Thus $a=A(p)-v_p\ge0$ gives nonnegative real Dirichlet
coefficients in the full quotient
$\mathcal Z_{\mathcal Q}/F$.
The compiled positive-series core explicitly allows real
coefficients, so the same positive continuation and
Euler-logarithm argument from
Theorem~\ref{thm:reverse-prime-capacity} makes that quotient a unit
on $\Re s>\eta$. The zero-free conclusion follows.

Finally $d_I\le H_I$, so
$\mathcal D_{j,k,\theta}\le R\nu([u,2u))\ll_Ru$.
If its displayed limsup were smaller than a reference
zero's real part $\beta>\tau_k$, choose $\eta$ strictly between
their maximum and $\beta$. The resulting power bound would
contradict the zero-free conclusion. This proves the
unconditional readout.
\end{proof}

This is a quantitative use of higher accuracy: $k=1,2,3,4$
permit cell-length exponents $1/2,3/4,5/6,7/8$, respectively,
with the same RH-side analytic threshold.
The new arithmetic quantity is a sum of maximal
\emph{one-sided subinterval capacity deficits}; it is not
automatically controlled by the deficit of each whole cell.
The capped moment theorem proves the transfer to that quantity,
including its factor $m_k$, and its dual identity preserves the
positive and negative contributions before layer-cake comparison.
It does not prove the required power bound for primes.
In particular, longer intervals, finite numerical diagnostics,
or a larger character portfolio alone do not certify an exponent
gain. The global certified boundary is still $7/8$.

The two theorems in this subsection have full ordinary proofs.
Finite moment compression and positive Dirichlet continuation
have compiled providers, but the compact separation argument,
real-capacity moment selection and analytic consumers above
have not yet been formalized in Lean. No such formalization
or literature priority is claimed.


\subsection{The two-moment defect as a finite signed prime test}
\label{sec:two-moment-capacity-test}

For two moments the polynomial dual can be evaluated exactly
from a finite list of integrated prime-capacity sums.
This makes the reverse construction explicit without relaxing
its signed main terms.

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite formula for the two-moment deficit]
\label{thm:two-moment-finite-capacity}
Let $\mu$ be a positive finite measure on $[a,b]$, where $a<b$,
with mass $H>0$ and first moment $M$, and assume
$m=M/H\in(a,b)$. Let
$\alpha=\sum_{i\in S}A_i\delta_{x_i}$, where $S$ is finite,
$x_i\in[a,b]$, $A_i\ge0$, and $C=\sum_i A_i$.
Define
\begin{align*}
 L(c)&=\frac{cH-M-\sum_i A_i(c-x_i)_+}{c-a}
                                      &&(c>m),\\
 R(c)&=\frac{M-cH-\sum_i A_i(x_i-c)_+}{b-c}
                                      &&(c<m).
\end{align*}
Set
\[
 T_L=\{b\}\cup\{x_i:m<x_i<b\},\qquad
 T_R=\{a\}\cup\{x_i:a<x_i<m\}.
\]
These are finite nonempty sets of distinct locations; repeated
atoms contribute their summed capacities in the formulas.
The least defect mass preserving zeroth and first moments is
\begin{equation}
 \delta_*=\max\left\{0,\ H-C,\
                    \max_{c\in T_L}L(c),\
                    \max_{c\in T_R}R(c)\right\}.
 \label{eq:two-moment-finite-defect}
\end{equation}
In particular every retained denominator is strictly positive.
The formula includes both boundary tests and the raw total
mass deficit, even when that last term is negative.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the exact dual in
Theorem~\ref{thm:capped-positive-moment-selection} for $k=2$.
A dual affine polynomial whose maximum on $[a,b]$ is
nonpositive has nonpositive objective. If its maximum is
$0<t<1$ and its objective is positive, division by $t$
increases that objective. Thus it suffices to consider the
zero polynomial and affine polynomials with maximum $1$.

The constant polynomial $1$ gives $H-C$.
A nonconstant normalized affine polynomial has one of the forms
\[
 1-\lambda(x-a)\quad\hbox{or}\quad1-\lambda(b-x),
                       \qquad\lambda>0.
\]
For the first form put $c=a+1/\lambda$.
Its exact objective is $L(c)$, since
$(1-\lambda(x-a))_+=(c-x)_+/(c-a)$.
When $c\le m$, its model integral $(cH-M)/(c-a)$ is
nonpositive, so its objective cannot improve on $0$.
This leaves $c>m$.
On each interval between consecutive atom locations, the
numerator of $L$ is affine in $c$. A quotient of an affine
function by $c-a>0$ is monotone or constant on that interval:
the sign of its derivative is constant.
Consequently its supremum for $m<c\le b$ is attained at a
location in $T_L$, or is bounded by its limiting value at $m$,
which is nonpositive.
For $c\ge b$ all atoms are active, and
\[
 L(c)=\frac{c(H-C)-M+\sum_i A_i x_i}{c-a}.
\]
Its supremum is bounded by the larger of $L(b)$ and its
limit $H-C$ at infinity. Thus the entire decreasing-affine
branch reduces to $0,H-C$, and the $T_L$ tests.

For the increasing-affine form set $c=b-1/\lambda$.
The identical argument gives $R(c)$, discards $c\ge m$
because its model integral is nonpositive, and reduces the
remaining branch to $T_R$ and the limit $H-C$ at negative
infinity. Conversely every retained test is the objective
of an admissible affine polynomial; $0$ and $H-C$ come
from admissible constants. The dual equality proves
\eqref{eq:two-moment-finite-defect}.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The principal compensation is explicit.}
For $a\le t\le b$ write
\[
 E_L(t)=\mu([a,t])-\alpha([a,t]),\qquad
 E_R(t)=\mu([t,b])-\alpha([t,b]).
\]
Fubini's theorem, with all endpoint atoms retained, gives
\begin{align}
 L(c)&=
 \frac{\displaystyle\int_a^c E_L(t)\,dt
            -\displaystyle\int_{(c,b]}(x-c)\,d\mu(x)}{c-a}
                                  &&(m<c\le b),
 \label{eq:two-moment-left-compensation}\\
 R(c)&=
 \frac{\displaystyle\int_c^b E_R(t)\,dt
            -\displaystyle\int_{[a,c)}(c-x)\,d\mu(x)}{b-c}
                                  &&(a\le c<m).
 \label{eq:two-moment-right-compensation}
\end{align}
For example,
$\int_a^c\alpha([a,t])\,dt=\sum_i A_i(c-x_i)_+$.
The analogous identity for $\mu$ supplies its left truncated
moment; subtracting the right tail restores the complete
signed moment $cH-M$. The right formula is proved the same way.
These are exact identities, including compensation across the
two sides of the strike $c$.

For the actual prime model $\mu=R\nu|_{[a,b]}$ in a cell
$[a,b]\subset[u,2u]$, $u\ge2$, the coefficient inequalities
\[
 \frac1{\zeta(2)}\le\frac1{\zeta(r+1)}\le1
                       \qquad(r\ge1)
\]
give the unconditional pointwise lower density
\[
 \nu'(x)\ge\frac{1-x^{-1}}{\zeta(2)\log x}
       \ge \ell_u:=
            \frac{1-u^{-1}}{\zeta(2)\log(2u)}>0.
\]
Hence the retained negative model tails have explicit reserves:
\begin{align}
 L(c)&\le
 \frac{\displaystyle\int_a^c E_L(t)\,dt
                         -R\ell_u(b-c)^2/2}{c-a},
 \label{eq:two-moment-left-reserve}\\
 R(c)&\le
 \frac{\displaystyle\int_c^b E_R(t)\,dt
                         -R\ell_u(c-a)^2/2}{b-c}.
 \label{eq:two-moment-right-reserve}
\end{align}
The domains are respectively $c\in T_L$ and $c\in T_R$.
No absolute value has replaced the signed integrated
prime-capacity discrepancy. At $c=b$ in the left test and
$c=a$ in the right test, the corresponding reserve is
exactly zero. Those endpoint tests and $H-C$ remain in
\eqref{eq:two-moment-finite-defect}; the interior reserve
does not estimate them.

The atomic support calculation used in the dual now has
its own compiled owner,
\texttt{CappedSupportFunction.lean}.
It proves the signed capped-dot upper bound and an actual
supported real maximizer attaining
$\sum_i A_i(P(x_i))_+$, for arbitrary real coefficients
$P(x_i)$, including negative ones.
This checks the exact box support term used in the separation
proof. The finite strike reduction, continuous moment selection,
and analytic zero readout retain their ordinary proofs here;
they are not reported as compiled.

For the prime application, choose $k=2$ and $\theta=3/4$.
The analytic error in
\eqref{eq:capped-high-moment-error} is then normally summable
for $\Re s>1/2$, while the cell length is $X^{3/4}$.
The complete arithmetic consumer is
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{I\subset[2^j,2^{j+1})}
 \max\left\{0,H_I-C_I,\
            \max_{c\in T_{L,I}}L_I(c),\
            \max_{c\in T_{R,I}}R_I(c)\right\}
       \ll_\epsilon 2^{j(\eta+\epsilon)} .
 \label{eq:two-moment-full-signed-consumer}
\end{equation}
Here $H_I=R\nu(I)$ is real, with no rounding, and the actual
prime capacity is
$C_I=\sum_{p\in I,\ p\ {\rm prime}}A(p)$.
The preceding theorem constructs capped weights with exactly
this optimal synthetic defect in every cell.
If \eqref{eq:two-moment-full-signed-consumer} is proved for
$\eta=3/4$, the positive quotient argument proves the
zero-free boundary $3/4$; $\eta=1/2$ would imply RH.
One can use this exact optimal defect in place of the sufficient
maximal interval deficit, since only its positive mass enters
the convergence majorant.

This is a universal finite formula and an independently bounded
model compensation, not a finite numerical experiment or a proved
prime-discrepancy saving. The missing estimates are the full
signed integrated-capacity terms including their endpoint
tests and total mass deficit. The only unconditional aggregate
bound installed here is $O_R(2^j)$, so the certified
zero-free boundary remains $7/8$. No literature priority
is asserted for this reduction.


\subsection{One-sided overweight control for a favorable prime model}
\label{sec:one-sided-prime-model-transfer}

The reverse order can also be expressed directly for an actual
weighted prime Euler product. It is enough to control its
overweight relative to the available reference capacities;
