For each fixed $k$, all sufficiently late cells are good
by the same unconditional prime-mesh argument as before.
The rounded vertex model has nonnegative weights, true
normalized moment errors at most $3u^{-3}$ and the previous
block mass bound. Those are precisely the inputs of the
complete Euler-logarithm argument
\eqref{eq:rational-model-complete-logarithm}--%
\eqref{eq:rational-model-summable-error}.
The initial interval and all bad cells are retained there
in the finite head factor. It follows unconditionally that
\[
 F_k^{\rm v}(s)=\frac{s}{s-1}\exp(G_k^{\rm v}(s)),
                     \qquad \Re s>1/2,
\]
with $G_k^{\rm v}$ holomorphic.
This proves favorability independently of the reference zeros.

Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:underweight-real-axis-transfer} with
this model and $\tau=1/2$. A hypothetical zero at real part
$\beta>1/2$ forces the dyadic deficit $D_{j,k}$ to have
limsup exponent at least $\beta$.
If the limsup exponent of $N_{j,k}$ were smaller than
$\beta$, choose
\[
 \max\left(1/(2k),
       \limsup_j\frac{\log(1+N_{j,k})}{j\log2}\right)
                               <\alpha<\beta.
\]
The exact finite comparison
$D_{j,k}\le N_{j,k}+2k u^{1/(2k)}$ would give
$D_{j,k}\ll u^\alpha$, a contradiction.
This proves \eqref{eq:deleted-prime-count-zero-readout}.
Using the limsup with $\epsilon/2$ and absorbing the added
$1$ proves the stated infinitely-often power lower bound.

For the RH direction put
\[
 E^{\rm v}_{j,k}
       =\sum_{p\in S_j}(w^{\rm v}_{p,k}-1)_+,\quad
 C_j=\#S_j,\quad W^{\rm v}_{j,k}=\sum_{p\in S_j}w^{\rm v}_{p,k}.
\]
The complete signed balance is
\begin{equation}
 D_{j,k}=E^{\rm v}_{j,k}+C_j-W^{\rm v}_{j,k}.
 \label{eq:deleted-prime-signed-forward-ledger}
\end{equation}
For sufficiently large $j$, the vertex-model overweight
is at most the exact real optimum $E_{j,k}$, by
\eqref{eq:rational-LP-rounded-binary}.
Under RH the existing optimal-repair estimate gives
$E^{\rm v}_{j,k}\ll_{k,\epsilon}u^{1/2+1/(2k)+\epsilon}$.
Its zeroth moment error and the prime-counting estimate
give $|C_j-W^{\rm v}_{j,k}|\ll_k u^{1/2}\log u$, exactly
as in the preceding Poisson proof.
Combining the signed balance with $N_{j,k}\le D_{j,k}$
proves the forward half of
\eqref{eq:deleted-prime-RH-count-equivalence}.
The finitely many initial blocks are absorbed in its constant.

If RH is false, choose a zero with real part $\beta>1/2$,
then choose a fixed $k\in\mathcal K$ with
$1/(2k)<\beta-1/2$, and choose
$0<\epsilon<\beta-1/2-1/(2k)$.
The claimed count upper bound would contradict
\eqref{eq:deleted-prime-count-zero-readout}.
This proves the reverse half.

Finally suppose the premise of
\eqref{eq:deleted-prime-fixed-k-zero-free} holds.
Since $1/(2k)\le1/4<\eta$, the finite comparison in
\eqref{eq:computed-deleted-prime-counts} gives
$D_{j,k}\ll_\epsilon u^{\eta+\epsilon}$.
Dyadic summation, including the finite initial prime factors,
gives the cumulative underweight hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:underweight-real-axis-transfer}.
Its zeta specialization excludes zeros on $\Re s>\eta$.
If $\mathcal D_k$ is finite, this premise holds with
$\eta=1/2$, which yields RH by reflection.
\end{proof}

This criterion places the hypothetical bad-zero response in
the cardinality of a predefined actual prime class.
The nonbinary weights contribute only the explicitly bounded
partial-capacity term in
\eqref{eq:computed-deleted-prime-counts}.
An independent arithmetic estimate for $N_{j,k}$ would still
be needed for a new unconditional zero-free boundary;
the installed boundary remains $7/8$.
Jurzak~\cite{JurzakPartialEuler} studies analytic-continuation
criteria for partial products on prime-index arithmetic lists.
Here the classes are selected by finite rational moment
programs, and the response is an unconditional lower exponent
for their counting functions. The finite vertex geometry and
positive-series continuation principle are classical inputs;
no established literature priority is asserted for this
application.

\subsection{Critical-line retention and the zero supremum in prime counts}
\label{sec:critical-deleted-prime-signal}

A second scale choice continues the favorable model through
the critical line. It keeps the same rational vertex algorithm,
but defines new prime sets; the preceding $\mathcal D_k$
retain their original definitions.

\begin{lemma}[Prime-count error to the right of the zero supremum]
\label{lem:prime-error-zero-supremum}
Put
\[
 \Theta_\zeta=\sup\{\Re\rho:\zeta(\rho)=0,\ 0<\Re\rho<1\}.
\]
Then $1/2\le\Theta_\zeta\le1$. For every fixed
$\Theta_\zeta<b<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \pi(X)-\operatorname{Li}_2(X)=O_b(X^b\log^2 X)\quad(X\ge2).
 \label{eq:prime-error-zero-supremum}
\end{equation}
The constant may depend on $b-\Theta_\zeta$ and is not
asserted uniform as that distance tends to zero.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The nontrivial zeros are nonempty and symmetric about
$\Re s=1/2$, giving the bounds for their supremum;
see \cite[\S25.10(i)]{DLMFZetaZeros}.
We supply the analytic estimate used below, keeping a
finite contour rather than an unsummed infinite explicit formula.

The classical Hadamard product for the completed zeta
function and the local zero count
$\#\{\rho:|\Im\rho-t|\le1\}=O(\log(|t|+2))$ give
\begin{equation}
 \left|\frac{\zeta'}{\zeta}(\sigma+it)\right|
       \ll_b\log(|t|+2)
       \quad(b\le\sigma\le2,\ |t|\ge2).
 \label{eq:zero-supremum-log-derivative}
\end{equation}
Indeed, subtract the completed logarithmic derivative at
$2+it$ from that at $\sigma+it$. The zero sum becomes
\[
 \sum_\rho\left(\frac1{\sigma+it-\rho}
                            -\frac1{2+it-\rho}\right).
\]
Zeros with $|\Im\rho-t|\le1$ contribute
$O_b(\log(|t|+2))$, since every first denominator has
modulus at least $b-\Theta_\zeta>0$.
For the other zeros each difference is
$O_b((\Im\rho-t)^{-2})$; summing unit height intervals
with the local zero count gives the same bound.
Stirling's formula controls the gamma and rational factors.
The logarithmic derivative at $2+it$ converges absolutely.
This proves \eqref{eq:zero-supremum-log-derivative}.

For a half-integer $x\ge3$, put $c=1+1/\log x$ and $T=x^2$.
Truncated Perron inversion gives
\[
 \psi(x)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{c-iT}^{c+iT}
       -\frac{\zeta'}{\zeta}(s)\frac{x^s}{s}\,ds
                           +O(x\log^2 x/T).
\]
Its error is bounded by
\[
 \sum_{n\ge2}\Lambda(n)(x/n)^c
                  \min\{1,(T|\log(x/n)|)^{-1}\}.
\]
On $x/2<n<2x$, use
$|\log(x/n)|\gg|n-x|/x$, $|n-x|\ge1/2$, and the
harmonic sum over these distances. Outside that interval
use $\Lambda(n)\le\log n$ and
$\sum_{n\ge2}(\log n)n^{-c}\ll(c-1)^{-2}$.
These give the stated error. The truncated kernel bound
follows by integrating $y^s/s$ and bounding the omitted tails.

Move the finite contour to $\Re s=b$.
No zero pole is crossed; the pole at $1$ contributes $x$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:zero-supremum-log-derivative} bounds the
horizontal segments by $O_b(x\log T/T)$ and the new vertical
segment by $O_b(x^b\log^2 T)$. The bounded portion of that
line has a finite bound since it misses $1$ and every zero.
Thus $\psi(x)-x=O_b(x^b\log^2 x)$.
The half-integer $\lfloor X\rfloor+1/2$ preserves $\psi(X)$
for arbitrary real $X$ and changes the main term by at most
$1/2$. Higher prime powers contribute
$O(\sqrt X\log^2 X)$, absorbed because $b>1/2$.
Partial summation proves \eqref{eq:prime-error-zero-supremum}.
These classical inputs give no new upper bound for
$\Theta_\zeta$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Computable prime classes retaining critical-line zeros]
\label{thm:critical-deleted-prime-model}
Fix an integer $k\ge4$. In each block $u=2^j$, $j\ge3$,
use the zeta portfolio, $A(p)=R=1$, and set
\begin{equation}
 \theta_k^0=1-\frac1k,\quad
 h_{j,k}=2^{\lfloor(k-1)j/k\rfloor},\quad
 \tau=u^{-3},\quad M=u^4.
 \label{eq:critical-prime-cell-scale}
\end{equation}
Use the same rational good-cell test, deterministic minimizing
LP vertex and downward rounding as
Theorem~\ref{thm:computable-deleted-prime-RH}, with this cell
length. Keep weight zero at primes below $8$ and on bad cells.
Write the output as $w^0_{p,k}$ and define
\[
 \mathcal D_k^0=\{p\text{ prime}:w^0_{p,k}=0\},\qquad
 \mathcal T_k^0=\{p\text{ prime}:0<w^0_{p,k}<1\}.
\]
These fixed prime classes are computable from integer inputs
$p,k$. For $S_j=\{p\text{ prime}:u\le p<2u\}$, put
\[
 N^0_{j,k}=\#(\mathcal D_k^0\cap S_j),\qquad
 D^0_{j,k}=\sum_{p\in S_j}(1-w^0_{p,k})_+.
\]
The full Euler model has the unconditional representation
\begin{equation}
 F_k^0(s)=\prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-w^0_{p,k}}
       =\frac{s}{s-1}\exp(G_k^0(s)),\qquad \Re s>0,
 \label{eq:critical-prime-favorable-product}
\end{equation}
where $G_k^0$ is holomorphic. The product initially means its
absolutely convergent full Euler logarithm on $\Re s>1$.
For every $j\ge3$,
\begin{equation}
 0\le D^0_{j,k}-N^0_{j,k}
       \le\#(\mathcal T_k^0\cap S_j)\le2k\,u^{1/k}.
 \label{eq:critical-prime-count-ledger}
\end{equation}
Every nontrivial zeta zero with real part $\beta>1/k$ forces
\begin{equation}
 \limsup_{j\to\infty}
       \frac{\log(1+N^0_{j,k})}{j\log2}\ge\beta.
 \label{eq:critical-prime-zero-readout}
\end{equation}
In particular, unconditionally, for every fixed $k\ge4$
and $\epsilon>0$,
\begin{equation}
 N^0_{j,k}\ge u^{1/2-\epsilon}
                       \quad\text{for infinitely many }j.
 \label{eq:critical-prime-unconditional-infinitude}
\end{equation}
Thus every $\mathcal D_k^0$ is infinite, independently of RH.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The earlier classes used $1-1/(2k)$; these use $1-1/k$.
Here $2/3<\theta_k^0<1$ and $u/h_{j,k}\le2u^{1/k}$.
The unconditional prime-mesh bound therefore makes all
sufficiently late cells good, for each fixed $k$.
The finite algorithm tests goodness itself and needs no
knowledge of that eventual threshold.
Its grid and LP data are rational, so the membership
decision terminates exactly as before.
The vertex theorem applies unchanged on every good cell.
Rounding preserves zero and unit coordinates; bad cells
have no partial coordinates. Count cells and apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:deleted-count-deficit-ledger} to obtain
\eqref{eq:critical-prime-count-ledger}.

We check the full new continuation region.
Fix a compact $K\subset\{\Re s>0\}$ and
$\sigma_0=\inf_{s\in K}\Re s>0$.
The complete Euler kernel and differentiated series are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 f_s(x)&=\sum_{m\ge1}x^{-ms}/m=-\log(1-x^{-s}),\\
 f_s^{(k)}(x)&=(-1)^k\sum_{m\ge1}
     \frac{(ms)(ms+1)\cdots(ms+k-1)}m\,x^{-ms-k}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
For $x\ge u$, the full derivative bound is
\[
 |f_s^{(k)}(x)|\le
 (k-1)!\prod_{r=0}^{k-1}(|s|+r)\,
             \frac{x^{-\Re s-k}}{(1-x^{-\Re s})^k}.
\]
No prime-power term has been removed. Now $h_{j,k}^k\le u^{k-1}$,
and the exact exponent identity is
\begin{equation}
 1-\sigma-k(1-\theta_k^0)=-\sigma .
 \label{eq:critical-prime-remainder-exponent}
\end{equation}
The rounded normalized moment errors are at most $3u^{-3}$.
Every Taylor coefficient of $f_s(a+hy)$ through degree $k-1$
is $O_{k,K}(u^{-\sigma_0})$.
The block's model plus actual positive mass is
$O_k(u/\log u)$, by the zeroth moment tolerance and the
zero weights on bad cells. The complete good-cell discrepancy
therefore satisfies
\begin{equation}
 |G^0_{j,k}(s)|\ll_{k,K}
       \frac{u^{-\sigma_0}}{\log u}
                       +u^{-3+1/k-\sigma_0}\qquad(s\in K).
 \label{eq:critical-prime-full-error}
\end{equation}
The second term retains the Taylor-polynomial residual
from all cells; moments are not claimed to cancel exactly.
Both terms are summable over dyadic blocks.
The exponent identity, mesh range and negativity of both
powers have separate checked Lean proofs in
\texttt{CriticalCellScale.lean}; the Euler analysis is ordinary.

Let $\mathcal H_k^0$ be $(1,8)$ together with all bad cells,
a bounded union. The full initial normalization is
\begin{equation}
 \log F_k^0(s)-\log\frac{s}{s-1}
       =-\int_{\mathcal H_k^0}f_s(x)\,d\nu(x)
                    +\sum_{j\ge3}G^0_{j,k}(s)\quad(\Re s>1).
 \label{eq:critical-prime-complete-normalization}
\end{equation}
The head integral is holomorphic on $\Re s>0$.
Near $x=1$, uniformly on $K$, its kernel is
$O_K(1+|\log(x-1)|)$ and $\nu'$ is bounded.
Together with \eqref{eq:critical-prime-full-error}, this
defines $G_k^0$ throughout $\Re s>0$ and proves
\eqref{eq:critical-prime-favorable-product}.
Only zeta is used: its nonsplit factor is $U_{\{1\}}=1$.
No general portfolio's nonsplit unit is extended below
its proved half-plane.

The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:underweight-real-axis-transfer}
now applies with any $0<\eta<1$.
All its factors are units on $\Re s>\eta$, zeta has no
real zero on $(\eta,1)$, and the positive Dirichlet
continuation theorem has no $1/2$ restriction on its endpoint.
Explicitly, set
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 d_p=(1-w^0_{p,k})_+,\qquad e_p=(w^0_{p,k}-1)_+,\\
 E_-=\prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-d_p},\qquad
 E_+=\prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-e_p}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
The complete identity is $\zeta E_+=F_k^0E_-$.
First assume a deficit bound above $\eta$, making $E_-$
a convergent unit. The meromorphic quotient $F_k^0E_-/\zeta$
is real analytic on the real ray after canceling the
pole at $1$. Its positive Dirichlet series really converges
above $\eta$, making $E_+$ a unit too.
Only then continue the cross-multiplied identity.
It excludes a zeta zero in that half-plane.

Thus every zero at real part $\beta>0$ forces the deficit
$D^0_{j,k}$ to have limsup exponent at least $\beta$.
For $\beta>1/k$ the partial allowance $2ku^{1/k}$ has
strictly smaller exponent and cannot absorb this signal.
This proves \eqref{eq:critical-prime-zero-readout}.
Critical-line zeros exist unconditionally
\cite[\S25.10(i)]{DLMFZetaZeros}.
Take $\beta=1/2>1/k$ and use the limsup with $\epsilon/2$
to absorb the added $1$. This proves
\eqref{eq:critical-prime-unconditional-infinitude}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The zero supremum recovered from literal prime counts]
\label{thm:prime-count-exponents-zero-supremum}
For these fixed computable classes define
\[
 \lambda_k=\limsup_{j\to\infty}
       \frac{\log(1+N^0_{j,k})}{j\log2}\qquad(k\ge4).
\]
Then, unconditionally,
\begin{equation}
 \Theta_\zeta\le\lambda_k\le\min\{1,\Theta_\zeta+1/k\},
 \qquad
 \lim_{k\to\infty}\lambda_k
                =\inf_{k\ge4}\lambda_k=\Theta_\zeta .
 \label{eq:prime-count-zero-supremum-sandwich}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 {\rm RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
       \lim_{k\to\infty}\lambda_k=\tfrac12\\
 \Longleftrightarrow\
 \left[\forall k\ge4,\epsilon>0,\quad
    N^0_{j,k}\ll_{k,\epsilon}u^{1/2+1/k+\epsilon}\right].
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:critical-prime-RH-count-criterion}
\end{equation}
Each $k$ is fixed before the dyadic limsup; no uniformity
in growing $k$ is asserted.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The zero readout gives $\lambda_k\ge\beta$ for every zero
with $\beta>1/k$. Since $\Theta_\zeta\ge1/2>1/k$, taking
the supremum gives $\lambda_k\ge\Theta_\zeta$, without
assuming that a rightmost zero is attained.
There are at most $u$ integer nodes in $S_j$, so
$\lambda_k\le1$. If $\Theta_\zeta=1$, these two bounds
already prove the sandwich and its limit.

Suppose $\Theta_\zeta<1$ and fix $\Theta_\zeta<b<1$.
The unconditional comparison
\eqref{eq:continuous-model-logarithmic-integral-error}
and Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-error-zero-supremum} give
\[
 F(t):=\nu([1,t])-\pi(t)=O_b(t^b\log^2 t)\quad(t\ge2),
\]
also for one-sided endpoint limits.
Take a late block where all cells are good.
For each exact real optimum $E_{I,k}$, choose an attaining
degree-less-than-$k$ dual polynomial $P_I$.
Use $P_I=0$ if the optimum is zero.
Otherwise positive-objective norming on this very good
cell gives $\|P_I\|_I\le64B_{k-1}$, as in
\eqref{eq:rational-model-dual-coefficient-budget}.
Writing $\pi$ also for its counting measure, retain
\begin{equation}
 E_{I,k}=\int_I P_I\,d(\nu-\pi)
                       -\sum_{p\in S_I}(P_I(p))_- .
 \label{eq:critical-prime-signed-cell-objective}
\end{equation}
The negative dual values remain in the exact objective.

A nonconstant polynomial of degree less than $k$ has at
most $k-1$ monotonicity intervals, so
$\operatorname{Var}_I(P_I)\le2(k-1)\|P_I\|_I$;
the constant case has variation zero.
Stieltjes integration by parts on $I=[a,b_I)$ uses
$F(a^-),F(b_I^-)$ and the integral of $F(t)P_I'(t)$.
Its endpoint and derivative contributions are bounded by
\[
 \left(|P_I(a)|+|P_I(b_I)|
                   +\operatorname{Var}_I(P_I)\right)
 \sup_{u\le t\le2u}\max\{|F(t)|,|F(t^-)|\}.
\]
Hence $E_{I,k}\ll_{k,b}u^b\log^2u$.
This uses the classical prime error to the right of
$\Theta_\zeta$, and proves no new zero-free strip.

There are at most $2u^{1/k}$ cells.
The rounded vertex overweight is at most the exact
optimum cellwise, so
$E^0_{j,k}\ll_{k,b}u^{b+1/k}\log^2u$.
The zeroth moment tolerance gives
$W^0_{j,k}=\nu([u,2u))+O_k(u^{-3+1/k})$.
With $C_j=\#S_j$, the full signed consumer is
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 N^0_{j,k}\le D^0_{j,k}
    &=E^0_{j,k}+C_j-W^0_{j,k}\\
    &\ll_{k,b}u^{b+1/k}\log^2u
                       +u^b\log^2u+u^{-3+1/k}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:critical-prime-full-signed-count-bound}
\end{equation}
Thus $\lambda_k\le b+1/k$.
Let $b\downarrow\Theta_\zeta$ and retain the independent
upper bound $\lambda_k\le1$.
The sandwich proves both the limit and the infimum.
Zero symmetry gives
$ {\rm RH}\Longleftrightarrow\Theta_\zeta=1/2$.
Under RH take $b>1/2$ arbitrarily close in the same bound
to obtain the count criterion. Conversely that criterion
gives $\lambda_k\le1/2+1/k$ for every fixed $k$;
let $k\to\infty$ and use $\lambda_k\ge\Theta_\zeta$.
\end{proof}

The size needed to retain the signal is now explicit:
the fixed $\mathcal D_k^0$ cannot have counting exponent
below $\Theta_\zeta$, and approach it within $1/k$.
They are infinite even if RH holds, unlike the preceding
classes whose finiteness was only a sufficient RH condition.
The construction consumes completed moment, vertex and
positive-series inputs. Its new analytic consumers have
the full ordinary proofs above; only the three scalar
cell-scale lemmas are newly Lean checked.
No independent arithmetic bound on these cardinalities
has been proved, and the installed boundary remains $7/8$.
No established literature priority is asserted.

\begin{theorem}[One computable prime class with the exact zero exponent]
\label{thm:single-computable-prime-RH}
There is one fixed computable class $\mathcal D_*$ of actual
primes, defined without any zero-location assumption, such that
\begin{equation}
 \limsup_{X\to\infty}
       \frac{\log(1+\pi_{\mathcal D_*}(X))}{\log X}
                         =\Theta_\zeta,\qquad
 \pi_{\mathcal D_*}(X)=\#\{p\le X:p\in\mathcal D_*\}.
 \label{eq:single-prime-class-zero-exponent}
\end{equation}
For every $\eta\in[1/2,1)$ the exact equivalence is
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \zeta(s)\ne0\quad(\Re s>\eta)\\
 \Longleftrightarrow\
 \left[\forall\epsilon>0,\quad
   \pi_{\mathcal D_*}(X)\ll_\epsilon X^{\eta+\epsilon}\right].
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:single-prime-class-strip-equivalence}
\end{equation}
In particular the square-root count criterion is equivalent
to RH. Unconditionally $\mathcal D_*$ is infinite, and its
dyadic count is at least $u^{1/2-\epsilon}$ at infinitely
many scales, for every $\epsilon>0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We give an effective diagonal construction with a uniform
analytic budget. Merely substituting a growing $k$ into the
preceding fixed-$k$ big-$O$ statements would not suffice.

For integers $k\ge4$, use the explicit rational norming
constant $B_{k-1}=2^{k-1}(k-1)^k/(k-1)!$ and put
\begin{equation}
 C_k=(2k)^{2k},\qquad
 Q_k=1+16kC_k+128kB_{k-1}.
 \label{eq:single-prime-rational-budget}
\end{equation}
For $j\ge3$ put $u=2^j$ and $m_j=\lfloor\sqrt j\rfloor$,
computed as an integer square root.
An integer $k$ is admissible at this block if
\begin{equation}
 4\le k\le m_j,\qquad Q_k\le2^{m_j},
 \quad\text{and every length-}h_{j,k}\text{ cell is good}.
 \label{eq:single-prime-admissible-order}
\end{equation}
The last condition is the previous finite rational good-cell
test on the actual primes. All these are decidable finite
tests. If any order is admissible, take the largest, $k_j$,
and run the corresponding rounded minimizing-vertex algorithm
on every cell of this block. If there is none, assign weight
zero throughout the block. Also keep weight zero below $8$.
Write the resulting rational weight as $w_p^*$ and define
\[
                         \mathcal D_*=\{p\text{ prime}:w_p^*=0\}.
\]
Given an integer prime $p$, its block, every candidate order,
all prime enumerations, rational moment approximations and
LP vertex comparisons require only terminating finite
computations. This proves computability, without asserting
that the algorithm is efficient.

For each fixed $k\ge4$, all its cells are good at every
sufficiently late block. Also $m_j\to\infty$ and
$2^{m_j}\to\infty$, so this fixed $k$ is eventually admissible
at every block. Consequently only finitely many blocks are
skipped, and
\begin{equation}
 k_j\longrightarrow\infty,\qquad
 k_j\le m_j,\qquad Q_{k_j}\le2^{m_j},\qquad m_j/j\longrightarrow0
 \label{eq:single-prime-order-and-budget}
\end{equation}
on all sufficiently late blocks. No monotonicity of $k_j$
is needed, and no unknown prime-gap threshold is used by
the algorithm.

We now verify the uniform budget in
\eqref{eq:single-prime-rational-budget}.
If $k\ge4$, $u\ge8$, $\Re s=\sigma\ge1/k$ and $|s|\le k$,
then
\[
 1-u^{-\sigma}\ge1-8^{-1/k}\ge\frac1{2k}.
\]
The last inequality follows from Bernoulli:
$(1-1/(2k))^k\ge1/2>1/8$.
The full differentiated Euler series gives, for
$1\le r\le k$,
\[
 \frac{h^r}{r!}|f_s^{(r)}(a)|
 \le\frac1r(2k)^{2r}u^{-\sigma}(h/u)^r,
 \qquad a\ge u.
\]
For $r=0$, $|f_s(a)|\le2k u^{-\sigma}$.
Thus every retained Taylor coefficient is at most
$C_k u^{-\sigma}$.
There are at most $2u^{1/k}$ cells, each with every normalized
moment discrepancy at most $3u^{-3}$.
Their polynomial residual is at most
$6kC_k u^{-3+1/k-\sigma}$.
The combined model and actual positive mass is at most $3u$:
$\nu([u,2u))\le u/\log u$ and the extra mass is at most
$3u^{-3}$ times the number of cells, which is at most $u$.
Using $h^k\le u^{k-1}$, the full Taylor remainder is at most
$3C_k u^{-\sigma}$.
We have therefore proved, uniformly in the indicated $k,s,u$,
\begin{equation}
 |G_{j,k}^0(s)|
       \le Q_k\bigl(u^{-\sigma}
                         +u^{-3+1/k-\sigma}\bigr).
 \label{eq:single-prime-uniform-Euler-error}
\end{equation}
All prime powers, all moment residuals and the mass in
every cell have been included.

Fix an arbitrary compact $K\subset\{\Re s>0\}$ and
$\sigma_0=\inf_K\Re s>0$. Since $k_j\to\infty$,
eventually $1/k_j\le\sigma_0$ and $\sup_K|s|\le k_j$.
Equations~\eqref{eq:single-prime-order-and-budget} and
\eqref{eq:single-prime-uniform-Euler-error} bound the
tail uniformly on $K$ by
\[
 2^{m_j-\sigma_0 j}
       +2^{m_j-(3-1/4+\sigma_0)j}.
\]
Both series converge, because $m_j/j\to0$.
The finite head consists of $(1,8)$ and the finitely many
skipped blocks, with every initial integral retained.
Exactly the complete normalization from
\eqref{eq:critical-prime-complete-normalization} now proves
\begin{equation}
 F_*(s)=\prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-w_p^*}
       =\frac{s}{s-1}\exp(G_*(s))\quad(\Re s>0),
 \label{eq:single-prime-favorable-model}
\end{equation}
with $G_*$ holomorphic. Initial absolute convergence on
$\Re s>1$ follows from the same block mass bound.

For a late block let $N_j^*=\#(\mathcal D_*\cap S_j)$ and
$D_j^*=\sum_{p\in S_j}(1-w_p^*)_+$.
The vertex and finite ledger give
\begin{equation}
 0\le D_j^*-N_j^*
       \le2k_j u^{1/k_j}=u^{o(1)} .
 \label{eq:single-prime-partial-allowance}
\end{equation}
Indeed $1/k_j\to0$ and
$\log(2k_j)/(j\log2)\le\log(2m_j)/(j\log2)\to0$.
The zeta-only underweight argument above, applied to
\eqref{eq:single-prime-favorable-model}, implies that every
zero of real part $\beta>0$ forces $D_j^*$ to have dyadic
limsup exponent at least $\beta$. The subpower allowance
in \eqref{eq:single-prime-partial-allowance} cannot absorb
any such positive-power signal. Therefore
\begin{equation}
 \lambda_*:=\limsup_{j\to\infty}
       \frac{\log(1+N_j^*)}{j\log2}\ge\Theta_\zeta .
 \label{eq:single-prime-unconditional-readout}
\end{equation}
A critical-line zero gives the stated infinitely-often
square-root lower exponent, so $\mathcal D_*$ is infinite.

For the opposite inequality suppose first $\Theta_\zeta<1$
and fix $\Theta_\zeta<b<1$.
The cumulative discrepancy $F(t)=\nu([1,t])-\pi(t)$ is
bounded by $C_b t^b\log^2t$ as above, with $C_b$ independent
of $k$. On any good cell, retain the signed dual identity
\eqref{eq:critical-prime-signed-cell-objective}.
The endpoint-plus-variation factor used there is at most
\[
                  2k\,64B_{k-1}\le Q_k .
\]
There is therefore no untracked fixed-$k$ constant:
summing cells gives
$E_j^*\ll_b Q_{k_j}u^{b+1/k_j}\log^2u$.
The zeroth moment tolerance and the same cumulative
discrepancy control the remaining signed mass balance.
Explicitly, with $C_j=\#S_j$ and $W_j^*=\sum_{p\in S_j}w_p^*$,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 N_j^*\le D_j^*
    &=E_j^*+C_j-W_j^*\\
    &\ll_b
      2^{m_j}u^{b+1/k_j}\log^2u
                         +u^b\log^2u+u^{-3+1/k_j}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:single-prime-full-signed-upper-bound}
\end{equation}
Its exponent is at most $b$, since
$m_j/j\to0$ and $1/k_j\to0$.
Let $b\downarrow\Theta_\zeta$ in this conclusion to obtain
$\lambda_*\le\Theta_\zeta$.
If $\Theta_\zeta=1$, the elementary bound $N_j^*\le u$
already gives the same conclusion.
Together with \eqref{eq:single-prime-unconditional-readout},
this proves $\lambda_*=\Theta_\zeta$.

Dyadic summation, using $\Theta_\zeta\ge1/2>0$, transfers
this exponent unchanged to the cumulative count.
For example every bound $N_j^*\ll u^{b+\epsilon}$ with
$b>0$ sums geometrically to
$\pi_{\mathcal D_*}(X)\ll X^{b+\epsilon}$;
the reverse exponent inequality follows from
$N_j^*\le\pi_{\mathcal D_*}(2u)$.
This proves \eqref{eq:single-prime-class-zero-exponent}.
Finally $\Theta_\zeta\le\eta$ is exactly the absence of
zeros on $\Re s>\eta$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:single-prime-class-zero-exponent} gives
\eqref{eq:single-prime-class-strip-equivalence};
zero symmetry specializes $\eta=1/2$ to RH.
\end{proof}

This single-class construction removes the order parameter
from the final arithmetic question. Its weights and its
zero-weight prime set are fixed in advance.
The uniform budgets above are ordinary proofs; they do
not follow merely by exchanging the earlier fixed-order
quantifiers. An independent estimate of this one class's
count would move the zero-free boundary by exactly the
same exponent. No such improved estimate is asserted here,
and the certified $7/8$ boundary is unchanged.

\subsection{Endpoint divergence and the arithmetic cost of a zero}
\label{sec:prime-class-endpoint-cost}

The exponent identity leaves an endpoint question:
a positive series can converge at its abscissa of convergence.
The full Euler quotients rule out that possibility here
when the endpoint is the real part of an actual zero.

We use a boundary fact about positive series.
If $f_j(n)\ge0$, every $\sum_n f_j(n)$ converges and is at
most $M$, and $f_j(n)\to g(n)\ge0$ for every $n$, then
$\sum_n g(n)$ converges. Indeed every finite sum of $g$
is the limit of the corresponding finite sums of $f_j$,
hence is at most $M$. Bounded nonnegative finite sums
imply summability.
The maintained \texttt{PositiveDirichletBoundary.lean}
proves this fact and its explicit specialization to
$a_n n^{-\sigma_j}$, $\sigma_j\to\alpha$,
in two standard-axiom-audited declarations.
No zero theorem is asserted to be Lean formalized by those lemmas.

\begin{theorem}[Both one-sided Euler masses diverge at a zero]
\label{thm:two-sided-prime-endpoint-divergence}
Let $w_p\ge0$, with $\sum_p w_p p^{-\sigma}<\infty$
for every $\sigma>1$. Suppose its complete Euler product
$F_w$ has a nowhere-zero holomorphic continuation on
$\{\Re s>\tau\}\setminus\{1\}$ and a simple pole at $1$,
where $0\le\tau<1$.
If $\rho=\beta+i\gamma$ is a nontrivial zeta zero with
$\beta>\tau$, put
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 d_p=(1-w_p)_+,\qquad e_p=(w_p-1)_+,\\
 \mathcal P_d(\sigma)=\sum_p d_p p^{-\sigma},\qquad
 \mathcal P_e(\sigma)=\sum_p e_p p^{-\sigma}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
Then both nonnegative series diverge at that endpoint:
\begin{equation}
             \mathcal P_d(\beta)=\mathcal P_e(\beta)=\infty .
 \label{eq:two-sided-prime-endpoint-divergence}
\end{equation}
The statement is unconditional and makes no rightmost-zero
or RH assumption.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Retain the full products
\[
 E_-=\prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-d_p},\qquad
 E_+=\prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-e_p},\qquad
                         \zeta E_+=F_wE_- .
\]
The identity holds initially on $\Re s>1$.
Assume first $\mathcal P_d(\beta)<\infty$.
Because $\beta>0$,
\[
 \sum_p\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{d_p p^{-r\beta}}r
       \le\frac{\mathcal P_d(\beta)}{1-2^{-\beta}}<\infty.
\]
Thus the logarithm of $E_-$ converges uniformly on the
closed half-plane $\Re s\ge\beta$. Its exponential is
continuous and nonzero there, and holomorphic in the interior.

On the real ray $\sigma>\beta$, the meromorphic quotient
$F_wE_-/\zeta$ is real analytic after cancellation of the
two simple poles at $1$.
Zeta has no real zero in $(0,1)$ or $(1,\infty)$.
This quotient agrees initially with the positive Dirichlet
series of $E_+$. The positive-continuation principle
therefore forces actual Dirichlet summability for $E_+$
at every real $\sigma>\beta$, and its prime coefficients
give $\mathcal P_e(\sigma)<\infty$ there.
This argument uses real-axis neighborhoods, not an
RH-dependent complex zero-free region.

As $\sigma\downarrow\beta$, the same real quotient remains
bounded: $F_w(\beta)$ and $E_-(\beta)$ are finite, whereas
$\zeta(\beta)\ne0$. Choose a sequence of such $\sigma$
tending to $\beta$. The positive-series boundary fact
just proved gives actual Dirichlet summability of $E_+$
at $\beta$. Its prime subseries then gives
$\mathcal P_e(\beta)<\infty$.
Consequently $E_+$ also has a continuous nonzero boundary
value at $\rho$, with every prime power retained.
Continue the cross-multiplied identity on $\Re s>\beta$,
then let $s=\rho+h$, $h\downarrow0$. It would give
\[
                 0\cdot E_+(\rho)=F_w(\rho)E_-(\rho)\ne0,
\]
a contradiction.

If instead $\mathcal P_e(\beta)<\infty$, interchange the
roles of the products and use the positive quotient
$E_-=\zeta E_+/F_w$. Its real continuation, cancellation
at $1$, and bounded boundary limit give
$\mathcal P_d(\beta)<\infty$ by the same argument,
and the same contradiction follows.
All products and boundary limits above are justified
by absolute convergence before a value at $\rho$ is used.
This proves \eqref{eq:two-sided-prime-endpoint-divergence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Logarithmic one-sided cost of a boundary zero]
\label{thm:prime-zero-logarithmic-cost}
In the preceding setting let the order of the zero at
$\rho$ be $m\ge1$. Assume in addition
\begin{equation}
       \mathcal P_d(\sigma)<\infty
                           \quad\text{for every }\sigma>\beta .
 \label{eq:prime-zero-cost-right-convergence}
\end{equation}
Then $\mathcal P_e$ also converges on that real ray, and
as $h\downarrow0$,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal P_d(\beta+h)&\ge\frac m2\log(1/h)-O_{\rho,w}(1),\\
 \mathcal P_e(\beta+h)&\ge\frac m2\log(1/h)-O_{\rho,w}(1).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:prime-zero-logarithmic-mass-cost}
\end{equation}
The constants are for this fixed zero and fixed model;
no height-uniform bound is asserted.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The same positive-continuation argument makes both complete
Euler products convergent units on $\Re s>\beta$.
Write
\[
 H_\pm(\sigma)=\log E_\pm(\sigma),\quad
 K(s)=F_w(s)/\zeta(s),\quad
 B(\sigma)=\log|F_w(\sigma)/\zeta(\sigma)|.
\]
The real function $B$ stays bounded as $\sigma\downarrow\beta$,
since its numerator and denominator are both nonzero at
that real point. The exact signed real balance is
\[
                       H_+(\sigma)-H_-(\sigma)=B(\sigma).
\]
For $s=\sigma+it$ the full Euler factors give
\begin{equation}
 |E_+(s)|\le E_+(\sigma),\qquad
 |E_-(s)|\ge\frac{E_-(2\sigma)}{E_-(\sigma)}.
 \label{eq:prime-zero-complete-product-norm}
\end{equation}
For the second inequality use
$|1-p^{-s}|\le1+p^{-\sigma}$ and the exact identity
$1+p^{-\sigma}=(1-p^{-2\sigma})/(1-p^{-\sigma})$.
Take the nonnegative powers $d_p$ and pass from finite
products by the already established absolute convergence.
Thus the scalar factor $E_-(2\sigma)$ is retained.

Since $F_w$ is nonzero at $\rho$ and $\zeta$ has order $m$
there, $|K(\rho+h)|\ge c_{\rho,w}h^{-m}$ for all sufficiently
small $h>0$, where $c_{\rho,w}>0$.
With $\sigma=\beta+h$, the complete quotient and real
compensation therefore give
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 m\log(1/h)+\log c_{\rho,w}
    &\le\log|K(\rho+h)|\\
    &\le H_+(\sigma)+H_-(\sigma)-H_-(2\sigma)\\
    &=2H_-(\sigma)+B(\sigma)-H_-(2\sigma).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:prime-zero-full-signed-pole-consumer}
\end{equation}
Here $H_-(2\sigma)\ge0$, and $B$ is bounded.
It follows that $H_-(\beta+h)\ge(m/2)\log(1/h)-O(1)$.
The signed real balance gives the same bound for $H_+$.

Finally the retained higher prime powers have bounded mass
at the endpoint:
\[
 0\le H_-(\sigma)-\mathcal P_d(\sigma)
       \le\frac{\mathcal P_d(2\beta)}{1-2^{-\beta}}
                  \quad(\sigma\ge\beta,\ \sigma>\beta).
\]
The numerator is finite by
\eqref{eq:prime-zero-cost-right-convergence}, since $2\beta>\beta$.
The analogous bound for $H_+$ uses
$\mathcal P_e(2\beta)<\infty$, already supplied by positive
continuation. Subtracting these bounded tails proves
\eqref{eq:prime-zero-logarithmic-mass-cost}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Endpoint size of the computable prime class]
\label{cor:computed-prime-endpoint-size}
The single class $\mathcal D_*$ satisfies, unconditionally,
\begin{equation}
                   \sum_{p\in\mathcal D_*}p^{-1/2}=\infty .
 \label{eq:computed-prime-root-divergence}
\end{equation}
If a zero $\rho$ of order $m$ attains
$\Re\rho=\beta=\Theta_\zeta<1$, then
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \sum_{p\in\mathcal D_*}p^{-\beta-h}
                \ge\frac m2\log(1/h)-O_\rho(1),\qquad h\downarrow0,\\
 \limsup_{X\to\infty}
        \frac{\pi_{\mathcal D_*}(X)\log X}{X^\beta}
                                  \ge\frac{m}{2\beta}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:computed-prime-rightmost-zero-size}
\end{equation}
For every critical-line zero of order $m$, regardless of RH,
\begin{equation}
 \limsup_{X\to\infty}
      \frac{\pi_{\mathcal D_*}(X)\log X}{\sqrt X}\ge m .
 \label{eq:computed-prime-unconditional-root-size}
\end{equation}
In particular the last limsup is at least $1$.
If RH is false, that limsup is infinite.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The strict-partial coordinates of the diagonal model have
subpower counting function. For every fixed $b>0$ they
therefore have finite sum $\sum p^{-b}$, by dyadic summation.
Their deficits are between $0$ and $1$.
At every prime, its deficit is $1$ on $\mathcal D_*$,
is strictly between $0$ and $1$ on those partial coordinates,
and is zero elsewhere. Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal P_d(\sigma)
      =\sum_{p\in\mathcal D_*}p^{-\sigma}
             +\sum_{\substack{p\text{ prime}\\0<w_p^*<1}}
                    (1-w_p^*)p^{-\sigma},
 \label{eq:computed-prime-full-endpoint-ledger}
\end{equation}
where the second sum is finite for every fixed $\sigma>0$.
The model $F_*$ is independently favorable on $\Re s>0$.
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:two-sided-prime-endpoint-divergence}
to an unconditional critical-line zero. The second sum
cannot supply its divergent deficit, proving
\eqref{eq:computed-prime-root-divergence}.

When $\beta=\Theta_\zeta$, the exact counting exponent
proved above makes $\mathcal P_d(\sigma)$ finite for all
$\sigma>\beta$. The logarithmic cost theorem applies.
The partial-coordinate sum in
\eqref{eq:computed-prime-full-endpoint-ledger} is bounded
uniformly for $\sigma\ge\beta$ near that endpoint,
so it gives the first inequality of
\eqref{eq:computed-prime-rightmost-zero-size}.

We check the count normalization explicitly.
If the second limsup in
\eqref{eq:computed-prime-rightmost-zero-size} is finite, call
it $L$. For any $A>L$,
$\pi_{\mathcal D_*}(X)\le A X^\beta/\log X$ after some
finite $X_A$. Positive partial summation gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{p\in\mathcal D_*}p^{-\beta-h}
   &=(\beta+h)\int_2^\infty
               \pi_{\mathcal D_*}(x)x^{-\beta-h-1}\,dx\\
   &\le A(\beta+h)\int_{X_A}^\infty
                           \frac{x^{-1-h}}{\log x}\,dx+O_A(1)\\
   &=A\beta\log(1/h)+O_A(1).
 \end{aligned}
\]
For the last equality set $t=\log x$, split the integral
at $t=1/h$, and use
$\int_{\log X_A}^\infty e^{-ht}\,dt/t=\log(1/h)+O_A(1)$.
The pole lower bound forces $A\beta\ge m/2$.
Let $A\downarrow L$ to obtain $L\ge m/(2\beta)$.
An infinite limsup already satisfies that conclusion.

If RH holds, every critical-line zero attains
$\beta=\Theta_\zeta=1/2$, so the factor $m/(2\beta)$ is
exactly $m$. If RH is false, $\Theta_\zeta>1/2$ and the
exact counting exponent of $\mathcal D_*$ makes the
square-root-normalized limsup infinite.
This proves \eqref{eq:computed-prime-unconditional-root-size}
in both cases.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Root-summable exclusions preserve the signal]
\label{cor:computed-prime-root-summable-exclusions}
Let $\mathcal A$ be any prime class with
$\sum_{p\in\mathcal A}p^{-1/2}<\infty$, and set
$\mathcal D'=\mathcal D_*\setminus\mathcal A$.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{p\in\mathcal D'}p^{-1/2}=\infty,\qquad
 \limsup_{X\to\infty}
       \frac{\log(1+\pi_{\mathcal D'}(X))}{\log X}=\Theta_\zeta .
 \label{eq:computed-prime-root-stable-exponent}
\end{equation}
The zero-free-strip and RH count equivalences hold for
$\mathcal D'$ as well, and so does
\eqref{eq:computed-prime-unconditional-root-size}.
If $\mathcal A$ is computable, $\mathcal D'$ is computable.

In particular one may exclude simultaneously all actual
Fermat primes and all actual Fibonacci primes:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal A_{\rm Fer}
   &=\{2^{2^n}+1:n\ge0,\ 2^{2^n}+1\text{ prime}\},\\
 \mathcal A_{\rm Fib}
   &=\{F_n:n\ge3,\ F_n\text{ prime}\}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
These are sets of prime values; overlaps in their union
are counted once. Neither class is assumed infinite.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Subtracting a finite positive subseries cannot remove the
divergence in \eqref{eq:computed-prime-root-divergence}.
Also
$\pi_{\mathcal A}(X)\le
\sqrt X\sum_{p\in\mathcal A}p^{-1/2}$.
If $\Theta_\zeta>1/2$, this smaller-exponent count cannot
absorb the $\Theta_\zeta$ signal of $\mathcal D_*$.
If $\Theta_\zeta=1/2$, the divergent root sum of
$\mathcal D'$ forces its counting exponent to be at least
$1/2$: a smaller exponent would make that sum convergent
by dyadic summation. Its exponent is at most that of
$\mathcal D_*$, since it is a subset. This proves
\eqref{eq:computed-prime-root-stable-exponent} and hence
the same strip and RH equivalences.

Under RH, the removed prime Dirichlet sum stays bounded
as $\sigma\downarrow1/2$, so subtracting it preserves the
logarithmic lower bound $ (m/2)\log(1/h)-O(1)$.
The partial-summation argument therefore preserves the
constant $m$ in the square-root-normalized count limsup.
If RH is false, the retained exponent is greater than
$1/2$ and that limsup is infinite.
Computability follows by testing membership in the two
specified computable sets.

Finally the two named classes have finite root sums
even before primality filtering:
\[
 \sum_{n\ge0}(2^{2^n}+1)^{-1/2}<\infty,\qquad
 \sum_{n\ge3}F_n^{-1/2}
                  \le\sum_{n\ge3}\varphi^{-(n-2)/2}<\infty,
 \quad \varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2}.
\]
The Fibonacci bound $F_n\ge\varphi^{n-2}$ for $n\ge2$
follows from the recurrence and $\varphi^2=\varphi+1$,
or the earlier Binet formula. Both prime-value membership
tests terminate by monotone integer sequence enumeration.
Their union is root-summable even when the two sets overlap.
This proves the stated actual-prime exclusions.
\end{proof}

These results give an unconditional endpoint size obstruction
for the constructed prime classes, stronger than their earlier
infinitely-often $X^{1/2-\epsilon}$ lower bounds.
The multiplicity parameter supplies a lower count cost,
not an independently proved upper bound on zero multiplicity.
The Euler and prime-class conclusions are ordinary full proofs;
only the two positive boundary-summability statements are newly
Lean checked. No new independent count upper estimate is
installed, and the certified zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.
No established literature priority is asserted.

\subsection{The independent arithmetic count problem}
\label{sec:independent-computable-prime-count}

Computability decides membership at each integer cutoff; it does
not give an asymptotic count bound. We now retain the signed
optimizer in an arithmetic form, without inserting the
$\Theta_\zeta$-dependent estimate used in
\eqref{eq:single-prime-full-signed-upper-bound}.

Take a sufficiently late admitted block, with $u=2^j$,
$k=k_j$, $h=h_{j,k}$ and cells $I=[a,a+h)$.
Here $h$ divides $u$, so there is no unaccounted terminal cell.
For the exact-moment problem on $I$ write $H_I=\nu(I)$,
$C_I=\#S_I$, and let $E_I$ be the minimum overweight.
Its minimum deficit is $D_I^{\rm ex}=E_I+C_I-H_I$.
The rounded algorithm has
\begin{equation}
 N_j^*\le\sum_I D_I^{\rm ex}+O(u^{-3+1/k}).
 \label{eq:independent-count-exact-consumer}
\end{equation}
Indeed its overweight is no larger than the exact optimum,
and its zeroth moment has the already proved total tolerance.
The exact dual, after substituting $Q=1-P$, is
\begin{equation}
 D_I^{\rm ex}
 =\max_{\substack{\deg Q<k\\Q(p)\ge0\ (p\in S_I)}}
       \left(\sum_{p\in S_I}\min\{Q(p),1\}
                                    -\int_I Q\,d\nu\right).
 \label{eq:independent-count-deficit-dual}
\end{equation}
This is the preceding finite LP duality, since
$(1-Q)_+=1-\min\{Q,1\}$ at the prime nodes.
An attaining polynomial can be chosen with
$\|Q-1\|_I\le64B_{k-1}$, using the proved positive-objective
norming and $P=0$ when $E_I=0$.

For any such tuple put $T(x)=\min\{1,\max\{0,Q_I(x)\}\}$ on
its cell, and $T=0$ outside $[u,2u)$.
Use the right cell at every shared endpoint.
Define the complete signed terms
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal A_j(T)&=\sum_{p\in S_j}T(p)-\int_u^{2u}T\,d\nu,\\
 \mathcal B_j(Q)&=\sum_I\int_I(Q_I-1)_+\,d\nu,\qquad
 \mathcal N_j(Q)=\sum_I\int_I(Q_I)_-\,d\nu .
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:independent-count-clipped-ledger}
\end{equation}
The objective in \eqref{eq:independent-count-deficit-dual},
summed over cells, is exactly
$\mathcal A_j(T)-\mathcal B_j(Q)+\mathcal N_j(Q)$.
In particular $Q_I=1$ retains
$\mathcal A_j(1)=\#S_j-\nu([u,2u))$ and both penalties vanish.
That constant row cannot be removed by centering the other
polynomials.

Here the continuum negativity really is a smaller error.
Let $G$ bound the first and last prime's distances to the
cell endpoints and all successive prime gaps in the block.
The proved prime-mesh input gives $G\ll u^{2/3}$ for all
sufficiently large $u$. If
$Q(a+hy)=\sum_{r=0}^{d}c_ry^r$, $d<k$, set
$A(Q)=\sum_r|c_r|$. Then
$|Q'|\le dA(Q)/h$ and $|Q''|\le d^2A(Q)/h^2$.
Between successive prime nodes $b,c$, where $Q(b),Q(c)\ge0$,
linear interpolation gives
\[
 Q(x)\ge-\tfrac12\|Q''\|_I(x-b)(c-x).
\]
Its negative integral is at most
$\|Q''\|_I(c-b)^3/12$.
At each end, the derivative bound gives at most
$\|Q'\|_I G^2/2$. Since $\nu'(x)\le1/\log u$, summing these
pieces, including both endpoints, proves
\begin{equation}
 \int_I(Q)_-\,d\nu
 \le \frac{A(Q)(d+d^2/12)G^2}{h\log u}.
 \label{eq:independent-count-negative-gap-bound}
\end{equation}
A constant polynomial has no negative part here.
For $d\ge1$, Lagrange interpolation at $0,1/d,\ldots,1$
bounds its coefficient sum by
\[
 A(Q)\le \|Q\|_I\,4^d d^d/d! .
\]
Indeed each numerator has coefficient sum at most $2^d$,
and the sum of reciprocal absolute denominators is
$d^d2^d/d!$.
The norming bound, $k\le\sqrt j$, and this explicit inequality
give $A(Q)k^2=\exp(O(k\log(k+1)))=u^{o(1)}$ uniformly
for all the retained tuples.
There are $u/h$ cells and $h\ge\tfrac12u^{1-1/k}$.
Consequently the full, uniform correction is
\begin{equation}
                 \mathcal N_j(Q)\ll u^{1/3+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:independent-count-subroot-negativity}
\end{equation}
Node positivity has not been confused with continuum positivity.

We next expand the remaining main term with every divisor sign.
For integer $n\ge2$,
$\Lambda(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(d)\log(n/d)$.
Also the defining density of $\nu$ gives the absolutely convergent
identity
\[
 \nu'(x)=\frac1{x\log x}
       \sum_{r\ge1}\frac{\mu(r)}r(x^{1/r}-1),\qquad x>1.
\]
To verify it, insert
$1/\zeta(\ell+1)=\sum_{r\ge1}\mu(r)r^{-\ell-1}$
in the defining exponential series and sum over $\ell\ge1$.
Absolute convergence follows from
$(x^{1/r}-1)/r=O_x(r^{-2})$.
Thus, with all endpoints and prime powers retained,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal A_j(T)
 &=\sum_{\substack{d,m\ge1\\u\le dm<2u}}
       \mu(d)\frac{\log m}{\log(dm)}T(dm)\\
 &\quad-\sum_{\substack{r\ge2,\ p\ {\rm prime}\\u\le p^r<2u}}
                         \frac{T(p^r)}r\\
 &\quad-\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{\mu(r)}r
       \int_u^{2u}\frac{x^{1/r}-1}{x\log x}T(x)\,dx .
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:independent-count-full-Mobius-row}
\end{equation}
The first two sums are finite.
For integer $R\ge1$ the last sum's tail has absolute value
at most $(2u)^{1/R}\log(2)/R$, since $0\le T\le1$.
In particular $R=u$ makes its tail $O(u^{-1})$ uniformly
in every adaptive polynomial tuple. This is a universal
truncation estimate, not a finite numerical test.
The reciprocal zeta identity above is used only at the
absolutely convergent integers $\ell+1>1$.

One actual cancellation test shows why the high divisor rows
must stay coupled. Put $T=1$ and take $n=2q\in[u,2u)$
with $q>\sqrt{2u}$ an odd prime.
Keeping only $d\le\sqrt{2u}$ leaves
$\log(2q)-\log q=\log2$ in the numerator.
The retained full row $d=q$ contributes $-\log2$;
$d=2q$ contributes zero. Thus these composite terms cancel
exactly. The positive small-divisor subpacket has size
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{\substack{u/2\le q<u\\q\ {\rm prime}}}
       \frac{\log2}{\log(2q)}
              \sim\frac{\log2}{2}\frac{u}{(\log u)^2}.
 \label{eq:independent-count-semiprime-cross-cancellation}
\end{equation}
Here the omitted finitely many small $u$ are immaterial.
This follows from the ordinary prime number theorem on
$[u/2,u)$ and $\log(2q)=\log u+O(1)$.
It rules out discarding that paired subpacket as a square-root
remainder. It is not a lower bound for the entire high-divisor
sum, which has further signs and cross terms.

The independent RH estimate being attacked is therefore the
uniform signed bound
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{Q}\{\mathcal A_j(T_Q)-\mathcal B_j(Q)\}
                         \ll_\epsilon u^{1/2+\epsilon},
 \label{eq:independent-count-live-arithmetic-estimate}
\end{equation}
over the explicitly bounded, prime-dependent dual tuples above.
Equations~\eqref{eq:independent-count-exact-consumer} and
\eqref{eq:independent-count-subroot-negativity} show that this
estimate would imply the required literal count bound and RH.
All mesh, clipping, rounding and model-series tails now have
displayed costs compatible with that endpoint.
The estimate \eqref{eq:independent-count-live-arithmetic-estimate}
itself has not been proved. The signed expansion and the
evaluated semiprime cancellation do not supply its upper bound.
These are ordinary calculations; no new Lean verification or
zero-free exponent is claimed in this subsection.
The certified boundary remains $7/8$.

\paragraph{What the reverse construction has and has not achieved.}
The independently favorable weighted model has been constructed;
its signal-retention size estimate has not been proved.
Large support by itself cannot replace that weighted estimate.
For example the computable rational perturbation
\[
 \widetilde w_p=w_p^*+2^{-p}\boldsymbol1_{\{w_p^*=0\}}
\]
has support equal to every prime. Its additional Euler factor is
\[
 U(s)=\exp\left(\sum_{p\in\mathcal D_*}
                    2^{-p}[-\log(1-p^{-s})]\right).
\]
The logarithm converges normally on every compact subset of
$\Re s>0$, so $U$ is a holomorphic unit there.
Thus $F_{\widetilde w}=F_*U$ has the same favorable region and
simple pole, without an RH assumption. But exactly
\[
 (\widetilde w_p-1)_+=(w_p^*-1)_+,\qquad
 (1-\widetilde w_p)_+=(1-w_p^*)_+
                       -2^{-p}\boldsymbol1_{\{w_p^*=0\}} .
\]
The overweight condition is unchanged, and the deficit changes
by a summable mass at every positive exponent.
Full support has therefore been obtained without reducing the
unresolved arithmetic burden.
Although the new zero-weight set is empty, its strict-partial
coordinates now contain all of $\mathcal D_*$; the subpower
partial-coordinate hypothesis needed for literal count recovery
is no longer available. The deleted-prime theorem cannot be
applied to this modified model by ignoring that hypothesis.

The original vertex construction does make the deficit
comparable to a literal count, but that comparison is not an
independent upper estimate. For dyadic endpoints $X=2^J$,
complete block mass matching, the finite initial head and the
summable moment errors give
$\sum_{p<X}w_p^*=\nu([1,X])+O(1)$.
The strict-partial support below $X$ is subpower.
Since total overweight is nonnegative, the exact signed ledger
therefore implies, for every $\epsilon>0$,
\[
 \pi_{\mathcal D_*}(X)
       \ge \pi(X)-\nu([1,X])-O_\epsilon(X^\epsilon),
                       \qquad X=2^J .
\]
Thus even its constant row retains an ordinary one-sided
prime-count discrepancy. The other rows add adaptive polynomial
tests and their compensating penalties; their uniform estimate
has not been established.
Compared with the earlier overweight and prime-Poisson energy
criteria, the single class improves the arithmetic formulation
and removes the final order parameter. No better independent
arithmetic bound, and hence no advantage for proving RH or a
smaller zero-free boundary, has yet been demonstrated.

\subsection{What selection by the theta sign can establish}
\label{sec:theta-sign-prime-test}

Here selection of prime factors and selection of prime cutoffs
must be distinguished. The following statements concern the
original full-prime Nicolas quantity
\[
 F(p)=\gamma+\log\log\theta(p)
                  +\sum_{q\le p,\ q\ {\rm prime}}\log(1-1/q).
\]
They do not replace this product by one over the selected class.

For consecutive primes $q<p$, $p\ge5$, put $T=\theta(q)>1$.
The exact increment is
\begin{equation}
 F(p)-F(q)=\int_T^{T+\log p}\frac{dt}{t\log t}
                                      +\log(1-1/p).
 \label{eq:theta-sign-Nicolas-increment}
\end{equation}
If $\theta(p)\le p$, this increment is strictly positive.
Indeed, writing $L=\log p>1$, its integral is at least
$-\log(1-L/p)/L$. The convergent logarithmic series gives
\[
 \frac{-\log(1-L/p)}L
 =\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{L^{m-1}}{mp^m}
 >\sum_{m\ge1}\frac1{mp^m}=-\log(1-1/p).
\]
If instead $\theta(q)\ge p$, the integral in
\eqref{eq:theta-sign-Nicolas-increment} is at most $1/p$,
whereas $\log(1-1/p)<-1/p$, so the increment is negative.
These are directional statements about changes in $F$;
neither gives the absolute inequality $F(p)<0$.

Define the actual prime classes
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 S_+&=\{p\ {\rm prime}:\theta(p)>p\},\\
 U&=\{p\ {\rm prime}:\theta(p)-\log p<p<\theta(p)\}
                                                   \subset S_+ .
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:theta-upcrossing-prime-class}
\end{equation}
The class $U$ consists of upward crossings at the prime jump.
Its membership is computable: both theta values are logarithms
of explicitly computable integer primorials, and neither equals
the integer $p$. Otherwise $e$ would satisfy an integer polynomial
$X^p-N=0$, contrary to its transcendence.
Certified approximations therefore decide both strict signs.

\begin{theorem}[Theta-upcrossing cutoffs retain Nicolas violations]
\label{thm:theta-upcrossing-RH-test}
RH is equivalent to $F(p)<0$ for every sufficiently large
$p\in U$. It is also equivalent to that inequality for every
sufficiently large $p\in S_+$.
If RH is false, there are infinitely many $p\in U$ with
$F(p)>0$. No cardinality or density lower bound for $U$ is
assumed in this statement.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under RH the established Nicolas inequality holds at all
sufficiently large cutoffs, so it holds on both selected classes.

Suppose RH is false. The proved two-sided excursion theorem
for $K$ gives positive values arbitrarily far out and negative
values arbitrarily far out. Consequently $\{x:K(x)>0\}$ has
infinitely many bounded positive components tending to infinity.
On the closure of each component, continuity gives an interior
positive maximum. Such a maximum cannot lie strictly between
primes: there $\theta(t)=c$ is constant and
$K'(t)=(t-c)\kappa(t)$ has either a constant sign or changes
from negative to positive, giving a minimum.
Thus a maximum occurs at a prime $p$.
Its one-sided derivative signs imply
$\theta(p)-\log p\le p\le\theta(p)$.
The non-equality just proved makes both inequalities strict,
so $p\in U$ and $K(p)>0$.

On $U$ the overshoot obeys $0<\theta(p)-p<\log p$.
The exact nonlinear correction therefore has the unconditional
Taylor bound
\[
 0\le D(p)\le\frac{\log p+1}{2p^2}.
\]
The positive prime-power tail is larger than this correction.
Indeed PNT gives at least $p/(3\log p)$ primes in $(p,2p]$
for all sufficiently large $p$. Each contributes at least
$1/(2q^2)\ge1/(8p^2)$ to $A(p)$, whence
\[
 A(p)\ge\frac1{24p\log p}>D(p)
\]
eventually. The exact identity $F=K-D+A$ now yields
$F(p)>K(p)>0$ at the infinitely many maximizing primes.
This contradicts the proposed eventual inequality on $U$,
and also contradicts it on $S_+$ since $U\subset S_+$.
\end{proof}

This is a qualitative cutoff-retention theorem, not an
independent proof of the inequality on those cutoffs.
In particular the theta-positive class has not been shown
to satisfy the RH condition unconditionally.
The earlier quantitative excursion proof uses maxima of
$K(x)-A_0x^{-b}$ and a wider theta-error band; the present
unweighted component maxima give the exact logarithmic band
but do not retain a claimed power-size amplitude.
No restricted moving-rank coefficient criterion follows here
without checking its comparison remainder against that amplitude.
The inputs are the existing Nicolas identities and excursions;
no established literature priority or new Lean check is claimed.

For selection inside an Euler product there is a further
hypothesis to keep visible. If an unweighted prime subset $S$
had a zero-free continuation of
$\prod_{p\in S}(1-p^{-s})^{-1}$ on $\Re s>1/2$, apart from
a simple pole of order one at $1$, the existing one-sided
transfer theorem would already imply RH: its weights are
bounded by the reference capacity $1$, so its overweight
is identically zero. No separate cardinality lemma is needed
under those full hypotheses.
Theta-sign selection has not proved them.
The weighted model avoids this conclusion precisely by
leaving an unestimated overweight; its prescribed zero-free
continuation does not itself provide an arithmetic saving.

\subsection{A greedy prime carrier with its inequality built in}
\label{sec:greedy-prime-cap}

A direct selection inside the prime measure avoids testing the
desired Nicolas sign at each member. List all primes increasingly
as $p_1,p_2,\ldots$. Starting with $A_0=M_0=0$, retain $p_j$
in $\mathcal S$ exactly when $A_{j-1}+\log p_j\le p_j$.
On retention put $A_j=A_{j-1}+\log p_j$, $M_j=M_{j-1}$;
on rejection put $A_j=A_{j-1}$, $M_j=M_{j-1}+\log p_j$.
Let $\mathcal D$ be the rejected prime class and write
\[
 \theta_{\mathcal S}(x)=A_{\pi(x)},\qquad
 M(x)=\theta_{\mathcal D}(x)=M_{\pi(x)},\qquad
 N_{\mathcal D}(x)=\#(\mathcal D\cap[2,x]).
\]
This is a fixed class, not a new choice at each cutoff.
Every step is decidable by certified logarithms: equality
$\log N=p_j$ with an integer primorial subproduct $N$ is
impossible by transcendence of $e$.

\begin{theorem}[Greedy cap and optimal deletion cost]
\label{thm:greedy-prime-cap}
The selected class satisfies, unconditionally,
$\theta_{\mathcal S}(x)\le x$ for every real $x\ge2$.
Put
\begin{equation}
 R(x)=\max\bigl(\{0\}\cup
       \{\theta(p)-p:\ p\le x,\ p\ {\rm prime}\}\bigr).
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-regulator}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
                     R(x)\le M(x)\le R(x)+\log x .
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-regulator-bound}
\end{equation}
For any other fixed prime class $\mathcal T$ satisfying
$\theta_{\mathcal T}(x)\le x$ for every $x\ge2$, its deleted
class $\mathcal T^c$ (complement inside the primes) obeys
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 M(x)&\le\theta_{\mathcal T^c}(x)+\log x,\\
 N_{\mathcal D}(x)&\le
    \#(\mathcal T^c\cap[2,x])\qquad(x\ge2).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-count-optimality}
\end{equation}
Thus the fixed greedy carrier minimizes the number of deletions
exactly at every cutoff among every class with this cap.
Moreover $\theta_{\mathcal S}(x)\sim x$ and
$\#(\mathcal S\cap[2,x])\sim x/\log x$ unconditionally.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The invariant $A_j\le p_j$ follows immediately by induction:
retention enforces it, while rejection preserves $A_{j-1}\le p_{j-1}<p_j$.
It holds between primes as well, proving the selected inequality.

Put $E_j=\theta(p_j)-p_j$ and
$R_j=\max(0,E_1,\ldots,E_j)$. The selection rule is exactly
\begin{equation}
 M_j=
 \begin{cases}
 M_{j-1},&E_j\le M_{j-1},\\
 M_{j-1}+\log p_j,&E_j>M_{j-1}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-exact-recursion}
\end{equation}
At a rejected prime, the cap invariant gives
$M_j\ge E_j$, while the rejection test gives
$M_j<E_j+\log p_j$. At a retained prime,
$E_j\le M_j=M_{j-1}$. Induction, using the increasing prime
weights $\log p_j$, therefore gives
$R_j\le M_j\le R_j+\log p_j$ in both cases.
This proves \eqref{eq:greedy-prime-regulator-bound} at primes
and hence at all real cutoffs.

For any other capped class,
$\theta_{\mathcal T^c}(t)=\theta(t)-\theta_{\mathcal T}(t)
\ge\theta(t)-t$. Its left side is nondecreasing and nonnegative,
so $\theta_{\mathcal T^c}(x)\ge R(x)$.
This proves the first optimality inequality.
For the sharper count comparison, apply
Theorem~\ref{thm:ordered-stream-greedy-optimality} to the actual
ordered prime stream, with weight $w_j=\log p_j$ and budget $p_j$.
The weights are nonnegative and increasing. Membership in any
competing capped class is a feasible selection at every prime
prefix, because its retained logarithmic sum is at most $p_j$.
The theorem therefore gives
$\#(\mathcal T\cap[2,x])\le\#(\mathcal S\cap[2,x])$.
Taking complements inside the same finite prime prefix proves
the exact deletion-count inequality. No logarithmic overhead
is needed.

Finally PNT gives $R(x)=o(x)$: split the defining maximum
into a fixed initial segment and a tail on which
$|\theta(t)-t|\le\epsilon t$.
Thus $M(x)=o(x)$ and $\theta_{\mathcal S}(x)=\theta(x)-M(x)\sim x$.
For the retained count, use the direct squeeze
\[
 \theta_{\mathcal S}(x)
 \le \#(\mathcal S\cap[2,x])\log x
 \le \pi(x)\log x .
\]
The lower bound follows term by term from $\log p\le\log x$,
and the upper bound from inclusion in the full prime prefix.
Divide by $x$ and use the theta and prime-count forms of PNT.
This proves the retained-prime count asymptotic.
\end{proof}

The complete statement of Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-cap},
including both density limits at real cutoffs and comparison
with every competing capped class, is now formalized by
\[
 \texttt{greedyPrimeClass\_\allowbreak complete\_\allowbreak cap}.
\]
The exact identification of the regulator with the displayed
finite maximum is checked by
\[
 \texttt{greedyPrimeRegulatorReal\_\allowbreak le\_\allowbreak iff}.
\]
The proof transfers actual prime indices to the literal filtered
prime sums and counts, including natural cutoffs zero and one,
and uses the established unconditional theta and count PNT.
No RH assumption is used.
The preceding certified-logarithm membership algorithm remains
a separate formalization obligation; the maintained class here
is defined by the exact real comparison.

The independent favorable condition can be written in the exact
integral form of the earlier RH criterion:
\begin{equation}
 K_{\mathcal S}(x)=
    \int_x^\infty(\theta_{\mathcal S}(t)-t)\kappa(t)\,dt\le0,
 \qquad
 K(x)=K_{\mathcal S}(x)+\int_x^\infty M(t)\kappa(t)\,dt.
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-K-transfer}
\end{equation}
Both integrals converge unconditionally.
For example the quantitative PNT error
$O(t\exp(-c\sqrt{\log t}))$ bounds its running maximum by
$O(t\exp(-c'\sqrt{\log t}))$ for a possibly smaller $c'>0$.
The integrand on the selected class is nonpositive pointwise.
No RH hypothesis supplies this inequality.


The same selected class satisfies an actual calibrated
Nicolas product inequality, not only the integral sign.
Define its finite missing-product constant and its error by
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 C_{\mathcal D}
   &=\sum_{p\in\mathcal D}-\log(1-1/p),\\
 F_{\mathcal S}(x)
   &=\gamma-C_{\mathcal D}+\log\log\theta_{\mathcal S}(x)
       +\sum_{\substack{p\le x\\p\in\mathcal S}}\log(1-1/p).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-calibrated-Nicolas}
\end{equation}
The constant is finite by the quantitative PNT regulator bound;
the error is defined once $\theta_{\mathcal S}(x)>1$.
The subtraction of $C_{\mathcal D}$ is essential.

\begin{theorem}[The selected class already has the product sign]
\label{prop:greedy-prime-product-sign}
There are constants $c_0>0$ and $X_0$ such that, unconditionally,
\begin{equation}
 F_{\mathcal S}(x)\le-\frac{c_0}{x}<0\qquad(x\ge X_0).
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-product-sign}
\end{equation}
The exact difference from the original all-prime error is
\begin{equation}
 F(x)-F_{\mathcal S}(x)
 =\log\log\theta(x)-\log\log\theta_{\mathcal S}(x)
       +\sum_{\substack{p>x\\p\in\mathcal D}}-\log(1-1/p).
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-product-signal}
\end{equation}
Both terms on the right are nonnegative, and both must be
estimated in a zero-signal transfer.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $D_{\mathcal S}$ and $A_{\mathcal S}$ for the earlier
nonlinear correction and prime tail with $\theta_{\mathcal S}$
and the selected primes in place of $\theta$ and all primes.
The calibrated exact identity is
\[
 F_{\mathcal S}=K_{\mathcal S}-D_{\mathcal S}+A_{\mathcal S}.
\]
Indeed subtract the original identity, and use
\[
 \int_x^\infty M(t)\kappa(t)\,dt
 =\frac{M(x)}{x\log x}
                  +\sum_{\substack{p>x\\p\in\mathcal D}}\frac1p,
\]
which is partial summation against $1/(t\log t)$.
The nonlinear corrections supply the difference of the two
$\log\log$ terms, and the remaining prime tail supplies exactly
the quadratic part of the missing logarithmic product.
This verifies the normalization as well as
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-product-signal}.
Concavity gives $D_{\mathcal S}\ge0$, while PNT gives
$0\le A_{\mathcal S}(x)\le A(x)=O(1/(x\log x))$.

There is also a definite negative area in $K_{\mathcal S}$.
Choose a constant $C\ge1$ with $\pi(2x)\le Cx/\log x$
eventually, and put $h=\log x/(8C)$.
Inside $[x,2x]$, the union of intervals $[p,p+h)$
over all primes $p\le2x$ has measure at most $x/8$.
At every remaining point $t$, its last full prime is at
most $t-h$. Since the selected theta function is bounded
by the last selected prime, the cap gives
$t-\theta_{\mathcal S}(t)\ge h$ there.
Also $\kappa(t)\ge1/(8x^2\log x)$ on $[x,2x]$ eventually.
Consequently
\[
 -K_{\mathcal S}(x)
 \ge\int_x^{2x}(t-\theta_{\mathcal S}(t))\kappa(t)\,dt
 \ge\frac{7}{512C x}.
\]
The pointwise cap makes the omitted part of the integral
nonnegative. The $O(1/(x\log x))$ upper bound on $A_{\mathcal S}$
is eventually smaller than half this supply.
The calibrated identity proves
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-product-sign}, with a smaller $c_0>0$.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[The size condition retains every off-line zero]
\label{thm:greedy-prime-RH-size}
For every fixed $1/2\le\eta<1$, the following are equivalent:
\[
 \zeta(s)\ne0\quad(\Re s>\eta),\qquad
 N_{\mathcal D}(X)\ll_\epsilon X^{\eta+\epsilon}
                         \quad\hbox{for every }\epsilon>0 .
\]
Here the zero statement concerns nontrivial zeros; the pole at $1$
is not a zero. In particular the same explicit selected class
has an independently proved favorable inequality, and
\begin{equation}
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \#\{p\le X:p\ {\rm prime},\ p\notin\mathcal S\}
             \ll_\epsilon X^{1/2+\epsilon}
                   \quad\hbox{for every }\epsilon>0.
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-RH-size}
\end{equation}
Under RH the stronger count
$N_{\mathcal D}(X)=O(\sqrt X\log X)$ follows from the usual
Chebyshev error bound.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose first that $\Theta_\zeta\le\eta$.
For $\eta<\kappa<1$, the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-error-zero-supremum} gives
$|\theta(t)-t|=O_\kappa(t^\kappa\log^2t)$.
The regulator bound and splitting the rejected primes
below and above $\sqrt X$ give
\[
 M(X)=O_\kappa(X^\kappa\log^2X),\qquad
 N_{\mathcal D}(X)=O_\kappa(X^\kappa\log X).
\]
Taking $\kappa$ strictly between $\eta$ and $\eta+\epsilon$
proves the claimed bound; when $\eta+\epsilon\ge1$,
the elementary count bound suffices.
Under RH insert instead
$|\theta(t)-t|=O(\sqrt t\log^2t)$ to obtain the stronger count.

Conversely suppose the stated count holds, and fix
$0<\epsilon<1-\eta$. Then
$M(t)\le\log t\,N_{\mathcal D}(t)
=O_\epsilon(t^{\eta+\epsilon}\log t)$.
The complete positive correction in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-K-transfer} consequently satisfies
\begin{equation}
 0\le K(x)-K_{\mathcal S}(x)
 \le C_\epsilon
       \frac{x^{\eta+\epsilon-1}}{1-\eta-\epsilon}.
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-zero-signal-error}
\end{equation}
The denominator is positive on the stated parameter domain.
The same count controls both terms in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-product-signal}: since
$\theta_{\mathcal S}(x)\sim\theta(x)\sim x$, its clock term is
$O(M(x)/(x\log x))$, and partial summation bounds the entire
missing-product tail by
$O_\epsilon(x^{\eta+\epsilon-1}/(1-\eta-\epsilon))$.
Consequently $0\le F(x)-F_{\mathcal S}(x)$ has this same
upper order. Positive excursions of the original Nicolas error
are therefore preserved as well, including the full normalization
and both deletion corrections.
If there were a zero of real part $\beta>\eta$,
choose also $\epsilon<\beta-\eta$ and
$1-\beta<b<1-\eta-\epsilon\le1/2$.
The unconditional positive excursion theorem gives
$\limsup x^bK(x)=+\infty$.
But $K_{\mathcal S}\le0$ and the displayed correction give
$\limsup x^bK(x)\le0$, since
$b+\eta+\epsilon-1<0$. This is a contradiction.
The zero-free implication is therefore established without
using an RH-dependent wave under the bad-zero hypothesis.
\end{proof}

This carrier supplies the two requested obligations separately.
The cap and $K_{\mathcal S}\le0$ are unconditional properties
of the constructed class. The root-size deletion estimate
would make it large enough to retain every off-line zero signal.
The independent bound proved so far is only
$N_{\mathcal D}(X)\ll_\epsilon X^{7/8+\epsilon}$, from the
installed boundary and the preceding theorem.
An improvement to $X^{3/4+\epsilon}$ would give the boundary
$3/4$ by the same complete consumer; the square-root target gives RH.

The actual size attack is now the concrete positive-record problem
\[
 \max_{\substack{p\le X\\p\ {\rm prime}}}(\theta(p)-p)_+
                  \ll_\epsilon X^{\eta+\epsilon}.
\]
The recursion and optimality theorem show exactly why changing
the capped selection cannot by itself improve its exponent:
every other capped prime class must delete at least this
running positive mass, and the greedy algorithm pays at most
one prime weight more. Every rejected prime is a strict new
positive record of $\theta(p)-p$, because its rejection requires
$\theta(p)-p>M(p^-)\ge R(p^-)$. In particular, if $q<p$
are consecutive full primes and $p$ is rejected, then
$p-q<\log p$. This necessary short-gap condition is insufficient
for the desired count: it discards the record-height comparison,
and supplies no power-saving upper bound on the number of
rejections. The retained exact target is the quantized record
process \eqref{eq:greedy-prime-exact-recursion}, not a count of
all short prime gaps. No independent improvement of the
positive-record bound has been proved here.
The construction simplifies the arithmetic object and proves
optimal selection; it is not claimed to make its remaining
size bound easier than the earlier RH criterion.
The cap, regulator, exact count optimality, and density claims in
Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-cap} have the Lean proofs identified above.
The product-sign and zero-signal transfer theorems in this subsection
remain ordinary proofs with separate formalization obligations.
No established originality priority is asserted.

\subsection{The signed energy of the positive-record process}
\label{sec:prime-record-energy}

The count attack must retain the stopped prime weights,
rather than assume an independent prime-gap law.
Put $E(t)=\theta(t)-t$, retain the running positive record
$R$ from \eqref{eq:greedy-prime-regulator}, and let
$\Delta R(p)=R(p)-R(p^-)\ge0$.
For every real $X\ge2$ define the finite signed quantity
\[
 \mathcal C_R(X)=
   \sum_{\substack{p\le X\\p\ {\rm prime}}}R(p^-)\log p
                              -\int_2^X R(t)\,dt,
 \qquad
 Q_R(X)=\sum_{\substack{p\le X\\p\ {\rm prime}}}(\Delta R(p))^2 .
\]
Then the exact energy identity is
\begin{equation}
 R(X)^2=2\mathcal C_R(X)+2R(X)(R(X)-E(X))+Q_R(X).
 \label{eq:prime-record-energy}
\end{equation}
To prove it, integrate the finite step function product $RE$.
The continuous part of $dE$ is $-dt$, its prime jump is
$\log p$, and $R$ has only prime jumps.
The right-endpoint form of integration by parts gives
\[
 R(X)E(X)=\mathcal C_R(X)+
       \sum_{\substack{p\le X\\p\ {\rm prime}}}R(p)\Delta R(p).
\]
Here $E(p)=R(p)$ whenever $\Delta R(p)>0$, and
$R(2^-)=0$ removes the initial endpoint.
Finally
\(2\sum_{\substack{p\le X\\p\ {\rm prime}}}
       R(p)\Delta R(p)=R(X)^2+Q_R(X)\).
All sums retain their finite prime support.

At a positive record prime $X$, the terminal correction vanishes:
\begin{equation}
 R(X)^2=2\mathcal C_R(X)+Q_R(X),
 \qquad 0\le Q_R(X)\le R(X)\log X.
 \label{eq:prime-record-energy-at-record}
\end{equation}
The last bound uses \(0\le\Delta R(p)\le\log p\) and
\(\sum_{\substack{p\le X\\p\ {\rm prime}}}\Delta R(p)=R(X)\).
At these record endpoints the complete signed bounds are
\[
 \frac{R(X)^2-R(X)\log X}{2}\le\mathcal C_R(X)
                                      \le\frac{R(X)^2}{2}.
\]
For \(A\ge0\), an independent bound \(\mathcal C_R(X)\le A\) implies
\[
 R(X)\le\log X+\sqrt{2A}.
\]
More generally, suppose \(a>0\), \(K\ge0\), \(X_0\ge2\), and
\(\mathcal C_R(p)\le Kp^{2a}\) at every strict record prime
\(p\ge X_0\), where strict means \(\Delta R(p)>0\).
Then the full bound, including all nonrecord cutoffs and the initial
range, is
\[
 R(X)\le
   \left(R(X_0)+\frac1a+\sqrt{2K}\right)X^a
                         \qquad(X\ge2).
\]
Indeed \(\log t\le t^a/a\) for \(t>0\), and the record-endpoint
root bound gives \(R(p)\le(a^{-1}+\sqrt{2K})p^a\).
Every positive running maximum is attained at a strict record jump.
If that prime is below \(X_0\), its height is bounded by \(R(X_0)\);
otherwise the preceding estimate applies. Monotonicity of \(t^a\)
and \(X^a\ge1\) give the displayed uniform constant.
The independent signed-energy hypothesis is retained throughout.

Consequently an independent estimate
$\mathcal C_R(X)\ll_\epsilon X^{3/2+\epsilon}$ at all sufficiently
large positive record primes would give
$R(X)\ll_\epsilon X^{3/4+\epsilon}$, hence the boundary $3/4$
through the complete deletion consumer.
The corresponding root target is
$\mathcal C_R(X)\ll_\epsilon X^{1+\epsilon}$.
The installed input supplies only
$R(X)\ll_\epsilon X^{7/8+\epsilon}$ and thus the record-endpoint
bound $\mathcal C_R(X)\ll_\epsilon X^{7/4+\epsilon}$.
Constants are independent of the record cutoff, but may depend
on $\epsilon$ and the stated source estimate.

This is a signed calculation and a checked failed application,
not an improved estimate. The prime-divisibility probability
law samples integers at a fixed prime cutoff; it does not make
the successive prime gaps, stopped at their positive records,
a mean-zero independent process. Thus its existing independence
hypothesis does not apply to $\mathcal C_R$.
Outside record endpoints, the terminal term in
\eqref{eq:prime-record-energy} must also remain present.
No new zero-free exponent follows from this identity.

The entire signed energy calculation and its quantitative record-to-cutoff
consumer are now Lean-checked.
\texttt{primeRecordEnergy\_\allowbreak identity} uses the actual finite
prime sum and ordinary real interval integral for every \(X\ge2\).
\texttt{primeRecordJump\_\allowbreak left\_\allowbreak limit} identifies
each prime jump with its one-sided limit; the first-prime left limit is
zero.
\texttt{greedyPrimeRegulatorReal\_\allowbreak integral} evaluates the
actual record integral by the finite sum of its right-continuous jumps.
The two finite product identities and the record-endpoint variation
bound are compiled with their full support.
\texttt{primeRecordEnergy\_\allowbreak root\_\allowbreak bound} and
\texttt{primeRecordEnergy\_\allowbreak power\_\allowbreak bound\_\allowbreak explicit}
retain the independent energy estimate.
\texttt{primeRecordEnergy\_\allowbreak all\_\allowbreak cutoff\_\allowbreak bound}
proves the displayed uniform constant, using strict record primes
and the finite initial record.
All proof owners are maintained, exported, and registered by exact name
for recurring foundational-axiom checks.
The separate zero-response theorems are required for the zero-free
interpretation; no independent power-saving estimate for
\(\mathcal C_R\) is supplied by these identities.

\subsection{An extremal probability law among capped prime classes}
\label{sec:capped-prime-probability-extremum}

The count comparison in Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-cap}
has an exact strengthening, with consequences for every finite
decreasing local probability law.

\begin{theorem}[Rank domination and exact optimality]
\label{thm:capped-prime-rank-extremum}
Fix a real cutoff $x\ge2$, and let $\mathcal T$ be any prime
class with $\theta_{\mathcal T}(t)\le t$ for $2\le t\le x$.
List the selected primes through $x$ as
$s_1<\cdots<s_a$ for the fixed greedy class $\mathcal S$,
and $t_1<\cdots<t_b$ for $\mathcal T$.
Then
\begin{equation}
                  a\ge b,\qquad s_i\le t_i\quad(1\le i\le b).
 \label{eq:capped-prime-rank-extremum}
\end{equation}
In particular the greedy class minimizes the number of deleted
primes exactly at every cutoff.
For every nonnegative nonincreasing function $w$ on primes,
\[
 \sum_{\substack{p\le x\\p\in\mathcal S}}w(p)
       \ge\sum_{\substack{p\le x\\p\in\mathcal T}}w(p).
\]
If $w$ is positive and strictly decreasing, equality is possible
only when the two selected prime sets through $x$ coincide.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Induct on the competitor's rank $i$.
Suppose $s_j\le t_j$ for $j<i$ and the greedy class has not
made its $i$th selection by $t_i$.
It has then selected exactly $i-1$ primes before $t_i$.
Since $\log$ is increasing,
\[
 A_{\pi(t_i)-1}+\log t_i
   =\sum_{j<i}\log s_j+\log t_i
   \le\sum_{j\le i}\log t_j
   =\theta_{\mathcal T}(t_i)\le t_i .
\]
The greedy rule therefore retains $t_i$, a contradiction.
For $i=1$ the same proof has empty preceding sums.
This proves every required rank and hence $a\ge b$.
The weighted comparison follows from $w(s_i)\ge w(t_i)$
on the matched ranks and the nonnegative unmatched terms.
For a positive strictly decreasing $w$, distinct selected
sets give either an unmatched positive term or a strict
inequality on at least one matched rank.
\end{proof}

The entire statement of Theorem~\ref{thm:capped-prime-rank-extremum}
is now formalized, including its local cap hypothesis
$2\le t\le x$, arbitrary decreasing weights, and the strict
equality criterion. Its complete certificate is
\[
 \texttt{greedyPrimeClass\_\allowbreak local\_\allowbreak rank\_\allowbreak complete}.
\]
The rank of a prime in a class is the number of class primes
strictly below it. The direct comparison at any specified
ordinal rank is checked by
\[
 \texttt{greedyPrimeClass\_\allowbreak local\_\allowbreak rank\_\allowbreak at}.
\]
The formal proof truncates only the competitor, so the
greedy class remains fixed at every cutoff.
The weighted comparison retains the exact signed prefix
ledger; equality with a positive strictly decreasing weight
forces all prefix count surpluses to vanish and hence the
two finite prime sets to coincide.

\begin{theorem}[Stochastic and coefficient extremality]
\label{thm:capped-prime-probability-extremum}
Let $\eta(p)\in[0,1]$ be nonincreasing on primes.
At a fixed cutoff $x$, let $K_{\mathcal V,x}$ denote the sum
of independent Bernoulli variables of success probabilities
$\eta(p)$ over the finite set $p\le x$, $p\in\mathcal V$.
For every capped competitor $\mathcal T$ as above and every
integer rank $r\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb P(K_{\mathcal S,x}\ge r)
                  \ge\mathbb P(K_{\mathcal T,x}\ge r).
 \label{eq:capped-prime-tail-extremum}
\end{equation}
The same comparison holds for expectations of every increasing
function on $\{0,\ldots,a\}$.
It implies
\[
 \mathbb E z^{K_{\mathcal S,x}}\ge\mathbb E z^{K_{\mathcal T,x}}
       \quad(z\ge1),\qquad
 \mathbb E z^{K_{\mathcal S,x}}\le\mathbb E z^{K_{\mathcal T,x}}
       \quad(0\le z\le1).
\]
Separately, for every nonnegative nonincreasing finite odds
function $v(p)$, one has coefficientwise domination
\begin{equation}
 [z^r]\prod_{\substack{p\le x\\p\in\mathcal S}}(1+v(p)z)
       \ge
 [z^r]\prod_{\substack{p\le x\\p\in\mathcal T}}(1+v(p)z)
                           \qquad(r\ge0).
 \label{eq:capped-prime-coefficient-extremum}
\end{equation}
No comparison of individual normalized point masses or of
peak heights is asserted.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The rank theorem gives $\eta(s_i)\ge\eta(t_i)$ for $i\le b$.
Pad the competitor's array by $a-b$ zeros.
Increasing one coordinate from $u$ to $v\ge u$ changes
the tail at rank $r+1$ by exactly
\[
 (v-u)\mathbb P(K=r)\ge0,
\]
where $K$ is the sum over the other coordinates.
This follows by conditioning on that coordinate:
the new tail is $(1-v)\mathbb P(K\ge r+1)+v\mathbb P(K\ge r)$.
The rank-zero tail is always one.
Iterating the replacement over the padded arrays proves
\eqref{eq:capped-prime-tail-extremum}, including coordinates
equal to zero or one.
For either finite law, use the common support bound $a$.
The exact finite Abel identity is
\[
 \mathbb E\phi(K)=\phi(0)+
   \sum_{k=0}^{a-1}\bigl(\phi(k+1)-\phi(k)\bigr)
                                      \mathbb P(K\ge k+1).
\]
The boundary term vanishes because $K\le a$; for the competitor,
all tails above its smaller support $b$ vanish as well.
Every difference in this sum is nonnegative, whereas $\phi(0)$
may have either sign and cancels between the two laws.
This proves the expectation assertion.
The two moment comparisons follow from the monotonicity of
$k\mapsto z^k$ in their stated domains, retaining $z=0,1$.

For the coefficient assertion, each matched factor has
$v(s_i)\ge v(t_i)\ge0$, so replacing the matched factors
increases every coefficient.
The remaining factors have nonnegative coefficients and
constant term one; appending them cannot decrease a coefficient.
This proves the assertion also for ranks above the competitor's
support and at rank zero.
\end{proof}

For the ordinary integer-divisibility law $\eta(p)=1/p$,
