the fixed-cutoff Bernoulli law is the finite prime-divisibility density.
More explicitly, put \(V_x=\{p\le x:p\text{ prime},\ p\in\mathcal V\}\)
and \(m=|V_x|\). For every integer \(N>0\) and every \(r\ge0\),
\[
 \left|\frac1N\#\{1\le n\le N:
             \#\{p\in V_x:p\mid n\}\ge r\}
       -\mathbb P(K_{\mathcal V,x}\ge r)\right|
 \le \frac{3^m}{N}.
\]
The bound follows from the proved coefficient error at parameter zero,
followed by summation over the finite support of the tail indicator.
Thus the greedy capped carrier maximizes every limiting upper-tail
density. The limit is over sampled integers with \(x\) fixed;
no uniform growing-cutoff sampling assertion is used.
The coefficient conclusion applies directly to \(v(p)=1/(p-1)\).
For affine odds \(v(p)=\rho/(p+\beta)\), it applies when
\(\rho>0\) and \(\beta>-2\).
The associated probability law
\(\eta(p)=\rho/(p+\beta+\rho)\) obeys the full stochastic comparison
for every \(\beta\ge-2\).
These are prime-coordinate laws; no sampling theorem for an affine
polynomial prime-pair family is asserted.

At \(\beta=-2\), only \(p=2\) is forced.
Write \(V_x^\circ=V_x\setminus\{2\}\) and
\[
 A_{\mathcal V,x}
    =\prod_{p\in V_x^\circ}(1-\eta(p))>0,\qquad
 Q_{\mathcal V,x}(z)
    =\prod_{p\in V_x^\circ}\left(1+\frac{\rho}{p-2}z\right).
\]
If \(2\in V_x\), then \(K_{\mathcal V,x}=1+K^\circ_{\mathcal V,x}\)
in distribution and, for every \(r\ge0\),
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P(K_{\mathcal V,x}=0)&=0,\\
 \mathbb P(K_{\mathcal V,x}=r+1)
      &=A_{\mathcal V,x}[z^r]Q_{\mathcal V,x}(z),\\
 \mathbb P(K_{\mathcal V,x}\ge r+1)
      &=\mathbb P(K^\circ_{\mathcal V,x}\ge r).
 \end{aligned}
\]
If \(2\notin V_x\), no shift occurs and
\(\mathbb P(K_{\mathcal V,x}=r)=A_{\mathcal V,x}[z^r]Q_{\mathcal V,x}(z)\).
The fixed greedy class contains \(2\), so its shifted identities apply
at every real cutoff \(x\ge2\).
These identities retain the survival factor, including the empty
remaining product. They do not extend the separate odds-coefficient
order to \(\beta=-2\).

Applying the decreasing weight \(w(p)=-\log(1-1/p)\) shows, at each
finite cutoff, that the greedy class minimizes the missing
logarithmic Euler-product cost among all capped competitors.
The passage to infinity also proves convergence, rather than assuming it
for the greedy class. For each finite set \(A\) of primes outside
\(\mathcal S\), choose a cutoff \(X\) containing \(A\). Nonnegativity and
the exact finite complement identity give
\[
 \sum_{p\in A}w(p)
 \le\sum_{\substack{p\le X,\ p\ {\rm prime}\\p\notin\mathcal S}}w(p)
 \le\sum_{\substack{p\le X,\ p\ {\rm prime}\\p\notin\mathcal T}}w(p)
 \le C_{\mathcal T^c}.
\]
If \(C_{\mathcal T^c}\) is finite, these bounds force convergence of the
nonnegative greedy missing-cost series and give
\(C_{\mathcal D}\le C_{\mathcal T^c}\).
The same proof works for every nonnegative decreasing prime weight
whose missing total for the competitor converges.
This sharpens the earlier additive count comparison to exact
optimality for counts and for all these probability observables.
It does not estimate the optimal count's growth:
the independent $3/4$ and square-root size bounds remain open.

The whole stochastic and coefficient theorem is now Lean-checked.
The actual finite product probability space is constructed from bit
tuples; each tuple has the product of its coordinate success and
failure weights. In
\path{Proof/PrimeClassDeletion/BernoulliFiniteLaw.lean},
these weights are proved nonnegative and normalized, and their
success-count masses equal the existing Bernoulli coefficients.
\texttt{List.bernoulliOutcomeTail\_eq} and
\texttt{List.bernoulliOutcomeExpectation\_eq}
identify its actual tail probabilities and expectations.
The finite generating polynomial is identified with the literal
prime product by
\texttt{primeClassBernoulliMass\_\allowbreak eq\_\allowbreak product\_\allowbreak coeff}.
The assembled certificate
\texttt{greedyPrimeClass\_\allowbreak local\_\allowbreak probability\_\allowbreak complete}
retains every rank, every increasing test on the actual finite
support, both moment ranges, and only the local competing cap
$2\le t\le x$.
Separately,
\texttt{greedyPrimeClass\_\allowbreak local\_\allowbreak odds\_\allowbreak coefficient}
proves the displayed coefficient inequality for the literal
prime products and arbitrary finite nonnegative decreasing odds.
All owners are exported through the prime-deletion assembly.

The whole adjacent ordinary-divisibility and affine application block
is also Lean-checked.
\texttt{greedyPrimeClass\_\allowbreak local\_\allowbreak divisibility\_\allowbreak limits}
proves both actual integer-density limits and their comparison, using
\texttt{ordinaryPrimeTail\_\allowbreak density\_\allowbreak error}'s
explicit \(3^m/N\) error.
\texttt{greedyPrimeClass\_\allowbreak local\_\allowbreak affine\_\allowbreak probability}
and
\texttt{greedyPrimeClass\_\allowbreak local\_\allowbreak affine\_\allowbreak odds\_\allowbreak coefficient}
retain the distinct closed and open parameter domains above.

The exceptional-coordinate certificate
\texttt{affinePrime\allowbreak Endpoint\_\allowbreak rank\_\allowbreak shift}
treats both prime cases. Its shifted and unshifted odds identities retain
the full survival factor.
The actual greedy endpoint is discharged by
\texttt{greedyPrimeClass\_\allowbreak two}
and its endpoint consumers.
All these owners are exported and included by exact declaration name
in the recurring foundational-axiom audit.

The infinite missing-product comparison is now Lean-checked in
\path{Proof/PrimeClassDeletion/CappedPrimeMissingCost.lean}.
The full general consumer
\texttt{greedyPrimeClass\_\allowbreak missing\_\allowbreak weight\_\allowbreak summable}
proves both convergence and the infinite comparison. Its logarithmic
specialization
\texttt{greedyPrimeClass\_\allowbreak missing\_\allowbreak logEulerCost}
has the exact prime filter and class complement in every summand;
cutoffs zero and one are included in the finite proof.
The complete owner is exported and registered for recurring axiom checks.
The signed record-energy identity, actual integral and left limits,
and the complete record-to-cutoff power implication are now Lean-checked
as described above. The independent signed-energy estimate and the
separate zero-response applications remain open obligations.
No established originality priority or new zero-free boundary is asserted.

\subsection{How a prime-counting wave becomes a persistent deletion signal}
\label{sec:greedy-prime-wave-envelope}

The exact regulator permits a more precise statement than a comparison
of growth exponents. It describes what happens to the whole wave,
including its negative phases. The next theorem is conditional on a
specified leading asymptotic for the \emph{entire} prime-counting error;
it does not assert that a single zeta zero supplies that asymptotic.

\begin{theorem}[The deletion envelope of a periodic leading error]
\label{thm:greedy-prime-wave-envelope}
Let $0<\beta<1$, let $T>0$, and let $f:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$
be continuous and $T$-periodic, with $f(v_0)>0$ for some $v_0$.
Suppose, as $x\to\infty$ through real cutoffs, that
\begin{equation}
 \theta(x)-x=x^\beta f(\log x)+o(x^\beta).
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-periodic-error}
\end{equation}
Define
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_{\beta,f}(v)=
   \max\left\{0,\ \sup_{u\ge0}e^{-\beta u}f(v-u)\right\}.
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-wave-envelope}
\end{equation}
Then $\Phi_{\beta,f}$ is continuous and $T$-periodic, and
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 R(x)&=x^\beta\Phi_{\beta,f}(\log x)+o(x^\beta),\\
 M(x)&=x^\beta\Phi_{\beta,f}(\log x)+o(x^\beta),\\
 N_{\mathcal D}(x)
   &=\frac{x^\beta}{\log x}\Phi_{\beta,f}(\log x)
                      +o\left(\frac{x^\beta}{\log x}\right).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-wave-count}
\end{equation}
In particular
\[
 0<f(v_0)e^{-\beta T}\le\Phi_{\beta,f}(v)
                                  \le\|f\|_\infty
 \qquad(v\in\mathbb R),
\]
so $N_{\mathcal D}(x)\asymp x^\beta/\log x$ at \emph{all}
sufficiently large cutoffs, including those on a negative phase of $f$.
Constants may depend on $\beta,T,f$ and the error in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-periodic-error}, but not on $x$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Between primes the function $\theta(t)-t$ decreases. Consequently
its positive running maximum over all $2\le t\le x$ is exactly $R(x)$.
For each $\epsilon>0$ choose $Y_\epsilon$ such that the error in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-periodic-error} is at most
$\epsilon t^\beta$ for $t\ge Y_\epsilon$.
On the remaining bounded initial interval the error is bounded by
a constant $C_\epsilon$.
The maximum of two functions differs by at most their uniform difference.
Replacing $\theta(t)-t$ by $t^\beta f(\log t)$ therefore changes
$R(x)$ by at most $\epsilon x^\beta+C_\epsilon$.
After division by $x^\beta$ this is $o(1)$.

Put $v=\log x$ and $u=\log(x/t)$.
The model maximum divided by $x^\beta$ is
\[
 \max\left\{0,\ \sup_{0\le u\le\log(x/2)}
                           e^{-\beta u}f(v-u)\right\}.
\]
Extending this domain to all $u\ge0$ changes the value by at most
$\|f\|_\infty(2/x)^\beta$.
This proves the first line of \eqref{eq:greedy-prime-wave-count}.
The second follows from $0\le M(x)-R(x)\le\log x$.

Partial summation on the \emph{actual deleted primes} gives
\[
 N_{\mathcal D}(x)=\frac{M(x)}{\log x}
                  +\int_2^x\frac{M(t)}{t\log^2t}\,dt.
\]
Here $M(t)=O(t^\beta)$, and the integral is
$O(x^\beta/\log^2x)$.
For completeness, split at $\sqrt x$:
the initial part is $O(x^{\beta/2})$, while the rest is bounded
by a constant times
$\log^{-2}x\int_{\sqrt x}^x t^{\beta-1}\,dt$.
This proves the count asymptotic, without identifying a mass estimate
with a count estimate.

Continuity follows from the uniform continuity of the periodic function
$f$: the modulus of continuity of every
$v\mapsto e^{-\beta u}f(v-u)$ is bounded by that of $f$,
uniformly in $u\ge0$. Taking a supremum and the positive part preserves
this bound. Periodicity is immediate.
For each $v$, choose $u\in[0,T]$ with
$v-u\equiv v_0\pmod T$.
The corresponding term is at least $e^{-\beta T}f(v_0)>0$,
which proves the uniform lower bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Joint almost-periodic waves]
\label{thm:greedy-prime-joint-wave-envelope}
Let $0<\beta<1$.
The conclusions \eqref{eq:greedy-prime-wave-count} also hold when $f$
is bounded, uniformly continuous and uniformly almost periodic,
provided $f(v_0)>0$ somewhere and
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-periodic-error} holds.
Here uniformly almost periodic means that for every $\epsilon>0$
there is $L>0$ such that every interval of length $L$ contains
a real $\tau$ with
$\sup_v|f(v+\tau)-f(v)|<\epsilon$.
The resulting $\Phi_{\beta,f}$ is uniformly almost periodic,
continuous and bounded away from zero.
In particular this applies to any finite joint wave
$f(v)=\sum_{j=1}^m A_j\cos(\tau_jv+\varphi_j)$ that is positive
somewhere; no common period of the frequencies is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The running-maximum and partial-summation parts of the preceding proof
use boundedness of $f$, not its periodicity.
They therefore give all three asymptotics once
$\Phi_{\beta,f}$ is defined by
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-wave-envelope}.
Uniform continuity again passes to this envelope.
Every $\epsilon$-almost period of $f$ is an
$\epsilon$-almost period of $\Phi_{\beta,f}$: each translated
term $e^{-\beta u}f(v-u)$ changes by at most $\epsilon$,
uniformly for $u\ge0$, and the same is true after taking
the supremum and the positive part.
Thus the envelope is uniformly almost periodic.

Put $a=f(v_0)/2>0$, and choose an almost-period length $L$
for tolerance $a$.
For any $v$, choose an $a$-almost period $\tau$ in
$[v-v_0-L,v-v_0]$ and put $u=v-v_0-\tau$.
Then $0\le u\le L$ and $f(v-u)=f(v_0+\tau)>a$.
Hence $\Phi_{\beta,f}(v)\ge a e^{-\beta L}>0$ uniformly in $v$.

For completeness the finite joint wave satisfies the stated
almost-period condition even with incommensurable frequencies.
Put $h(t)=(e^{i\tau_1t},\ldots,e^{i\tau_mt})$.
Compactness of the product of the $m$ unit circles supplies, for any
$\delta>0$, a finite $\delta$-net for this orbit with centres
$h(t_1),\ldots,h(t_q)$ in the orbit itself.
Let $c=\max_j t_j$ and $L=\max(1,c-\min_j t_j)$.
For any real $b$, some $j$ satisfies
$\max_k|e^{i\tau_k(b+c)}-e^{i\tau_kt_j}|<\delta$.
Then $\tau=b+c-t_j\in[b,b+L]$ and
$\max_k|e^{i\tau_k\tau}-1|<\delta$.
It follows, uniformly in $v$, that
$|f(v+\tau)-f(v)|\le\delta\sum_j|A_j|$.
Choosing $\delta$ sufficiently small proves the required
almost-period condition. Finite joint waves are also bounded and
uniformly continuous, so all hypotheses have been checked.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Explicit response to one leading harmonic]
\label{thm:greedy-prime-cosine-envelope}
Under Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-wave-envelope}'s hypotheses, suppose
$f(v)=A\cos(\tau v+\varphi)$ with $A>0$ and $\tau>0$.
Put $\alpha=\arctan(\beta/\tau)$, $w=\tau v+\varphi$, and let
$d(v)$ be the representative of $w-\alpha$ modulo $2\pi$
in $[0,2\pi)$.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \Phi_{\beta,f}(v)
   &=A\max\left\{\cos w,\
       \cos\alpha\exp\left(-\frac{\beta}{\tau}d(v)\right)\right\},\\
 A\cos\alpha\,e^{-2\pi\beta/\tau}
   &\le\Phi_{\beta,f}(v)\le A.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-cosine-envelope}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $g(u)=e^{-\beta u}\cos(w-\tau u)$ one has
\[
 g'(u)=e^{-\beta u}
             \bigl(\tau\sin(w-\tau u)-\beta\cos(w-\tau u)\bigr).
\]
Its positive local maxima occur at
$u=d(v)/\tau+2\pi k/\tau$ for integers $k\ge0$.
Each successive such maximum is the preceding one multiplied by
$e^{-2\pi\beta/\tau}<1$.
The remaining interior stationary points are negative local minima,
and $g(u)\to0$ as $u\to\infty$.
Thus the maximum on $u\ge0$ is attained either at $u=0$ or at the
first nonnegative positive local maximum.
Their two values give the formula.
The displayed bounds follow from $0\le d(v)<2\pi$ and $\cos\alpha>0$.
Although $d(v)$ jumps at its modular endpoint, the maximum formula
is continuous there, as also follows from the preceding theorem.
\end{proof}

This is a precise memory effect of the \emph{same} selected class:
a negative phase of the original error cannot undo primes already deleted.
It is not an independent upper bound on that class's size.
For actual zeta applications, a putative zero alone does not justify
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-periodic-error}; other frequencies and their
remainders must first be controlled.
No assumption of an attained largest zero real part, a unique leading
zero pair, or an RH-dependent Fourier limit is used or inferred here.
The certified unconditional boundary remains $7/8$.

In the prime-deletion assembly, \texttt{GreedyRegulator.lean}
contains the finite regulator core, and
\texttt{GreedyPrimeRegulator.lean} identifies its clock with the actual
greedy prime class.
Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-wave-envelope}, including all three
real-cutoff asymptotics, is now proved by the maintained declaration
\texttt{greedyPrime\_periodic\_wave} in
\texttt{PrimeWavePeriodic.lean}.
Its proof retains the full leading-error hypothesis
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-periodic-error}; it does not infer that hypothesis
from the existence of a zero.
The formal theorem permits every $\beta>0$, so it covers the stated
range $0<\beta<1$.

The maintained proof includes the literal positive-supremum formula,
the finite sup norm, continuity and periodicity of the envelope, and
the lower bound $f(v_0)e^{-\beta T}$.
\texttt{PrimeWaveRecordComparison.lean} proves the uniform finite-prefix
comparison with its initial-range and logarithmic overshoot terms;
\texttt{PrimeWaveLimits.lean} proves the actual regulator and deleted-mass
limits.
\texttt{PrimeClassAbel.lean} proves the exact partial-summation formula
for every actual prime class, with the first prime $2$ retained.
\texttt{PrimeClassAbelBigO.lean} proves the displayed
$O(x^\beta/\log^2x)$ remainder from a uniform class-mass power bound.
\texttt{PrimeWaveCount.lean} then proves the actual deleted-count
asymptotic.
The companion declaration
\texttt{greedyPrime\_periodic\_wave\_count\_bounds} gives, for every
sufficiently large real $x$, the explicit comparison constants
$f(v_0)e^{-\beta T}/2$ and $\|f\|_\infty+1$.

The envelope contraction, almost-period inheritance and uniform positive
recurrence bound are also formalized in
\texttt{DampedWaveRecurrence.lean}.
The full joint-wave theorem, its finite incommensurable-frequency
specialization, and the explicit cosine optimization remain separate
formalization obligations.

\subsection{An infinitely-often size criterion from positive Gaussian probes}
\label{sec:greedy-prime-Gaussian-liminf}

The leading-wave hypothesis in the preceding subsection can be removed
for the purpose of detecting an off-critical zero in the deletion count.
The class $\mathcal S$, its complement $\mathcal D$, and the functions
$R,M,N_{\mathcal D}$ are exactly the same fixed objects as above.
They do not depend on a hypothetical zero or on any probe parameter.

\begin{theorem}[A forbidden zero forces deletions at every large cutoff]
\label{thm:greedy-prime-uniform-zero-response}
Suppose $\zeta(\rho_0)=0$, where
$\rho_0=\beta_0+i\gamma_0$ is nontrivial and $\beta_0>1/2$.
For every $\epsilon>0$ there are constants $d_\epsilon>0$ and
$X_\epsilon$ such that
\begin{equation}
 N_{\mathcal D}(X)\ge d_\epsilon X^{\beta_0-\epsilon}
                         \qquad(X\ge X_\epsilon).
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-uniform-zero-response}
\end{equation}
The constants may depend on the chosen zero and $\epsilon$, but not on
the real cutoff $X$.
No largest zero real part, single leading frequency or RH hypothesis
is assumed.
\end{theorem}

We first establish the exact smoothed identity and its errors.
Write $E_\theta(t)=\theta(t)-t$ and $E_\psi(t)=\psi(t)-t$,
with $\theta(t)=\psi(t)=0$ for $0<t<2$.
For $X>1$, $V>0$ and real $\tau$, put
\begin{equation}
 G_V(u)=\frac{e^{-u^2/(2V)}}{\sqrt{2\pi V}},
 \qquad
 H_\tau^\theta(X,V)=
   \int_{\mathbb R}E_\theta(Xe^u)G_V(u)e^{-i\tau u}\,du,
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-Gaussian-probe}
\end{equation}
and define $H_\tau^\psi$ in the same way.
These integrals converge absolutely: the elementary bound
$\theta(t),\psi(t)=O(t\log(2t))$ for $t\ge2$ suffices.

\begin{lemma}[Signed Gaussian explicit formula with the prime powers retained]
\label{lem:greedy-prime-Gaussian-explicit}
Fix $0<c<1$ and a real $\tau$, and put $V=c\log X$.
Let $\mathcal Z$ be the set of distinct nontrivial zeta zeros,
and let $m_\rho$ denote their multiplicities.
For $V\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 H_\tau^\psi(X,V)
   &=-\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal Z}
       \frac{m_\rho}{\rho}X^\rho
            \exp\left(\frac V2(\rho-i\tau)^2\right)\\
   &\quad-\log(2\pi)e^{-V\tau^2/2}
                 +O_\tau(X^{-1+c/2}).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-Gaussian-explicit}
\end{equation}
The zero sum is absolutely convergent.
Uniformly in real $\tau$, the complete prime-power correction satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 |H_\tau^\psi-H_\tau^\theta|
 &\le
 2X^{1/2}e^{V/8}
       \left(\log X+\frac V2+\sqrt{\frac{2V}{\pi}}\right)\\
 &=O_c\left(X^{1/2+c/8}\log X\right).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-Gaussian-prime-powers}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We use the classical unconditional meromorphic continuation,
functional equation and zero-count estimates for $\zeta$.
In particular $0<\Re\rho<1$ for $\rho\in\mathcal Z$,
the zero set is locally finite,
$N(T)=O(T\log(2+T))$, and
$\zeta'/\zeta(-1+it)=O(\log(2+|t|))$.
The last estimate follows directly from the functional equation and
absolute convergence of $\zeta'/\zeta$ on $\Re s=2$.
None of these facts assumes a zero-free substrip of the critical strip.

Gaussian-smoothed Perron inversion on $\Re s=2$ gives
\[
 \int_{\mathbb R}\psi(Xe^u)G_V(u)e^{-i\tau u}\,du
 =
 \frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(2)}
       -\frac{\zeta'(s)}{\zeta(s)}
        \frac{X^s}{s}
        \exp\left(\frac V2(s-i\tau)^2\right)\,ds .
\]
For example apply Mellin inversion to
$\int_{\log(n/X)}^\infty G_V(u)e^{-i\tau u}\,du$
and then sum against $\Lambda(n)$.
The Dirichlet series is absolutely convergent on this line,
and the vertical Gaussian gives absolute convergence of the
resulting contour integral.
The Gaussian moment is exactly
$\int G_V(u)e^{(s-i\tau)u}\,du
=\exp(V(s-i\tau)^2/2)$.

Move the line only to $\Re s=-1$.
On horizontal contours tending to infinity through heights avoiding
zeros, the usual logarithmic-derivative estimate is
$O(\log^2(2+|t|))$ on the bounded strip $-1\le\Re s\le2$.
Such heights exist by the local zero-count bound; the factor
$e^{-V(t-\tau)^2/2}$ makes their integrals vanish.
The pole at $s=1$ contributes
$X\exp(V(1-i\tau)^2/2)$.
This cancels exactly the Gaussian integral of $Xe^u$ in
$E_\psi$.
A zero $\rho$ contributes the signed residue
$-m_\rho X^\rho\exp(V(\rho-i\tau)^2/2)/\rho$.
At $s=0$ the residue is
$-(\zeta'/\zeta)(0)e^{-V\tau^2/2}
=-\log(2\pi)e^{-V\tau^2/2}$.
There is no trivial zero in this contour strip.

On the new line the modulus of the exponential factor is
$e^{V/2}e^{-V(t-\tau)^2/2}$.
For $V\ge1$ the remaining vertical integral, after extracting
$X^{-1}e^{V/2}$, is bounded by a constant depending on $\tau$.
This proves the remainder in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-Gaussian-explicit}.
The zero-count bound and Gaussian decay in $\Im\rho$ prove absolute
convergence of its zero sum.
It would be invalid to continue this shift to $-\infty$ and sum all
trivial zeros: the Gaussian grows quadratically in the real direction.

Finally $Q(t)=\psi(t)-\theta(t)$ is nonnegative and satisfies the
existing bound $Q(t)\le2\sqrt t\log t$ for $t\ge2$.
Below the first prime power $4$, $Q=0$, so there is no omitted
small-input correction.
The same bound is the input used in the maintained
Nicolas prime-power-tail estimates.
Since $|e^{-i\tau u}|=1$, its complete Gaussian contribution is at most
\[
 2X^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb R}
       e^{u/2}(\log X+|u|)G_V(u)\,du .
\]
Use
$e^{u/2}G_V(u)=e^{V/8}G_V(u-V/2)$ and
$\int|u|G_V(u)\,du=\sqrt{2V/\pi}$.
This gives \eqref{eq:greedy-prime-Gaussian-prime-powers}.
Only this explicitly separated prime-power remainder has been
estimated in modulus; the full signed zero sum remains in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-Gaussian-explicit}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A Gaussian shift exposes a strictly dominant zero]
\label{lem:greedy-prime-Gaussian-exposure}
For every fixed $c>0$ define
\begin{equation}
 A_c(\rho,\tau)
   =\beta+\frac c2\bigl(\beta^2-(\gamma-\tau)^2\bigr),
 \qquad
 B_c(\tau)=\max_{\rho\in\mathcal Z}A_c(\rho,\tau),
 \quad \rho=\beta+i\gamma.
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-Gaussian-exponents}
\end{equation}
If a zero $\rho_0$ has $\beta_0>1/2$, one can choose $\tau>0$
such that the maximum at $\tau$ is attained at a unique distinct zero
$\rho_*$ and
\begin{equation}
 B_c(\tau)>
   \max\left\{B_c(0),\ \frac12+\frac c8,\ \beta_0\right\}.
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-Gaussian-gap}
\end{equation}
The maximum is over distinct zeros; multiplicity at $\rho_*$
is retained as the nonzero coefficient $m_{\rho_*}/\rho_*$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The maximum exists: uniformly for $\tau$ in a compact interval,
\[
 A_c(\rho,\tau)\le1+\frac c2-\frac c2(\gamma-\tau)^2
                                      \longrightarrow-\infty
\]
as $|\gamma|\to\infty$, and there are only finitely many zeros in
any bounded part of the critical strip.
There are no real nontrivial zeros; conjugation permits us to
take $\gamma_0>0$.

Put $b=\beta_0+c\beta_0^2/2$.
Then $b>\max(\beta_0,1/2+c/8)$.
If $B_c(0)<b$, the choice $\tau=\gamma_0$ gives
$B_c(\tau)\ge b>B_c(0)$ and the required strict inequalities.
If $B_c(0)\ge b$, choose a maximizer at $0$ with $\gamma>0$,
using its conjugate if necessary.
For every $0<\tau<2\gamma$,
\[
 A_c(\rho,\tau)-A_c(\rho,0)
                =\frac c2\,\tau(2\gamma-\tau)>0.
\]
Thus in this case too there is a positive $\tau$ with all the
strict inequalities in \eqref{eq:greedy-prime-Gaussian-gap}.

It remains to remove ties without changing these inequalities.
Locally the maximum is over finitely many candidate zeros, by the
uniform tail bound above.
For two different heights, the difference of their exponents is
an affine function of $\tau$ with nonzero slope
$c(\gamma-\gamma')$.
At the same height the exponent strictly increases with $\beta$,
since the difference for $\beta'<\beta$ is
$(\beta-\beta')(1+c(\beta+\beta')/2)>0$.
Consequently only finitely many crossing parameters need be avoided
in a sufficiently small interval about the chosen $\tau$.
Perturbing within that interval gives a unique maximizing zero,
while continuity of the finite maximum preserves every strict gap.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-uniform-zero-response}]
It suffices to treat $0<\epsilon<1$.
Choose $0<a<1/4$ so small that
$\kappa=a^2+2a<\epsilon/2$, and set $c=a^2$.
Fix $\tau$ and the unique $\rho_*$ given by the exposure lemma,
and abbreviate $B=B_c(\tau)$ and $q=1/2+c/8$.
The uniqueness supplies a positive gap between $B$ and every other
zero exponent: only finitely many zeros have exponent near $B$.

For $V=c\log X$, the signed term at $\rho_*$ in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-Gaussian-explicit} has modulus
$(m_{\rho_*}/|\rho_*|)X^B$.
The sum of all other zero terms is $O(X^{B-\delta})$
for some $\delta>0$.
To justify this also for the infinite tail, fix $X_1>1$ and use the
summable majorant
$\sum_\rho(m_\rho/|\rho|)X_1^{A_c(\rho,\tau)-B+\delta}$.
The prime-power error is $O(X^q\log X)$, strictly smaller since $q<B$.
The $s=0$ term and the left contour remainder are smaller as well.
It follows that, for all sufficiently large $X$,
\[
 |H_\tau^\theta(X,V)|\ge dX^B,\qquad
 H_0^\theta(X,V)=o(X^B)
\]
for some $d>0$.
The second estimate uses the full unshifted zero sum and the
strict gap $B_c(0)<B$; its prime-power error is again retained.
The oscillation of the leading residue cannot cancel its modulus,
because there is a unique dominant distinct zero.

Now retain the \emph{coupled signed} expression
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 P_\phi(X)
   &=H_0^\theta(X,V)+
                  \Re\bigl(e^{i\phi}H_\tau^\theta(X,V)\bigr)\\
   &=\int_{\mathbb R}E_\theta(Xe^u)G_V(u)
                         \bigl(1+\cos(\tau u-\phi)\bigr)\,du .
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-positive-Gaussian}
\end{equation}
Its kernel is nonnegative for every phase.
Choosing the phase to maximize the first line gives, eventually,
\[
 \max_\phi P_\phi(X)=H_0^\theta(X,V)+|H_\tau^\theta(X,V)|
                                      \ge\frac d2X^B .
\]
In particular the unshifted term has not been discarded.
It is lower order by the independent exponent gap, rather than by
a main-term triangle or Gram estimate.

Put $Y=X^{1+\kappa}$.
For $u\le\kappa\log X$ one has
$E_\theta(Xe^u)\le R(Y)$.
This includes $Xe^u<2$, where $E_\theta(Xe^u)=-Xe^u<0$.
The kernel in \eqref{eq:greedy-prime-positive-Gaussian} is at most $2G_V$.
For the remaining tail use only the elementary
$\theta(t)\le t\log t$ for $t\ge2$.
Writing $L=\log X$, $z=(\kappa-c)L/\sqrt V$ and
$\overline\Phi(z)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\int_z^\infty e^{-v^2/2}\,dv$, one has
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \int_{\kappa L}^\infty e^u(L+u)G_V(u)\,du
  &=e^{V/2}\left((L+V)\overline\Phi(z)
                  +\sqrt{\frac V{2\pi}}e^{-z^2/2}\right)\\
  &\ll_c L X^{c/2-2}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-Gaussian-tail}
\end{equation}
Indeed $\kappa-c=2a$ and $V=a^2L$, so $z=2\sqrt L$;
use $\overline\Phi(z)\le e^{-z^2/2}$.
Consequently, uniformly in the phase,
\[
 P_\phi(X)\le2R(Y)+O_c(X^{-1+c/2}\log X).
\]
Combining this with the positive lower bound proves
$R(Y)\ge d'X^B$ for every sufficiently large $X$.

The substitution $Y=X^{1+\kappa}$ covers every sufficiently large real
$Y$, not just a subsequence.
Since $B>\beta_0$ and $\beta_0<1$,
\[
 \frac{B}{1+\kappa}>
 \frac{\beta_0}{1+\kappa}\ge\beta_0-\kappa
                                      >\beta_0-\epsilon/2.
\]
Every denominator is positive.
Finally
$N_{\mathcal D}(Y)\log Y\ge M(Y)\ge R(Y)$.
Absorbing this single logarithm into the remaining $\epsilon/2$
margin proves \eqref{eq:greedy-prime-uniform-zero-response}.
All probe parameters were fixed before allowing $X$ to grow.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Infinitely-often deletion-size criterion]
\label{thm:greedy-prime-liminf-RH}
For every fixed $1/2\le\eta<1$, the following are equivalent:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \Re\rho\le\eta\qquad(\rho\in\mathcal Z),\\
 \liminf_{\substack{n\to\infty\\n\in\mathbb N,\ n\ge2}}
       \frac{\log(1+N_{\mathcal D}(n))}{\log n}\le\eta .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-liminf-RH}
\end{equation}
Equivalently the second condition says that for every $\epsilon>0$
and every integer $N$ there exists an integer $n\ge\max(N,2)$ with
$N_{\mathcal D}(n)\le n^{\eta+\epsilon}$.
In particular $\eta=1/2$ gives an RH equivalence for this fixed
computable prime class.
If RH is false and $\Theta_\zeta$ is the supremum of the nontrivial
zero real parts, then the counting exponent has an actual limit:
\[
 \lim_{\substack{n\to\infty\\n\in\mathbb N,\ n\ge2}}
       \frac{\log(1+N_{\mathcal D}(n))}{\log n}
                                      =\Theta_\zeta .
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The zero-free condition and
Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-RH-size} give the stronger all-cutoff
upper bound $N_{\mathcal D}(X)\ll_\epsilon X^{\eta+\epsilon}$.
It implies the stated lower-limit condition and the equivalent
infinitely-often formulation, absorbing its constant into
$\epsilon/2$ of exponent margin.
Conversely a zero with $\beta_0>\eta$ has $\beta_0>1/2$.
The uniform lower bound
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-uniform-zero-response}, for every positive
$\epsilon$, implies that the displayed lower limit is at least
$\beta_0>\eta$, a contradiction.
The elementary equivalence between the lower-limit formulation and
the integer subsequence formulation follows by taking logarithms;
the extra $1$ changes the quotient by $o(1)$ on any such subsequence.

If RH is false, $\Theta_\zeta>1/2$.
Taking the supremum over all off-critical zeros in the uniform lower
bound gives a lower limit at least $\Theta_\zeta$.
For $\Theta_\zeta<1$ the existing zero-strip-to-count estimate gives
an upper limit at most $\Theta_\zeta$.
If $\Theta_\zeta=1$, the elementary $N_{\mathcal D}(n)\le n$
gives that same upper limit.
This proves the last assertion.
\end{proof}

The independent size burden has changed quantifiers: an unbounded sequence
of favorable integer cutoffs is sufficient; an estimate at every cutoff
is no longer required.
No such independent sequence estimate at exponent $3/4$ or $1/2$
is proved here, and no claim that finding it is arithmetically easier
is made. The certified unconditional zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.
At $\beta_0=1/2$ the strict separation from the prime-power exponent
fails; no all-scale square-root lower bound under RH is asserted.

\newpage
\begin{theorem}[Stabilizing probability-profile rank test]
For an integer $n\ge2$, form the finite Bernoulli probability profile%
\label{thm:greedy-prime-RH-rank-clock}
\[
 P_{\mathcal S,n}(z)
 =\prod_{\substack{p\le n\\p\in\mathcal S}}
       \left(1-\frac1p+\frac zp\right).
\]
Define the computable bit
\begin{equation}\label{eq:greedy-prime-RH-rank-bit}
 b(n)=
 \boldsymbol1_{\left\{
 \pi(n)-\deg P_{\mathcal S,n}\le\sqrt n(\log n)^2
 \right\}}.
\end{equation}
This binary sequence stabilizes unconditionally, and its limit is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:greedy-prime-RH-rank-bit-limit}
 \lim_{n\to\infty}b(n)=
 \begin{cases}
 1,&\text{if RH holds},\\
 0,&\text{if RH fails}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Consequently, RH is equivalent both to $b(n)=1$ for infinitely many
integers $n$ and to $b(n)=1$ for all sufficiently large integers $n$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Every factor has positive leading coefficient $1/p$. Thus, also for the
empty product,
\[
 \deg P_{\mathcal S,n}=\#\{p\le n:p\in\mathcal S\},
 \qquad
 \pi(n)-\deg P_{\mathcal S,n}=N_{\mathcal D}(n).
\]
These are finite independent Bernoulli variables with success probabilities
$1/p$ at a fixed cutoff. No simultaneous growing-cutoff sampling limit
is used.

Under RH, Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-RH-size} gives
$N_{\mathcal D}(n)\ll\sqrt n\log n$. Its fixed implied constant is absorbed
by the additional factor $\log n$, so $b(n)=1$ eventually.
If RH fails, functional reflection gives a zero with
$\beta>1/2$. Put $\delta=(\beta-1/2)/2>0$ and apply
Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-uniform-zero-response}
with $\varepsilon=\delta$. It gives
$N_{\mathcal D}(n)\ge c n^{1/2+\delta}$ at every sufficiently large
integer $n$, for some fixed $c>0$.
Since $c n^\delta>(\log n)^2$ eventually, $b(n)=0$ eventually.

For completeness, each finite bit is computable. The greedy membership
test already has effective comparisons at integer cutoffs.
The threshold $\sqrt n(\log n)^2$ cannot be an integer: otherwise
$(\log n)^2$ and hence $\log n$ would be algebraic, contradicting
Lindemann's theorem since $\log n\ne0$ and $e^{\log n}=n$ is algebraic.
Certified rational intervals for the logarithm therefore eventually
separate the threshold from the integer $N_{\mathcal D}(n)$.
This proves computability of each bit, not an effective unconditional
bound on its last change. No finite run of zeros or ones certifies the
limiting bit, and the theorem supplies no decision algorithm for RH.
\end{proof}

The nine spectral-exponent identities, strict comparisons and
localization-parameter statements in
\path{Proof/PrimeClassDeletion/GaussianRecordExponents.lean}
are Lean-checked.
They include the exact signed shift, strict dominance over the
prime-power exponent, the positive-denominator exponent comparison
and the cross-multiplied Gaussian tail identity.
The complex contour formula, its infinite zero-sum audit,
phase-positive integral argument and the resulting prime-count/RH
theorems have full ordinary proofs here.
They are not claimed as separately compiled Lean declarations.
The required analytic formalization still includes the
Gaussian-smoothed Perron contour shift and its zero-sum limit.
No established literature priority is asserted.

\subsection{A critical-line record floor and a genuine counting-exponent limit}
\label{sec:greedy-prime-critical-record-floor}

The preceding Gaussian argument separates an off-critical zero from the
square-prime term by a strict exponent gap. At the critical line that gap
vanishes. A compact window and coherent use of many zero frequencies
supply a different mechanism. Throughout this subsection the literal
classes $\mathcal S,\mathcal D$ are unchanged.

\begin{lemma}[Compact positive window with the square-prime term retained]
\label{lem:greedy-prime-compact-critical-profile}
For $h>0$, define
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 W_h(u)&=\frac1h\left(1-\frac{|u|}{h}\right)_+,\qquad
 K_h(u)=e^{-u/2}W_h(u),\\
 w(y)&=\left(\frac{\sin(y/2)}{y/2}\right)^2,\qquad w(0)=1.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here $r_+=\max(r,0)$.
Assume RH and count positive zero heights $\gamma$ with multiplicities
$m_\gamma$. Then, for this fixed $h$,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 J_h(X)&=\int_{\mathbb R}(\theta(Xe^u)-Xe^u)K_h(u)\,du,\\
 \frac{J_h(X)}{\sqrt X}&=f_h(\log X)+o_h(1),\\
 f_h(v)&=-1-2\sum_{\gamma>0}
 m_\gamma w(h\gamma)
 \frac{\tfrac12\cos(\gamma v)+\gamma\sin(\gamma v)}
      {\tfrac14+\gamma^2}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-compact-critical-profile}
\end{equation}
The series converges absolutely and uniformly in real $v$.
In particular $f_h$ is uniformly almost periodic.
The constant $-1$ is the full leading square-prime contribution.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The triangular Fourier pair is
\[
 \int_{\mathbb R}W_h(u)e^{itu}\,du=w(ht).
\]
Its entire Mellin multiplier is
\[
 \Phi_h(s)=\int_{\mathbb R}K_h(u)e^{su}\,du
 =\left(\frac{\sinh((s-\tfrac12)h/2)}
                  {(s-\tfrac12)h/2}\right)^2,
\]
with its removable value at $s=1/2$.
On every fixed vertical strip,
$\Phi_h(\sigma+it)=O_h((1+|t|)^{-2})$.
The same Perron argument as in
Lemma~\ref{lem:greedy-prime-Gaussian-explicit}, now with this compact
kernel, gives the unconditional identity
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \int_{\mathbb R}(\psi(Xe^u)-Xe^u)K_h(u)\,du
 &=-\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal Z}
       \frac{m_\rho}{\rho}X^\rho\Phi_h(\rho)\\
 &\quad-\log(2\pi)\Phi_h(0)+O_h(X^{-1}).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-compact-explicit}
\end{equation}
For clarity, its initial integrand on $\Re s=2$ is the complete
\[
                     -(\zeta'/\zeta)(s)X^s\Phi_h(s)/s .
\]
Shift only to $\Re s=-1$.
The pole at $1$ cancels the integral of $Xe^u$, the pole at $0$
gives the displayed constant, and no trivial zero is crossed.
On zero-avoiding horizontal heights the integrand is
$O_{h,X}(\log^2(2+|t|)/(1+|t|)^3)$.
On the left vertical line the integral is absolutely convergent and
$O_h(X^{-1})$. The same decay and the zero count make the zero sum
absolutely convergent. This also justifies the contour limit for
the piecewise linear window; differentiability at its corners is
not needed.

The prime number theorem, together with $\theta(t)=O(t)$, gives
\[
 Q(t):=\psi(t)-\theta(t)
   =\theta(\sqrt t)+O(t^{1/3}\log t)
   =\sqrt t+o(\sqrt t).
\]
Indeed the terms with prime-power exponent at least $3$ are at most
$O(\log t)$ terms, each $O(t^{1/3})$.
Since $|u|\le h$ on the kernel support, this asymptotic is uniform
at $t=Xe^u$ as $X\to\infty$, and
\[
 \int Q(Xe^u)K_h(u)\,du
       =\sqrt X\int W_h(u)\,du+o_h(\sqrt X)
       =\sqrt X+o_h(\sqrt X).
\]
Under RH, $\Phi_h(1/2+i\gamma)=w(h\gamma)$.
Divide \eqref{eq:greedy-prime-compact-explicit} by $\sqrt X$ and
pair conjugate zeros to obtain
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-compact-critical-profile}.
For fixed $h$, its coefficients are $O_h(m_\gamma/\gamma^3)$
at large height. Their sum converges by
$N(T)=O(T\log(2+T))$.
Finite trigonometric sums are uniformly almost periodic by the
compact-torus argument used above, and their uniform limit has
the same property. No independence of zero ordinates is assumed.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Coherent critical frequencies exceed the square-prime bias]
\label{lem:greedy-prime-critical-coherence}
Under RH, the profiles in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-compact-critical-profile} satisfy
\begin{equation}
                  f_h(-2h)=\frac12\log(1/h)+O(1)
                         \qquad(h\downarrow0).
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-critical-coherence}
\end{equation}
The implied constant is uniform for sufficiently small $h>0$.
In particular, some fixed $h$ has $f_h(-2h)>0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Keep the complete signed expression
\[
 f_h(-2h)=-1-
 \sum_{\gamma>0}\frac{m_\gamma w(h\gamma)\cos(2h\gamma)}
                         {\tfrac14+\gamma^2}
 +2\sum_{\gamma>0}
       \frac{m_\gamma w(h\gamma)\gamma\sin(2h\gamma)}
                         {\tfrac14+\gamma^2}.
\]
The cosine sum is $O(1)$ uniformly in $h$, since $0\le w\le1$
and $\sum m_\gamma/(1/4+\gamma^2)<\infty$.
Write the last sum without its factor $2$ as $S_h$.
Replacing $\gamma/(1/4+\gamma^2)$ by $1/\gamma$ changes $S_h$
by $O(h)$: use $|\sin(2h\gamma)|\le2h\gamma$ and that same
convergent reciprocal-square sum.

We give the signed evaluation, rather than an unsigned zero bound.
The Riemann--von Mangoldt formula, with multiplicities, is
\[
 N(t)=\frac{t}{2\pi}\log\frac{t}{2\pi}
                     -\frac{t}{2\pi}+O(\log(2+t)).
\]
Stieltjes integration by parts consequently gives
\begin{equation}
 S_h=\frac1{2\pi}\int_2^\infty
       w(ht)\frac{\sin(2ht)}t\log\frac{t}{2\pi}\,dt+O(1).
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-critical-Stieltjes}
\end{equation}
Here the bounded initial interval contributes $O(h)$.
To verify the uniform remainder, set
$g(y)=w(y)\sin(2y)/y$ with its removable value at $0$.
Then
\[
 \int_0^\infty |g'(y)|(1+\log(2+y))\,dy<\infty,
 \qquad w(ht)\sin(2ht)/t=h g(ht).
\]
Thus the error integral against $O(\log(2+t))$ is
$O(h\log(2/h))$, and the endpoint errors are bounded.
This proves the claimed uniformity in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-critical-Stieltjes}.

Substitute $y=ht$ there. The integral with the additional
$\log(y/(2\pi))$ is absolutely convergent, including at $0$.
It follows that
\[
 S_h=\frac{\log(1/h)}{2\pi}\,I+O(1),\qquad
 I=\int_0^\infty w(y)\frac{\sin(2y)}y\,dy.
\]
This signed integral is positive and has the exact value $\pi/2$.
Indeed, if $I(a)=\int_0^\infty w(y)\sin(ay)/y\,dy$ for $a\ge0$,
Fourier inversion for $W_1(u)=(1-|u|)_+$ gives
\[
 I'(a)=\int_0^\infty w(y)\cos(ay)\,dy
                         =\pi(1-a)_+.
\]
Differentiation is justified by integrability of $w$;
$I(0)=0$ therefore gives $I(2)=\pi/2$.
Consequently $2S_h=\tfrac12\log(1/h)+O(1)$.
Together with the retained constant $-1$ and the bounded
cosine contribution this proves
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-critical-coherence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[An unconditional square-root floor for deleted primes]
\label{thm:greedy-prime-critical-record-floor}
There are constants $c>0$ and $X_0$ such that, for every real
$X\ge X_0$,
\begin{equation}
              R(X)\ge c\sqrt X,\qquad
              N_{\mathcal D}(X)\ge\frac{c\sqrt X}{\log X}.
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-critical-record-floor}
\end{equation}
In particular the same fixed computable deleted-prime class
$\mathcal D$ is infinite unconditionally.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First assume RH. Choose a fixed small $h$ for which
$d=f_h(-2h)>0$.
Uniform almost periodicity supplies $L>0$ such that every real
interval of length $L$ contains a $v$ with $f_h(v)\ge3d/4$:
translate the relatively dense set of $d/4$-almost periods by $-2h$.
The error in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-compact-critical-profile} tends to zero
for all $X\to\infty$, so at such sufficiently large $v$, with
$Y=e^v$, one has
\[
                         J_h(Y)\ge(d/2)\sqrt Y .
\]
Given any sufficiently large real cutoff $X$, choose this $v$ in
$[\log X-h-L,\log X-h]$.
Every point $Ye^u$ in the compact kernel support is then at most $X$.
Since $\theta(t)-t\le R(X)$ for every $0<t\le X$, including $t<2$,
and $K_h\ge0$,
\[
 J_h(Y)\le C_h R(X),\qquad
 C_h=\int_{\mathbb R}K_h(u)\,du=\Phi_h(0)>0.
\]
Also $\sqrt Y\ge e^{-(h+L)/2}\sqrt X$.
This proves the first inequality in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-critical-record-floor} under RH.
The regulator comparison $M(X)\ge R(X)$ and
$N_{\mathcal D}(X)\log X\ge M(X)$ give its second inequality.

If RH fails, take a zero with real part $\beta>1/2$ and put
$\epsilon=(\beta-1/2)/2$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-uniform-zero-response} gives
$N_{\mathcal D}(X)\ge d_\epsilon X^{1/2+\epsilon}$
at every sufficiently large cutoff.
Now use both sides of the regulator estimate:
\[
 R(X)\ge M(X)-\log X
       \ge(\log2)N_{\mathcal D}(X)-\log X
       \ge \sqrt X
\]
eventually. The last inequality holds because $\epsilon>0$.
This proves the same first inequality in the other case;
the same regulator argument again gives the second.
The two cases establish the unconditional assertion.
No RH-dependent Fourier identity was used under the bad-zero
hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[An exact all-scale exponent and its inverse prime sequence]
\label{thm:greedy-prime-all-scale-exponent}
Unconditionally,
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{\log(1+N_{\mathcal D}(X))}{\log X}
       =\Theta_\zeta .
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-all-scale-exponent}
\end{equation}
If $d_j$ is the $j$th prime in $\mathcal D$, then
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{\log d_j}{\log j}=\frac1{\Theta_\zeta},
 \qquad
 d_j\ll j^2(\log j)^2 .
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-inverse-exponent}
\end{equation}
In particular RH is equivalent to $d_j=j^{2+o(1)}$.
Under RH there are constants $a,A>0$ such that, eventually,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{a j^2}{(\log j)^2}\le d_j\le A j^2(\log j)^2 .
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-RH-inverse-bounds}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under false RH, the exponent limit was proved in
Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-liminf-RH}.
Under RH the unconditional lower floor just proved and the
upper bound $N_{\mathcal D}(X)\ll\sqrt X\log X$ from
Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-RH-size} squeeze the exponent to
$1/2=\Theta_\zeta$. These cases prove
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-all-scale-exponent} for all real cutoffs.

The class is infinite by
Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-critical-record-floor}.
Its prime enumeration is strictly increasing and
$N_{\mathcal D}(d_j)=j$ exactly.
Since $1/2\le\Theta_\zeta\le1$, substituting $X=d_j$ into
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-all-scale-exponent} and inverting its
positive limit gives the first formula in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-inverse-exponent}.
For its quantitative upper bound, put
$X=A j^2(\log j)^2$ in the unconditional count lower floor.
For a sufficiently large fixed $A$ this gives
$N_{\mathcal D}(X)\ge j$ eventually, hence $d_j\le X$.
In particular $\log d_j\le3\log j$ eventually after increasing
the cutoff. Under RH, the count upper bound at $d_j$ then gives
$j\le C\sqrt{d_j}\log d_j\le3C\sqrt{d_j}\log j$,
which proves the lower inequality in
\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-RH-inverse-bounds}.
Finally zero symmetry gives
$\Theta_\zeta=1/2$ if and only if RH, proving the stated
inverse-sequence equivalence.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact record-to-count calibration and logarithmic concentration]
\label{thm:greedy-prime-record-count-calibration}
Unconditionally,
\begin{equation}
 N_{\mathcal D}(X)\sim\frac{M(X)}{\log X}
                  \sim\frac{R(X)}{\log X}\qquad(X\to\infty).
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-record-count-calibration}
\end{equation}
For every fixed $0\le a<1$,
\begin{equation}
               \frac{N_{\mathcal D}(X^a)}{N_{\mathcal D}(X)}
                                      \longrightarrow0.
 \label{eq:greedy-prime-deletion-log-concentration}
\end{equation}
Thus if $P_n$ is sampled uniformly from the nonempty finite set
$\{p\in\mathcal D:p\le n\}$ at a sufficiently large integer cutoff,
then $\log P_n/\log n$ tends to $1$ in probability.
This is a statement about that specified uniform finite sample.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The denominators are positive for all sufficiently large $X$,
by Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-critical-record-floor}.
Fix $0<a<1$ and write $\Theta=\Theta_\zeta\ge1/2$.
The actual exponent limit in
Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-all-scale-exponent}, applied at both
$X$ and $X^a$, gives
\[
 \frac{N_{\mathcal D}(X^a)}{N_{\mathcal D}(X)}
     =X^{-(1-a)\Theta+o(1)}\longrightarrow0.
\]
For $a=0$ the numerator is zero.
Now retain the entire finite weighted mass
\[
 M(X)=\sum_{\substack{p\le X\\p\in\mathcal D}}\log p .
\]
Its terms from $X^a<p\le X$ each have weight at least $a\log X$,
and every term has weight at most $\log X$. Hence
\[
 a\left(1-\frac{N_{\mathcal D}(X^a)}{N_{\mathcal D}(X)}\right)
 \le\frac{M(X)}{N_{\mathcal D}(X)\log X}\le1 .
\]
First let $X\to\infty$ for each fixed $a$, then let $a\uparrow1$.
The middle ratio tends to $1$.
The regulator sandwich $0\le M(X)-R(X)\le\log X$ and the lower
floor $R(X)\ge c\sqrt X$ also give $M(X)/R(X)\to1$.
This proves \eqref{eq:greedy-prime-record-count-calibration}.
Finally $P_n\le n$, and for each fixed $0<a<1$ the probability
that $\log P_n/\log n\le a$ is exactly
$N_{\mathcal D}(n^a)/N_{\mathcal D}(n)$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:greedy-prime-deletion-log-concentration} proves
the asserted convergence in probability.
\end{proof}

These results concern the very same automatically favorable capped class,
rather than a new model or a selected subsequence of cutoffs.
The compact window retains the square-prime bias and obtains positivity
from its signed interaction with all critical zero frequencies.
They strengthen the earlier limsup readout for moment-rounded classes:
for this greedy class the exponent is a genuine limit, including when RH
holds. The Bernoulli profile above consequently has a degree deficit
at least $c\sqrt n/\log n$ at every sufficiently large integer cutoff.

This is a lower-count theorem. The independent upper-count estimate
at exponent $3/4$ or $1/2$ is still unproved, and the certified
unconditional zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.
All the compact-window contour, zero-count Stieltjes estimate,
almost-periodic recurrence and arithmetic conclusions in this subsection
have ordinary proofs above. They are not claimed as Lean declarations.
Their analytic Lean blockers are the compact-window Perron shift,
the Riemann--von Mangoldt Stieltjes asymptotic and the uniform
almost-periodic passage for the full zero series.
The classical explicit formula and zero-count law are inputs;
no established originality priority is asserted.

\subsection{Optimal prime-component sampling and its moment spectrum}
\label{sec:deleted-component-sampling}

The fixed deleted class also defines a statistic of ordinary integers,
with every prime power retained.
Let $\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}$ be the positive integers all of whose
prime factors belong to $\mathcal D$, and put
\[
 A_{\mathcal D}(m)=\prod_{\substack{p\in\mathcal D\\p\mid m}}p^{v_p(m)}.
\]
The empty product is $1$.
The sampled variable is the integer $m\in\{1,\ldots,N\}$;
$\mathcal D$ is fixed before sampling, and $N$ is the sample endpoint.
This is neither a polynomial input nor a substitution into a two-input
family. Every finite $A_{\mathcal D}(m)$ is computable by factorization
and the already certified greedy membership test.

Write $\Theta=\Theta_\zeta$.
The installed unconditional boundary gives $\Theta\le7/8<1$.
In particular $\sum_{p\in\mathcal D}1/p<\infty$.
The results below use that installed input; they do not improve it.

\begin{lemma}[Every reciprocal tail has an exact exponent]
\label{lem:deleted-prime-weighted-tail}
For every fixed $s>\Theta$,
\begin{equation}
 T_s(X):=\sum_{\substack{p\in\mathcal D\\p>X}}p^{-s}
                       =X^{\Theta-s+o(1)}\qquad(X\to\infty).
 \label{eq:deleted-prime-weighted-tail}
\end{equation}
The implied asymptotics are for each fixed $s$; no uniformity as
$s\downarrow\Theta$ is asserted.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-all-scale-exponent},
$N_{\mathcal D}(X)=X^{\Theta+o(1)}$.
For $0<\epsilon<s-\Theta$, partial summation retains the exact
endpoint correction
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 T_s(X)&=-X^{-s}N_{\mathcal D}(X)
              +s\int_X^\infty N_{\mathcal D}(t)t^{-s-1}\,dt\\
       &\ll_{s,\epsilon}X^{\Theta+\epsilon-s}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The upper constant can contain $(s-\Theta-\epsilon)^{-1}$.
For a fixed $b>1$, logarithmic concentration from
Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-record-count-calibration} gives
$N_{\mathcal D}(X)/N_{\mathcal D}(X^b)\to0$.
The independently positive interval contribution therefore gives
\[
 T_s(X)\ge
 \frac{N_{\mathcal D}(X^b)-N_{\mathcal D}(X)}{X^{bs}}
       =X^{b(\Theta-s)+o(1)}.
\]
Take logarithms, then let $\epsilon\downarrow0$ in the upper bound
and $b\downarrow1$ in the lower bound.
This proves \eqref{eq:deleted-prime-weighted-tail}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A full prime-power law with a uniform sampling error]
\label{thm:deleted-component-sampling-law}
Let
\[
 c_{\mathcal D}=\prod_{p\in\mathcal D}(1-1/p)>0,\qquad
 \nu(a)=\frac{c_{\mathcal D}}a
                         \quad(a\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}).
\]
Then $\nu$ is a probability law on $\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}$.
Equivalently, its valuations at primes of $\mathcal D$ are independent,
with probabilities $(1-1/p)p^{-k}$ at $k\ge0$.
Only finitely many of these valuations are nonzero almost surely.

For an integer $N\ge1$ let
\[
 \mu_N(a)=\frac1N
       \#\{1\le m\le N:A_{\mathcal D}(m)=a\}.
\]
For every fixed $\Theta<\sigma<1$, the complete Euler constant
\begin{equation}
 B_\sigma=\prod_{p\in\mathcal D}
                  \frac{1+p^{-\sigma}}{1-p^{-\sigma}}<\infty
 \label{eq:deleted-component-Euler-budget}
\end{equation}
gives, with total variation equal to half the sum of absolute
point-mass differences,
\begin{equation}
 d_{\rm TV}(\mu_N,\nu)
       \le\min\{1,\tfrac12 B_\sigma N^{\sigma-1}\}
                         \qquad(N\ge1).
 \label{eq:deleted-component-uniform-TV}
\end{equation}
The constant does not depend on a prime cutoff or on a statistic
subsequently applied to the component.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The reciprocal prime sum converges by the preceding lemma with $s=1$.
Thus the product defining $c_{\mathcal D}$ is positive.
The nonnegative Euler expansion, including every prime power, gives
\[
 \sum_{a\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}}\frac1a
           =\prod_{p\in\mathcal D}(1-1/p)^{-1}
           =c_{\mathcal D}^{-1}.
\]
This proves normalization.
For the equivalent product measure, the probability that the
valuation at $p$ is nonzero is $1/p$.
The summable union bound shows that the probability of any nonzero
valuation beyond a prime cutoff tends to zero.
Hence the product law is concentrated on finite-support configurations,
and its mass at each $a$ is exactly $c_{\mathcal D}/a$.

Write $\mu_{\rm Mob}$ for the ordinary M\"obius function, to distinguish
it from $\mu_N$.
For each $a\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}$ the full signed finite identity is
\begin{equation}
 \mu_N(a)=
 \sum_{\substack{e\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}\\e\ {\rm squarefree}\\ae\le N}}
      \mu_{\rm Mob}(e)\frac{\lfloor N/(ae)\rfloor}{N}.
 \label{eq:deleted-component-exact-Mobius}
\end{equation}
Indeed $A_{\mathcal D}(m)=a$ means $a\mid m$ and $m/a$ has no
prime factor in $\mathcal D$; inclusion-exclusion on those actual
prime divisors gives the formula.
There is no coprimality restriction between $a$ and $e$:
a prime dividing $a$ must also be excluded from $m/a$.
Thus all higher valuations and their cross terms remain present.
Absolute convergence at exponent $1$ also gives
\[
 \nu(a)=
 \sum_{\substack{e\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}\\e\ {\rm squarefree}}}
                    \frac{\mu_{\rm Mob}(e)}{ae}.
\]
Subtract these identities, giving zero floor terms when $ae>N$.
For any integer $q\ge1$ and $0<\sigma<1$,
\[
 0\le \frac1q-\frac{\lfloor N/q\rfloor}{N}
          \le N^{\sigma-1}q^{-\sigma}.
\]
For $q\le N$ use the rounding bound $1/N$; for $q>N$ use $1/q$.
These two domains form the entire supported sum.

Only this explicit finite-sampling remainder is now bounded in
absolute value. Summing it over the whole state space gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{a\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}}|\mu_N(a)-\nu(a)|
 &\le N^{\sigma-1}
   \sum_{a\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}}
   \sum_{\substack{e\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}\\e\ {\rm squarefree}}}
                                      (ae)^{-\sigma}\\
 &=N^{\sigma-1}
       \prod_{p\in\mathcal D}
                    \frac{1+p^{-\sigma}}{1-p^{-\sigma}}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The prime-power geometric denominator and the squarefree numerator
are both charged in this product.
Its convergence follows from
$\sum_{p\in\mathcal D}p^{-\sigma}<\infty$ and
$1-p^{-\sigma}\ge1-2^{-\sigma}>0$.
Tonelli applies to the displayed nonnegative majorant.
Dividing by $2$ and using that total variation is at most $1$
proves \eqref{eq:deleted-component-uniform-TV}.
This is a uniform law for the full component; a fixed-cutoff sampling
limit has not been substituted for a growing-cutoff estimate.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The exact statistical convergence exponent]
\label{thm:deleted-component-exact-rate}
Put $\Delta_N=d_{\rm TV}(\mu_N,\nu)$.
If $L(a)$ is the largest prime factor of $a$, with $L(1)=1$, let
$\Delta_N^{\max}$ be the total variation distance of the two
$L$-laws and let $K_N^{\max}$ be their Kolmogorov distance.
Then all three are positive and
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\log\Delta_N}{\log N}&=\Theta-1,\\
 \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\log\Delta_N^{\max}}{\log N}&=\Theta-1,\\
 \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\log K_N^{\max}}{\log N}&=\Theta-1.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:deleted-component-exact-rate}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every fixed $1/2\le\eta<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \Re\rho\le\eta\quad(\rho\in\mathcal Z)\\
 \Longleftrightarrow\
 \Delta_N\ll_\epsilon N^{\eta-1+\epsilon}
                         \quad\hbox{for every }\epsilon>0.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:deleted-component-rate-strip}
\end{equation}
In particular RH is equivalent to the square-root convergence
exponent $\Delta_N=N^{-1/2+o(1)}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under $\nu$ the exact tail of the largest deleted prime is
\[
 q(X)=\nu\{a:L(a)>X\}
       =1-\prod_{\substack{p\in\mathcal D\\p>X}}(1-1/p)
       \sim T_1(X)=X^{\Theta-1+o(1)}.
\]
For the comparison, the logarithm of the reciprocal tail product is
$T_1(X)+O(T_1(X)/X)$, and $T_1(X)\to0$.
Every prime divisor of $A_{\mathcal D}(m)$ is at most $N$ when
$m\le N$. The empirical tail above $N$ is therefore exactly zero.
At that moving cutoff the limiting tail is $q(N)>0$, since
$\mathcal D$ is infinite.
The uniform full-law theorem and contraction under deterministic
maps consequently give
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 0<q(N)\le K_N^{\max}\le\Delta_N^{\max}\le\Delta_N,\\
 \Delta_N\le\tfrac12 B_\sigma N^{\sigma-1}
                         \qquad(\Theta<\sigma<1).
 \end{gathered}
\]
Take logarithms and then $\sigma\downarrow\Theta$.
This proves \eqref{eq:deleted-component-exact-rate}.
It also proves both directions of
\eqref{eq:deleted-component-rate-strip}.
Zero symmetry gives the RH specialization.
The phrase square-root exponent allows every positive exponent margin;
no bound $O(N^{-1/2})$ is asserted.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A positive divisor identity gives the full moment spectrum]
\label{thm:deleted-component-moment-spectrum}
For every fixed real $t>0$, put
\[
 U_t(N)=\frac1N\sum_{m=1}^N A_{\mathcal D}(m)^t.
\]
Then unconditionally
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\log U_t(N)}{\log N}
                         =\max\{0,\Theta+t-1\}.
 \label{eq:deleted-component-moment-spectrum}
\end{equation}
For $0<t<1-\Theta$ its finite limiting moment is
\[
 U_t(\infty)
 =c_{\mathcal D}\prod_{p\in\mathcal D}(1-p^{t-1})^{-1},
\]
and the deficit has a strict sign and an exact decay exponent:
\begin{equation}
 0<U_t(\infty)-U_t(N)
                 =N^{\Theta+t-1+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:deleted-component-moment-deficit}
\end{equation}
The square-root statistic always diverges, with
\begin{equation}
 U_{1/2}(N)\gg\log\log N,\qquad
 \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\log U_{1/2}(N)}{\log N}
                                      =\Theta-\frac12 .
 \label{eq:deleted-component-square-root-readout}
\end{equation}
In particular RH is equivalent to
$U_{1/2}(N)\ll_\epsilon N^\epsilon$ for every $\epsilon>0$;
boundedness of this square-root mean would be false even under RH.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $d\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}$ define the positive Jordan weight
\[
 J_t(1)=1,\qquad
 J_t(d)=d^t\prod_{p\mid d}(1-p^{-t})\quad(d>1).
\]
On a prime power,
$J_t(p^k)=p^{kt}-p^{(k-1)t}$; telescoping and multiplication give
$\sum_{d\mid a}J_t(d)=a^t$.
Since the $\mathcal D$-smooth divisors of $m$ are exactly the
divisors of $A_{\mathcal D}(m)$, the complete finite consumer is
\begin{equation}
 U_t(N)=
 \sum_{\substack{d\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}\\d\le N}}
             J_t(d)\frac{\lfloor N/d\rfloor}{N}.
 \label{eq:deleted-component-Jordan-consumer}
\end{equation}
Every weight is nonnegative. This identity includes all prime powers;
it is not a bound obtained by discarding signed main terms.

For every $r>\Theta+t$ the full positive Euler budget is
\[
 Z_t(r):=\sum_{d\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}}J_t(d)d^{-r}
      =\prod_{p\in\mathcal D}\frac{1-p^{-r}}{1-p^{t-r}}<\infty.
\]
The local identity follows by summing every $k\ge1$;
convergence follows from $r-t>\Theta$.
All denominators are positive, since $r>t$.

If $\Theta+t<1$, $Z_t(1)<\infty$ and dominated convergence in
\eqref{eq:deleted-component-Jordan-consumer} gives
$U_t(N)\to Z_t(1)=U_t(\infty)$.
Also $U_t(N)\ge1$, so its exponent is zero.
If $\Theta+t\ge1$, choose any $r>\Theta+t$, hence $r>1$.
The same exact identity gives
\[
 U_t(N)\le\sum_{\substack{d\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}\\d\le N}}
                        \frac{J_t(d)}d
          \le N^{r-1}Z_t(r).
\]
Together with $U_t(N)\ge1$, this proves the upper exponent,
including equality $\Theta+t=1$.

For the remaining lower exponent, fix $0<a<1$.
There are $N^{\Theta+o(1)}$ deleted primes in $(N^a,N]$ by
logarithmic concentration.
At each such prime, eventually $J_t(p)\ge p^t/2$ and
$\lfloor N/p\rfloor/N\ge1/(2p)$.
Their positive contribution to the same consumer is at least
\[
 \frac14 N^{\min\{t-1,a(t-1)\}}
       \bigl(N_{\mathcal D}(N)-N_{\mathcal D}(N^a)\bigr).
\]
Its lower exponent tends to $\Theta+t-1$ as $a\uparrow1$.
This proves \eqref{eq:deleted-component-moment-spectrum}.

In the subcritical case $0<t<1-\Theta$, subtraction retains the
exact nonnegative rounding and tail deficit
\[
 U_t(\infty)-U_t(N)=
 \sum_{d\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal D}}J_t(d)
       \left(\frac1d-\frac{\lfloor N/d\rfloor}{N}\right).
\]
Choose $\Theta+t<r<1$ and use the same two-domain rounding inequality
as before. The deficit is at most $N^{r-1}Z_t(r)$.
Its terms at deleted primes $p>N$ are at least
$\tfrac12 T_{1-t}(N)$ eventually.
Lemma~\ref{lem:deleted-prime-weighted-tail} therefore supplies a
strictly positive lower bound of exponent $\Theta+t-1$.
Let $r\downarrow\Theta+t$ to obtain
\eqref{eq:deleted-component-moment-deficit}.

Finally use the unconditional count floor
$N_{\mathcal D}(x)\ge c\sqrt x/\log x$.
Partial summation gives
\[
 \sum_{\substack{p\le N\\p\in\mathcal D}}p^{-1/2}
 \ge \frac12\int_{X_0}^N
                    N_{\mathcal D}(x)x^{-3/2}\,dx
 \ge\frac c2\log\log N-O(1).
\]
The prime terms of the positive Jordan consumer at $t=1/2$ give
\[
 U_{1/2}(N)\ge
 \frac12\sum_{\substack{p\le N\\p\in\mathcal D}}
                 (p^{-1/2}-p^{-1}).
\]
The reciprocal-prime sum is finite, so this proves the asserted
logarithmic divergence.
Since $\Theta\ge1/2$, the moment-spectrum formula at $t=1/2$
gives exactly $\Theta-1/2$.
Reflection again proves the RH equivalence.
\end{proof}

The same uniform law also controls the previously studied probability
profiles. If $K_{\mathcal D}(m)$ counts distinct deleted prime divisors,
its limiting probability generating function is the entire product
\[
 P_{\mathcal D}(z)=
             \prod_{p\in\mathcal D}(1-1/p+z/p).
\]
Uniform convergence on every compact $z$-set follows from
$\sum_{p\in\mathcal D}1/p<\infty$.
For its coefficients $b_k$ and the empirical probabilities
$b_{N,k}=N^{-1}\#\{m\le N:K_{\mathcal D}(m)=k\}$, contraction of
total variation gives the full profile bound
\[
                  \sum_{k\ge0}|b_{N,k}-b_k|
                                  \le B_\sigma N^{\sigma-1}.
\]
This is an upper estimate for the count-profile error; its exponent
is not claimed to be sharp for that particular statistic.
In contrast the full component, largest-prime total variation and
largest-prime Kolmogorov error all have the exact exponent above.

The elementary fixed-prime CRT law and the earlier Fermat avoidance
density are completed inputs for the sampling interpretation.
The present conclusion concerns all valuations in one fixed infinite
greedy class, a uniform error for the entire law, and its exact
zero-supremum exponent. It differs from the earlier prime-Poisson
repair criterion, whose measures use fractional repair weights and
whose zero response was a dyadic limsup.
The positive Jordan identity supplies a genuine one-sided moment
deficit, with no omitted prime-power or cutoff correction.

For the RH programme the exact numerical consumer is now
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \Theta\le\eta
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \sum_{m=1}^N\sqrt{A_{\mathcal D}(m)}
             \ll_\epsilon N^{\eta+1/2+\epsilon}\\
 \hbox{for every }\epsilon>0,\qquad \frac12\le\eta<1 .
 \end{gathered}
\]
The installed $7/8$ gives mean exponent at most $3/8$;
boundary $3/4$ requires at most $1/4$; RH requires exponent zero.
No independent improvement of that mean estimate is proved here.
The certified zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.

The pointwise rounding bound for all $N,q\ge1$ and
$0\le\sigma\le1$, together with the exact finite signed
residue-sum identity and its weighted error estimate, is
Lean-verified in \texttt{PrimeClassSamplingFloor}.
The declaration \texttt{floorSamplingError\_le\_rpow} proves
the rounding estimate used above.
The finite sampling argument is also Lean-verified directly for the
actual integer counts, rather than under a sampling-error hypothesis.
For a class $\mathcal P$ of primes put
$S_Y=\{p\in\mathcal P:p\le Y\}$ and
\[
 B_{S_Y}(z)=\prod_{p\in S_Y}(1-1/p+z/p),\qquad
 C_{N,k}=\#\{1\le m\le N:K_{\mathcal P}(m)=k\}.
\]
For every $1\le N\le Y$, $0\le\sigma\le1$, and
$S_\sigma=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}p^{-\sigma}<\infty$, the maintained
declaration
\[
 \texttt{actualSample\_coefficient\_error\_le\_exp}
\]
proves
\[
 \sum_{k=0}^{|S_Y|}
 \left|\frac{C_{N,k}}N-[z^k]B_{S_Y}(z)\right|
 \le N^{\sigma-1}\prod_{p\in S_Y}(1+2p^{-\sigma})
 \le e^{2S_\sigma}N^{\sigma-1}.
\]
The proof uses the exact subset expansion with coefficient
$\lfloor N/\prod_{p\in T}p\rfloor/N$, the signed floor remainder,
and the identity that the coefficient norm of $(z-1)^r$ is $2^r$.
Thus the estimate is uniform in the independently varying cutoff $Y$.
The infinite count-profile consumer is now also Lean-verified.
For $0\le\sigma<1$ with the same summability hypothesis, the declaration
\[
 \texttt{classEulerProfile\_count\_sampling\_law}
\]
proves that the literal product
$P_{\mathcal P}(z)=\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}(1-1/p+z/p)$ is entire,
that its Taylor coefficients $b_k$ satisfy
\[
 b_k\ge0,\qquad \sum_{k\ge0}b_k=1,\qquad
 \sum_{k\ge0}\left|\frac{C_{N,k}}N-b_k\right|
       \le e^{2S_\sigma}N^{\sigma-1}\quad(N\ge1).
\]
The formal proof constructs the coefficient limits from the uniform
finite sampling estimate, passes every finite coefficient sum to the
limit, and then proves the full infinite sum bound and normalization.
Uniform convergence of the finite Euler products and Cauchy's coefficient
estimate identify this same limiting law with the entire product's
Taylor coefficients. The separate declaration
\[
 \texttt{classEulerProfile\_eq\_zero\_iff}
\]
proves that its zeros are exactly $1-p$ for $p\in\mathcal P$.
All these statements include finite and empty classes and the smallest
prime $2$; no infinitude or additional sampling-law hypothesis is used.

Normalization of the full prime-power component law, its absolutely
summable M\"obius floor expansion, the full total-variation estimate
retaining all valuations, and the positive Jordan moment consumer remain
to be formalized. The verified count-profile law does not establish
their exact convergence and moment spectra.
No originality priority over the literature is established.

\subsection{Universal optimality of capped prime-component laws}
\label{sec:geometric-cap-extremality}

The exact rank theorem has consequences for entire prime-power laws.
Here a capped class $\mathcal T$ always means a subset of the primes
satisfying $\theta_{\mathcal T}(x)\le x$ for every $x\ge2$.
Its complement inside the primes is $\mathcal E$.
For a prime class $\mathcal V$, let $A_{\mathcal V}(m)$ be the
component of $m$ supported on $\mathcal V$, with all valuations retained.
The previous subsection defines this statistic for $\mathcal D$;
the same definition applies to $\mathcal E$.

\begin{lemma}[Geometric prime factors are ordered by every concave loss]
\label{lem:geometric-prime-concave-order}
For a real $p>1$, let $G_p$ have probabilities
$\mathbb P(G_p=k)=(1-p^{-1})p^{-k}$ at $k\ge0$, and put $Z_p=p^{G_p}$.
For $x\ge1$, with $k=\lfloor\log x/\log p\rfloor$,
\begin{equation}
 S_p(x):=\mathbb E\min(Z_p,x)
                =(k+1)(1-p^{-1})+xp^{-k-1}.
 \label{eq:geometric-prime-capped-mean}
\end{equation}
For every $x>1$, $S_p(x)$ is strictly decreasing in $p>1$.
Consequently, for $1<p\le q$ and every nonnegative nondecreasing
concave function $g:[1,\infty)\to[0,\infty)$,
\[
                            \mathbb E g(Z_q)\le\mathbb E g(Z_p),
\]
allowing infinite expectations.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The probabilities at $j\le k$, multiplied by $p^j$, each contribute
$1-p^{-1}$, while the entire remaining tail has probability $p^{-k-1}$.
This proves \eqref{eq:geometric-prime-capped-mean}, including $x=p^k$.
For fixed $x>1$, on a cell where $k$ is constant its derivative is
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\partial S_p(x)}{\partial p}
      =\frac{k+1}{p^2}\left(1-\frac{x}{p^k}\right)\le0.
 \label{eq:geometric-prime-capped-derivative}
\end{equation}
All denominators are positive. The derivative is strictly negative
inside each cell.
At a boundary $x=p^k$, $k\ge1$, the formulas for $k$ and $k-1$
both equal $k+1-k/p$.
There are only finitely many such boundaries on any compact interval
$1<p_0\le p\le p_1$.
Thus the function is continuous and strictly decreasing on that
interval. When $x=1$ it is identically $1$.

We supply the test-function passage.
For a continuous nonnegative nondecreasing concave $g$, interpolate
its values on nested finite dyadic grids in $[1,2^n]$ and make the
interpolant constant past $2^n$.
These nonnegative concave interpolants increase to $g$.
Each is a constant plus a nonnegative linear combination of
$\min(z,x_i)-1$, since its successive nonnegative slopes decrease
and its last slope is zero.
The capped-mean inequality therefore compares its expectations.
Monotone convergence gives the comparison for $g$.

A finite concave nondecreasing function can only have an upward
right jump at the endpoint $1$.
Writing $b=g(1+)-g(1)\ge0$, it is the sum of a continuous
nonnegative nondecreasing concave function and $b\boldsymbol1_{\{z>1\}}$.
The latter indicator is the increasing limit, as $\epsilon\downarrow0$,
of $(\min(z,1+\epsilon)-1)/\epsilon$.
Its expectation has the same order.
This covers the endpoint case and all stated test functions.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The greedy deletion law is the least concave-cost law]
\label{thm:greedy-geometric-concave-extremum}
Let $\mathcal T$ be any capped class and let $\mathcal E$ be its
deleted complement.
Take independent geometric valuations at the primes of $\mathcal E$,
and let $Y_{\mathcal E}$ be their product, allowing the value $+\infty$.
Let $Y_{\mathcal D}$ have the proper integer-valued law $\nu$
from Theorem~\ref{thm:deleted-component-sampling-law}.
For every nonnegative nondecreasing concave $g$ as above, put
$g(+\infty)=\lim_{z\to\infty}g(z)$. Then
\begin{equation}
                    \mathbb E g(Y_{\mathcal D})
                           \le\mathbb E g(Y_{\mathcal E}).
 \label{eq:greedy-geometric-concave-extremum}
\end{equation}
If $\mathcal T\ne\mathcal S$, the capped-mean comparison is strict
at every threshold $x>1$:
\begin{equation}
       \mathbb E\min(Y_{\mathcal D},x)
                    <\mathbb E\min(Y_{\mathcal E},x).
 \label{eq:greedy-geometric-strict-capped-mean}
\end{equation}
Thus the greedy law is uniquely least in this concave-cost order.
The comparison concerns limiting laws, rather than exact ordering
of arbitrary finite-sample component distributions.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:capped-prime-rank-extremum} gives
$N_{\mathcal D}(x)\le N_{\mathcal E}(x)$ at every cutoff.
The unconditional lower floor shows that both deleted classes are
infinite. List their primes as $d_1<d_2<\cdots$ and
$e_1<e_2<\cdots$. The count comparison gives
\[
                                 d_j\ge e_j\qquad(j\ge1).
\]
For any independent nonnegative multiplier $B\ge1$,
the function $z\mapsto g(Bz)$ is again nondecreasing and concave.
Conditioning on $B$ therefore preserves the preceding one-prime
comparison, with no integrability hypothesis needed for a
nonnegative expectation.
Replace the coordinates one at a time in a finite product to obtain
the comparison for its first $n$ geometric factors.
The products increase to $Y_{\mathcal D}$ and $Y_{\mathcal E}$.
Monotone convergence gives \eqref{eq:greedy-geometric-concave-extremum}.
For $\mathcal D$ only finitely many valuations are nonzero almost
surely, so its limit really has the already proved law $\nu$.

If $\sum_{p\in\mathcal E}1/p=\infty$, then $Y_{\mathcal E}=+\infty$
almost surely. Indeed, for each finite $L$, the event
$Y_{\mathcal E}\le L$ requires all valuations at $p>L$ to vanish;
their finite avoidance products tend to zero.
In this case the right side of
\eqref{eq:greedy-geometric-strict-capped-mean} is $x$, whereas the
left side is strictly below $x$ because
$\mathbb P(Y_{\mathcal D}=1)=c_{\mathcal D}>0$.

In the remaining case both products are finite almost surely.
If the classes differ, some rank $j$ has $d_j>e_j$.
Form an auxiliary independent product by replacing only the
$j$th greedy coordinate by the factor with base $e_j$.
Such an auxiliary coordinate product need not itself be a
prime-component law; only its independence is used.
All other greedy factors equal $1$ with positive probability
\[
                    \prod_{i\ne j}(1-1/d_i)>0.
\]
On that event the strict one-prime capped-mean difference applies;
on every other event its conditional difference is nonnegative.
The auxiliary law therefore has strictly greater capped mean than
the greedy law at each $x>1$.
Changing its remaining coordinates to the $e_i$ coordinates can
only increase that mean, by the non-strict product comparison
already proved. This proves strictness.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Maximal retained density and minimal component entropy]
\label{thm:greedy-component-density-entropy}
For every capped $\mathcal T$, the set of positive integers all of
whose prime factors belong to $\mathcal T$ has a natural density
\begin{equation}
 c_{\mathcal E}=\prod_{p\in\mathcal E}(1-1/p)
                           \le c_{\mathcal D}.
 \label{eq:greedy-component-density-extremum}
\end{equation}
The inequality is strict unless $\mathcal T=\mathcal S$.

Among capped competitors with $\sum_{p\in\mathcal E}1/p<\infty$,
let $\nu_{\mathcal E}(a)=c_{\mathcal E}/a$ on
$\mathcal E$-smooth positive integers.
Their Shannon entropies, in natural logarithms, satisfy
\begin{equation}
 H(\nu_{\mathcal D})\le H(\nu_{\mathcal E}),\qquad
 H(\nu_{\mathcal V})=
 \sum_{p\in\mathcal V}
 \left[-\log(1-1/p)+\frac{\log p}{p-1}\right].
 \label{eq:greedy-component-entropy-extremum}
\end{equation}
Here $H(\nu_{\mathcal D})<\infty$; a competitor's entropy may
be infinite. Equality holds only for $\mathcal T=\mathcal S$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If the reciprocal sum over $\mathcal E$ converges, the finite
CRT avoidance law and its uniform reciprocal-tail bound prove
the density formula, exactly as in the earlier Fermat avoidance
argument. If it diverges, upper density is at most every finite
avoidance product, and those products tend to zero.
Thus the stated density exists in both cases.

The weight $w(p)=-\log(1-1/p)$ is positive and strictly decreasing.
The deleted-prime rank comparison $d_j\ge e_j$ therefore gives
$\sum_jw(d_j)\le\sum_jw(e_j)$.
If the latter sum diverges, the density inequality is strict.
If it converges and the classes differ, one strict rank gives
a positive strict gap between the sums.
Exponentiating proves \eqref{eq:greedy-component-density-extremum}
and its equality characterization.

For a proper component law, normalization and Tonelli give
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 H(\nu_{\mathcal V})
   &=-\log c_{\mathcal V}+\mathbb E\log Y_{\mathcal V}\\
   &=-\log c_{\mathcal V}
              +\sum_{p\in\mathcal V}\frac{\log p}{p-1}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
This identity allows infinite entropy.
The single-prime entropy
$h(p)=-\log(1-1/p)+\log p/(p-1)$ obeys
\[
                              h'(p)=-\frac{\log p}{(p-1)^2}<0
                                      \qquad(p>1).
\]
It is positive and strictly decreasing.
The same rank comparison proves the entropy inequality and
strictness when the classes differ.
For the greedy class, $h(p)\ll(1+\log p)/p$ is summable by the
proved count exponent $\Theta<1$.
This proves finiteness of its entropy.
Entropy is asserted here only for the proper countable integer-valued
laws, namely competitors with summable deleted reciprocal primes.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Adaptive finite caps cannot reduce the moment exponent]
\label{thm:adaptive-capped-moment-exponent}
For an integer $N\ge2$, let
$\mathcal P_N=\{p\le N:p\text{ prime}\}$.
A subset $\mathcal T\subseteq\mathcal P_N$ is admissible if
\[
       \sum_{\substack{p\in\mathcal T\\p\le q}}\log p\le q
                            \qquad(q\in\mathcal P_N).
\]
For $0<t\le1$ define the finite optimization
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 V_t(N)&=\min_{\mathcal T\ {\rm admissible}} U_t(\mathcal T;N),\\
 U_t(\mathcal T;N)
   &=\frac1N\sum_{m=1}^N
       \left(\prod_{\substack{p\in\mathcal P_N\setminus\mathcal T\\p\mid m}}
                         p^{v_p(m)}\right)^t .
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:adaptive-capped-moment-definition}
\end{equation}
With $c_t=(1-2^{-t})/2>0$, every admissible choice satisfies
\begin{equation}
 U_t(\mathcal T;N)\ge
 c_t\sum_{\substack{p\le N\\p\in\mathcal D}}p^{t-1}
 \ge c_t N^{t-1}N_{\mathcal D}(N).
 \label{eq:adaptive-capped-uniform-moment-floor}
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\log V_t(N)}{\log N}
                          =\max\{0,\Theta+t-1\}.
 \label{eq:adaptive-capped-moment-exponent}
\end{equation}
In particular there are $c>0,N_0$, independent of the admissible
selection, such that
\begin{equation}
 V_{1/2}(N)\ge c\log\log N\quad(N\ge N_0),\qquad
 \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\log V_{1/2}(N)}{\log N}
                                      =\Theta-\frac12 .
 \label{eq:adaptive-capped-critical-floor}
\end{equation}
For each fixed $1/2\le\eta<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \Theta\le\eta
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 V_{1/2}(N)\ll_\epsilon N^{\eta-1/2+\epsilon}
                           \quad\hbox{for every }\epsilon>0.
 \label{eq:adaptive-capped-moment-strip}
\end{equation}
The value and an attaining subset for $V_{1/2}(N)$ are computable
from the integer $N$, without an efficiency claim.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
There are finitely many subsets of $\mathcal P_N$, and the empty
subset is admissible. Thus the minimum exists.
The finite prime inequalities are equivalent to the cap throughout
$2\le x\le N$: the theta function is constant between prime nodes.
Theorem~\ref{thm:capped-prime-rank-extremum} therefore applies at
every prefix of this same finite problem.
If $e_1<\cdots<e_b$ are the primes in
$\mathcal E_N=\mathcal P_N\setminus\mathcal T$ and
$d_1<\cdots<d_a$ are those in $\mathcal D\cap[2,N]$, it gives
$b\ge a$ and $e_j\le d_j$ for $j\le a$.
Since $p^{t-1}$ is nonnegative and nonincreasing for $0<t\le1$,
\[
                  \sum_{p\in\mathcal E_N}p^{t-1}
                    \ge\sum_{\substack{p\le N\\p\in\mathcal D}}p^{t-1}.
\]

Apply the exact positive Jordan divisor identity to
$\mathcal E_N$; it does not require a fixed infinite class.
Retaining its nonnegative prime terms gives
\[
 U_t(\mathcal T;N)\ge
     \sum_{p\in\mathcal E_N}(p^t-1)\frac{\lfloor N/p\rfloor}{N}.
\]
For every such prime,
$p^t-1\ge(1-2^{-t})p^t$ and
$\lfloor N/p\rfloor/N\ge1/(2p)$.
These inequalities prove the first bound in
\eqref{eq:adaptive-capped-uniform-moment-floor}.
The second uses $p^{t-1}\ge N^{t-1}$ for $p\le N$.
All constants are independent of $\mathcal T$ and $N$.

The fixed greedy choice restricted to $\mathcal P_N$ is admissible,
so $V_t(N)\le U_t(N)$ for its previously proved moment mean.
That mean has exponent $\max(0,\Theta+t-1)$.
On the other side the new uniform floor and
$N_{\mathcal D}(N)=N^{\Theta+o(1)}$ give the lower exponent
$\Theta+t-1$, while the elementary $V_t(N)\ge1$ supplies zero
when that exponent is nonpositive.
This proves \eqref{eq:adaptive-capped-moment-exponent}.
For $t=1/2$, partial summation of the unconditional deletion
count floor gives
$\sum_{p\le N,p\in\mathcal D}p^{-1/2}\gg\log\log N$.
Substitute this into the same uniform inequality to obtain
\eqref{eq:adaptive-capped-critical-floor}.
Since $\Theta\ge1/2$, its exponent yields
\eqref{eq:adaptive-capped-moment-strip}, including RH at $\eta=1/2$.

Finally enumerate the finite prime set and all its subsets.
Every admissibility comparison is $\log M\le q$ for an integer
prime subproduct $M$ and a positive integer prime $q$.
Equality is impossible by transcendence of $e^q$, so certified
logarithm intervals decide it.
At $t=1/2$ each cost is a finite sum of square roots of integers
