divided by $N$, hence is algebraic.
Exact algebraic comparisons, including equality, select a minimum
and an attaining subset.
This proves the computability assertion on its stated integer domain.
\end{proof}

The limiting-law order and the finite optimization have different
quantifiers. The former compares full geometric laws for fixed
global capped classes. The latter is uniform over every admissible
subset chosen separately at each sample size.
The selection must be made before the integer $m$ is sampled.
Randomizing that selection independently of $m$ preserves the lower
bound by averaging. Thus a cutoff-dependent or independently
randomized family cannot conceal the same moment exponent.

The one-prime result is a concave-cost order.
It does not imply ordinary stochastic order: for the factors with
bases $2$ and $3$, their tails at $3/2$ are $1/2$ and $1/3$,
whereas at $5/2$ they are $1/4$ and $1/3$.
These exact crossing tails concern the one-prime comparison;
no claim about failure of stochastic order for a particular
whole greedy-versus-competitor pair is made.

The new conclusions use the completed rank theorem and full
prime-power law. They extend the earlier Bernoulli count-profile
extremality to geometric components, concave losses, actual integer
density, entropy, and a finite optimization with a uniform exponent.
They do not supply an independent upper estimate for that exponent.
The certified boundary is still $7/8$; $3/4$ requires
$V_{1/2}(N)\ll_\epsilon N^{1/4+\epsilon}$, and RH requires
$V_{1/2}(N)\ll_\epsilon N^\epsilon$ for every $\epsilon>0$.
The universal logarithmic lower floor is compatible with RH.

All proofs in this subsection are ordinary proofs.
No new Lean verification is claimed for the geometric capped mean,
its concave-test extension, the infinite-product comparison or the
adaptive finite optimization. Those are the concrete formalization
consumers; the earlier finite Bernoulli tail comparisons remain
separate compiled inputs.
No established originality priority is asserted.

\subsection{A signed certificate for greedy rank optimality}
\label{sec:ordered-stream-greedy}

The exact rank comparison has a finite mechanism that does not
depend on primes.  It is useful to retain this mechanism rather
than just its cardinality consequence.  Let $w_0,w_1,\ldots$ be
nondecreasing real weights and let $b_0,b_1,\ldots$ be arbitrary
real budgets.  For a selection $a\subseteq\mathbb N_0$, set
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 C_a(n)&=\#(a\cap\{0,\ldots,n-1\}),\\
 S_a(n)&=\sum_{\substack{0\le j<n\\j\in a}}w_j,\\
 n\in g&\ \Longleftrightarrow\ S_g(n)+w_n\le b_n .
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:ordered-stream-greedy-ledger}
\end{equation}
The last line defines the greedy selection recursively, starting
with $S_g(0)=0$.  A competitor $t$ is admissible when
$S_t(n)+w_n\le b_n$ at every index $n\in t$.
The budget test is imposed at selected indices.  If budgets
decrease, this definition does not assert a budget inequality at
an index where nothing is selected.

\begin{theorem}[Greedy rank optimality with arbitrary budgets]
\label{thm:ordered-stream-greedy-optimality}
For every such admissible competitor and every $n\ge0$,
\[
 C_t(n)\le C_g(n),\qquad
 S_g(n)-S_t(n)\le (C_g(n)-C_t(n))w_n .
\]
In particular, equal counts imply $S_g(n)\le S_t(n)$, and
$n-C_g(n)\le n-C_t(n)$.
Define the signed count--cost slack by
$Q_n=(C_g(n)-C_t(n))w_n-(S_g(n)-S_t(n))$.
For arbitrary selections $g,t$, before imposing either greedy
admissibility or monotonicity, its exact identities are
\begin{equation}
 Q_{n+1}-Q_n
   =(C_g(n+1)-C_t(n+1))(w_{n+1}-w_n)
 \label{eq:ordered-stream-greedy-slack}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 Q_n=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}
       (C_g(j+1)-C_t(j+1))(w_{j+1}-w_j).
 \label{eq:ordered-stream-greedy-abel}
\end{equation}
For the greedy selection and an admissible competitor,
$Q_n\ge0$.  More precisely, every selected competitor index $i$
has a greedy index of the same zero-based rank at or before it:
\begin{equation}
 i\in t\ \Longrightarrow\
 \exists j\le i:\quad j\in g,\qquad C_g(j)=C_t(i).
 \label{eq:ordered-stream-greedy-rank}
\end{equation}
No monotonicity of $b_n$ or nonnegativity of $w_n$ is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $\Delta C_n=C_g(n)-C_t(n)$ and
$\Delta S_n=S_g(n)-S_t(n)$.
The two exact recursions give
\[
 \Delta S_{n+1}-\Delta S_n
       =(\Delta C_{n+1}-\Delta C_n)w_n .
\]
Substitution proves
\eqref{eq:ordered-stream-greedy-slack}; telescoping from $Q_0=0$
proves \eqref{eq:ordered-stream-greedy-abel}.  All endpoints
and signs are retained.

We prove $\Delta C_n\ge0$ and $Q_n\ge0$ simultaneously.
Both vanish at zero.  Assume both inequalities at $n$.
The count gap can become negative at $n+1$ only when it is
zero at $n$, the competitor selects $n$, and greedy does not.
But when $\Delta C_n=0$, the inequality $Q_n\ge0$ says
$S_g(n)\le S_t(n)$.  Competitor admissibility would give
\[
 S_g(n)+w_n\le S_t(n)+w_n\le b_n,
\]
so greedy must select $n$, a contradiction.  Therefore
$\Delta C_{n+1}\ge0$.  Since $w_{n+1}-w_n\ge0$, the exact
slack recursion now implies $Q_{n+1}\ge Q_n\ge0$.
This closes the joint induction and gives all count and cost
conclusions.

Finally fix $i\in t$ and put $k=C_t(i)$.
Then $C_t(i+1)=k+1$, so $C_g(i+1)\ge k+1$.
The greedy count starts at zero and changes only by zero or
one.  Consequently some $j\le i$ is selected with
$C_g(j)=k$, proving the rank assertion.
\end{proof}

For the prime construction take the stream to be the increasing
primes, $w_n=\log p_n$ and $b_n=p_n$.
This gives the rank mechanism used in
Theorem~\ref{thm:capped-prime-rank-extremum}, which in turn
feeds the full-law and adaptive finite-cap results above.
The signed slack is a finite certificate for that ordering;
it supplies no independent upper estimate for the square-root
moment and changes no zero-free boundary.

Theorem~\ref{thm:ordered-stream-greedy-optimality} is now
Lean-verified in its full finite ordered-stream form.
The module
\texttt{PrimeClassDeletion.}\allowbreak\texttt{CapacitySelection}
implements the actual greedy recursion and proves its signed invariant.
The maintained module
\texttt{CapacitySelectionFinite} identifies the recursive counts and costs
with the literal finite-set cardinalities and sums used here.
Its declarations
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \texttt{prefixSlack\_step},\qquad \texttt{prefixSlack\_sum},\\
 \texttt{greedySelection\_finset\_prefix\_optimality},\\
 \texttt{greedySelection\_finset\_rank\_le}
 \end{gathered}
\]
verify the exact difference and Abel identities, every count and cost
conclusion, nonnegative slack, and the actual selected same-rank witness.
They include $n=0$, arbitrary real budgets, and signed nondecreasing weights.
The analytic prime enumeration and the infinite geometric
probability-law consumers remain separate formalization obligations.

\subsection{Rare profiles, arithmetic records, and spectral dimension}
\label{sec:thin-prime-profile}

The full sampling law also controls ranks growing with the
sample size.  The following statements apply first to an arbitrary
fixed infinite prime class $\mathcal P=\{p_1<p_2<\cdots\}$ with
\begin{equation}
 0<\alpha<1,\qquad
 \lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{\log p_j}{\log j}=\frac1\alpha .
 \label{eq:thin-prime-index-hypothesis}
\end{equation}
Equivalently $N_{\mathcal P}(X)=X^{\alpha+o(1)}$;
bracketing $X$ between successive $p_j$ proves the equivalence.
For positive integers $m$ define
$K_{\mathcal P}(m)=\#\{p\in\mathcal P:p\mid m\}$.
The prime class is fixed before sampling $m$ uniformly from
$\{1,\ldots,N\}$.  The integer $N$ is the sample endpoint,
$k$ is a factor-count level, and $r$ below is a generating-function
argument.

For every $\sigma>\alpha$ the prime sum
$\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}p^{-\sigma}$ converges.
Indeed, choose $\epsilon>0$ with
$\sigma(1/\alpha-\epsilon)>1$ and compare
$p_j^{-\sigma}$ with $j^{-\sigma(1/\alpha-\epsilon)}$.
In particular $\sum_{\mathcal P}1/p<\infty$.
There is therefore a proper independent count law
$K_{\mathcal P}=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}B_p$, where
$\mathbb P(B_p=1)=1/p$ and $\mathbb P(B_p=0)=1-1/p$.
Only finitely many $B_p$ are nonzero almost surely.
Put
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 c_{\mathcal P}&=\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}(1-1/p)>0,\\
 P_{\mathcal P}(z)
   &=\mathbb E z^{K_{\mathcal P}}
     =\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}(1-1/p+z/p)
     =c_{\mathcal P}\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}
                              (1+z/(p-1)),\\
 b_k&=\mathbb P(K_{\mathcal P}=k),\qquad
 q_k=\mathbb P(K_{\mathcal P}\ge k).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:thin-prime-profile-definitions}
\end{equation}
All denominators $p-1$ are positive.
The products converge uniformly on compact sets, so
$P_{\mathcal P}$ is entire, with nonnegative coefficients $b_k$.
Its mean $\sum_{\mathcal P}1/p$ is finite.

\begin{theorem}[Reciprocal indices of growth and rare probabilities]
\label{thm:thin-prime-rare-profile}
For every class satisfying
\eqref{eq:thin-prime-index-hypothesis}, let
$M_{\mathcal P}(r)=\max_{|z|\le r}|P_{\mathcal P}(z)|$.
Then $M_{\mathcal P}(r)=P_{\mathcal P}(r)$ and
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{r\to\infty}
       \frac{\log\log M_{\mathcal P}(r)}{\log r}=\alpha .
 \label{eq:thin-prime-entire-index}
\end{equation}
Every $b_k$ and $q_k$ is positive, including $b_0=c_{\mathcal P}$
and $q_0=1$.  At large ranks the point mass and the tail have
the same exact logarithmic decay:
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{-\log b_k}{k\log k}
  =\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{-\log q_k}{k\log k}
  =\frac1\alpha .
 \label{eq:thin-prime-rare-coefficient-index}
\end{equation}
More explicitly, for each fixed $\alpha<\sigma<1$, put
$C_\sigma=\sigma^{-1}\sum_{\mathcal P}p^{-\sigma}<\infty$.
For every integer $k\ge2$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{c_{\mathcal P}}{\prod_{j=1}^k(p_j-1)}
 \le b_k\le q_k
 \le \exp\!\left(C_\sigma k-\frac{k}{\sigma}\log k\right).
 \label{eq:thin-prime-coefficient-bounds}
\end{equation}
No uniform constant as $\sigma\downarrow\alpha$ is asserted.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $r\ge1$, independence and nonnegative product expansion give
\[
 \log P_{\mathcal P}(r)
  =\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}
                   \log(1+(r-1)/p)
  \le C_\sigma r^\sigma.
\]
Here $\log(1+y)\le y^\sigma/\sigma$ for $y\ge0$ and
$0<\sigma<1$: for $t>0$,
$t^{1-\sigma}\le1+t$, and integration of
$(1+t)^{-1}\le t^{\sigma-1}$ proves the inequality.
For $r\ge2$, each factor with $p\le r$ is at least $3/2$,
whereas all other factors are at least one.  Thus
\[
 \log P_{\mathcal P}(r)
       \ge(\log(3/2))N_{\mathcal P}(r)
       =r^{\alpha+o(1)}.
\]
Nonnegative Taylor coefficients imply
$|P_{\mathcal P}(z)|\le P_{\mathcal P}(|z|)$ and equality
in the maximum at $z=r$.  The last two bounds show that the
lower growth index is at least $\alpha$ and its upper index
is at most every fixed $\sigma>\alpha$.
Let $\sigma\downarrow\alpha$ after taking the limit in $r$.
This proves \eqref{eq:thin-prime-entire-index}.

The event in which exactly the first $k$ Bernoulli variables
are one has probability
\[
 \left(\prod_{j=1}^kp_j^{-1}\right)
       \prod_{j>k}(1-1/p_j)
       =\frac{c_{\mathcal P}}{\prod_{j=1}^k(p_j-1)}>0.
\]
It supplies the independent positive lower bound for $b_k$.
For $r\ge1$, exponential Markov gives
$q_k\le r^{-k}P_{\mathcal P}(r)$.
Taking $r=k^{1/\sigma}$ proves the upper bound in
\eqref{eq:thin-prime-coefficient-bounds}.

Finally, \eqref{eq:thin-prime-index-hypothesis} and elementary
integral bounds for $\sum_{j\le k}\log j$ give
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^k\log(p_j-1)
       =\left(\frac1\alpha+o(1)\right)k\log k .
\]
Replacing $\log p_j$ by $\log(p_j-1)$ changes this sum by
a bounded amount, since $\sum_j1/p_j<\infty$.
The positive configuration bound therefore makes the upper
decay index of $b_k$ at most $1/\alpha$.
The exponential upper bound makes the lower decay index of
$q_k$ at least $1/\sigma$ for every $\sigma>\alpha$.
Since $b_k\le q_k$, letting $\sigma\downarrow\alpha$ proves
both limits in \eqref{eq:thin-prime-rare-coefficient-index}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Arithmetic and independent records agree]
\label{thm:thin-prime-record-duality}
For integer $N\ge1$ put
$H_{\mathcal P}(N)=\max_{1\le m\le N}K_{\mathcal P}(m)$.
On one probability space let $K_1,K_2,\ldots$ be independent
copies of $K_{\mathcal P}$ and put
$W_{\mathcal P}(N)=\max_{1\le i\le N}K_i$.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 H_{\mathcal P}(N)
       &\sim\alpha\,\frac{\log N}{\log\log N},\\
 W_{\mathcal P}(N)
       &\sim\alpha\,\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}
                                      \quad\hbox{almost surely},\\
 \frac{W_{\mathcal P}(N)}{H_{\mathcal P}(N)}
       &\longrightarrow1\quad\hbox{almost surely}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:thin-prime-record-limits}
\end{equation}
The last ratio is asserted for all sufficiently large $N$,
when its denominator is positive.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $Q_0=1$ and $Q_k=\prod_{j=1}^kp_j$.
An integer with $k$ distinct prime divisors in $\mathcal P$
is divisible by their product, which is at least $Q_k$.
Conversely $Q_k$ has exactly $k$ such prime divisors.
Consequently, including $k=0$ and both integer endpoints,
\begin{equation}
 H_{\mathcal P}(N)=\max\{k\ge0:Q_k\le N\},\qquad
 \log Q_k=\left(\frac1\alpha+o(1)\right)k\log k .
 \label{eq:thin-prime-primorial-inverse}
\end{equation}
The maximum is finite because $Q_k\ge2^k$, and tends to
infinity with $N$.  If $h=H_{\mathcal P}(N)$, then
$Q_h\le N<Q_{h+1}$, so
$\log N\sim h\log h/\alpha$ and
$\log\log N\sim\log h$.  This proves the deterministic limit.

Write $\ell_N=\log N/\log\log N$ for $N\ge3$.
For fixed $0<\epsilon<\alpha$, the tail limit in
\eqref{eq:thin-prime-rare-coefficient-index} gives, for
$k_N^\pm=\lceil(\alpha\pm\epsilon)\ell_N\rceil$,
\[
 Nq_{k_N^+}=N^{-\epsilon/\alpha+o(1)},\qquad
 Nq_{k_N^-}=N^{\epsilon/\alpha+o(1)}.
\]
A union bound and independence, respectively, give
\[
 \mathbb P(W_{\mathcal P}(N)\ge k_N^+)
       \le Nq_{k_N^+},\qquad
 \mathbb P(W_{\mathcal P}(N)<k_N^-)
       \le \exp(-Nq_{k_N^-}).
\]
Both bounds are summable along $N=2^j$.
Borel--Cantelli, followed by an intersection over a countable
sequence $\epsilon\downarrow0$, proves the almost-sure limit
along these dyadic samples.
The record $W_{\mathcal P}(N)$ is nondecreasing, $\ell_N$
is eventually increasing, and
$\ell_{2^{j+1}}/\ell_{2^j}\to1$.
Bracketing each integer $N$ between successive powers of two
therefore proves the full almost-sure limit.
Combining with the deterministic limit proves the ratio.
No independence of the arithmetic values
$K_{\mathcal P}(1),K_{\mathcal P}(2),\ldots$ is assumed.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform relative recovery at growing ranks]
\label{thm:thin-prime-growing-rank-recovery}
For integers $N\ge1$ and $k\ge0$ define the actual empirical
mass and tail by
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 b_{N,k}&=\frac1N\#\{m\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:K_{\mathcal P}(m)=k\},\\
 q_{N,k}&=\frac1N\#\{m\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:K_{\mathcal P}(m)\ge k\}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Fix
\begin{equation}
 0<c<\alpha(1-\alpha),\qquad
 0<\delta<1-\alpha-\frac c\alpha,\qquad
 L_N=\left\lfloor c\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}\right\rfloor
 \quad(N\ge3).
 \label{eq:thin-prime-growing-rank-range}
\end{equation}
Then, for all sufficiently large integer $N$,
\begin{equation}
 \max_{\substack{k\in\mathbb N_0\\0\le k\le L_N}}
 \left(\left|\frac{b_{N,k}}{b_k}-1\right|
       +\left|\frac{q_{N,k}}{q_k}-1\right|\right)
       \ll_{\mathcal P,c,\delta}N^{-\delta}.
 \label{eq:thin-prime-growing-rank-relative}
\end{equation}
The constant and the starting point are independent of $k$.
All denominators are positive; in this range the empirical
masses are also positive eventually.
For any integer sequence
$k_N\sim c\log N/\log\log N$ with
$0<c<\alpha(1-\alpha)$, one moreover has
\begin{equation}
 b_{N,k_N}=N^{-c/\alpha+o(1)},\qquad
 q_{N,k_N}=N^{-c/\alpha+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:thin-prime-growing-rank-power}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For each $\alpha<\sigma<1$ define the finite constant
\[
 B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}
       =\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}
                    \frac{1+p^{-\sigma}}{1-p^{-\sigma}}.
\]
The normalization and full sampling proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:deleted-component-sampling-law} require only
these summable Euler products, and therefore apply to this
fixed class $\mathcal P$.
For clarity, on its positive smooth state space
$\mathcal H_{\mathcal P}$ the exact finite identity is
\[
 \mu_{\mathcal P,N}(a)=
 \sum_{\substack{e\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal P}\\
                   e\ {\rm squarefree}\\ae\le N}}
      \mu_{\rm Mob}(e)\frac{\lfloor N/(ae)\rfloor}{N},
 \qquad
 \nu_{\mathcal P}(a)=\frac{c_{\mathcal P}}a .
\]
Here $\mu_{\mathcal P,N}$ is the law of the full
$\mathcal P$-component of a uniform $m\in\{1,\ldots,N\}$.
There is no coprimality restriction between $a$ and $e$.
The infinite counterpart for $\nu_{\mathcal P}$ is absolutely
convergent.  After subtraction, only the rounding and tail
remainder is estimated, using
\[
 0\le q^{-1}-N^{-1}\lfloor N/q\rfloor
          \le N^{\sigma-1}q^{-\sigma}\qquad(q\ge1).
\]
This includes the domain $q>N$, where the floor is zero.
Summing over all $a$ and all squarefree $e$ charges both the
prime-power denominator and the squarefree numerator.
Contraction to $K_{\mathcal P}$ consequently gives
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k\ge0}|b_{N,k}-b_k|
       \le B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}N^{\sigma-1},\qquad
 |q_{N,k}-q_k|\le B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}N^{\sigma-1}.
 \label{eq:thin-prime-profile-sampling-budget}
\end{equation}
This is uniform in the statistic level $k$.

The positive coefficient lower bound in
Theorem~\ref{thm:thin-prime-rare-profile} implies, for each
$\epsilon>0$, a constant $A_\epsilon>0$ such that
\[
 b_k\ge A_\epsilon
          \exp\!\left(-\left(\frac1\alpha+\epsilon\right)
                        k\log(\max\{2,k\})\right)
                                      \qquad(k\ge0).
\]
The finitely many initial ranks, including zero and one,
are absorbed using $b_k>0$.
For $0\le k\le L_N$, eventually
$k\log(\max\{2,k\})\le c\log N$.
Thus the independent positive supply and its negative
sampling allowance remain explicit:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 b_k&\ge A_\epsilon N^{-c(1/\alpha+\epsilon)},\\
 b_{N,k}&\ge
 A_\epsilon N^{-c(1/\alpha+\epsilon)}
          -B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}N^{\sigma-1}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Choose fixed $\sigma>\alpha$ and $\epsilon>0$ sufficiently
close to $\alpha$ and zero, respectively, that
\[
 \sigma-1+c(1/\alpha+\epsilon)<-\delta .
\]
Such choices exist precisely because of the strict
parameter inequalities in
\eqref{eq:thin-prime-growing-rank-range}.
Divide the two absolute sampling errors by
$b_k\ge A_\epsilon N^{-c(1/\alpha+\epsilon)}$ and
$q_k\ge b_k$.  This proves
\eqref{eq:thin-prime-growing-rank-relative}, with a bound
uniform in the entire displayed finite rank range.
It also proves eventual positivity of the empirical masses.

Finally, if $k_N\sim c\ell_N$, choose
$c<c'<\alpha(1-\alpha)$.
Then $k_N\le\lfloor c'\ell_N\rfloor$ eventually and the
relative estimate with $c'$ applies.
Since $k_N\log k_N/\log N\to c$, the exact coefficient and
tail limits prove
\eqref{eq:thin-prime-growing-rank-power}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact adaptive minimax for the factor-count record]
\label{thm:greedy-adaptive-support-minimax}
Take the fixed greedy retained and deleted classes
$\mathcal S,\mathcal D$ of the preceding subsections, and
write $\Theta=\Theta_\zeta$.
For each integer $N\ge2$, let
$\mathcal P_N=\{p\le N:p\ {\rm prime}\}$ and let
$\mathcal T\subseteq\mathcal P_N$ range over all choices
satisfying the logarithmic cap at every prime through $N$.
Put $\mathcal E_N=\mathcal P_N\setminus\mathcal T$.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \min_{\mathcal T\ {\rm admissible}}\quad
       \max_{1\le m\le N}K_{\mathcal E_N}(m)
      &=H_{\mathcal D}(N),\\
 H_{\mathcal D}(N)
      &\sim\Theta\,\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:greedy-adaptive-support-minimax}
\end{equation}
The first equality is exact at every $N$ and is attained by
$\mathcal T=\mathcal S\cap\mathcal P_N$.
A competitor may be chosen separately at every $N$, before
taking its worst case over $m$.
Among fixed global capped classes, $\mathcal S$ is the
unique class whose record is no larger than every
competitor's record at every integer cutoff.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:greedy-prime-all-scale-exponent} supplies
\eqref{eq:thin-prime-index-hypothesis} for $\mathcal D$
with $\alpha=\Theta$.
Here $1/2\le\Theta\le7/8<1$ unconditionally.
The record asymptotic follows from
Theorem~\ref{thm:thin-prime-record-duality}.

For exact finite optimality put $h=H_{\mathcal D}(N)$.
If $h=0$, every competing maximum is nonnegative.
If $h>0$, let $d_1,\ldots,d_h$ be the first $h$ deleted
primes.  Their product is at most $N$, so all lie in
$\mathcal P_N$.
The exact rank comparison of
Theorem~\ref{thm:capped-prime-rank-extremum} gives at least
$h$ primes $e_1<\cdots<e_h$ in $\mathcal E_N$, with
$e_j\le d_j$ for $1\le j\le h$.
Their product is an integer at most $N$ with exactly
$h$ prime divisors in $\mathcal E_N$.
Thus the competing record is at least $h$.
The greedy prefix is admissible and gives exactly the
original statistic on every $m\le N$, proving equality.

For a fixed different global cap, its complement has
ranks $e_j\le d_j$ for every $j$, with at least one strict
inequality.  At such a rank $h$,
$\prod_{j\le h}e_j<\prod_{j\le h}d_j$.
At the integer cutoff $N=\prod_{j\le h}e_j$, its record
is at least $h$, whereas the greedy record is below $h$.
This proves the asserted uniqueness across all cutoffs.
No uniqueness of a finite optimizer at each separate $N$
is asserted.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A fully discrete rare-count readout of the zero supremum]
\label{cor:greedy-discrete-rare-RH}
Fix a rational number $0<c<7/64$.
For integer $N\ge4$ define the entirely discrete ranks
\begin{equation}
 u_N=\lfloor\log_2N\rfloor,\qquad
 v_N=\lfloor\log_2u_N\rfloor,\qquad
 \kappa_c(N)=\left\lfloor\frac{c u_N}{v_N}\right\rfloor .
 \label{eq:greedy-binary-rank-selector}
\end{equation}
Here $u_N\ge2$ and $v_N\ge1$.
Both logarithmic floors are integer bit-length operations.
Define actual integer counts
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 C_c^=(N)&=\#\{m\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:
                     K_{\mathcal D}(m)=\kappa_c(N)\},\\
 C_c^{\ge}(N)&=\#\{m\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:
                     K_{\mathcal D}(m)\ge\kappa_c(N)\}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Then, unconditionally, for either $\star\in\{=,\ge\}$,
\begin{equation}
 C_c^\star(N)=N^{1-c/\Theta+o(1)},\qquad
 \Theta=\frac{c}{
       1-\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}
                        \frac{\log C_c^\star(N)}{\log N}} .
 \label{eq:greedy-rare-integer-counts}
\end{equation}
The counts are positive for every sufficiently large $N$.
For each fixed $1/2\le\eta<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \Theta\le\eta
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 C_c^\star(N)\ll_\epsilon N^{1-c/\eta+\epsilon}
                         \quad\hbox{for every }\epsilon>0.
 \label{eq:greedy-rare-integer-strip}
\end{equation}
At $\eta=1/2$ this is equivalent to RH.
The limiting entire-function order is $\Theta$, the
rare-probability decay index is $1/\Theta$, and both the
arithmetic record and its independent-law counterpart have
record constant $\Theta$.

In particular, for $\mathcal P=\mathcal D$, the uniform
relative estimate holds for every
$0<c<7/64$ and every $0<\delta<1/8-8c/7$.
One explicit unconditional specialization is
\begin{equation}
 \max_{\substack{k\in\mathbb N_0\\
       0\le k\le\lfloor(\log N)/(10\log\log N)\rfloor}}
 \left(\left|\frac{b_{N,k}}{b_k}-1\right|
       +\left|\frac{q_{N,k}}{q_k}-1\right|\right)
                                  \ll N^{-1/100}.
 \label{eq:greedy-rare-one-tenth-budget}
\end{equation}
The same $O(N^{-1/100})$ relative estimate holds at the
single binary rank $\kappa_{1/10}(N)$.
All these discrete counts are computable from integer $N$,
without an efficiency assertion.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $1/2\le\Theta\le\eta<1$ and
$0<c<\eta(1-\eta)$ one has
\[
 1-\Theta-\frac c\Theta
  -\left(1-\eta-\frac c\eta\right)
       =(\eta-\Theta)\left(1-\frac c{\eta\Theta}\right)
       \ge0.
\]
Indeed $c<\eta(1-\eta)\le\eta\Theta$.
Taking the installed $\eta=7/8$ proves that
$c<7/64$ is inside the actual growing-rank range, and
every $\delta<1/8-8c/7$ is allowed.

The bit-length definitions give
$u_N=(\log N)/\log2+O(1)$ and
$v_N=(\log\log N)/\log2+O(1)$.
Hence
$\kappa_c(N)\sim c\log N/\log\log N$.
Apply \eqref{eq:thin-prime-growing-rank-power} to this
sequence, for the same fixed class $\mathcal D$.
Multiplying each probability by $N$ proves both count
exponents in \eqref{eq:greedy-rare-integer-counts}.
The denominator in its recovery formula is $c/\Theta>0$.

The exact count exponent is at most $1-c/\eta$ if and
only if $\Theta\le\eta$.
A sequence with an existing logarithmic exponent has that
upper exponent precisely when it satisfies every displayed
$\epsilon$-upper bound; this proves
\eqref{eq:greedy-rare-integer-strip}.
Zero symmetry gives the RH specialization.
The remaining growth and record indices follow from the
two preceding general theorems.

For $c=1/10$ the available margin is
$1/8-8c/7=3/280>1/100$, proving
\eqref{eq:greedy-rare-one-tenth-budget}.
For the binary rank choose a fixed $c'>1/10$ sufficiently
close to $1/10$ that
$1/100<1/8-8c'/7$.
Eventually
$\kappa_{1/10}(N)\le
 \lfloor c'\log N/\log\log N\rfloor$,
so the same uniform theorem proves its pointwise relative
estimate.
Finally the already proved finite decidability of the
greedy class determines its prime membership through $N$.
Bit lengths, rational floors and integer factorization
then compute the two displayed finite counts.
\end{proof}

For $c=1/10$, the installed boundary supplies count upper
exponent $31/35$; a boundary $3/4$ would supply $13/15$,
and RH supplies $4/5$, each with an arbitrary positive
$\epsilon$ allowance.
No independent arithmetic estimate reducing that count
exponent is proved here.  The certified zero-free boundary
remains $7/8$.
The growing-rank interval is a proved sufficient range;
no failure of relative approximation outside it is claimed.

The Bernoulli-product and ordinary prime-factor-count
background is classical; compare
\cite[Proposition~3.1 and \S4]{KN}.
That proposition treats a divergent sum of Bernoulli
parameters.  The present class has a finite parameter sum,
a fixed entire limiting law, and rare levels of order
$\log N/\log\log N$ tied to its all-scale prime exponent.
The independent-record argument is also elementary.
The specific conclusions here are the spectral
interpretation for the greedy class, its exact adaptive
record minimum, and the uniform relative arithmetic
recovery.  No literature-priority claim is made.

These are full ordinary proofs.  No new Lean verification
is claimed for the countable Euler law, the coefficient
asymptotics, the growing-rank transfer or the independent
record limit.
The maintained finite greedy count--cost and rank
comparisons are compiled inputs to the cap mechanism.
Concrete remaining formalization tasks are countable
Euler-law normalization, its elementary-symmetric
coefficient limits, the uniform asymptotic division, and
the dyadic Borel--Cantelli consumer.

\subsection{Affine samples, optimal prime filters, and a disappearing law}
\label{sec:affine-thin-prime-filters}

Fix a prime class $\mathcal P$ satisfying
\eqref{eq:thin-prime-index-hypothesis}, with exponent $\alpha$.
Write $\mathcal H_{\mathcal P}$ for its positive smooth integers and
$A_{\mathcal P}(m)=\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}p^{v_p(m)}$.
All samples in this subsection are \emph{integer} samples, different
from the earlier affine prime-pair ensembles.  The local congruence
count is classical.  The conclusions concern uniform moving filters,
their optimal product budget, and rare events whose exponent survives
convergence of the whole count law to zero.
The comparison with the divergent-mean framework of \cite{KN} made
above still applies; no literature priority claim is required.

\begin{theorem}[Full affine law with its forced component]
\label{thm:affine-thin-full-law}
Let $A,q,M$ be arbitrary positive integers, and sample
$F(n)=A+qn$ uniformly over $0\le n<M$.  Put
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 Y=A+q(M-1),\quad g=\gcd(A,q),\quad
 A_0=A/g,\quad q_0=q/g,\quad R=Y/g,\\
 \mathcal V=\mathcal P\setminus\{p:p\mid q_0\},\quad
 \mathcal Q=\mathcal P\setminus\{p:p\mid q\},\quad
 r=K_{\mathcal P}(g).
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:affine-thin-domains}
\end{equation}
Let $Z_{\mathcal V}$ have the full component law
$\mathbb P(Z_{\mathcal V}=a)=c_{\mathcal V}/a$ on
$\mathcal H_{\mathcal V}$.
For each fixed $\alpha<\sigma<1$ the actual full component law
$\mu_{A,q,M}$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \|\mu_{A,q,M}
       -\operatorname{Law}(A_{\mathcal P}(g)Z_{\mathcal V})\|_1
   &\le B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}R^\sigma/M,\\
 d_{\rm TV}(\mu_{A,q,M},
       \operatorname{Law}(A_{\mathcal P}(g)Z_{\mathcal V}))
   &\le\min\{1,B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}R^\sigma/(2M)\}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:affine-thin-full-budget}
\end{equation}
The induced model for the distinct count is exactly
$r+K_{\mathcal Q}$, with independent Bernoulli probabilities
$1/p$ for $p\in\mathcal Q$.
The valuation mask uses $q_0$, while the count mask uses $q$.

Every small-prime exception is included.  At a prime $p\mid q$,
write $a=v_p(A)$ and $b=v_p(q)$.
If $a<b$, its valuation is forced to equal $a$.
If $a\ge b$, its model is $b+G_p$, where
$\mathbb P(G_p=j)=(1-1/p)p^{-j}$ for $j\ge0$.
At $p\nmid q$ it is $G_p$.  These model coordinates are independent.
This includes $p=2$, $M=1$, and $g>1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Reduction by $g$ gives $F_0(n)=A_0+q_0n$, with
$\gcd(A_0,q_0)=1$ and $1\le F_0(n)\le R$.
In particular $R=A_0+q_0(M-1)\ge M$.
No prime dividing $q_0$ divides any $F_0(n)$.
For $d\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal V}$ let
$C_d=\#\{0\le n<M:d\mid F_0(n)\}$.
Since $\gcd(d,q_0)=1$, its congruence is one residue class
modulo $d$, so $|C_d/M-1/d|\le1/M$.
If $d>R$, then $C_d=0$.  Hence for every such $d$,
\begin{equation}
 \left|C_d/M-1/d\right|
       \le (R^\sigma/M)d^{-\sigma}.
 \label{eq:affine-thin-rounding}
\end{equation}
For $d\le R$ use $1\le(R/d)^\sigma$.
For $d>R$ use $M\le R\le R^\sigma d^{1-\sigma}$.

For each $a\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal V}$ the exact identity is
\[
 \mathbb P(A_{\mathcal V}(F_0(n))=a)
  =\sum_{\substack{e\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal V}\\
                   e\ {\rm squarefree}\\ae\le R}}
           \mu_{\rm Mob}(e)C_{ae}/M .
\]
There is no restriction $\gcd(a,e)=1$.
Its model counterpart is the absolutely convergent sum
$c_{\mathcal V}/a=\sum_e\mu_{\rm Mob}(e)/(ae)$ over
all squarefree $e\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal V}$.
Subtract these complete signed identities.
Only their rounding and omitted-tail remainder is bounded by
\eqref{eq:affine-thin-rounding}.
Summing that bound over all $a$ and squarefree $e$ gives
\[
 \frac{R^\sigma}{M}
 \sum_{a\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal V}}a^{-\sigma}
 \sum_{\substack{e\in\mathcal H_{\mathcal V}\\e\ {\rm squarefree}}}
                      e^{-\sigma}
 =\frac{R^\sigma}{M}
       \prod_{p\in\mathcal V}
                  \frac{1+p^{-\sigma}}{1-p^{-\sigma}}
 \le\frac{R^\sigma}{M}B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}.
\]
All series converge since $\sigma>\alpha$.
Multiplication by $A_{\mathcal P}(g)$ is injective and preserves
both distances.
The identity
$A_{\mathcal P}(F(n))=A_{\mathcal P}(g)A_{\mathcal V}(F_0(n))$
proves \eqref{eq:affine-thin-full-budget}.

For the distinct count, primes dividing $g$ are already present.
A prime dividing $q$ but not $g$ never occurs.
All other coordinates are the geometric presence indicators,
with probability $1/p$.  Thus both the arithmetic statistic and
its model equal $r$ plus the count from $\mathcal Q$.
Comparing $v_p(A)$ and $v_p(q)$ after division by $g$ gives
the full local classification, with no omitted exceptional prime.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform positive supply after a moving finite filter]
\label{lem:masked-thin-positive-supply}
For finite $S\subset\mathcal P$ put
$b_k^S=\mathbb P(K_{\mathcal P\setminus S}=k)$ and
$q_k^S=\mathbb P(K_{\mathcal P\setminus S}\ge k)$.
If $|S|\le J$, then
\begin{equation}
 b_k^S\ge c_{\mathcal P}p_{J+k}^{-k}\ (k\ge1),\quad
 b_0^S\ge c_{\mathcal P},\quad
 b_k^S\le q_k^S\le q_k\ (k\ge0).
 \label{eq:masked-thin-positive-supply}
\end{equation}
For $S=\mathcal P\cap\{p:p\mid q\}$, integer $1\le q\le Y$,
fixed $c>0$ and $\epsilon>0$, a constant $C_\epsilon>0$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \min_{0\le k\le\lfloor c\log Y/\log\log Y\rfloor}b_k^S
       \ge C_\epsilon Y^{-c/\alpha-\epsilon}
 \label{eq:masked-thin-uniform-supply}
\end{equation}
for all sufficiently large $Y$, uniformly in $q$.
Individual point masses need not be monotone under filtering.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The $i$th surviving prime $t_i$ is at most $p_{J+i}$.
The event in which exactly its first $k$ coordinates are one has
probability $c_{\mathcal P\setminus S}/\prod_{i\le k}(t_i-1)$,
and $c_{\mathcal P\setminus S}\ge c_{\mathcal P}>0$.
This proves the lower bounds.
Bernoulli coupling with the original law proves the tail upper bound.

For the second assertion,
$J=K_{\mathcal P}(q)\le H_{\mathcal P}(Y)
=O(\log Y/\log\log Y)$ by
\eqref{eq:thin-prime-primorial-inverse}.
Put $L=\lfloor c\log Y/\log\log Y\rfloor$.
Bounding $J+L$ by a fixed multiple of $\log Y/\log\log Y$
and using \eqref{eq:thin-prime-index-hypothesis} gives
$L\log p_{J+L}\le(c/\alpha+o(1))\log Y$, uniformly.
For $1\le k\le L$, $p_{J+k}^{k}\le p_{J+L}^{L}$;
the zero-rank bound is independent of $J$.
This proves \eqref{eq:masked-thin-uniform-supply}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform affine recovery of rare profiles]
\label{thm:affine-thin-rare-recovery}
Fix $0<h\le1$, $0\le\gamma<1$, $h+\gamma\le1$,
and $0<\rho\le1$, with $h>(1-\gamma)\alpha$.
Use \eqref{eq:affine-thin-domains} and suppose
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 M\ge\rho Y^h,\quad g\ge Y^\gamma,\quad
 0<c<\alpha\{h-(1-\gamma)\alpha\},\\
 0<\delta<h-(1-\gamma)\alpha-c/\alpha .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:affine-thin-rare-range}
\end{equation}
Write $b_{A,q,M}(j),q_{A,q,M}(j)$ for the empirical point
mass and tail of $K_{\mathcal P}(F(n))$.
With $S=\mathcal P\cap\{p:p\mid q\}$ one has
\begin{equation}
 \max_{0\le k\le\lfloor c\log Y/\log\log Y\rfloor}
 \left(
 \left|\frac{b_{A,q,M}(r+k)}{b_k^S}-1\right|
 +\left|\frac{q_{A,q,M}(r+k)}{q_k^S}-1\right|
 \right)\ll Y^{-\delta}.
 \label{eq:affine-thin-relative-recovery}
\end{equation}
The constant and starting point depend only on
$\mathcal P,h,\gamma,\rho,c,\delta$.
For every rank sequence $k_Y\sim c\log Y/\log\log Y$
in this strict range, both empirical probabilities at $r+k_Y$
equal $Y^{-c/\alpha+o(1)}$, uniformly over these families.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For large $Y$, $M\ge2$ and hence $q\le Y$.
The full-law error contracts to every count event with allowance
$B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}R^\sigma/M$.
The independent positive supply and full negative allowance are
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 b_k^S&\ge C_\epsilon Y^{-c/\alpha-\epsilon},\\
 b_{A,q,M}(r+k)&\ge C_\epsilon Y^{-c/\alpha-\epsilon}
                      -B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}R^\sigma/M .
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:affine-thin-signed-consumer}
\end{equation}
Since $R\le Y^{1-\gamma}$ and $M\ge\rho Y^h$,
the relative error is at most a constant times
$Y^{(1-\gamma)\sigma-h+c/\alpha+\epsilon}$.
Choose fixed $\alpha<\sigma<1$ and $\epsilon>0$ close
enough to $\alpha$ and zero that this exponent is below $-\delta$.
The strict range permits this choice.
The same division works for tails since $q_k^S\ge b_k^S$.

At $k_Y\sim c\log Y/\log\log Y$, the positive supply and
the original tail upper bound
\eqref{eq:thin-prime-coefficient-bounds} give
$b_{k_Y}^S,q_{k_Y}^S=Y^{-c/\alpha+o(1)}$ uniformly in $q\le Y$.
In the upper bound take the limit first and then
$\sigma\downarrow\alpha$.
Choose a slightly larger permitted $c'$ to contain these ranks,
and apply the relative estimate.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact optimal filter under a product budget]
\label{thm:thin-prime-product-filter-optimality}
For integer $B\ge1$ put $J=H_{\mathcal P}(B)$ and
$S_*=\{p_1,\ldots,p_J\}$, empty when $J=0$.
Among all finite $S\subset\mathcal P$ with $\prod_{p\in S}p\le B$,
the set $S_*$ minimizes
$\mathbb P(K_{\mathcal P\setminus S}\ge k)$ for every integer
$k\ge0$ simultaneously.
For each $k\ge1$ it is the unique minimizer.
This is a tail-order assertion, not minimization of each point mass.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Every feasible $S$ has $|S|\le J$, since any $j$ primes
of $\mathcal P$ have product at least $\prod_{i\le j}p_i$.
Its successive surviving primes satisfy $t_i\le p_{J+i}$.
Using independent uniforms $U_i$, couple the indicators
$\mathbf1_{\{U_i\le1/t_i\}}$ and
$\mathbf1_{\{U_i\le1/p_{J+i}\}}$.
Their sums are finite almost surely and ordered.
The prefix $S_*$ is feasible by the exact primorial inverse,
so this proves every tail inequality.

If $S\ne S_*$, at least one coordinate satisfies $t_i<p_{J+i}$;
otherwise the survivor sets and hence the deleted sets coincide.
At that coordinate there is a positive probability of selecting
only the larger indicator.
Independently, the other coordinates of the smaller law have
positive probability of summing to $k-1$: select any $k-1$
of them and set all others to zero.
The infinite product of their failure probabilities is positive.
On this event the smaller total is $k-1$ and the larger at least $k$.
Thus the tail inequality is strict for every $k\ge1$.
For $k=0$ all tails are one.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp budget threshold for erasing a rare-event exponent]
\label{thm:thin-prime-filter-budget-phase}
Let $X\to\infty$ through integers, let $B_X\ge3$ be integers,
and suppose
\[
 \frac{\log\log B_X}{\log\log X}\longrightarrow s,
 \qquad 0\le s<\infty.
\]
Let $k_X\sim c\log X/\log\log X$ be positive integers,
where $c>0$ is fixed.
Take minima over the finite nonempty family
$\mathfrak S_X=\{S\subset\mathcal P:\prod_{p\in S}p\le B_X\}$.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{-\log\min_{S\in\mathfrak S_X}b_{k_X}^S}{\log X}
     &\longrightarrow c\Phi_\alpha(s),\\
 \frac{-\log\min_{S\in\mathfrak S_X}q_{k_X}^S}{\log X}
     &\longrightarrow c\Phi_\alpha(s),\\
 \Phi_\alpha(s)&=1+(1/\alpha-1)\max\{1,s\}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:thin-prime-filter-budget-phase}
\end{equation}
The prefix filter $S_*=\{p_1,\ldots,p_{H_{\mathcal P}(B_X)}\}$
attains both limiting exponents.
For $s\le1$, every feasible filter has point mass and tail
$X^{-c/\alpha+o(1)}$, uniformly over all filters.
In particular every polynomial product budget preserves this exponent.
Some larger budgets also have $s=1$; polynomial growth is not
claimed to be a sharp boundary within that plateau.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Every prime in a feasible nonempty set is at most $B_X$.
Thus the family is finite; it contains the empty set, and all
of its count point masses are positive.
Put $J=H_{\mathcal P}(B_X)$ and $k=k_X$.
The primorial inverse gives
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\log(1+J)}{\log\log X}\longrightarrow s.
 \label{eq:thin-prime-filter-budget-rank}
\end{equation}
For $s>0$, use
$H_{\mathcal P}(B)\sim\alpha\log B/\log\log B$.
For $s=0$, the bound $0\le J\le\log B_X/\log2$
also covers bounded budgets.

If $s\le1$, then $\log(J+k)/\log\log X\to1$.
The uniform bound $b_k^S\ge c_{\mathcal P}p_{J+k}^{-k}$
gives probability at least $X^{-c/\alpha+o(1)}$.
The unfiltered bound $q_k^S\le q_k=X^{-c/\alpha+o(1)}$
gives the matching upper probability.
Both bounds are independent of $S$, proving all plateau assertions.

If $s>1$, then $J/k\to\infty$.
For every feasible filter, its first $J$ surviving primes
are at most $p_{2J}$.
Retain all ways of selecting $k$ of these $J$ coordinates
in the positive coefficient expansion:
\begin{equation}
 b_k^S\ge c_{\mathcal P}{J\choose k}p_{2J}^{-k}
       \ge c_{\mathcal P}(J/k)^kp_{2J}^{-k}.
 \label{eq:thin-prime-filter-binomial-supply}
\end{equation}
Indeed, each factor $(J-i)/(k-i)$ in the binomial product
is at least $J/k$ for $0\le i<k$.
Since
$\log p_{2J}=(1/\alpha+o(1))\log J$,
$\log J=(s+o(1))\log\log X$, and
$\log k=(1+o(1))\log\log X$,
this gives upper decay exponent
$c\{1+s(1/\alpha-1)\}$ uniformly over all filters.

For the prefix filter the remaining mean satisfies
\[
 \lambda_J=\sum_{j>J}p_j^{-1}
                   =J^{\,1-1/\alpha+o(1)}.
\]
For the upper bound compare with
$\sum_{j>J}j^{-1/\alpha+\epsilon}$ for fixed sufficiently
small $\epsilon>0$.
For the lower bound retain $J<j\le2J$ and use $J/p_{2J}$.
Letting $\epsilon\downarrow0$ proves the exponent.
Its factorial moment gives
\[
 q_k^{S_*}\le
  \mathbb E{K_{\mathcal P\setminus S_*}\choose k}
              \le\lambda_J^k/k!.
\]
Monotone convergence justifies the infinite nonnegative expansion.
The estimate $\log k!=k\log k-k+O(\log k)$ now makes
the lower decay exponent of this tail at least
$c\{1+s(1/\alpha-1)\}$.
Since $b_k^{S_*}\le q_k^{S_*}$, the prefix supplies the
matching upper bounds for both minimum probabilities.
Together with the uniform positive supply this proves both limits.
Its exact tail minimization was proved above.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[An optimal disappearing law still carries the zero index]
\label{cor:primorial-filter-zero-index}
Let $\mathcal P$ satisfy \eqref{eq:thin-prime-index-hypothesis}
and fix $0<b<1-\alpha$.
For sufficiently large integer $X$ put
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 B_X=\lfloor X^b\rfloor,\quad
 J_X=H_{\mathcal P}(B_X),\quad Q_X=\prod_{j\le J_X}p_j,\quad
 M_X=1+\left\lfloor\frac{X-1}{Q_X}\right\rfloor,\\
 e_X=\frac1{M_X}\#\{0\le n<M_X:
                         K_{\mathcal P}(1+Q_Xn)\ge1\}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:primorial-filter-arithmetic-sample}
\end{equation}
Let $E_X$ be the minimum nonzero-count probability over all
unit residue classes $a\bmod q$ with $1\le q\le B_X$,
sampled uniformly over
$\{1\le m\le X:m\equiv a\pmod q\}$.
These finite sample sets are nonempty.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 e_X,\ E_X
  &=\left(\frac{\log X}{\log\log X}\right)^{1-1/\alpha+o(1)},\\
 \operatorname{Law}(K_{\mathcal P}(1+Q_Xn))
  &\longrightarrow\delta_0\quad\hbox{in total variation}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:primorial-filter-vanishing-profile}
\end{equation}
Thus the explicit progression is asymptotically optimal for
suppressing nonzero counts at this modulus budget, but does
not remove their logarithmic exponent.

For the computable greedy deleted class $\mathcal D$, take
$b=1/16$ and $\Theta=\Theta_\zeta$.  One has
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{\log e_X}{\log\log X}
 =\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac{\log E_X}{\log\log X}
 =1-\frac1\Theta,\\
 {\rm RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \text{for every $\epsilon>0$, }\
 e_X\ll_\epsilon(\log X)^{-1+\epsilon}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:primorial-filter-RH}
\end{equation}
The same equivalence holds with $E_X$.
For the binary rank selector $\kappa_{1/20}(X)$ of
\eqref{eq:greedy-binary-rank-selector}, the actual progression
point mass and tail moreover satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P_X(K_{\mathcal D}=\kappa_{1/20}(X))
       &=X^{-1/(20\Theta)+o(1)},\\
 \mathbb P_X(K_{\mathcal D}\ge\kappa_{1/20}(X))
       &=X^{-1/(20\Theta)+o(1)}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:primorial-filter-rare-zero-index}
\end{equation}
Both have relative error $O(X^{-1/200})$ from the model with
its first $J_X$ deleted-prime coordinates removed.
In this specialization, integer $X$ and the integer root
$B_X=\lfloor X^{1/16}\rfloor$ give computable finite cutoffs,
progressions and counts.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Use representatives $1\le a\le q$, $\gcd(a,q)=1$.
For $q\le X^b$, the sample size is $M_a=X/q+O(1)$,
hence at least $X/(2q)$ eventually, uniformly in $a,q$.
The largest sample is at most $X$ and the gcd shift is zero.
For any fixed $\alpha<\sigma<1-b$, the full affine law gives
the uniform event-probability error
\begin{equation}
 O_{\mathcal P,\sigma}(X^{\sigma+b-1})
 \label{eq:primorial-filter-uniform-error}
\end{equation}
from each masked Bernoulli model.

Its erased set is $S=\mathcal P\cap\{p:p\mid q\}$,
with prime product at most $q\le B_X$.
The optimality theorem minimizes the model probability
of a nonzero count at $S_*=\{p_1,\ldots,p_{J_X}\}$.
This filter is realized by the allowed modulus $Q_X\le B_X$
and residue one.
Thus both $E_X$ and $e_X$ differ from this optimal model
probability by \eqref{eq:primorial-filter-uniform-error}.
Taking a minimum changes an additive uniform error by at most
that error; no exact finite empirical optimizer is asserted.

The primorial inverse gives
$J_X\sim\alpha b\log X/\log\log X$.
Writing $\lambda_J=\sum_{j>J}p_j^{-1}$, one has
\[
 1-\prod_{j>J}(1-1/p_j)\sim\lambda_J
                         =J^{1-1/\alpha+o(1)}.
\]
Indeed, $\lambda_J\to0$ and
$\sum_{j>J}p_j^{-2}\le p_{J+1}^{-1}\lambda_J=o(\lambda_J)$.
Expand the logarithm of the product and then its exponential.
This positive logarithmic-scale mass dominates the polynomial
error \eqref{eq:primorial-filter-uniform-error}, proving the
two asymptotics in \eqref{eq:primorial-filter-vanishing-profile}.
The total variation distance of a nonnegative integer law
from $\delta_0$ is its probability of being nonzero,
which also proves the asserted convergence.

For $\mathcal D$, the proved range
$1/2\le\Theta\le7/8$ makes $b=1/16<1-\Theta$ admissible.
The logarithmic limits follow because
$\log(\log X/\log\log X)/\log\log X\to1$.
Their common value is at least $-1$, with equality precisely
when $\Theta=1/2$, equivalently RH.
The upper bounds for every $\epsilon>0$ force the limit
to be at most $-1$, and equality implies all those bounds.
This proves both equivalences.

The model filter has polynomial budget, so the phase theorem
preserves its rare exponent.
For the relative error apply the affine rare-recovery theorem:
the largest sample satisfies $X-Q_X<Y_X\le X$, so $Y_X\sim X$,
and $M_X\ge\frac12Y_X^{15/16}$ eventually.
Take $h=15/16$, $\gamma=0$, $\rho=1/2$.
For $c=1/20$ the available margin is
\[
 \frac{15}{16}-\Theta-\frac{1}{20\Theta}
 \ge\frac{15}{16}-\frac78-\frac{1/20}{7/8}
 =\frac3{560}>\frac1{200}.
\]
The first inequality holds since
$t\mapsto15/16-t-1/(20t)$ is decreasing on $[1/2,7/8]$.
Choose $c'>1/20$ close enough that this margin still
exceeds $1/200$.
The binary rank is asymptotic to
$(1/20)\log X/\log\log X$ and lies in the $c'$ range.
The relative error and both rare exponents follow.
The integer sixteenth root and finite tests for the computable
class implement the sample without a decision about a real cutoff.
\end{proof}

These full affine and optimization results have ordinary proofs;
they are not advertised as Lean declarations.
The RH equivalence is not an independent arithmetic upper estimate.
The installed $\Theta\le7/8$ gives the upper exponent $-1/7$
for $e_X,E_X$ on the logarithmic scale; RH requires $-1$.
No improvement of the certified zero-free boundary is claimed.

\subsection{Conditional prime localization and reconstruction from the zero bin}
\label{sec:conditional-thin-prime-reconstruction}

Continue with the explicit progression of
\eqref{eq:primorial-filter-arithmetic-sample}, for a fixed
$0<b<1-\alpha$.
Let $\mathbb P_X$ be uniform measure on its $M_X$ integers and put
\[
 C_X=A_{\mathcal P}(1+Q_Xn),\qquad
 K_X=K_{\mathcal P}(1+Q_Xn),\qquad
 b_{X,k}=\mathbb P_X(K_X=k).
\]
Thus $C_X>1$ is exactly the event $K_X\ge1$.
Abbreviate $J=J_X$ and write
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \lambda_J=\sum_{j>J}p_j^{-1},\quad
 c_J=\prod_{j>J}(1-1/p_j),\quad
 W_J=\sum_{j>J}(p_j-1)^{-1},\\
 S_{2,J}=\sum_{j>J}(p_j-1)^{-2},\qquad
 v=1/\alpha-1>0 .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:conditional-thin-parameters}
\end{equation}
All sums converge and all displayed normalizations are positive.
The previous proofs give
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 J\sim\alpha b\,\frac{\log X}{\log\log X},\quad
 p_{J+1}=J^{1/\alpha+o(1)},\quad
 \lambda_J=J^{-v+o(1)},\quad
 1-c_J\sim\lambda_J,\\
 d_{\rm TV}(\operatorname{Law}(C_X),
       \operatorname{Law}(Z_J))\le\Delta_X,\qquad
 \Delta_X\ll X^{-\xi},\quad \xi=1-b-\sigma>0,
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:conditional-thin-model-budget}
\end{equation}
where $\alpha<\sigma<1-b$ is fixed and
$Z_J=\prod_{j>J}p_j^{G_{p_j}}$ has the full independent
geometric law.  The last estimate is the full-component estimate
of Theorem~\ref{thm:affine-thin-full-law}, not just convergence
of count distributions.  Since $\Delta_X=o(\lambda_J)$,
both the zero and nonzero empirical bins are positive eventually.
Classical Poisson approximation is background; the relative
growing-rank statement and its arithmetic normalization below
are proved directly.

\begin{theorem}[A growing rare profile determined by the zero bin]
\label{thm:zero-bin-poisson-reconstruction}
Define the parameter from the actual finite sample alone by
\begin{equation}
 \widehat\lambda_X=-\log b_{X,0}.
 \label{eq:zero-bin-poisson-parameter}
\end{equation}
It is finite and positive for large $X$, and
$\widehat\lambda_X\sim\lambda_J$.
For every fixed $0<\epsilon<1/2$,
\begin{equation}
 \max_{0\le k\le\lfloor J^{1/2-\epsilon}\rfloor}
 \left|
 \frac{b_{X,k}}
      {e^{-\widehat\lambda_X}\widehat\lambda_X^k/k!}-1
 \right|
       \le J^{-2\epsilon+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:zero-bin-relative-poisson-window}
\end{equation}
In particular, the whole displayed growing collection of rare
point masses is recoverable to relative accuracy from the zero
bin.  This asserts a relative point-mass estimate, beyond the
unconditional convergence of both laws to $\delta_0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For the model let $\beta_{J,k}=\mathbb P(K_{\mathcal P\setminus
\{p_1,\ldots,p_J\}}=k)$.
With $w_j=(p_j-1)^{-1}$ for $j>J$ its generating function is
$c_J\prod_{j>J}(1+w_jz)$.
Normalize the positive weights $w_j$ to a probability law by
division by $W_J$.
In $k$ independent draws from that law, the probability that
all labels are distinct is exactly
$k!\beta_{J,k}/(c_JW_J^k)$.
The union bound on collisions of pairs therefore proves the
finite quantitative inequality
\begin{equation}
 0\le1-\frac{k!\beta_{J,k}}{c_JW_J^k}
       \le {k\choose2}\frac{S_{2,J}}{W_J^2}
                           \qquad(k\ge0).
 \label{eq:zero-bin-collision-identity}
\end{equation}
For $k=0$ the middle expression is zero.
The countable formula follows either by the independent-draw
construction or by monotone convergence from finite supports.

For large $J$, $p_{J+1}\to\infty$, so
\[
 W_J=\lambda_J(1+O(p_{J+1}^{-1})),\qquad
 S_{2,J}\ll p_{J+1}^{-1}\lambda_J .
\]
Put $t_J=-\log c_J$.
Expansion of $-\log(1-x)$, with $0\le x\le1/p_{J+1}$,
also gives
\[
 t_J=\lambda_J+O(p_{J+1}^{-1}\lambda_J),\qquad
 |W_J-t_J|\ll p_{J+1}^{-1}\lambda_J .
\]
The full sampling bound gives
$|b_{X,0}-c_J|\le\Delta_X$.
As both terms tend to one, the logarithm is Lipschitz on
their common eventual interval $[1/2,1]$, whence
\[
 |\widehat\lambda_X-t_J|\ll\Delta_X,\quad
 \widehat\lambda_X\sim\lambda_J,\quad
 \left|\frac{W_J}{\widehat\lambda_X}-1\right|
       \ll p_{J+1}^{-1}+\Delta_X/\lambda_J .
\]
Furthermore $c_J/b_{X,0}=1+O(\Delta_X)$.

Set $L=\lfloor J^{1/2-\epsilon}\rfloor$.
The collision allowance in \eqref{eq:zero-bin-collision-identity}
is at most
\[
 L^2S_{2,J}/W_J^2
       \le J^{1-2\epsilon}
              J^{-1/\alpha+v+o(1)}
       =J^{-2\epsilon+o(1)}.
\]
The normalization error after raising to any $k\le L$ is
bounded by a constant times
$L(p_{J+1}^{-1}+\Delta_X/\lambda_J)$.
Its first part is
$J^{1/2-\epsilon-1/\alpha+o(1)}
=o(J^{-2\epsilon})$, since $\epsilon<1/2$ and $\alpha<1$;
its second part decays faster than every fixed power of $J$.
The elementary estimate
$|(1+u)^k-1|\le e^{2k|u|}-1$ for $|u|\le1/2$
justifies that normalization bound.
Using $e^{-\widehat\lambda_X}=b_{X,0}$,
these estimates prove the claimed relative comparison first
for the model masses $\beta_{J,k}$.

It remains to transfer relative, rather than absolute, accuracy.
Let
$\pi_{X,k}=e^{-\widehat\lambda_X}
\widehat\lambda_X^k/k!$.
Since $\widehat\lambda_X=J^{-v+o(1)}<1$ eventually,
its minimum on $0\le k\le L$ is at $L$, and
\[
 -\log\pi_{X,L}=O(L\log J)=o(\log X).
\]
Thus $\min_{k\le L}\pi_{X,k}=X^{-o(1)}$.
The full sampling error $|b_{X,k}-\beta_{J,k}|\le\Delta_X$
divided by this minimum is at most $X^{-\xi+o(1)}$,
again smaller than every fixed negative power of $J$.
This completes \eqref{eq:zero-bin-relative-poisson-window}.
At $k=0$ the empirical ratio is exactly one.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The conditional surviving component is one localized prime]
\label{thm:conditional-thin-single-prime-localization}
Under the same hypotheses,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb P_X(C_X\ {\rm is\ prime}\mid C_X>1)
       =1-O(J^{-v+o(1)}).
 \label{eq:conditional-thin-single-prime}
\end{equation}
Moreover, for every fixed $\eta>0$, put
$t=\min\{1,\alpha\eta/2\}>0$.
For all sufficiently large integer $X$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb P_X\left(
 \left|\frac{\log C_X}{\log\log X}-\frac1\alpha\right|>\eta
                 \,\middle|\, C_X>1\right)
       \le J^{-vt+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:conditional-thin-prime-size}
\end{equation}
Consequently $\log C_X/\log\log X\to1/\alpha$
in conditional probability, even though
$\mathbb P_X(C_X>1)\to0$.
Prime powers and multiple distinct factors are both included
in the error.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For the full model $Z_J$, the probability of at least two
distinct active prime coordinates is at most $\lambda_J^2/2$,
by its second factorial moment.
The probability of a repeated prime factor is at most
$\sum_{j>J}p_j^{-2}\le p_{J+1}^{-1}\lambda_J$,
because $\mathbb P(G_p\ge2)=p^{-2}$ exactly.
These exhaust the ways a nontrivial component can fail to
be a prime.  Since $\mathbb P(Z_J>1)=1-c_J\sim\lambda_J$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb P(Z_J\ {\rm composite}\mid Z_J>1)
       \ll\lambda_J+p_{J+1}^{-1}
       =J^{-v+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:conditional-thin-composite-allowance}
\end{equation}

Here conditioning must be justified quantitatively.
If two probability measures $\mu,\nu$ have total variation
at most $\Delta$ and $\nu(E)>\Delta$, then
\begin{equation}
 d_{\rm TV}(\mu(\,\cdot\mid E),\nu(\,\cdot\mid E))
       \le\frac{2\Delta}{\nu(E)-\Delta}.
 \label{eq:conditional-thin-TV-transfer}
\end{equation}
Indeed, for each measurable $B\subseteq E$, subtract
$\mu(B)/\mu(E)$ and $\nu(B)/\nu(E)$, keeping the numerator
difference and normalization difference separately.
Each contributes at most $\Delta/\mu(E)$, and
$\mu(E)\ge\nu(E)-\Delta$.
Apply this with the full component laws and $E=\{a>1\}$.
The conditional error is
$O(\Delta_X/\lambda_J)=X^{-\xi+o(1)}$, smaller than every
fixed negative power of $J$.
This proves \eqref{eq:conditional-thin-single-prime} for
the actual arithmetic samples.

Every nontrivial model component is at least $p_{J+1}$.
The same is exactly true for every actual sample, since
$1+Q_Xn$ is coprime to all of $p_1,\ldots,p_J$.
As $\log p_{J+1}/\log\log X\to1/\alpha$, the lower
size exception in \eqref{eq:conditional-thin-prime-size}
is eventually empty.

For the upper exception retain the full composite allowance
and consider the remaining case of a single prime.
Let $T=\lfloor J^{1+t}\rfloor$.
The unconditional probability that this prime has label
$j>T$ is at most $\lambda_T$.
The already proved mean exponent gives
\[
 \frac{\lambda_T}{1-c_J}
       =J^{-vt+o(1)}.
\]
If $J<j\le T$, then
\[
 \frac{\log p_j}{\log\log X}
       \le\frac{1+t}{\alpha}+o(1)
       \le\frac1\alpha+\frac\eta2+o(1)
       <\frac1\alpha+\eta
\]
eventually, by the choice of $t$.
Thus the model upper exception is bounded by the sum of
$J^{-v+o(1)}$ and $J^{-vt+o(1)}$.
Since $t\le1$, the latter controls both.
Transfer once more with
\eqref{eq:conditional-thin-TV-transfer}; its polynomially
decaying error is negligible.
This proves the full conditional bound with all multiplicities
retained.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Computable conditional quantiles recover the zero supremum]
\label{cor:conditional-thin-quantile-RH}
For fixed $0<\tau<1$ define the lower conditional quantile
\begin{equation}
 m_\tau(X)=
 \min\{a\in\{1,\ldots,X\}:
       \mathbb P_X(C_X\le a\mid C_X>1)\ge\tau\}.
 \label{eq:conditional-thin-quantile}
\end{equation}
It exists for all large integer $X$ and satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\log m_\tau(X)}{\log\log X}\longrightarrow\frac1\alpha,
 \label{eq:conditional-thin-quantile-limit}
\end{equation}
uniformly for $\tau$ in any fixed compact subinterval of $(0,1)$.

For $\mathcal P=\mathcal D$, $b=1/16$ and each rational
$\tau\in(0,1)$, this quantile is a computable integer.
Its limit is $1/\Theta_\zeta$.
In particular, for the conditional median $m_{1/2}(X)$,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 {\rm RH}
 &\ \Longleftrightarrow\
       m_{1/2}(X)=(\log X)^{2+o(1)}\\
 &\ \Longleftrightarrow\
       \text{for every $\epsilon>0$, }\
       m_{1/2}(X)\gg_\epsilon(\log X)^{2-\epsilon}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:conditional-thin-median-RH}
\end{equation}
Similarly $\Theta_\zeta\le3/4$ is equivalent to the
lower bounds $(\log X)^{4/3-\epsilon}$ for every $\epsilon>0$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The conditioning event has positive probability eventually,
and the actual component always lies in $\{1,\ldots,X\}$.
Hence the finite set in the quantile definition is nonempty.
For every $\eta>0$ the localization theorem puts probability
$1-o(1)$ inside the interval
$[(\log X)^{1/\alpha-\eta},(\log X)^{1/\alpha+\eta}]$.
For any fixed $0<\tau_0\le1/2$, this brackets every quantile
with $\tau_0\le\tau\le1-\tau_0$ eventually.
Using slightly smaller interior windows removes any endpoint
rounding issue.  Take logarithms and let $\eta\downarrow0$
to prove \eqref{eq:conditional-thin-quantile-limit} uniformly.

For rational $\tau$, compare finite integer counts by
cross-multiplication and choose the first component value
meeting the threshold.  The progression uses an integer
sixteenth root and the proved computable class $\mathcal D$,
so no real-cutoff decision is needed.
For this class $\alpha=\Theta_\zeta\ge1/2$, so its quantile
exponent is at most two, with equality exactly under RH.
The family of median lower bounds forces its exponent to
be at least two, and equality implies all those bounds.
The same argument with $4/3$ proves the $3/4$ equivalence.
\end{proof}

These are ordinary proofs of relative probability and conditional
localization results, not new Lean declarations.
The classical independent-coordinate and collision arguments are
explicit inputs, rather than a claim that Poisson approximation
itself is new.
The new arithmetic conclusions use the full affine sampling budget
and its positive rare-event denominator.
The installed bound gives median exponent at least $8/7$;
the $3/4$ target requires $4/3$ and RH requires two.
No independent improvement of that median lower bound is proved,
and the certified zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.

\subsection{Three-bin reconstruction beyond the Poisson collision scale}
\label{sec:three-bin-reconstruction}

Retain the fixed thin prime class and the actual integer progression
of \eqref{eq:primorial-filter-arithmetic-sample}.
Use $J,c_J,W_J,\lambda_J,\Delta_X,\xi$ from
\eqref{eq:conditional-thin-model-budget}.
The square-root scale in the preceding Poisson comparison comes
from collisions of labels in the coefficient expansion.
We now retain that term rather than requiring it to tend to zero.
Nonuniform birthday probabilities and logarithmic expansions
are classical; see Mase~\cite[\S\S3--5]{MaseCollision}.
The lemma below supplies an explicit uniform remainder for our
moving weights.  The arithmetic conclusion reconstructs actual
sample probabilities from three rational empirical bins.
No priority claim for the classical collision expansion is made.

\begin{lemma}[A uniform pair-collision correction]
\label{lem:uniform-pair-collision-correction}
Let $(a_i)$ be a finite or countable probability vector, with
$a_i\ge0$, $\sum_i a_i=1$, and
$m=\sup_i a_i\le1/4$.
Write $\theta_r=\sum_i a_i^r$ for $r\ge2$.
Let $D_n$ be the probability that $n$ independent draws have
distinct labels, with $D_0=1$.
If $n\ge0$ is an integer and $nm\le1/4$, then $D_n>0$ and
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \left|\log D_n+{n\choose2}\theta_2\right|
       &\le8n^3(\theta_3+\theta_2^2),\\
 \left|\log\frac{D_n}{(1-\theta_2)^{\binom n2}}\right|
       &\le10n^3(\theta_3+\theta_2^2).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:uniform-pair-collision-correction}
\end{equation}
Every logarithm has a positive argument; $1-\theta_2\ge3/4$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $e_j$ be the elementary symmetric sum of degree $j$ in
the $a_i$, with $e_0=1$.
Then $D_j=j!e_j$.
On the event of $j$ distinct draws, let $h_j$ be the
conditional expected total weight of their occupied labels.
The next draw avoids those labels with probability $1-h_j$,
so the exact recurrence and pointwise bound are
\begin{equation}
 \frac{D_{j+1}}{D_j}=1-h_j,\qquad
 0\le h_j\le jm,\qquad h_0=0.
 \label{eq:pair-collision-exact-hazard}
\end{equation}
These statements inductively show $D_j>0$ through $j=n+1$:
at every required step $jm\le1/4$.
There is no division by a zero coefficient.

Conditional on $j$ distinct labels, an unordered set $S$
of size $j$ has probability $\prod_{i\in S}a_i/e_j$.
Writing $e_j^{(i)}$ for the sum omitting label $i$ gives
\[
 h_j=\frac{\sum_i a_i^2e_{j-1}^{(i)}}{e_j}
                                      \quad(j\ge1).
\]
For $j\ge2$ put
$u_{j-1,i}=\mathbb P(i\in S\mid |S|=j-1)$ and
$V_{j-1}=\sum_i a_i^2u_{j-1,i}$.
The elementary identity
$e_{j-1}^{(i)}=e_{j-1}(1-u_{j-1,i})$
and the preceding exact recurrence give
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 h_j&=\frac{j(\theta_2-V_{j-1})}{1-h_{j-1}},\\
 0\le u_{j-1,i}
   &=\frac{a_i e_{j-2}^{(i)}}{e_{j-1}}
       \le\frac{(j-1)a_i}{1-h_{j-2}},\\
 0\le V_{j-1}
   &\le\frac{(j-1)\theta_3}{1-h_{j-2}}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:pair-collision-signed-recurrence}
\end{equation}
All sums converge absolutely by nonnegativity and $\sum_i a_i=1$.
The same formulas follow on finite supports first and then by
monotone convergence if the support is infinite.
For $j=1$, directly $h_1=\theta_2$.

Every denominator in the required range is at least $3/4$.
The recurrence, with its negative term retained, implies
$h_j\le(4/3)j\theta_2$.
Subtracting the leading term before estimating gives, for $j\ge2$,
\[
 h_j-j\theta_2
   =\frac{j(\theta_2h_{j-1}-V_{j-1})}{1-h_{j-1}},
 \qquad
 |h_j-j\theta_2|
   \le2j^2(\theta_2^2+\theta_3).
\]
The latter bound also holds at $j=0,1$.
For $0\le h_j\le1/4$,
$|\log(1-h_j)+h_j|\le h_j^2$, and
$h_j^2\le2j^2\theta_2^2$.
Summing these two remainders in
$\log D_n=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\log(1-h_j)$ and using
$\sum_{j<n}j=\binom n2$ proves the first bound, with the
displayed constant eight (and room to spare).
Finally
$|-\log(1-\theta_2)-\theta_2|
\le\theta_2^2/[2(1-\theta_2)]\le\theta_2^2$.
Its extra contribution is at most $n^2\theta_2^2/2$;
for $n\ge1$ this is absorbed by the second displayed constant.
For $n=0$ both assertions are exact.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Rational reconstruction from the first three empirical bins]
\label{thm:three-bin-rational-reconstruction}
For the actual progression probabilities $b_{X,k}$ define
\begin{equation}
 \widehat W_X=\frac{b_{X,1}}{b_{X,0}},\qquad
 \widehat q_X=\frac{2b_{X,0}b_{X,2}}{b_{X,1}^2},\qquad
 \mathcal R_{X,k}
  =\frac{b_{X,0}\widehat W_X^k}{k!}
                              \widehat q_X^{\binom k2}.
 \label{eq:three-bin-rational-reference}
\end{equation}
For all sufficiently large integer $X$, these quantities are
positive and $0<\widehat q_X<1$.
For every fixed $0<\epsilon<2/3$,
\begin{equation}
 \max_{0\le k\le\lfloor J^{2/3-\epsilon}\rfloor}
       \left|\frac{b_{X,k}}{\mathcal R_{X,k}}-1\right|
        \le J^{-3\epsilon+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:three-bin-relative-window}
\end{equation}
The reference matches $b_{X,0},b_{X,1},b_{X,2}$ exactly.
For a computable $\mathcal P$ and a computable integer budget,
it is rational and computable from the first three finite counts
and the integer input $k$.
In particular this applies to $\mathcal D$ with $b=1/16$.
The reference need not sum to one over all $k$; the conclusion
is the stated uniform relative estimate on its finite rank range.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $j>J$ set
$a_j=(p_j-1)^{-1}/W_J$ and
$\theta_{r,J}=\sum_{j>J}a_j^r$.
Their maximum and second moment have the exact logarithmic indices
\begin{equation}
 m_J=J^{-1+o(1)},\qquad
 \theta_{2,J}=J^{-1+o(1)},\qquad
 \theta_{3,J}+\theta_{2,J}^2\le J^{-2+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:three-bin-weight-indices}
\end{equation}
Indeed $m_J=(p_{J+1}-1)^{-1}/W_J=J^{-1+o(1)}$.
The upper bounds follow from
$\theta_{2,J}\le m_J$ and $\theta_{3,J}\le m_J^2$.
For the matching second-moment lower bound retain $J<j\le2J$:
\[
 \theta_{2,J}\ge
       \frac{J(p_{2J}-1)^{-2}}{W_J^2}=J^{-1+o(1)}.
\]
Here $W_J\sim\lambda_J=J^{1-1/\alpha+o(1)}$, as proved
in the preceding subsection.

Write $\beta_{J,k}$ for the model count masses.
The exact distinct-label coefficient formula gives
$\beta_{J,k}=c_JW_J^kD_k/k!$.
Put $q_J=1-\theta_{2,J}$ and
\[
 R^0_{J,k}=c_JW_J^kq_J^{\binom k2}/k!.
\]
For $L=\lfloor J^{2/3-\epsilon}\rfloor$,
$Lm_J\to0$.
The collision lemma applies uniformly to $0\le k\le L$:
\begin{equation}
 \beta_{J,k}=R^0_{J,k}e^{E_{J,k}},\qquad
 \max_{k\le L}|E_{J,k}|
       \le10L^3(\theta_{3,J}+\theta_{2,J}^2)
       \le J^{-3\epsilon+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:three-bin-model-consumer}
\end{equation}

The exact first three model coefficients are
\[
 \beta_{J,0}=c_J,\quad
 \beta_{J,1}=c_JW_J,\quad
 \beta_{J,2}=\tfrac12c_JW_J^2q_J.
\]
Each empirical coefficient differs from its model by at most
$\Delta_X$.  Since $c_J\to1$ and $W_J\sim\lambda_J$,
these identities prove
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 b_{X,0}=c_J(1+O(\Delta_X)),\qquad
 \widehat W_X/W_J=1+O(\Delta_X/\lambda_J),\\
 \widehat q_X-q_J=O(\Delta_X/\lambda_J^2).
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:three-bin-parameter-transfer}
\end{equation}
In particular $b_{X,0},b_{X,1},b_{X,2}>0$ eventually.
The error in $\widehat q_X$ is polynomially small in $X$,
whereas $1-q_J=\theta_{2,J}=J^{-1+o(1)}$.
Thus $0<\widehat q_X<1$ eventually, including the strict
upper inequality.

All factors in the two references are positive.
Taking their logarithmic ratio and charging both growing powers
in \eqref{eq:three-bin-rational-reference} gives
\[
 \max_{k\le L}\left|\log\frac{\mathcal R_{X,k}}{R^0_{J,k}}\right|
 \ll \Delta_X
       +L\Delta_X/\lambda_J
       +L^2\Delta_X/\lambda_J^2
 =X^{-\xi+o(1)}.
\]
Also uniformly on this range,
\[
 -\log R^0_{J,k}
   \le O(1+L\log J+L^2\theta_{2,J})=o(\log X).
\]
Use $J\asymp\log X/\log\log X$ and $2/3-\epsilon<1$
for the first growing term; the second is at most
$J^{1/3-2\epsilon+o(1)}$.
Consequently both reference minima are at least $X^{-o(1)}$.

For clarity the complete relative consumer, including the signed
arithmetic discrepancy, is
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{b_{X,k}}{\mathcal R_{X,k}}-1
  ={}&\frac{R^0_{J,k}}{\mathcal R_{X,k}}(e^{E_{J,k}}-1)
       +\left(\frac{R^0_{J,k}}{\mathcal R_{X,k}}-1\right)\\
     &+\frac{b_{X,k}-\beta_{J,k}}{\mathcal R_{X,k}} .
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:three-bin-full-relative-consumer}
\end{equation}
The first term is bounded by
$J^{-3\epsilon+o(1)}$ using the uniform logarithmic remainder.
The other two are $X^{-\xi+o(1)}$, since
$|b_{X,k}-\beta_{J,k}|\le\Delta_X$ and the reference minimum
is $X^{-o(1)}$.
This proves \eqref{eq:three-bin-relative-window}.
Equivalently the positive model supply is
$R^0_{J,k}e^{-|E_{J,k}|}$ and the negative sampling allowance
is $\Delta_X$; all empirical masses on the range are
therefore positive eventually.
Substitution at $k=0,1,2$ proves exact matching of the three bins.
Finally all displayed empirical parameters and reference values
use only rational operations, integer powers and factorials.
Finite enumeration proves the computability assertion on the
stated decidable integer domains.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A genuine transition for the uncorrected Poisson law]
\label{cor:three-bin-poisson-transition}
Let $\widehat\lambda_X=-\log b_{X,0}$ and
$\pi_{X,k}=e^{-\widehat\lambda_X}\widehat\lambda_X^k/k!$.
For $k_X=\lfloor J^u\rfloor$ one has
\begin{equation}
 \begin{cases}
 b_{X,k_X}/\pi_{X,k_X}\longrightarrow1,
                         &0<u<1/2,\\
 b_{X,k_X}/\pi_{X,k_X}
        =\exp[-J^{\,2u-1+o(1)}]\longrightarrow0,
                         &1/2<u<2/3 .
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:three-bin-poisson-transition}
\end{equation}
In the second range the three-bin reference still has relative
error tending to zero.
Thus the correction extends the logarithmic rank exponent
from $1/2$ to $2/3$ for a directly reconstructed reference.
No limit at $u=1/2$ is claimed under the present hypotheses.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Below $1/2$ use
Theorem~\ref{thm:zero-bin-poisson-reconstruction} with
$\epsilon=1/2-u$.
For $1/2<u<2/3$, the three-bin theorem applies with
$\epsilon=2/3-u$ and gives $b_{X,k_X}/\mathcal R_{X,k_X}\to1$.
Since $e^{-\widehat\lambda_X}=b_{X,0}$,
\[
 \log\frac{\mathcal R_{X,k_X}}{\pi_{X,k_X}}
   =k_X\log\frac{\widehat W_X}{\widehat\lambda_X}
       +{k_X\choose2}\log\widehat q_X .
\]
The already proved parameter bounds give
$\widehat W_X/\widehat\lambda_X
 =1+O(p_{J+1}^{-1}+\Delta_X/\lambda_J)$.
Its growing-power contribution tends to zero, since
$u<2/3<1/\alpha$.
On the other hand,
$-\log\widehat q_X=\theta_{2,J}(1+o(1))=J^{-1+o(1)}$.
The second term is therefore $-J^{2u-1+o(1)}$ and tends
to minus infinity.
The logarithm of the remaining empirical relative error is $o(1)$.
This proves the second formula with its sign intact.
\end{proof}

The collision lemma is a quantitative version of classical
nonuniform birthday analysis.  The arithmetic reconstruction and
transition above are full ordinary proofs, not Lean declarations.
They improve the simple reconstructed profile, rather than the
earlier Euler-model sampling range, which already allowed ranks
of order $J$.  They supply no independent estimate changing
$\Theta_\zeta$; the certified zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.

\subsection{A finite-bin hierarchy for every fixed sublinear rank power}
\label{sec:finite-bin-hierarchy}

The three-bin theorem extends to every fixed interpolation order.
The underlying polynomial-degree phenomenon belongs to classical
nonuniform birthday analysis; compare
Mase~\cite[Proposition~5.2]{MaseCollision}.
We give the analytic remainder and the full empirical transfer,
including all powers of the measured parameters.
The conclusion concerns the actual integer samples already fixed
in \eqref{eq:primorial-filter-arithmetic-sample}.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform interpolation of collision logarithms]
\label{lem:all-order-collision-interpolation}
Let $(a_i)_{i\in I}$ be a probability vector on a finite or
countable set, put $m=\sup_{i\in I}a_i>0$, and let $D_n$
be the probability that $n$ independent draws have distinct labels.
Write $F(n)=\log D_n$ when $D_n>0$.
For integers $r\ge1$ and $n\ge0$ such that
$m\max\{n,r\}\le1/16$, every required $D_i$ is positive and
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \left|F(n)-\sum_{j=0}^r{n\choose j}\Delta^jF(0)\right|
       \le C_r n^{r+1}m^r,\\
 C_r=8^r\left(1+\frac{2^r r^{r+1}}{r!}\right),\qquad
 \Delta^jF(0)=\sum_{i=0}^j(-1)^{j-i}{j\choose i}F(i).
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:all-order-collision-interpolation}
\end{equation}
Here $F(0)=F(1)=0$, so the sum may start at $j=2$.
The constant is explicit and depends only on $r$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $\theta_\ell=\sum_{i\in I}a_i^\ell$, including $\theta_1=1$.
Then $0\le\theta_\ell\le m^{\ell-1}$.
Use the falling factorial
$(x)_s=x(x-1)\cdots(x-s+1)$, with $(x)_0=1$.
Define polynomials $B_n(z)$ by
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 B_0(z)&=1,\\
 B_n(z)&=\sum_{\ell=1}^n(-1)^{\ell-1}
        (n-1)_{\ell-1}\theta_\ell z^{\ell-1}B_{n-\ell}(z)
                                      \quad(n\ge1).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:collision-deformed-newton}
\end{equation}
Their formal exponential generating function is
\[
 \sum_{n\ge0}B_n(z)\frac{t^n}{n!}
  =\exp\left(t+
       \sum_{\ell\ge2}
          \frac{(-1)^{\ell-1}}{\ell}
                     \theta_\ell z^{\ell-1}t^\ell\right).
\]
The recurrence follows by differentiation in $t$, or verifies
this identity coefficient by coefficient.
At $z=1$ the right side is $\prod_{i\in I}(1+a_it)$,
so $B_n(1)=D_n$.
The countable product converges locally uniformly since
$\sum_i a_i=1$, and the coefficient identity follows by
its finite-product limits.
Also $B_n(0)=1$ for every $n$.

We first establish a uniform analytic logarithm.
Fix an integer $N\ge1$ and set $\rho_N=(8Nm)^{-1}$.
On $|z|\le\rho_N$, induction in $1\le s\le N$ gives
\begin{equation}
 B_s(z)\ne0,\qquad
 R_s(z)=B_s(z)/B_{s-1}(z),\qquad
 |R_s(z)-1|\le1/3 .
 \label{eq:collision-deformed-zero-free}
\end{equation}
Indeed $R_1=1$.
After division by $B_{s-1}$, the terms of the recurrence
with $\ell\ge2$ contain $\ell-1$ reciprocal earlier ratios.
If those ratios have distance at most $1/3$ from one,
each reciprocal has modulus at most two.
Thus
\[
 |R_s(z)-1|
   \le\sum_{\ell=2}^s(2sm|z|)^{\ell-1}
   \le\frac{2sm|z|}{1-2sm|z|}
   \le\frac13 .
\]
This proves nonvanishing and completes the induction on the
closed disc.  In particular the logarithms
$\log R_s(z)=\sum_{j\ge1}(-1)^{j-1}(R_s(z)-1)^j/j$
are analytic there and normalized to zero at $z=0$.
Define $H_N(z)=\sum_{s=1}^N\log R_s(z)$.
It satisfies $e^{H_N(z)}=B_N(z)$, $H_N(0)=0$, and
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 |\log R_s(z)|&\le\tfrac32|R_s(z)-1|\le4sm|z|,\\
 |H_N(z)|&\le4N^2m|z|\le N/2
                              \quad(|z|\le\rho_N).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Nonvanishing on the closed disc also gives analyticity in
a neighborhood of it, as needed for Cauchy's estimate.
Write $f_\ell(N)=[z^\ell]H_N(z)$.
If $Nm\le1/16$, then $\rho_N\ge2$, and Cauchy's estimate
and a geometric tail give, for every $r\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 \left|H_N(1)-\sum_{\ell=1}^{r-1}f_\ell(N)\right|
       \le\frac N2\frac{(8Nm)^r}{1-8Nm}
       \le8^rN^{r+1}m^r.
 \label{eq:collision-uniform-taylor-tail}
\end{equation}
On the real interval from zero to one the ratios are positive,
so the chosen logarithm has $H_N(1)=\log D_N$.
This also proves all positivity assertions in the lemma.

We next prove that each $f_\ell(N)$, initially defined for
nonnegative integers, is a polynomial in $N$ of degree at most
$\ell+1$, with $f_\ell(0)=0$.
This step is formal near $z=0$, where every $B_N(0)=1$,
and therefore does not require a bound on $Nm$.
Write $H_N=\log B_N$ for this normalized formal series.
The recurrence is equivalently
\begin{equation}
 e^{H_{N+1}-H_N}
  =1+\sum_{s=1}^N(-1)^s(N)_s\theta_{s+1}z^s
                          e^{H_{N-s}-H_N}.
 \label{eq:collision-log-degree-recurrence}
\end{equation}
Induct on $\ell$.
In the coefficient of $z^\ell$ on the right, only $s\le\ell$
occurs and every exponential coefficient uses $f_j$ with
$j\le\ell-s<\ell$.
By induction, $f_j(N-s)-f_j(N)$ has degree at most $j$.
Consequently that exponential coefficient has degree at most
$\ell-s$; multiplication by $(N)_s$ gives degree at most $\ell$.
When $N<s$, extend the coefficient term by zero using
$(N)_s=0$; only the already constructed lower-order
polynomials are used in this extension.
On the left the coefficient is
$f_\ell(N+1)-f_\ell(N)$ plus a polynomial of degree at most
$\ell$ built from lower differences.
Thus $f_\ell(N+1)-f_\ell(N)$ is a polynomial of degree at most
$\ell$ for all $N\ge0$.
A discrete antiderivative in the binomial polynomial basis,
with value zero at zero, has degree at most $\ell+1$.
This proves the induction, including the first case, where the
right coefficient is $-N\theta_2$.

For the desired order $r$, the polynomial
$Q_r(N)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{r-1}f_\ell(N)$ has degree at most $r$.
Let $I_r$ denote polynomial interpolation on $0,1,\ldots,r$;
in Newton form,
$(I_rF)(n)=\sum_{j=0}^r\binom nj\Delta^jF(0)$.
If $n\le r$, it equals $F(n)$ exactly.
Otherwise $n\ge r\ge1$.
For every node $i\le r$, as well as at $n$,
\eqref{eq:collision-uniform-taylor-tail} bounds
$T(i)=F(i)-Q_r(i)$ by $8^r i^{r+1}m^r$, with $T(0)=0$.
The absolute sum of the Lagrange interpolation weights at
an integer $n\ge r$ is at most
\[
 n^r\sum_{i=0}^r\frac1{i!(r-i)!}
                   =\frac{2^r n^r}{r!}.
\]
Since $I_rQ_r=Q_r$, it follows that
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 |F(n)-(I_rF)(n)|
 &\le |T(n)|+|(I_rT)(n)|\\
 &\le8^r n^{r+1}m^r
       +8^r r^{r+1}m^r\,\frac{2^r n^r}{r!}\\
 &\le C_r n^{r+1}m^r .
 \end{aligned}
\]
The case $n=0$ is exact.  This proves the full stated bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The empirical finite-bin hierarchy]
\label{thm:empirical-finite-bin-hierarchy}
Fix an integer $r\ge1$.
For the progression counts $b_{X,i}$ put
\begin{equation}
 A_{X,i}=i!\,b_{X,i},\qquad
 \Gamma_{X,j}=\prod_{i=0}^j
       A_{X,i}^{\,(-1)^{j-i}\binom ji}
                              \quad(0\le j\le r),
 \label{eq:finite-bin-empirical-parameters}
\end{equation}
and define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R^{(r)}_{X,k}
       =\frac1{k!}\prod_{j=0}^r
                       \Gamma_{X,j}^{\binom kj}\qquad(k\ge0).
 \label{eq:finite-bin-rational-reference}
\end{equation}
For large $X$, every measured bin used here is positive,
so even the negative integer powers are defined.
For each fixed $0<\epsilon<r/(r+1)$,
\begin{equation}
 \max_{0\le k\le\lfloor J^{\,r/(r+1)-\epsilon}\rfloor}
    \left|\frac{b_{X,k}}{\mathcal R^{(r)}_{X,k}}-1\right|
       \le J^{-(r+1)\epsilon+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:finite-bin-hierarchy-relative}
\end{equation}
The reference exactly matches bins $0,\ldots,r$.
It is rational and computable from those $r+1$ finite counts
on the decidable integer domains of the preceding theorems.
For $r=2$ it is exactly the three-bin reference
\eqref{eq:three-bin-rational-reference}.
No normalization over all ranks is asserted.
All orders and constants in this arithmetic statement are fixed
before letting $X\to\infty$; no uniformity in growing $r(X)$
is claimed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the normalized prime weights
$a_j=(p_j-1)^{-1}/W_J$ for $j>J$.
Their maximum is $m_J=J^{-1+o(1)}$ by
\eqref{eq:three-bin-weight-indices}.
Let $D_k$ denote their distinct-label probability and
$F_J(k)=\log D_k$.
The exact model coefficients satisfy
$\beta_{J,k}=c_JW_J^kD_k/k!$.
For $i\le r$ define the model parameters and reference
by replacing every $b_{X,i}$ in
\eqref{eq:finite-bin-empirical-parameters}--%
\eqref{eq:finite-bin-rational-reference} with $\beta_{J,i}$.
Denote the reference by $R^{0,(r)}_{J,k}$.
The logarithm of $i!\beta_{J,i}$ is
$\log c_J+i\log W_J+F_J(i)$.
Newton interpolation reproduces its constant and linear parts.
Consequently the interpolation lemma gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \beta_{J,k}=R^{0,(r)}_{J,k}e^{E_{J,k}},\\
 \max_{0\le k\le L}|E_{J,k}|
       \le C_r L^{r+1}m_J^r
       \le J^{-(r+1)\epsilon+o(1)},\qquad
 L=\lfloor J^{r/(r+1)-\epsilon}\rfloor .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:finite-bin-model-consumer}
\end{equation}
Indeed $\max\{L,r\}m_J\to0$.

For each fixed $i\le r$, $D_i\to1$, either by the
pair-collision bound or by a finite union bound.
Thus $\beta_{J,i}\sim\lambda_J^i/i!$, including the
zero-bin limit $\beta_{J,0}=c_J\to1$.
Since $|b_{X,i}-\beta_{J,i}|\le\Delta_X$, all measured
bins are positive eventually and
\[
 \max_{i\le r}
 \left|\log\frac{i!b_{X,i}}{i!\beta_{J,i}}\right|
       \ll_r \Delta_X/\lambda_J^r .
\]
The finite-difference coefficients have absolute sum at most
$2^j$ at order $j$.
Charging the power $\binom kj$ of every parameter therefore gives
\begin{equation}
 \max_{k\le L}
   \left|\log\frac{\mathcal R^{(r)}_{X,k}}{R^{0,(r)}_{J,k}}\right|
       \ll_r(1+L)^r\Delta_X/\lambda_J^r
       =X^{-\xi+o(1)} .
 \label{eq:finite-bin-parameter-loss}
\end{equation}
In particular no order-$r$ parameter error is charged as
though it appeared only to its first power.

The analytic bound in the lemma, evaluated at $z=1$,
also gives $|\log D_k|\le4k^2m_J$ on this range.
As $W_J=J^{1-1/\alpha+o(1)}$, we obtain
\[
 -\log\beta_{J,k}
       \le O(1+L\log J+L^2m_J)=o(\log X)
                                  \qquad(0\le k\le L).
\]
Here $L=J^u$ up to a floor with fixed $u<1$,
so $L\log J=o(\log X)$ and
$L^2m_J=J^{2u-1+o(1)}=o(\log X)$.
Both references therefore have minimum at least $X^{-o(1)}$
on the full range, by
