\eqref{eq:finite-bin-model-consumer} and
\eqref{eq:finite-bin-parameter-loss}.

Retain the complete relative identity
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{b_{X,k}}{\mathcal R^{(r)}_{X,k}}-1
 ={}&\frac{R^{0,(r)}_{J,k}}{\mathcal R^{(r)}_{X,k}}
                          (e^{E_{J,k}}-1)
      +\left(\frac{R^{0,(r)}_{J,k}}{\mathcal R^{(r)}_{X,k}}-1\right)\\
   &+\frac{b_{X,k}-\beta_{J,k}}{\mathcal R^{(r)}_{X,k}} .
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:finite-bin-complete-consumer}
\end{equation}
Its first error is $J^{-(r+1)\epsilon+o(1)}$.
Its second and third errors are $X^{-\xi+o(1)}$:
use the parameter bound and divide the full sampling allowance
$\Delta_X$ by the proved reference minimum.
This proves \eqref{eq:finite-bin-hierarchy-relative}, including
eventual positivity of all empirical masses on the range.
The positive supply is the model reference times
$e^{-|E_{J,k}|}$; its negative allowance is precisely $\Delta_X$.

Newton interpolation at each node $k\le r$ reproduces
$\log(k!b_{X,k})$ exactly, proving exact bin matching.
The definitions use integer powers of positive rational numbers,
so all finite references are rational and computable as asserted.
At $r=2$, $\Gamma_{X,0}=b_{X,0}$,
$\Gamma_{X,1}=b_{X,1}/b_{X,0}$ and
$\Gamma_{X,2}=2b_{X,0}b_{X,2}/b_{X,1}^2$,
which recovers the preceding formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Finite data recover every fixed sublinear power]
\label{cor:finite-bin-every-sublinear-power}
Fix $0<u<1$ and any integer $r>u/(1-u)$.
Then the first $r+1$ empirical bins give
\begin{equation}
 \max_{0\le k\le\lfloor J^u\rfloor}
 \left|\frac{b_{X,k}}{\mathcal R^{(r)}_{X,k}}-1\right|
       \le J^{-\{r-(r+1)u\}+o(1)}\longrightarrow0.
 \label{eq:finite-bin-sublinear-power}
\end{equation}
The number of bins is independent of $X$.
In particular four bins suffice for every fixed rank exponent
$u<3/4$, and five suffice for every fixed $u<4/5$.
These are exponents of the rank range, not zero-free boundaries.
The statement does not include all sequences $k=o(J)$,
nor the linear endpoint $k\asymp J$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Set $\epsilon=r/(r+1)-u>0$ in
Theorem~\ref{thm:empirical-finite-bin-hierarchy}.
The exponent $(r+1)\epsilon=r-(r+1)u$ is positive.
Taking $r=3$ and $r=4$ proves the two displayed examples.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Sharp scale for the canonical interpolation formula]
\label{cor:uniform-collision-interpolation-sharpness}
Let $D_{N,n}$ be the no-collision probability for $n$ draws
from the uniform distribution on $N$ labels.
For fixed $r\ge1$ and $N>r$ let
\[
 T^{(r)}_{N,n}
       =\exp\left(\sum_{j=0}^r{n\choose j}
                          \Delta^j\log D_{N,0}\right),
\]
where the finite differences use the values
$\log D_{N,i}$ for $0\le i\le r$.
If $n=n_N\to\infty$ and $n=o(N)$, then
\begin{equation}
 \log\frac{D_{N,n}}{T^{(r)}_{N,n}}
       =-\frac{n^{r+1}}{r(r+1)N^r}(1+o(1)).
 \label{eq:uniform-hierarchy-sharpness}
\end{equation}
In particular, for every fixed $t>0$ and
$n_N\sim tN^{r/(r+1)}$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{D_{N,n_N}}{T^{(r)}_{N,n_N}}
       \longrightarrow
        \exp\left(-\frac{t^{r+1}}{r(r+1)}\right)<1.
 \label{eq:uniform-hierarchy-critical-limit}
\end{equation}
Thus the exponent $r/(r+1)$ cannot be enlarged for this
canonical interpolation scheme without another correction.
This is not an information-theoretic lower bound for every
procedure using finitely many bins: in the uniform model, its
parameter $N$ can itself be recovered from a low-order collision
probability.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $n\le N$ the exact probability is
$D_{N,n}=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(1-j/N)$.
For $n=o(N)$, expand each logarithm through order $r$:
\[
 \log D_{N,n}
   =-\sum_{\ell=1}^r\frac1{\ell N^\ell}
                    \sum_{j=0}^{n-1}j^\ell
       +O_r(n^{r+2}/N^{r+1}).
\]
The same expansion at the finitely many nodes $0,\ldots,r$
has remainder $O_r(N^{-r-1})$.
Their interpolation weights are $O_r(n^r)$.
The polynomial $\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}j^\ell$ has degree
$\ell+1$ and leading coefficient $1/(\ell+1)$;
this follows by discrete integration of $n^\ell$.
For $\ell<r$ it is reproduced exactly by interpolation of
degree $r$.
For $\ell=r$, the difference from its interpolant vanishes
at $0,\ldots,r$ and has leading coefficient $1/(r+1)$.
It is therefore exactly $(n)_{r+1}/(r+1)$.
Subtracting the interpolated node expansion yields
\[
 \log\frac{D_{N,n}}{T^{(r)}_{N,n}}
   =-\frac{(n)_{r+1}}{r(r+1)N^r}
      +O_r\left(\frac{n^{r+2}+n^r}{N^{r+1}}\right).
\]
Since $n\to\infty$ and $n/N\to0$, the remainder divided by
$n^{r+1}/N^r$ tends to zero, and
$(n)_{r+1}/n^{r+1}\to1$.
This proves both limits.
At a larger fixed power $n=\lfloor N^u\rfloor$ with
$r/(r+1)<u<1$, the logarithm tends to minus infinity,
which proves the claimed obstruction for this formula.
\end{proof}

The hierarchy and its arithmetic consumer are full ordinary proofs;
no additional Lean declarations are asserted.
It replaces an infinite Euler reference by finitely many actual
integer-count frequencies on every fixed sublinear power range.
The earlier Euler-law sampling theorem already reached ranks of
order $J$ and is not weakened or superseded by this change of
reference.
For $\mathcal D$ all statements apply unconditionally with
$b=1/16$, but they do not independently bound $\Theta_\zeta$.
The certified zero-free boundary is still $7/8$.

\subsection{Adaptive reconstruction with a growing number of empirical bins}
\label{sec:adaptive-bin-reconstruction}

The preceding arithmetic theorem fixes the interpolation order.
We now keep its explicit analytic constant and prove a new
empirical transfer with an order growing with the sample.
Only $O(\log J)=O(\log\log X)$ measured bins will be needed.
The cutoff is chosen from rational measured data, rather than
from an unavailable exact value of the maximal model weight.
The classical collision ingredients and their relation to
Mase~\cite{MaseCollision} remain as described above;
the growing-order arithmetic transfer is proved here.

\begin{lemma}[Stability with every interpolation power charged]
\label{lem:adaptive-bin-stability}
Let $(a_i)$ be a probability vector as in
Lemma~\ref{lem:all-order-collision-interpolation}, with maximum
$m>0$ and distinct-label probabilities $D_k$.
Fix $c_0,W>0$ and put $\beta_k=c_0W^kD_k/k!$.
Let $1\le r\le L$ be integers with $Lm\le1/16$.
Suppose nonnegative observations $b_k$, $0\le k\le L$,
satisfy $|b_k-\beta_k|\le\Delta$.
Define
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 u=\min_{0\le i\le r}\beta_i,\qquad
 v=\min_{0\le k\le L}\beta_k,\qquad
 S(L,r)=\sum_{j=0}^r2^j{L\choose j},\\
 E=C_rL^{r+1}m^r,\qquad
 \eta=\frac{2\Delta}{u}S(L,r),
 \qquad 0\le\Delta\le u/2 .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-noise-budget}
\end{equation}
Then $u,v>0$, every observed node $b_i$, $i\le r$, is
positive, and its reference
\[
 R_k=\frac1{k!}\prod_{j=0}^r
       \left\{\prod_{i=0}^j(i!b_i)^{(-1)^{j-i}\binom ji}\right\}^{\binom kj}
                         \qquad(0\le k\le L)
\]
obeys the completely explicit bound
\begin{equation}
 \max_{0\le k\le L}\left|\frac{b_k}{R_k}-1\right|
       \le e^{E+\eta}-1+\frac{\Delta}{v}e^{E+\eta}.
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-stability-bound}
\end{equation}
Moreover $R_k=b_k$ for $0\le k\le r$ and
$S(L,r)\le(r+1)(1+2L)^r$.
No constant hidden in this statement depends on the order.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
All model nodes are positive by the interpolation lemma.
The assumption on $\Delta$ gives $b_i\ge\beta_i/2>0$
at the measured nodes and
$|\varepsilon_i|\le2\Delta/u$, where
$\varepsilon_i=\log(b_i/\beta_i)$.
Let $R_k^0$ be the reference with model nodes $\beta_i$.
Interpolation reproduces the constant and linear terms in
$\log(k!\beta_k)=\log c_0+k\log W+\log D_k$.
Thus $\beta_k=R_k^0e^{E_k}$ with $|E_k|\le E$.
Exactly, including all powers of the empirical parameters,
\[
 \Lambda_k=\log(R_k/R_k^0)
   =\sum_{j=0}^r{k\choose j}
            \sum_{i=0}^j(-1)^{j-i}{j\choose i}\varepsilon_i .
\]
For integer $0\le k\le L$,
$\binom kj\le\binom Lj$, with the usual zero convention
when $j>k$.
It follows that $|\Lambda_k|\le\eta$.
Retain the signed identity
\begin{equation}
 \frac{b_k}{R_k}-1
       =e^{E_k-\Lambda_k}-1
                         +\frac{b_k-\beta_k}{R_k},
 \qquad R_k=\beta_ke^{\Lambda_k-E_k}
                         \ge v e^{-E-\eta}.
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-signed-consumer}
\end{equation}
Estimating only these proved remainders gives
\eqref{eq:adaptive-bin-stability-bound}.
The positive arithmetic supply is $\beta_k$; the competing
negative allowance is $\Delta$.
Newton interpolation is exact at its nodes, proving bin matching.
Finally $\binom Lj\le L^j$ and each $j\le r$ contributes
at most $(1+2L)^r$, which proves the stated bound on $S$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[An empirical reconstruction at almost linear rank]
\label{thm:adaptive-bin-almost-linear}
Use the same fixed thin prime class
$p_j=j^{1/\alpha+o(1)}$, $0<\alpha<1$, and the actual
progression sample of
\eqref{eq:primorial-filter-arithmetic-sample}, with
$0<b<1-\alpha$.
Fix a positive rational $c$ and an integer $A\ge1$ such that
\begin{equation}
       0<c<\alpha(1-b-\alpha).
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-parameter-domain}
\end{equation}
For every integer $X\ge4$ and every positive rational $c$,
use the same discrete formula as in
\eqref{eq:greedy-binary-rank-selector}:
\[
 U_X=\lfloor\log_2X\rfloor,\qquad
 V_X=\lfloor\log_2U_X\rfloor,\qquad
 \kappa_c(X)=\left\lfloor cU_X/V_X\right\rfloor .
\]
Here $U_X\ge2$ and $V_X\ge1$.
This defines the selector on the full parameter domain above;
the earlier rare-count corollary used its smaller interval
$0<c<7/64$.
For sufficiently large integer $X$, define
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \ell_J=1+\lfloor\log_2J\rfloor,\qquad r_X=(A+3)\ell_J,\\
 \widehat W_X=b_{X,1}/b_{X,0},\qquad
 \widehat m_X=\frac{2}{(p_{J+1}-1)\widehat W_X},\\
 L_X=\min\left\{
       \left\lfloor\frac1{256\widehat m_X}\right\rfloor,
                                      \kappa_c(X)\right\}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-computable-cutoff}
\end{equation}
Here $\ell_J$ is the binary length of the positive integer $J$.
Use \eqref{eq:finite-bin-empirical-parameters}--%
\eqref{eq:finite-bin-rational-reference} at the variable
order $r_X$, and denote its value by
$\mathcal R^{(r_X)}_{X,k}$.
All its input bins are positive eventually, and
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 L_X=J^{1+o(1)},\qquad L_X=O(J),\qquad
 r_X=O(\log J)=O(\log\log X),\\
 \max_{0\le k\le L_X}
  \left|\frac{b_{X,k}}{\mathcal R^{(r_X)}_{X,k}}-1\right|
       \ll_{\mathcal P,b,c,A,\delta}J^{-A}+X^{-\delta}
       \ll_{\mathcal P,b,c,A,\delta}J^{-A},\\
 0<\delta<1-b-\alpha-c/\alpha .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-relative-window}
\end{equation}
The number of measured bins is $r_X+1$.
Each reference is positive and rational and matches those
measured bins exactly.
For decidable $\mathcal P$ and a computable integer budget
$B_X$, every cutoff and reference here is computable using
finite integer counts and rational arithmetic.
In particular rational $b$ supplies such a budget by integer
root extraction.
No value of $\alpha$ is needed as an input to the algorithm
once the fixed choices $b,c,A$ are admissible.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First check the selector on its complete stated domain.
The bit lengths satisfy
$U_X=\log_2X+O(1)$ and
$V_X=\log_2\log_2X+O(1)$.
As $V_X\ge1$, the ratio is defined for every $X\ge4$.
For any fixed positive rational $c$, these estimates and the
outer floor give
$\kappa_c(X)\sim c\log X/\log\log X$.
This argument does not use the parameter restriction of the
earlier corollary.

Fix the displayed $\delta$ and choose a fixed $\sigma>\alpha$
sufficiently close to $\alpha$ that
\[
 \xi=1-b-\sigma>c/\alpha+\delta .
\]
The full sampling theorem supplies
$|b_{X,k}-\beta_{J,k}|\le\Delta_X\ll X^{-\xi}$
for every $k$, uniformly.
It also gives
$J\sim\alpha b\log X/\log\log X$.
Write $m=m_J$ for the largest normalized prime weight.
The exact first two model bins are
$\beta_{J,0}=c_J$ and $\beta_{J,1}=c_JW_J$.
Since $c_J\to1$, $W_J=J^{1-1/\alpha+o(1)}$ and
$\Delta_X/W_J\to0$, division of these two bins gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \frac{\widehat W_X}{W_J}=1+O(\Delta_X/W_J),\qquad
 \frac{\widehat m_X}{m_J}
             =\frac{2W_J}{\widehat W_X}\longrightarrow2,\\
 m_J\le\widehat m_X\le3m_J\quad\hbox{eventually}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-maxweight-certificate}
\end{equation}
Both measured bins are positive before making either division.
Here
$m_J=((p_{J+1}-1)W_J)^{-1}=J^{-1+o(1)}$
is an exact maximum, not just an upper estimate.

Both entries of the minimum defining $L_X$ have logarithmic
index one in $J$:
the first is $J^{1+o(1)}$, and
\[
 \frac{\kappa_c(X)}J\longrightarrow\frac{c}{\alpha b}.
\]
Taking floors does not change these statements, since both
entries tend to infinity.
Thus $L_X=J^{1+o(1)}$ and $L_X=O(J)$.
In particular $L_X\ge r_X$ eventually.
Also $L_Xm_J\le1/256$ and $r_Xm_J\to0$.
Every use of the explicit interpolation lemma is therefore
within its stated domain.

We first bound the analytic error without hiding dependence on $r_X$.
The elementary integral bound on $\log(r!)$ gives
$r!\ge(r/e)^r$ for every integer $r\ge1$, and hence
\begin{equation}
 C_r\le(r+1)(16e)^r,\qquad
 C_rL_X^{r+1}m_J^r
       \le(r+1)L_X\,4^{-r}
                   \quad(r=r_X).
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-uniform-order-remainder}
\end{equation}
Indeed $16eL_Xm_J\le e/16<1/4$.
Since $\ell_J>\log_2J$, we have
$4^{-r_X}\le J^{-2(A+3)}$.
With $L_X=O(J)$, the last bound in
\eqref{eq:adaptive-bin-uniform-order-remainder} is
$O_A((\log J)J^{-2A-5})=O_A(J^{-A})$.
All constants used to obtain this geometric decrease are
valid for every order, not only for fixed $r$.

We next control the observed nodes, including their factorials.
For $0\le i\le r_X$, the analytic logarithm estimate in the
interpolation lemma gives
\[
 D_i\ge e^{-4i^2m_J}\ge\tfrac12
 \quad\hbox{eventually},\qquad
 \beta_{J,i}=\frac{c_JW_J^iD_i}{i!}.
\]
Since $r_X=O_A(\log J)$, $c_J\ge1/2$ eventually and
$-\log W_J=O(\log J)$, it follows, uniformly over the nodes,
that
\begin{equation}
 u_X=\min_{0\le i\le r_X}\beta_{J,i}
            \ge \exp(-C_A(\log J)^2),\qquad
             \Delta_X/u_X\le X^{-\xi+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-growing-node-supply}
\end{equation}
For example, when $W_J\le1$ one may use the explicit lower
bound $u_X\ge W_J^{r_X}/(4r_X^{r_X})$.
Thus $\Delta_X\le u_X/2$ eventually and all measured nodes
are positive.
The complete parameter amplification is bounded by
\begin{equation}
 \eta_X=\frac{2\Delta_X}{u_X}S(L_X,r_X)
       \le \Delta_X\exp(O_A((\log J)^2))
       \le X^{-\xi+o(1)}.
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-growing-parameter-budget}
\end{equation}
Here we used $L_X=O(J)$,
$S(L_X,r_X)\le(r_X+1)(1+2L_X)^{r_X}$ and
$(\log J)^2=o(\log X)$.
This is the required fresh growing-order transfer; it is
not a substitution into a fixed-order implied constant.

The remaining allowance is the sampling discrepancy divided
by the full positive model mass at the largest ranks.
For every $0\le k\le L_X$,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 -\log\beta_{J,k}
  &\le O(1)+k\log(1/W_J)+\log(k!)+4k^2m_J\\
  &\le (1/\alpha+o(1))k\log J+O(k)
   \le(c/\alpha+o(1))\log X .
 \end{aligned}
\]
For the second line use
$\log(1/W_J)=(1/\alpha-1+o(1))\log J$,
$\log(k!)\le k\log(1+L_X)$,
$L_X=O(J)$ and $k^2m_J\le k/256$.
For the final line use
$k\le\kappa_c(X)\sim c\log X/\log\log X$
and $\log J\sim\log\log X$.
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 v_X=\min_{0\le k\le L_X}\beta_{J,k}
        \ge X^{-c/\alpha-o(1)},\qquad
 \Delta_X/v_X\le X^{-\xi+c/\alpha+o(1)}
                                      =O(X^{-\delta}).
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-full-positive-supply}
\end{equation}
In particular this supply dominates the arithmetic allowance,
so the empirical bins throughout the output range are positive.

Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:adaptive-bin-stability} with these
$u_X,v_X,\eta_X$, and with
$E=O_A(J^{-A})$.
Its full signed consumer yields
$O_A(J^{-A})+O(X^{-\delta})$, as asserted.
The model mass has not been replaced by zero or by a centered
variance in this step.
Exact matching and rationality follow from the interpolation
formula.
Computability uses binary lengths, the rational selector
$\kappa_c$, integer prime cutoffs and integer powers of positive
rational counts.
For finitely many small inputs a direct zero test can report
that the required measured nodes are not yet positive;
the theorem asserts eventual positivity, not a claimed
uniform effective threshold in its asymptotic hypotheses.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A linear window under power asymptotics]
\label{cor:adaptive-bin-linear-power-law}
In addition to the preceding hypotheses, suppose
$p_j\sim Cj^{1/\alpha}$ for some constant $C>0$.
Put $v=1/\alpha-1$.
Then the same data-driven cutoff has the definite positive limit
\begin{equation}
 \frac{L_X}{J}\longrightarrow
       \min\left\{\frac1{512v},\frac{c}{\alpha b}\right\}>0 .
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-linear-limit}
\end{equation}
Thus $O(\log J)$ actual empirical bins reconstruct a fixed
positive fraction of the linear rank range with error
$O(J^{-A})$, for each prescribed fixed integer $A\ge1$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, the bounds
$(1-\varepsilon)Cj^{1/\alpha}\le p_j
 \le(1+\varepsilon)Cj^{1/\alpha}$ hold at all sufficiently
large indices.
Integral comparison of the convergent power tail, followed
by $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, gives
\[
 W_J\sim\frac{J^{1-1/\alpha}}{Cv},\qquad
 m_J=\frac1{(p_{J+1}-1)W_J}\sim\frac vJ .
\]
The subtraction of one changes the tail by a relative
$o(1)$, since its smallest prime tends to infinity.
By \eqref{eq:adaptive-bin-maxweight-certificate},
$\widehat m_X\sim2v/J$.
The two integer entries defining $L_X$, divided by $J$,
therefore tend to $1/(512v)$ and $c/(\alpha b)$.
Floors change either ratio by at most $1/J$.
Their minimum has the stated limit, and the error estimate
is that of the theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[An unconditional adaptive profile for the greedy class]
\label{cor:adaptive-bin-greedy-class}
For $\mathcal P=\mathcal D$, take $b=1/16$, $c=1/20$ and
any fixed integer $A\ge1$.
The construction uses no value of $\Theta_\zeta$ as input and,
unconditionally, satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \max_{0\le k\le L_X}
  \left|\frac{b_{X,k}}{\mathcal R^{(r_X)}_{X,k}}-1\right|
       \ll_A J^{-A}+X^{-1/200},\qquad L_X=J^{1+o(1)} .
 \label{eq:adaptive-bin-greedy-unconditional}
\end{equation}
It uses only $O_A(\log\log X)$ measured bins.
No stronger regularity of the deleted primes is assumed.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The proved range $1/2\le\Theta_\zeta\le7/8$ implies
\[
 1-\frac1{16}-\Theta_\zeta-\frac1{20\Theta_\zeta}
       \ge\frac3{560}>\frac1{200}.
\]
Indeed the left side is decreasing on $[1/2,7/8]$:
its derivative is $-1+1/(20\Theta_\zeta^2)<0$.
Thus the parameter condition holds with
$\alpha=\Theta_\zeta$, and the theorem allows $\delta=1/200$.
All finite decisions use the proved decidable class
$\mathcal D$, the integer sixteenth root and rational counts;
neither the choices nor their computation require knowing
$\Theta_\zeta$.
\end{proof}

These are full ordinary proofs and do not add Lean declarations.
The constants in the new stability lemma are uniform in the order;
the subsequent arithmetic constants depend on the fixed class
and the displayed parameters.
The earlier relative sampling theorem already compared the
integer law with an infinite Euler reference on a linear range.
The new conclusion constructs its reference from
$O(\log\log X)$ actual empirical bins and one surviving prime.
For the general index hypothesis, $L_X=J^{1+o(1)}$ does not
assert coverage of every possible sequence $k=o(J)$.
The stronger linear limit requires the extra power asymptotic
and is not asserted for $\mathcal D$.
These profile reconstruction results supply no independent
bound on $\Theta_\zeta$; the certified boundary remains $7/8$.

\subsection{The largest coordinate and the cutoff from empirical bins alone}
\label{sec:empirical-moment-cutoff}

The remaining prime input in the preceding cutoff can also be
removed.  Newton's identities recover power sums from the first
coefficients, while a growing power sum locates the largest
coordinate.  Both facts are classical algebraic and norm
mechanisms.  The point here is a uniform error estimate for
their signed composition, followed by the actual integer-sampling
consumer.  No individual surviving prime is needed by the new
cutoff once $X,J$ and the indicated empirical bins are supplied.
This does not eliminate the class-membership computation needed
to form those finite counts.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform stability of collision-moment recovery]
\label{lem:empirical-collision-moment-stability}
Let $(a_i)$ be a finite or countable probability vector,
$m=\sup_i a_i>0$, and let $e_j$ be its elementary symmetric
coefficients, with $e_0=1$.
For an integer $t\ge1$, suppose real numbers
$\widehat e_0,\ldots,\widehat e_t$ satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \widehat e_0=1,\qquad
 \max_{1\le j\le t}|\widehat e_j-e_j|\le\varepsilon,
                   \qquad 0\le\varepsilon\le\tfrac12 .
 \label{eq:empirical-moment-coefficient-error}
\end{equation}
Define the recovered moments by the signed triangular identities
\begin{equation}
 s\widehat e_s
    =\sum_{j=1}^s(-1)^{j-1}
                \widehat\theta_j\widehat e_{s-j}
                         \qquad(1\le s\le t).
 \label{eq:empirical-moment-newton}
\end{equation}
For $\theta_s=\sum_i a_i^s$ and every $1\le s\le t$,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 |\widehat\theta_s-\theta_s|
                   \le s4^s\varepsilon,\qquad
                  m^s\le\theta_s\le m^{s-1},\\
 m\le(\widehat\theta_s+s4^s\varepsilon)^{1/s}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:empirical-moment-recovery-bound}
\end{equation}
The final root has a strictly positive argument.
The constants are uniform in $t$, in the probability vector,
and in the signs of the recovered moments.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The nonnegative expansion of $(\sum_i a_i)^j$ gives
$0\le e_j\le1/j!$.
The full product $E(z)=\prod_i(1+a_i z)$ converges locally
uniformly, and near zero
\[
 \log E(z)=\sum_{s\ge1}\frac{(-1)^{s-1}}s\theta_sz^s .
\]
Indeed the coordinate logarithms converge absolutely on every
closed disc of radius less than one, since $\sum_i a_i=1$.
Let $E_t(z)=\sum_{j=0}^te_jz^j$ and
$\widehat E_t(z)=\sum_{j=0}^t\widehat e_jz^j$.
The normalized logarithm of $E_t$ has the same coefficients
as $\log E$ through degree $t$.
For the observed polynomial, its own recovered moments satisfy
\[
 [z^s]\log\widehat E_t(z)
                       =(-1)^{s-1}\widehat\theta_s/s .
\]
It follows directly by comparing coefficients in
$\widehat E_t'=\widehat E_t(\log\widehat E_t)'$;
the coefficient of degree $s-1$ is exactly
\eqref{eq:empirical-moment-newton}.
Thus the signed recursion, including every lower-order cross
term, precedes the error estimate.

On $|z|\le1/4$ one has
\[
 |E_t(z)-1|\le e^{1/4}-1<1/3,\qquad
 |\widehat E_t(z)-E_t(z)|\le\varepsilon/3\le1/6 .
\]
Every point on the segment between the two polynomials stays
within distance less than $1/2$ of one.
Their normalized logarithms are therefore analytic on a
neighborhood of this disc, and integration along that segment gives
\[
 |\log\widehat E_t(z)-\log E_t(z)|
       \le2|\widehat E_t(z)-E_t(z)|
       \le2\varepsilon/3 .
\]
Cauchy's estimate at radius $1/4$ now proves the first inequality
of \eqref{eq:empirical-moment-recovery-bound}, with room in its
constant.  No tail of a truncated Newton recursion is discarded.
The two power-sum bounds follow from $a_i\le m$ and
$\sum_i a_i=1$, taking a supremum for the lower bound.
In particular $\theta_s>0$.
Finally $\widehat\theta_s+s4^s\varepsilon\ge\theta_s\ge m^s>0$,
which proves the remaining assertion.
\end{proof}

We will use rational upper roots without an oracle for real roots.
For an integer $s\ge1$ and a positive rational $y$, choose the
unique integer $h$ with $2^{hs}\le y<2^{(h+1)s}$.
Among $q=s,s+1,\ldots,2s$, take the least one for which
$(q2^h/s)^s\ge y$, and define
\begin{equation}
       \mathsf U_s(y)=q2^h/s,\qquad
       y^{1/s}\le\mathsf U_s(y)
                         \le(1+1/s)y^{1/s}.
 \label{eq:empirical-moment-rational-root}
\end{equation}
These are finite integer or rational comparisons.
The choice exists because $q=2s$ works.
If $q>s$, minimality shows that the upward rounding error
is less than $2^h/s\le y^{1/s}/s$; if $q=s$, the root is
exact.  This proves both bounds, including exact dyadic endpoints.
The search for $h$ terminates in either direction since $y>0$.

\begin{theorem}[An almost optimal cutoff measured from the bins]
\label{thm:empirical-moment-cutoff}
Use the fixed prime class, progression, and parameter domain of
Theorem~\ref{thm:adaptive-bin-almost-linear}:
$p_j=j^{1/\alpha+o(1)}$, $0<b<1-\alpha$, and rational
$0<c<\alpha(1-b-\alpha)$.
For large integer $X$, write $\ell_J=1+\lfloor\log_2J\rfloor$
and define
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 t_X=\ell_J^2,\qquad \widehat W_X=b_{X,1}/b_{X,0},\\
 \widehat e_{X,i}
       =\frac{b_{X,i}}{b_{X,0}\widehat W_X^{\,i}}
                              \quad(0\le i\le t_X).
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-observed-coefficients}
\end{equation}
Use \eqref{eq:empirical-moment-newton} to compute
$\widehat\theta_{X,s}$ through $s=t_X$, and put
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \widetilde m_X
        =\mathsf U_{t_X}(2\widehat\theta_{X,t_X}),\qquad
 \widetilde L_X=\min\left\{
       \left\lfloor\frac1{256\widetilde m_X}\right\rfloor,
                                      \kappa_c(X)\right\},\\
 L_X^\star=\min\left\{
       \left\lfloor\frac1{256m_J}\right\rfloor,
                                      \kappa_c(X)\right\}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-measured-and-ideal}
\end{equation}
The last cutoff is a theoretical benchmark only.
All observed nodes are positive eventually, and the recovered
moment used in the root is positive eventually.
With constants depending on the fixed class and the displayed
parameters, one has
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 1\le\frac{\widetilde m_X}{m_J}
                   \le1+O(1/\log J),\qquad
 0\le1-\frac{\widetilde L_X}{L_X^\star}
                         =O(1/\log J),\\
 \widetilde L_X=J^{1+o(1)},\qquad
 \widetilde L_X=O(J),\\
 \max_{0\le k\le\widetilde L_X}
 \left|\frac{b_{X,k}}{\mathcal R^{(t_X)}_{X,k}}-1\right|
       \ll \exp(-(\log J)^2)+X^{-\delta},\\
 0<\delta<1-b-\alpha-c/\alpha .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-profile}
\end{equation}
Here $\mathcal R^{(t_X)}$ is the same rational empirical Newton
reference as before, now using its first $t_X+1$ bins.
It exactly matches those bins and needs no further surviving
prime as input.
This uses $O((\log\log X)^2)$ measured bins and a single
construction works for every fixed power accuracy $J^{-A}$.
For $\mathcal P=\mathcal D$, $b=1/16$, $c=1/20$, one may
take $\delta=1/200$ unconditionally, with no value of
$\Theta_\zeta$ supplied to the computation.
All assertions are eventual; no uniform effective onset
threshold is assumed in the index hypothesis.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\delta$ and choose a fixed $\sigma>\alpha$ so that
$\xi=1-b-\sigma>c/\alpha+\delta$.
Use the full signed sampling theorem, which gives
$|b_{X,k}-\beta_{J,k}|\le\Delta_X\ll X^{-\xi}$ for all $k$.
Let $m=m_J=J^{-1+o(1)}$, and abbreviate $t=t_X$.
The exact model identity is
\[
 \beta_{J,i}=c_JW_J^ie_i,\qquad e_i=D_i/i! .
\]
In particular the observed coefficients in
\eqref{eq:empirical-cutoff-observed-coefficients}
have no extra factorial.  They obey
$\widehat e_{X,0}=\widehat e_{X,1}=1$.
The order-two normalization, for example, gives exactly
\[
 \widehat\theta_{X,1}=1,\qquad
 \widehat\theta_{X,2}
       =1-\frac{2b_{X,0}b_{X,2}}{b_{X,1}^2},
\]
consistent with the pair-collision correction above.

First retain the growing-node positive supply.
Since $t=O((\log J)^2)$ and $m=J^{-1+o(1)}$,
$t^2m\to0$.
The collision logarithm bound gives $D_i\ge1/2$ for all
$0\le i\le t$, eventually.
As $c_J\ge1/2$ and $W_J\le1$ eventually, one may use
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 u_X=\min_{0\le i\le t}\beta_{J,i}
       \ge\frac{W_J^t}{4t^t}
       \ge\exp(-C(\log J)^3),\\
       \Delta_X/u_X\le X^{-\xi+o(1)} .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-node-supply}
\end{equation}
The last step uses $-\log W_J=O(\log J)$ and
$(\log J)^3=o(\log X)$.
Thus every observed node is positive before any division.

Let $\zeta_i=\log(b_{X,i}/\beta_{J,i})$.
For $\Delta_X\le u_X/2$, $|\zeta_i|\le2\Delta_X/u_X$.
Subtracting the empirical normalization exactly gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \log\frac{\widehat e_{X,i}}{e_i}
       =\zeta_i-\zeta_0-i(\zeta_1-\zeta_0),\\
 \left|\log\frac{\widehat e_{X,i}}{e_i}\right|
                 \le4(i+1)\Delta_X/u_X .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-normalization-error}
\end{equation}
All empirical powers and both normalizing denominators remain
in this identity.
Since $e_i\le1$, the coefficient error consequently satisfies
\[
 \varepsilon_X=\max_{1\le i\le t}
                        |\widehat e_{X,i}-e_i|
       \le8(t+1)\Delta_X/u_X
       \le X^{-\xi+o(1)}
\]
eventually, when the logarithmic bound is at most one.
The recovery lemma applies.
Moreover $\theta_t\ge m^t\ge\exp(-C(\log J)^3)$, so
\begin{equation}
 \left|\frac{\widehat\theta_{X,t}}{\theta_t}-1\right|
     \le\frac{t4^t\varepsilon_X}{\theta_t}
     \le\Delta_X\exp(O((\log J)^3))
     \le X^{-\xi+o(1)} .
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-relative-moment}
\end{equation}
This proves positivity of the recovered high moment without
assuming that arbitrary empirical Newton moments are positive.

Eventually $\theta_t\le2\widehat\theta_{X,t}\le3\theta_t$.
Combining both power-sum inequalities of the lemma with
the rational upper-root bounds gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 m\le\widetilde m_X
       \le(1+1/t)\,3^{1/t}m^{\,1-1/t},\\
 0\le\log(\widetilde m_X/m)
       \le\log(1+1/t)+\frac{\log3+\log(1/m)}t
       =O(1/\log J).
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-largest-coordinate}
\end{equation}
This proves the asserted relative accuracy of the maximum.
The unobserved vector is used in the proof, not in the formula
for $\widetilde m_X$.

For completeness account for both integer cutoffs.
Write $\widetilde m_X/m=1+\rho_X$ with
$0\le\rho_X=O(1/\log J)$.
Monotonicity and a floor allowance of one give
\[
  \frac{L_X^\star}{1+\rho_X}-1
           \le\widetilde L_X\le L_X^\star .
\]
Indeed the unrounded capacity $1/(256m)$ is at least
$L_X^\star$, while the other entry $\kappa_c(X)$ is an integer
at least $L_X^\star$.
Both entries defining $L_X^\star$ have logarithmic index one
in $J$, and $\kappa_c(X)=O(J)$ by the independently checked
selector formula in the preceding theorem.
Thus $L_X^\star=J^{1+o(1)}=O(J)$ and its reciprocal is
$o(1/\log J)$.
Division by this positive cutoff proves the claimed ratio,
$\widetilde L_X=J^{1+o(1)}$ and $\widetilde L_X=O(J)$.
In particular $t\le\widetilde L_X$ eventually.
Also $\widetilde L_Xm\le1/256$.

We can now apply the explicit interpolation and stability
lemmas on the full output range.
With $L=\widetilde L_X$, their analytic remainder satisfies
\begin{equation}
 E=C_tL^{t+1}m^t
       \le(t+1)L\,4^{-t}
       \le\exp(-(\log J)^2)
                         \quad\hbox{eventually}.
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-superpolynomial-remainder}
\end{equation}
To check the final constant, note that
$t=(\log J)^2/(\log2)^2+O(\log J)$, so the logarithm
of the middle expression is at most
$-(2/\log2+o(1))(\log J)^2$.
The complete interpolation-parameter allowance is
\begin{equation}
 \eta_X=\frac{2\Delta_X}{u_X}S(L,t)
       \le\Delta_X\exp(O((\log J)^3))
       \le X^{-\xi+o(1)} .
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-parameter-allowance}
\end{equation}
Here $S(L,t)\le(t+1)(1+2L)^t$, and
\eqref{eq:empirical-cutoff-node-supply} charges the noise in
every measured bin.

Finally the full positive mass over $k\le L$ satisfies the
same physical estimate, with all terms retained:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \begin{aligned}
 -\log\beta_{J,k}
       &\le O(1)+k\log(1/W_J)+\log(k!)+4k^2m\\
       &\le(c/\alpha+o(1))\log X,
 \end{aligned}\\
 v_X=\min_{0\le k\le L}\beta_{J,k}
       \ge X^{-c/\alpha-o(1)},\qquad
              \Delta_X/v_X=O(X^{-\delta}).
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-full-sampling-budget}
\end{equation}
The second inequality uses $L=O(J)$,
$k^2m\le k/256$, and $k\le\kappa_c(X)$.
The final estimate follows from
$\xi>c/\alpha+\delta$.

The exact signed consumer
\eqref{eq:adaptive-bin-signed-consumer}, or equivalently its
proved bound \eqref{eq:adaptive-bin-stability-bound}, gives
\[
 e^{E+\eta_X}-1+(\Delta_X/v_X)e^{E+\eta_X}
       \ll \exp(-(\log J)^2)+X^{-\delta}.
\]
This includes both the error in the inferred cutoff and all
compensation by the empirical normalizations.
The arithmetic positive supply is still $\beta_{J,k}$;
its negative allowance is $\Delta_X$.
Since both $\exp(-(\log J)^2)$ and $X^{-\delta}$ are
$o(J^{-A})$ for every fixed $A$, the same construction
gives every fixed power accuracy.
All its operations, including the upper root, are finite
rational or integer computations once the bins and $J$ are given.
For finitely many small inputs one may test for a zero node
or a nonpositive recovered moment before performing the divisions
and root; the theorem proves those tests eventually pass.
The $\mathcal D$ specialization follows from the already checked
margin $15/16-\Theta_\zeta-1/(20\Theta_\zeta)\ge3/560>1/200$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The same calibration within a logarithmic bin budget]
\label{cor:empirical-cutoff-logarithmic-budget}
Fix an integer $A\ge1$, and instead take
$t_X=(A+3)\ell_J$ in all the empirical formulas of
Theorem~\ref{thm:empirical-moment-cutoff}.
Denote the resulting maximum and cutoff by
$\widetilde m_X^{(A)}$ and $\widetilde L_X^{(A)}$.
Then, eventually,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 m_J\le\widetilde m_X^{(A)}
       \le\{2^{1/(A+3)}+o(1)\}m_J,\\
            \widetilde L_X^{(A)}=J^{1+o(1)}=O(J),\\
 2^{-1/(A+3)}
       \le\liminf_{X\to\infty}
           \frac{\widetilde L_X^{(A)}}{L_X^\star}
       \le\limsup_{X\to\infty}
           \frac{\widetilde L_X^{(A)}}{L_X^\star}\le1,\\
 \max_{0\le k\le\widetilde L_X^{(A)}}
 \left|\frac{b_{X,k}}{\mathcal R^{(t_X)}_{X,k}}-1\right|
                          \ll_A J^{-A}+X^{-\delta}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-logarithmic-version}
\end{equation}
Only $O_A(\log\log X)$ empirical bins are used, with no
additional surviving-prime input.
Any prescribed fixed relative uncertainty above one for the
maximum can be reached by choosing a sufficiently large fixed
$A$; no uniformity as $A$ varies with $X$ is asserted.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The proof just given applies with the smaller order, keeping
each dependence explicit.
Now the node supply is at least
$\exp(-C_A(\log J)^2)$, and both coefficient recovery and
relative moment recovery have error at most
$\Delta_X\exp(O_A((\log J)^2))$.
The rational-root bound is unchanged.
Since
\[
 m_J^{-1/t_X}\longrightarrow2^{1/(A+3)},
\]
it gives the stated one-sided bound on the maximum.
The floor comparison with $L_X^\star$, divided by that
diverging cutoff, gives the displayed liminf and limsup.
Also $\widetilde L_X^{(A)}m_J\le1/256$.
The explicit analytic remainder is now at most
$(t_X+1)\widetilde L_X^{(A)}4^{-t_X}
 =O_A((\log J)J^{-2A-5})$.
The full parameter and sampling allowances are bounded just
as before, giving the asserted relative error.
Finally $2^{1/(A+3)}\downarrow1$, which proves the last
fixed-accuracy assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Exact calibrated linear limits under power asymptotics]
\label{cor:empirical-cutoff-linear-limits}
If $p_j\sim Cj^{1/\alpha}$ for some $C>0$, put
$v=1/\alpha-1$.
The two fully empirical cutoffs above satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{\widetilde L_X}{J}
       &\longrightarrow
               \min\left\{\frac1{256v},\frac{c}{\alpha b}\right\},\\
 \frac{\widetilde L_X^{(A)}}J
       &\longrightarrow
          \min\left\{\frac1{256\,2^{1/(A+3)}v},
                                      \frac{c}{\alpha b}\right\}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:empirical-cutoff-linear-limits}
\end{equation}
Both limits are strictly positive.
The first reaches the full benchmark capacity of this
interpolation argument; the earlier prime-assisted cutoff had
capacity constant $1/(512v)$ before the same physical rank cap.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The preceding power-asymptotic corollary gives $m_J\sim v/J$.
The quadratic-order maximum estimate therefore yields
$\widetilde m_X\sim v/J$, proving the first limit.

For the logarithmic order $t=(A+3)\ell_J$, let
$q_J=\lfloor J/t\rfloor$.
Then $q_J\to\infty$, $q_J=o(J)$, and monotonicity of
the prime weights gives
\[
 \theta_t\ge q_Ja_{J+q_J}^{\,t},\qquad
 \frac{a_{J+q_J}}{m_J}
       =\frac{p_{J+1}-1}{p_{J+q_J}-1}\longrightarrow1 .
\]
The last limit follows directly from the common asymptotic
$p_j\sim Cj^{1/\alpha}$ and $q_J/J\to0$.
Also $q_J^{1/t}\to2^{1/(A+3)}$.
Together with $\theta_t\le m_J^{t-1}$, this proves
\[
 \frac{\theta_t^{1/t}}{m_J}\longrightarrow2^{1/(A+3)} .
\]
Relative empirical recovery and
$1\le\mathsf U_t(y)/y^{1/t}\le1+1/t$ then show that
$\widetilde m_X^{(A)}/m_J$ has that same limit.
Taking the two capacity minima, with floors costing at most
$1/J$ after division, proves the second displayed limit.
The comparison constant $1/(512v)$ is
\eqref{eq:adaptive-bin-linear-limit}.
No such additional power asymptotic is asserted for
$\mathcal D$.
\end{proof}

All results in this subsection have full ordinary proofs.
They do not add Lean declarations.
Power-sum recovery and approximation of a maximum by high
moments are classical ingredients, not new zero detectors.
The new arithmetic consumer preserves their precision through
the signed empirical normalization and through the rare-event
denominator, and it no longer enumerates a surviving prime
to choose the reference's range.
The quadratic bin budget improves calibration to relative
error $O(1/\log J)$ and gives superpolynomial accuracy in $J$;
the logarithmic budget retains the preceding fixed-power
accuracy with no extra prime input.
Neither statement proves a new estimate on
$\Theta_\zeta$; the certified zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.

\subsection{Finite integer signs recover a thin prime class}
\label{sec:finite-integer-prime-decoding}

Exact profile zeros already identify prime labels in
Theorem~\ref{thm:prime-denominator-profile-rigidity};
Theorem~\ref{thm:prime-profile-stable-recovery} gives their
separation. Those results do not bound the displacement caused
by an empirical function error.
We now control the entire error on a growing disc and preserve
the signs at negative half-integers.
The decoder will use integer arithmetic, without locating roots.
The forward Bernoulli-product mechanism is classical; compare
\cite[Proposition~3.1]{KN}.
The finite arithmetic sign transfer, its quantitative range,
and the decoding capacity below are the conclusions proved here;
no established literature priority is claimed.

Fix any set of primes $\mathcal P$, possibly finite or empty,
for which
\begin{equation}
 0<\sigma<1,\qquad
 S_{\mathcal P,\sigma}=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}p^{-\sigma}<\infty .
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-summability}
\end{equation}
For positive integers $m$ use the actual divisor count
$K_{\mathcal P}(m)=\#\{p\in\mathcal P:p\mid m\}$.
This count is finite at every $m$.
For integer $N\ge4$ put
\begin{equation}
 C_{N,k}=\#\{1\le m\le N:K_{\mathcal P}(m)=k\},
       \qquad b_{N,k}=C_{N,k}/N .
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-actual-counts}
\end{equation}
The prime class is fixed before sampling.
The independent reference has success probabilities $1/p$ and
the proper entire generating function
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 P_{\mathcal P}(z)=\sum_{k\ge0}\beta_kz^k
       =c_{\mathcal P}\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}(1+z/(p-1)),\\
 c_{\mathcal P}=\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}(1-1/p)>0 .
 \end{gathered}
\]
No asymptotic prime index or infinitude is assumed in this subsection.

Fix positive rational numbers $h,c$ satisfying
\begin{equation}
 h\sigma<1,\qquad 0<c<(1-\sigma)/h .
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-parameter-domain}
\end{equation}
All parameters are fixed before $N$ tends to infinity.
Define the integer cutoffs and empirical polynomial by
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 U_N=\lfloor\log_2N\rfloor,\quad
 V_N=\lfloor\log_2U_N\rfloor,\\
 M_N=\left\lfloor cU_N/V_N\right\rfloor,\quad
 R_N=\lfloor U_N^h\rfloor,\\
 T_N(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{M_N}b_{N,k}z^k .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-polynomial}
\end{equation}
Here $U_N\ge2$, $V_N\ge1$ and $R_N\ge1$.
The selector formula is valid for every positive rational $c$,
as independently checked in
Theorem~\ref{thm:adaptive-bin-almost-linear}.
For $h=a/b>0$ with positive integers $a,b$,
$R_N$ is the integer $b$th root of $U_N^a$.
Consequently no comparison with an unspecified real cutoff is used.

\begin{lemma}[Sampling on a growing complex disc]
\label{lem:finite-decoding-complex-error}
For every fixed
\begin{equation}
 0<\eta<\min\{1-\sigma-hc,\ c(1/\sigma-h)\},
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-error-exponent}
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{|z|\le2R_N}|T_N(z)-P_{\mathcal P}(z)|
                      \ll_{\mathcal P,\sigma,h,c,\eta}N^{-\eta}.
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-complex-error}
\end{equation}
The constant and eventual threshold are independent of $z$.
This includes finite and empty $\mathcal P$.
If $\sigma$ is rational, the choice $c=\sigma(1-\sigma)$
balances the two displayed losses and permits every
$\eta<(1-\sigma)(1-h\sigma)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The complete count-profile law identifies the reference coefficients
with the Taylor coefficients of the actual entire Euler product.
Its signed finite-subset sampling identity and the rounding estimate
\[
 0\le q^{-1}-N^{-1}\lfloor N/q\rfloor
                 \le N^{\sigma-1}q^{-\sigma}\quad(q\ge1)
\]
give the uniform finite-reference bound. Passing every partial
coefficient sum to the reference limit gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \sum_{k\ge0}|b_{N,k}-\beta_k|
     \le B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}N^{\sigma-1},\\
 B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}
       =\exp(2S_{\mathcal P,\sigma})<\infty .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-arithmetic-input}
\end{equation}
This uses the count law alone and includes finite and empty prime classes.

The coefficient proof of
\eqref{eq:thin-prime-coefficient-bounds} likewise requires only
this summability. In detail, with
$A_\sigma=\sigma^{-1}S_{\mathcal P,\sigma}$,
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \log P_{\mathcal P}(r)\le A_\sigma r^\sigma\quad(r\ge1),\\
 \beta_k\le\exp\{A_\sigma k-(k/\sigma)\log k\}\quad(k\ge2).
 \end{gathered}
\]
The first inequality follows from
$\log(1+y)\le y^\sigma/\sigma$.
The second follows by bounding a nonnegative coefficient with
$r^{-k}P_{\mathcal P}(r)$ and taking $r=k^{1/\sigma}$.
No positive lower bound for high coefficients is required;
they may be zero in a finite class.

Write $M=M_N$ and $R=R_N$.
Retain the complete signed error before bounding its two remainders:
\begin{equation}
 T_N(z)-P_{\mathcal P}(z)
       =\sum_{k=0}^M(b_{N,k}-\beta_k)z^k
                              -\sum_{k>M}\beta_kz^k .
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-complete-error}
\end{equation}
On $|z|\le2R$, the first remainder is at most
\[
 B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}N^{\sigma-1}(2R)^M
                  =N^{-(1-\sigma-hc)+o(1)} .
\]
This charges the full growing test $(2R)^M$.
For the second, $M\to\infty$ and, eventually, $M+1\ge2$.
Put
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 a_N&=\sigma^{-1}\log(M+1)-\log(2R)-A_\sigma\\
    &=(1/\sigma-h+o(1))\log\log N\longrightarrow\infty .
 \end{aligned}
\]
For every $k>M$, the coefficient bound gives
$\beta_k(2R)^k\le e^{-a_Nk}$.
Consequently
\[
 \sum_{k>M}\beta_k(2R)^k
       \le\frac{e^{-a_N(M+1)}}{1-e^{-a_N}}
       =N^{-c(1/\sigma-h)+o(1)} .
\]
Here $M\sim c\log N/\log\log N$ and
$\log R=(h+o(1))\log\log N$, by the integer selector formulas.
Both exponents are positive by the parameter domain.
For each stated strict $\eta$, the first remainder is eventually at most
$B_{\mathcal P,\sigma}N^{-\eta}$.
The tail slope eventually satisfies $a_N\ge\log2$, so its denominator
is at least $1/2$, and the second remainder is at most $2N^{-\eta}$.
Thus the constant in \eqref{eq:finite-decoding-complex-error} can be taken as
$\exp(2S_{\mathcal P,\sigma})+2$, with an eventual threshold depending
on the fixed parameters.
Finally substituting $c=\sigma(1-\sigma)$ makes both
exponents $(1-\sigma)(1-h\sigma)$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Formalization status.}
The complete statement of Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-decoding-complex-error},
including the balanced specialization, is Lean-verified by
\texttt{finite\_\allowbreak decoding\_\allowbreak complex\_\allowbreak error} and
\texttt{finite\_\allowbreak decoding\_\allowbreak complex\_\allowbreak error\_\allowbreak balanced} in
\texttt{Helpers/}\allowbreak\texttt{PrimeClassDecoderComplexError.lean}.
The proof derives the entire-product growth, all coefficient and series
tails, the full signed error decomposition, and both losses for the
literal integer selectors $U_N,V_N,M_N,R_N$.
Its threshold is uniform over the whole disc and its constant is explicit.
The signed half-integer supply and the mathematical decoding guarantees
below are also Lean-verified. The computation-cost clauses are tracked
separately and are not included in that verification claim.

\begin{theorem}[Exact prime labels from finite integer sign tests]
\label{thm:finite-integer-prime-decoding}
Use \eqref{eq:finite-decoding-summability}--%
\eqref{eq:finite-decoding-polynomial}.
For every integer $0\le n\le R_N$ compute the integer
\begin{equation}
 I_{N,n}=\sum_{k=0}^{M_N}(-1)^k C_{N,k}
                       (2n+1)^k2^{M_N-k}.
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-integer-probe}
\end{equation}
For all sufficiently large integer $N$, every such integer
is nonzero and, simultaneously for all those $n$,
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{sgn}I_{N,n}
                 =(-1)^{N_{\mathcal P}(n+1)} .
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-sign-parity}
\end{equation}
In particular the completely finite decoder
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \widehat{\mathcal P}_{N;h,c}
  &=\{n+1:1\le n\le R_N,\ I_{N,n-1}I_{N,n}<0\}\\
  &=\mathcal P\cap\{2,\ldots,R_N+1\}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-exact-class}
\end{equation}
is eventually exact.
It also gives every prefix count through $R_N+1$ by summing
the indicated sign changes.
Given $N,h,c$ and the $M_N+1$ integer counts, it uses no
additional class-membership or primality queries.
There are $O(R_NM_N)$ integer arithmetic operations, on integers
of $O_{h,c}(\log N)$ bits.
The cost of obtaining the input counts is not included.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We give an independent lower bound for the magnitude of the
signed model at the actual evaluation points.
For any integer $R\ge1$ and $0\le n\le R$, put
$w_n=-n-1/2$ and $S=S_{\mathcal P,\sigma}$.
Use the actual probability-normalized Euler factors:
\[
 P_{\mathcal P}(z)=\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}
                 \left(1+\frac{z-1}{p}\right),\qquad
 f_p(w_n)=\frac{p-n-3/2}{p}.
\]
Every numerator is a nonzero half-integer and has absolute value
at least $1/2$. For $p\le4R+2$,
\[
 |f_p(w_n)|\ge\frac1{2p}\ge\frac1{12R},\qquad
 N_{\mathcal P}(4R+2)\le6^\sigma SR^\sigma .
\]
For $p>4R+2$, put $t=(n+3/2)/p$. Then $0\le t<1/2$, so
$\log f_p(w_n)=\log(1-t)\ge-2t\ge-5R/p$.
The tail over the same class satisfies
\[
 \sum_{\substack{p\in\mathcal P\\p>4R+2}}\frac1p
                            \le R^{\sigma-1}S.
\]
Moreover $f_p(w_n)<0$ if and only if $p\le n+1$.
Thus for every finite class prefix with endpoint $Y\ge4R+2$,
multiplication by $(-1)^{N_{\mathcal P}(n+1)}$ gives exactly the
product of the factor magnitudes. Bounding that signed finite
product and passing to its proved Euler-product limit gives
\begin{equation}
 0<e^{-6^\sigma SR^\sigma\log(12R)-5SR^\sigma}
 \le(-1)^{N_{\mathcal P}(n+1)}P_{\mathcal P}(w_n).
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-independent-supply}
\end{equation}
This bound includes every probability-normalization factor:
it is proved directly for $1+(z-1)/p$, without removing the
constant factor from the product.

Now take $R=R_N$.
Since $h\sigma<1$,
$R_N^\sigma\log(2R_N)=o(\log N)$.
Thus the independent lower bound in
\eqref{eq:finite-decoding-independent-supply} is $N^{-o(1)}$,
uniformly at all evaluation points.
Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-decoding-complex-error} makes the
arithmetic and truncation allowance $O(N^{-\eta})$ for a
fixed $\eta>0$.
It is eventually strictly smaller than that magnitude.
Both quantities are real at $w_n$, so their signs agree:
\[
 \operatorname{sgn}T_N(w_n)
                       =\operatorname{sgn}P_{\mathcal P}(w_n).
\]
This preserves the signed model signal rather than replacing
it by an unsigned coefficient mass.
The exact integer normalization is
\begin{equation}
             I_{N,n}=N2^{M_N}T_N(-n-1/2),
                   \qquad N2^{M_N}>0 .
 \label{eq:finite-decoding-exact-normalization}
\end{equation}
It proves nonvanishing and
\eqref{eq:finite-decoding-sign-parity}.
Subtracting the two adjacent prime counts gives the class
criterion, because
$N_{\mathcal P}(n+1)-N_{\mathcal P}(n)$ is one precisely
when the integer $n+1$ belongs to $\mathcal P$, and is zero otherwise.

At $n=0$ the sign is positive.
The step $n=1$ detects $2$, and $n=2$ detects $3$, with no
exceptional-prime deletion.
All later composite labels produce no sign change.
The same proof includes a finite class without knowing its
last prime. For the empty class all probes are the positive
integer $N2^{M_N}$, so the decoded set is empty even before
the eventual range is reached.

The algorithm is well defined for every $N\ge4$, even if a
small-input probe vanishes: the strict integer inequality still
defines a finite set.
Its guarantee is eventual, with a threshold depending on the
fixed class and parameters.
For the evaluation algorithm, write $M=M_N$, $R=R_N$ and
$x_n=-2n-1$. Initialize $A_0=0$, $V_0=1$ and, for $0\le j\le M$, compute
\[
 A_{j+1}=x_nA_j+C_{N,M-j}V_j,\qquad V_{j+1}=2V_j .
\]
Induction gives $V_j=2^j$ and
\[
 A_j=\sum_{i<j}C_{N,M-i}x_n^{j-1-i}2^i,\qquad A_{M+1}=I_{N,n}.
\]
Thus the evaluation uses only the supplied counts through rank $M$.
The executable decoder is unchanged if any higher counts are changed.
Five arithmetic operations per step include the coefficient-index
subtraction, the two value multiplications, addition and power doubling.
Charging four setup operations per point and multiplication and
comparison at every adjacent test gives, eventually,
\[
 (R+1)\{5(M+1)+4\}+2R\le30RM .
\]
Here $R,M\ge1$ eventually. The literal executable program may instead
evaluate both endpoints separately at each of the $R$ adjacent tests.
Charging those repeated evaluations gives the valid bound
\[
 2R\{5(M+1)+4\}+2R\le30RM
\]
as well. Thus no caching assumption is needed for its complexity.
Input-count acquisition is excluded.

The actual sample partition gives $\sum_{k=0}^{M}C_{N,k}\le N$.
The output satisfies $|I_{N,n}|\le N(2R+1)^M2^M$.
Every partial Horner accumulator, its next multiplication,
scaled coefficient and stored power is bounded by
\[
 B_N=N(2R+1)^M4^M=N(4R+2)^M2^M .
\]
This follows by retaining the partial sum above, bounding each
coefficient by $N$ and using $j\le M+1\le2^M$.
Products of adjacent probes are charged the full bound $B_N^2$.
The literal selector limits give
\[
 \frac{M_N}{\log N}\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \frac{M_N\log(2R_N)}{\log N}\longrightarrow ch,\qquad
 \log B_N\le(2+ch)\log N
\]
eventually. For the binary width
$b(z)=1+\lfloor\log_2(1+|z|)\rfloor$, put
$D_{h,c}=(2+ch)/\log2+2>0$.
Every value bounded by $B_N$ has width at most
$D_{h,c}\log N$ eventually; comparison products have width at most
$2D_{h,c}\log N$. All thresholds are simultaneous across the
evaluation range and all Horner steps.
Each shorter prefix uses the same signs and gives its exact class count.

The rational parameters also admit an integer-only construction of both
selectors. Write $h=a/d$ and $c=u/v$ with positive integers and
$d,v\ge1$. Compute $U_N$ and $V_N$ by successive divisions by $2$.
Their numbers of visited tests are exactly $U_N+1$ and $V_N+1$,
including inputs $0$ and $1$, and $V_N\le U_N$.
The rank is the integer quotient
\[
 M_N=\frac{uU_N}{vV_N}\ \hbox{rounded down},
\]
with quotient zero when $V_N=0$, agreeing with the original formula.
For the radius, form $Y=U_N^a$ and start at $r=0$; increase $r$
while $(r+1)^d\le Y$. The returned integer is exactly $R_N$.
This forward search visits at most $R_N+1$ tests.
An arbitrary a priori termination fuel does not enter that test count.

Charge five operations per binary-log test, $a$ multiplications to
form $Y$, $d+6$ operations per root test and twelve fixed setup/rank
operations. The resulting preprocessing count is at most
\[
 (a+d+28)(U_N+R_N+1).
\]
The proved selector limits imply
\[
 \frac{\log\log N}{R_N}\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \frac{\log N}{R_NM_N}\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \frac{U_N+R_N+1}{R_NM_N}\longrightarrow0.
\]
For the first limit, divide $\log R_N/R_N\to0$ by the earlier
normalized logarithmic radius limit. Combine that limit with the
normalized rank limit to obtain the second; the binary-cutoff limit
then gives the third.
Consequently preprocessing is eventually at most $R_NM_N$.
The complete arithmetic count, including the literal program's
repeated evaluations, is therefore at most $31R_NM_N$.

Finally put $E=(a+1)(d+1)$ and $K=a+d+u+v+1$.
For $U_N\ge1$, every root-search power with base at most $U_N^a+1$
and exponent at most $d+1$ is at most $(U_N+1)^E$.
Both rank multiplication inputs and their quotient are bounded by
$K(U_N+1)^E$. The same scalar bounds all fixed parameter integers.
For every fixed $K,E$, the proved limit
\[
 \frac{\log\{K(U_N+1)^E\}}{\log N}\longrightarrow0
\]
implies $K(U_N+1)^E\le N$ eventually.
The binary-log values and termination fuel are at most $N+1$.
Thus every retained preprocessing integer is bounded by
$N+1+K(U_N+1)^E\le3N\le B_N$ eventually.
This proves its claimed logarithmic binary width on the same range.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Formalization status.}
The complete statement of Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-integer-prime-decoding}
is Lean-verified by
\texttt{finite\_\allowbreak integer\_\allowbreak prime\_\allowbreak decoding\_\allowbreak complete}
in \texttt{Helpers/}\allowbreak\texttt{PrimeClassDecoderComplete.lean}.
It derives the actual signed infinite-product supply and literal integer
signs, nonvanishing, all prefix sets and counts, executable selection from
rational parameters and observed counts alone, the full preprocessing
and evaluation arithmetic budget, and all retained binary-width bounds.
One eventual threshold covers all ranges and all these conclusions.
The executable program makes no class-membership or primality queries
and no caching assumption enters its displayed cost.
The theorem includes $2$, composites and finite or empty classes.
The capacity classification and RH corollaries below remain separate
formalization obligations; this completion makes no new claim about
the zero-free boundary.

\subsection{Exact decoding capacity and an operational RH criterion}
\label{sec:prime-decoding-capacity}

The degree budget supplies a converse to the preceding analytic
construction. This gives a critical exponent for this particular
polynomial sign decoder, not an impossibility theorem for every
algorithm that might use the same counts.

For every positive rational $h,c$, the sign-test set in the
first line of \eqref{eq:finite-decoding-exact-class} is defined
by the integer formula, without the analytic parameter
restrictions. Its eventual equality with the class is the
property to be required, rather than part of that unrestricted
definition.

For a class satisfying
\eqref{eq:finite-decoding-summability} for at least one
$0<\sigma<1$, define its convergence exponent and admissible
decoding powers by
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \tau_{\mathcal P}=
    \inf\{s>0:\ \sum_{p\in\mathcal P}p^{-s}<\infty\}
                         \in[0,1),\\
 \mathcal H_{\rm dec}(\mathcal P)=
 \left\{h\in\mathbb Q_{>0}:
   \begin{array}{l}
   \text{there is a fixed $c\in\mathbb Q_{>0}$ such that}\\
   \widehat{\mathcal P}_{N;h,c}
      =\mathcal P\cap\{2,\ldots,R_N+1\}\\
   \text{for every sufficiently large integer $N$}
   \end{array}\right\},\\
 \mathcal C_{\rm dec}(\mathcal P)
       =\sup\mathcal H_{\rm dec}(\mathcal P)\in[0,+\infty].
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:prime-decoding-capacity-definition}
\end{equation}
In this definition the counts and the decoder are always
\eqref{eq:finite-decoding-actual-counts} and
\eqref{eq:finite-decoding-integer-probe}--%
\eqref{eq:finite-decoding-exact-class};
$M_N=\lfloor cU_N/V_N\rfloor$ and $R_N=\lfloor U_N^h\rfloor$.
The constant $c$ may depend on $h$ and the class, but never on $N$.

\begin{theorem}[The exact critical decoding exponent]
\label{thm:prime-decoding-critical-exponent}
With the convention $1/0=+\infty$,
\begin{equation}
             \mathcal C_{\rm dec}(\mathcal P)=1/\tau_{\mathcal P}.
 \label{eq:prime-decoding-critical-exponent}
\end{equation}
Every positive rational $h<1/\tau_{\mathcal P}$ is admissible.
No positive rational $h>1/\tau_{\mathcal P}$ is admissible.
The theorem makes no assertion about attainment at equality.

If additionally
$N_{\mathcal P}(x)=x^{\alpha+o(1)}$, $0<\alpha<1$, then
$\tau_{\mathcal P}=\alpha$.
For any fixed positive rational $h>1/\alpha$ and $c$, every
decoder based on
the consecutive signs of a nonzero real polynomial of degree
at most $M_N$ misses a fraction $1-o(1)$ of the class primes
in $\{2,\ldots,R_N+1\}$.
This last assertion allows arbitrary real coefficients, not
only the actual empirical polynomial.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The set of convergent exponents is upward closed.
Consequently every $s>\tau_{\mathcal P}$ is a convergent
exponent: the definition of the infimum supplies a convergent
exponent strictly below $s$, and comparison gives convergence at $s$.
If $0<h<1/\tau_{\mathcal P}$, choose
\[
 \tau_{\mathcal P}<\sigma<\min\{1,1/h\},
       \qquad 0<c<(1-\sigma)/h
\]
with $c$ rational.
The preceding decoder theorem applies, proving admissibility.
When $\tau_{\mathcal P}=0$, this works for every fixed
positive rational $h$ and already proves infinite capacity.

For the converse let $h$ be admissible, with its fixed $c$.
For every $N$, $T_N$ is a nonzero polynomial:
its constant coefficient is at least $1/N$, because
$K_{\mathcal P}(1)=0$.
A strict sign change between $T_N(-n+1/2)$ and
$T_N(-n-1/2)$ forces a real zero in the open interval
$(-n-1/2,-n+1/2)$.
Those intervals are pairwise disjoint.
A nonzero polynomial of degree at most $M_N$ has at most
$M_N$ distinct real roots.
Thus the following is an exact degree constraint, before any
analytic relaxation:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
       |\widehat{\mathcal P}_{N;h,c}|\le M_N,\\
       N_{\mathcal P}(R_N+1)\le M_N
                 \quad\hbox{whenever decoding is exact}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:prime-decoding-degree-obstruction}
\end{equation}
Zeros at shared interval endpoints cannot create a strict
sign change and cause no exception.

To obtain a bound at every large integer cutoff $x$, put
$q=\lceil(x+1)^{1/h}\rceil$ and $N=2^q$.
Then $U_N=q$, $R_N\ge x+1$,
$q\sim x^{1/h}$, and
$M_N=O_c(q/\log q)$.
The eventual exactness hypothesis therefore gives
\[
 N_{\mathcal P}(x)\ll_{h,c}x^{1/h}/\log x .
\]
For any $s>1/h$, summing over dyadic prime intervals now gives
\[
 \sum_{p\in\mathcal P}p^{-s}
    \le\text{a finite initial sum}
       +\sum_{j\ge j_0}2^{-js}N_{\mathcal P}(2^{j+1})
    <\infty .
\]
It follows that $\tau_{\mathcal P}\le1/h$.
For $\tau_{\mathcal P}>0$ this says
$h\le1/\tau_{\mathcal P}$, proving the required upper bound
on the capacity.
Rational density and the proved admissibility below that value
give the exact supremum.

Under the additional exact counting index,
convergence holds for every $s>\alpha$ by dyadic comparison.
If $0<s<\alpha$, a convergent sum would imply
$N_{\mathcal P}(x)\le x^s\sum_{\mathcal P}p^{-s}$,
contradicting $N_{\mathcal P}(x)=x^{\alpha+o(1)}$.
Thus $\tau_{\mathcal P}=\alpha$.
Finally if $h\alpha>1$,
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 N_{\mathcal P}(R_N+1)=(\log N)^{h\alpha+o(1)},\qquad
       M_N=(\log N)^{1+o(1)},\\
       \frac{M_N}{N_{\mathcal P}(R_N+1)}\longrightarrow0 .
 \end{gathered}
\]
The degree argument applies to any nonzero real polynomial
and permits at most $M_N$ reported labels.
At least $N_{\mathcal P}(R_N+1)-M_N$ true labels must be
missed, proving the asserted fraction.
This is a restriction of the polynomial sign-code scheme;
it is not an information-theoretic bound on arbitrary
finite-data procedures.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A finite integer-sign criterion for RH]
\label{cor:prime-decoding-RH}
For the fixed greedy deleted class $\mathcal D$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal C_{\rm dec}(\mathcal D)=1/\Theta_\zeta,\qquad
               \frac87\le\mathcal C_{\rm dec}(\mathcal D)\le2 .
 \label{eq:prime-decoding-zero-supremum}
\end{equation}
In particular
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 {\rm RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
                   \mathcal C_{\rm dec}(\mathcal D)=2,\\
 \Theta_\zeta\le3/4\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
                   \mathcal C_{\rm dec}(\mathcal D)\ge4/3 .
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:prime-decoding-RH-equivalences}
\end{equation}
An equivalent literal form of the first statement is:
for every rational $0<h<2$ there is a fixed positive rational
$c$ such that the integer tests
\eqref{eq:finite-decoding-integer-probe} recover exactly
$\mathcal D\cap\{2,\ldots,\lfloor U_N^h\rfloor+1\}$ for
every sufficiently large integer $N$.

Unconditionally, one explicit choice is
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 h=10/9,\qquad \sigma=8/9,\qquad c=8/81,\\
 \sup_{|z|\le2R_N}|T_N(z)-P_{\mathcal D}(z)|
                          =O(N^{-1/1000}).
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:prime-decoding-explicit-unconditional}
\end{equation}
The corresponding integer signs eventually decode the full
displayed prefix, including the labels $2$ and $3$ when present.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The proved all-scale counting index for $\mathcal D$ is
$N_{\mathcal D}(x)=x^{\Theta_\zeta+o(1)}$, with
$1/2\le\Theta_\zeta\le7/8$.
The critical-exponent theorem therefore gives
$\mathcal C_{\rm dec}(\mathcal D)=1/\Theta_\zeta$ and the
displayed bounds.
The already established equivalence
$ \mathrm{RH}\Longleftrightarrow\Theta_\zeta=1/2$ proves the
first criterion, and positivity of $\Theta_\zeta$ gives the
$3/4$ criterion.
Admissibility of every $h<1/\Theta_\zeta$ proves the literal
RH implication.
Conversely admissibility of all rational $h<2$ implies
$\Theta_\zeta\le1/h$ for all those $h$, hence
$\Theta_\zeta\le1/2$.

For the numerical specialization, $\sigma=8/9>7/8$ makes
$S_{\mathcal D,\sigma}$ finite without RH.
Also $h\sigma=80/81<1$ and
$c=\sigma(1-\sigma)$.
Both errors of the complex-disc lemma have exponent
$(1-\sigma)(1-h\sigma)=1/729>1/1000$.
The independent half-integer magnitude is $N^{-o(1)}$,
so that error preserves every required sign eventually.
This proof supplies no new independent upper estimate on
$\Theta_\zeta$; it uses the installed $7/8$ boundary.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Prime values of exponential sequences have infinite capacity]
\label{cor:prime-decoding-sparse-special-classes}
Let each of the following denote the set of distinct
\emph{prime values} of the indicated sequence:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal P_{\rm Fermat}
   &=\{2^{2^j}+1:j\ge0,\ 2^{2^j}+1\text{ prime}\},\\
 \mathcal P_{\rm Mersenne}
   &=\{2^m-1:m\ge2,\ 2^m-1\text{ prime}\},\\
 \mathcal P_{\rm Fib}
   &=\{F_m:m\ge0,\ F_m\text{ prime}\},
       \qquad F_0=0,\ F_1=1 .
 \end{aligned}
\]
The Fibonacci recurrence is $F_{m+2}=F_{m+1}+F_m$.
For each class, whether it is finite or infinite,
\begin{equation}
                \tau_{\mathcal P}=0,\qquad
                \mathcal C_{\rm dec}(\mathcal P)=+\infty .
 \label{eq:prime-decoding-special-capacities}
\end{equation}
Thus every fixed rational logarithmic power of the prime
cutoff can be recovered by the corresponding finite sign
decoder, with an admissible fixed bin constant $c$.
This does not decide infinitude of any of the three classes.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For every fixed $\sigma>0$ the unfiltered sequence bounds give
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{p\in\mathcal P_{\rm Fermat}}p^{-\sigma}
       &\le\sum_{j\ge0}2^{-\sigma2^j}<\infty,\\
 \sum_{p\in\mathcal P_{\rm Mersenne}}p^{-\sigma}
       &\le\sum_{m\ge2}2^{-\sigma(m-1)}<\infty .
 \end{aligned}
\]
The composite sequence values occur only in these upper bounds;
the observations and decoded classes retain actual prime values.
For Fibonacci, Binet's identity with the golden ratio
$\phi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$ gives
\[
 F_m=\frac{\phi^m-(-\phi^{-1})^m}{\sqrt5}
       \ge\frac{\phi^m}{2\sqrt5}\quad(m\ge2).
\]
Consequently
$\sum_{p\in\mathcal P_{\rm Fib}}p^{-\sigma}
 \le(2\sqrt5)^\sigma\sum_{m\ge3}\phi^{-m\sigma}<\infty$.
The Fibonacci values at indices zero, one and two are nonprime;
the prime values from index three onward are distinct.
The other two sequences are strictly increasing.
Each class is a set, so a value is counted once.
They are separate observations; their overlaps, for example
the prime $3$, introduce no joint-independence assertion.

Since all positive exponents converge, each convergence
exponent is zero.
The capacity theorem proves the claim, including the case
of a finite class with an unknown final prime.
The sequences and primality filters are decidable on integer
domains, so their finite counts and sign tests are computable.
\end{proof}

Removing any prime class $\mathcal Q$ with
$\tau_{\mathcal Q}=0$ preserves the complete set of
convergent positive exponents:
\[
 \left\{s>0:\sum_{p\in\mathcal P\setminus\mathcal Q}p^{-s}<\infty\right\}
 =
 \left\{s>0:\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}p^{-s}<\infty\right\}.
\]
Consequently $\tau_{\mathcal P\setminus\mathcal Q}=\tau_{\mathcal P}$.
For the capacity conclusion retain this section's hypothesis:
$\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}p^{-s_0}<\infty$ for some $0<s_0<1$.
Then
$\mathcal C_{\rm dec}(\mathcal P\setminus\mathcal Q)
 =\mathcal C_{\rm dec}(\mathcal P)$.
This includes removing any subclass of the union of the three
displayed special prime classes, regardless of their overlaps.

Here is the complete summability argument.
Every prime class has a convergent exponent, since its
inverse-square series is bounded by the natural inverse-square
series. Thus the infimum defining $\tau_{\mathcal Q}$ has
a nonempty defining set, and $\tau_{\mathcal Q}=0$ implies
convergence at every positive exponent.
For each such exponent and every finite cutoff $X$, use the
exact partition of partial sums:
\[
 \sum_{\substack{p\le X\\p\in\mathcal P}}p^{-s}
 =
 \sum_{\substack{p\le X\\p\in\mathcal P\setminus\mathcal Q}}p^{-s}
 +\sum_{\substack{p\le X\\p\in\mathcal P\cap\mathcal Q}}p^{-s}.
\]
The last series converges by comparison with the series for
$\mathcal Q$. This proves the equality of convergence sets;
the capacity classification gives the stated capacity equality.
Finite unions of the special classes converge at every
positive exponent by the sum bound, as does every subclass.
The general deletion argument is Lean-verified by
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \texttt{primeClassConvergentExponents\_\allowbreak diff\_\allowbreak eq},\\
 \texttt{primeClassConvergenceExponent\_\allowbreak diff\_\allowbreak eq},\\
 \texttt{integerDecoderCapacity\_\allowbreak diff\_\allowbreak eq}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
The union and arbitrary-subclass applications are also checked.
Applying this to $\mathcal D$ uses its class-specific summability
input; the general invariance proof does not verify that input
or prove the RH endpoint.

The complete finite decoder theorem is now formalized in Lean,
including the growing-disc approximation, independent signed
half-integer supply, exact recovery, executable rational selectors,
and the full arithmetic and bit-cost clauses.
The maintained declaration
\[
 \texttt{finite\_\allowbreak integer\_\allowbreak prime\_\allowbreak
 decoding\_\allowbreak complete}
\]
verifies Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-integer-prime-decoding}.

The critical-capacity theorem, including every clause of
Theorem~\ref{thm:prime-decoding-critical-exponent}, is verified by
\[
 \texttt{integer\_\allowbreak decoder\_\allowbreak capacity\_\allowbreak classification}.
\]
Its converse uses the exact samples
$N_j=2^{2^{bj}}$ for $h=a/b$.
They satisfy $U_{N_j}=2^{bj}$ and $R_{N_j}=2^{aj}$.
The actual degree constraint then bounds each geometric prime
shell, and a finite-subsums argument proves convergence for
every $s>b/a$.
The proof retains the infinite-capacity case $\tau_{\mathcal P}=0$,
identifies $\tau_{\mathcal P}=\alpha$ under the counting-index
hypothesis, and proves the missed-fraction limit for arbitrary
real polynomial coefficients.
It makes no endpoint-attainment assertion.
The sharper all-cutoff inverse estimate is also formalized:
\[
 \texttt{integerDecoderAdmissible\_\allowbreak all\_\allowbreak cutoff\_\allowbreak inverse\_\allowbreak bound}
\]
proves that each admissible positive rational $h$ has a fixed $K>0$
such that
$N_{\mathcal P}(x)\le Kx^{1/h}/\log x$
at every sufficiently large integer cutoff.
For $h=a/b$ the proof retains the full factor $bj$ in
$M_{N_j}\le c\,2^{bj}/(bj)$ and uses
$j=\lfloor\log_{2^a}x\rfloor+1$.
It gives $K=c\,2^b a\log2/b$, with the witness $c$
from admissibility.
Corollary~\ref{cor:prime-decoding-sparse-special-classes} is now
verified for the three actual prime-value classes by
\[
 \texttt{special\_\allowbreak prime\_\allowbreak value\_\allowbreak capacity}.
\]
Its certificate includes convergence at every positive exponent,
zero convergence exponent, infinite capacity, admissibility of
every positive rational power, and finite membership tests.
The real Binet identity and the displayed golden-ratio lower
bound are checked by
\texttt{fibonacci\_binet\_real} and
\texttt{fibonacci\_golden\_exponential\_lower}.
The membership tests search only indices
$n\le p$ for Fermat and Mersenne values and $n\le p+2$
for Fibonacci values, with the primality filter retained.
The monotonicity and small-index claims in the proof are
also checked. No infinitude hypothesis is used.
The general sparse-deletion argument and the three special-class
applications above are now formalized.
The RH corollaries and their class-specific inputs remain
separate formalization obligations.

These exact decoders use $M_N+1=O(\log N/\log\log N)$ bins
and recover prime labels on a growing prefix, a distinct conclusion
from the existing full-profile inverse theorem.
For $\mathcal D$ the certified zero-free boundary remains $7/8$;
the operational endpoint $2$ is an equivalence, not a newly
proved arithmetic estimate at that endpoint.

\subsection{Selecting certified good primes before estimating their size}
\label{sec:certified-good-prime-class}

We now reverse the order of the two obligations.
First construct a decidable class whose members satisfy the
Nicolas inequality independently; then ask how large that
same class must be to retain every forbidden zero signal.
Here, as in the preceding subsection, the selected primes
are cutoffs for the full-prime product.

Fix a deterministic certified rational approximation $a_p$,
for each prime $p\ge5$, such that
$|a_p-F(p)|\le p^{-3}$. Such an approximation is computable
from the finite primorial and finite prime product, together
with certified logarithms and Euler's constant.
Define the actual prime classes and their counting functions by
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal G&=\{p\ge5:\ p\ {\rm prime},\ a_p\le-2p^{-3}\},\\
 \mathcal B&=\{p\ge5:\ p\ {\rm prime},\ a_p>-2p^{-3}\},\\
 G(X)&=\#(\mathcal G\cap[5,X]),\qquad
 B(X)=\#(\mathcal B\cap[5,X]).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:certified-good-prime-definition}
\end{equation}
The rational comparison decides membership, including equality.
There is no need to decide whether the real number $F(p)$
equals zero. For every selected prime, unconditionally,
\begin{equation}
 p\in\mathcal G\ \Longrightarrow\ F(p)\le-p^{-3}<0,
 \qquad
 p\in\mathcal B\ \Longrightarrow\ F(p)>-3p^{-3}.
 \label{eq:certified-good-prime-inequality}
\end{equation}
Thus the member inequality is proved before any size assertion.

\begin{lemma}[Persistence through consecutive prime indices]
\label{lem:prime-index-persistence}
Suppose $|\theta(t)-t|=O_\kappa(t^\kappa\log^2t)$
for some $1/2\le\kappa\le1$. For consecutive primes $q<p$
with $X\le p\le2X$, one has, uniformly for sufficiently large $X$,
\begin{equation}
                    |F(p)-F(q)|
              \le C_\kappa X^{\kappa-2}\log^2X .
 \label{eq:prime-index-jump-bound}
\end{equation}
Here $C_\kappa>0$ is independent of the primes and $X$.
If $0<b<1/2$, $\kappa+b>1$, and arbitrarily large primes $P$
satisfy $F(P)>2P^{-b}$, then arbitrarily large $P$ satisfy
\begin{equation}
 B(2P)\ge c_\kappa P^{2-\kappa-b}/\log^2P.
 \label{eq:prime-index-bad-count}
\end{equation}
The same conclusion with $G$ in place of $B$ follows from
arbitrarily large $P$ with $F(P)<-2P^{-b}$.
The thresholds may depend on $b$, $\kappa$ and their input bounds.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $L=\log p$, $T=\theta(q)=\theta(p)-L$, and
$h(t)=1/(t\log t)$. PNT ensures that the segment
$[T,T+L]$ lies in $[p/2,2p]$ eventually, also when $\kappa=1$.
On this segment,
$|h'(t)|\ll1/(p^2\log p)$.
Since $Lh(p)=1/p$, the exact increment
\eqref{eq:theta-sign-Nicolas-increment} gives
\[
 \begin{split}
 F(p)-F(q)
 &=\int_T^{T+L}(h(t)-h(p))\,dt
                           +\log(1-1/p)+1/p,\\
 |F(p)-F(q)|
 &\ll\frac{|\theta(p)-p|+\log p+1}{p^2}.
 \end{split}
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:prime-index-jump-bound}.
The cancellation of $1/p$ has been performed exactly;
a bound on either uncancelled summand would lose that cancellation.

Increase $C_\kappa$ if necessary and put
\[
 M_P=\left\lfloor
       \frac{P^{2-\kappa-b}}{4C_\kappa\log^2P}
      \right\rfloor .
\]
Because $0<2-\kappa-b<1$, this tends to infinity and is
$o(P/\log P)$. PNT therefore supplies at least $M_P$
successor primes between $P$ and $2P$.
Telescoping the signed increment, followed by its proved
local variation bound, changes $F$ by at most $P^{-b}/4$
over these successors.
A positive starting value $>2P^{-b}$ consequently remains
$>P^{-b}>0$. Every one of these primes belongs to $\mathcal B$.
For a negative starting value $<-2P^{-b}$, every successor
satisfies $F(p)<-P^{-b}<-3p^{-3}$ eventually.
Then $a_p\le F(p)+p^{-3}<-2p^{-3}$, so these primes
belong to $\mathcal G$. Taking a fixed smaller positive
multiple of the displayed expression for $M_P$ proves both bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact size burden for the certified good class]
\label{thm:certified-good-prime-size}
The class $\mathcal G$ satisfies $F(p)<0$ on every member
without assuming RH. Nevertheless its counting exponent alone
does not retain the forbidden signal: unconditionally,
\[
        \limsup_{X\to\infty}
           \frac{\log(1+G(X))}{\log X}=1.
\]
More precisely the complementary count has the dichotomy
\begin{equation}
 \limsup_{X\to\infty}\frac{\log(1+B(X))}{\log X}
 =
 \begin{cases}
 0,&\text{if RH holds},\\
 1,&\text{if RH fails}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:certified-good-prime-dichotomy}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \mathrm{RH}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 B(X)=O(X^{1-\delta})\text{ for some fixed }0<\delta<1 .
 \label{eq:certified-good-prime-RH-size}
\end{equation}
The constant and eventual cutoff in this proposed size estimate
must be independent of $X$. They are not supplied here.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Under RH, \eqref{eq:nicolas-wave} and $\beta_0<2$ give
$F(p)\le-c/(\sqrt p\log p)$ eventually for some fixed $c>0$.
Thus $a_p\le-2p^{-3}$ eventually: $\mathcal B$ is finite,
and $\mathcal G$ contains every sufficiently large prime.
This proves the RH branches.

Suppose RH is false. Fix a zero with real part $\beta>1/2$
and an exponent $1-\beta<b<1/2$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:power-excursions} supplies both signs of $F$
with arbitrarily large multiples of $x^{-b}$.
The function $F$ is constant between consecutive primes.
PNT makes the last prime below $x$ asymptotic to $x$,
so the two signs occur at arbitrarily large primes
with the starting inequalities in
Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-index-persistence}.

Write $\Theta=\Theta_\zeta$ as in
Lemma~\ref{lem:prime-error-zero-supremum}.
If $\Theta<1$, fix $\Theta<\kappa<1$.
The proof of that lemma gives
$|\theta(x)-x|=O_\kappa(x^\kappa\log^2x)$.
The condition $\kappa+b>1$ follows from $\kappa>\Theta\ge\beta$.
The persistence lemma gives, for each sign along its own
unbounded sequence, at least
$c_\kappa P^{2-\kappa-b}/\log^2P$ primes in that class below $2P$.
Let $\beta$ approach $\Theta$ from the actual zeros,
$\kappa$ decrease to $\Theta$, and $b$ decrease to $1-\beta$.
The counting exponents $2-\kappa-b$ approach $1$.
No zero attaining $\Theta$ is assumed and no constant is
claimed uniform in these limits.
The elementary upper bound $G(X),B(X)\le X$ proves that
both limsup exponents are exactly $1$.

If $\Theta=1$, use $\kappa=1$ and the elementary PNT bound
$|\theta(x)-x|=O(x)$ in the persistence lemma.
Zeros with $\beta$ arbitrarily close to $1$ permit $b>1-\beta$
arbitrarily close to zero, giving exponents $1-b$ tending to $1$.
This proves the remaining case and the dichotomy.
A bound in \eqref{eq:certified-good-prime-RH-size}
would give a complementary limsup exponent at most $1-\delta$,
contradicting the false-RH branch.
The converse follows from finiteness of $\mathcal B$ under RH.
\end{proof}

This construction meets the first reverse-selection obligation
literally: its selected primes already satisfy the inequality.
It also proves the retention threshold for that same class:
omitting only a power-saving number of primes would force an
off-line zero's persistent positive signal to hit $\mathcal G$,
which is impossible by \eqref{eq:certified-good-prime-inequality}.
Merely proving that $\mathcal G$ has exponent $1$ is insufficient,
since both classes have exponent $1$ under false RH.

The attempted direct size argument from theta-sign monotonicity
has a precise unresolved edge. Its exact input is
\[
 F(p)=F(3)+
 \sum_{\substack{5\le r\le p\\r\ {\rm prime}}}
 \left\{\int_{\theta(r)-\log r}^{\theta(r)}
                \frac{dt}{t\log t}+\log(1-1/r)\right\}.
\]
The theta sign controls an individual increment as proved above,
whereas membership of $\mathcal B$ depends on this entire signed
prefix sum. Thus no bound on the number of increments of either
sign supplies a bound on $B(X)$. The jump estimate proves
persistence and the required burden; it does not supply the
independent upper count in
\eqref{eq:certified-good-prime-RH-size}.
That upper bound remains unproved. These are full ordinary proofs,
not new Lean-checked declarations or a claimed originality priority.
The certified zero-free boundary remains $7/8$.

\subsection{Nested Fermat carriers and a joint Fibonacci profile}
\label{sec:fermat-fibonacci-joint}

Fermat numbers need not be prime to supply exact clocks. Write
$T_j=2^{2^j}+1$ for $j\ge0$ and choose its least prime factor $R_j$.
Here the sampled value is $2^n-1$, with scalar exponent $n\ge0$:
we test $R_j\mid2^n-1$. This is a different observable from
testing divisibility directly on the Fermat sequence $T_n$.
The cutoff $k$ selects the labels $0\le j<k$, independently of
the sizes of $R_j$ and $2^n-1$.

\begin{theorem}[Fermat divisors give a nested dyadic clock]
\label{thm:fermat-dyadic-clock}
If $r>2$ divides $T_j$, then
\[
 \operatorname{ord}_{r}(2)=2^{j+1},\qquad
 r\mid2^n-1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad2^{j+1}\mid n.
\]
In particular all prime factors of a fixed $T_j$ have exactly the
same event on this sampled sequence. The selected primes $R_j$
are pairwise distinct, and $R_0=3$.
For
\[
 C_k(n)=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{1}_{R_j\mid2^n-1}
       =\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{1}_{2^{j+1}\mid n},
\]
uniform sampling over $0\le n<2^k$ gives
\[
