 \mathbb P(C_k=t)=2^{-t-1}\quad(0\le t<k),\qquad
 \mathbb P(C_k=k)=2^{-k}.
\]
Thus, with $G_k(z)=\mathbb E z^{C_k}$,
\begin{align}
 G_k(z)&=\frac12\sum_{t=0}^{k-1}(z/2)^t+(z/2)^k,
 \label{eq:fermat-geometric-pgf}\\
 (2-z)G_k(z)&=1+(1-z)(z/2)^k,\qquad
 \mathbb E C_k=1-2^{-k}.
 \label{eq:fermat-geometric-compensation}
\end{align}
The cross-multiplied identity holds for every complex $z$,
including $z=2$, where $G_k(2)=1+k/2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In $\mathbb Z/r\mathbb Z$ the hypothesis gives
$2^{2^j}=-1\ne1$, while $2^{2^{j+1}}=1$.
In particular $2$ is a unit. Its order divides $2^{j+1}$ but
does not divide $2^j$, so the order is exactly $2^{j+1}$.
The power test proves the claimed divisibility equivalence,
including $n=0$. A shared prime factor at two Fermat labels
would consequently have two different exact orders, which is
impossible. The first selected factor is the prime $T_0=3$.

For $t<k$, exactly $2^{k-t-1}$ residues in a complete period
are divisible by $2^t$ and not by $2^{t+1}$.
Their count $C_k$ is $t$. Only the zero residue is divisible by
$2^k$, and it has count $k$. This proves the distribution and
\eqref{eq:fermat-geometric-pgf}, also when $k=0$.
The indicator definition handles the zero residue without assigning
a finite $2$-adic valuation to zero. Multiplying the finite
geometric sum proves the cross identity without division by $2-z$.
Alternatively, splitting the sampled indices into even and odd
parts gives $G_0=1$ and $G_{k+1}=(1+zG_k)/2$.
Finally, summing the threshold probabilities gives
$\mathbb E C_k=\sum_{j<k}2^{-j-1}=1-2^{-k}$.
\end{proof}

These events are nested, not independent Bernoulli trials.
Nor is an infinitude of Fermat primes assumed: $R_j$ exists
even when $T_j$ is composite. Direct sampling of $T_n$ would
give a different law: each fixed $R_j$ divides only $T_j$,
so its density among Fermat indices is zero.

\begin{theorem}[A common-index product law and its exact compensation]
\label{thm:fermat-fibonacci-profile}
Let $S$ be a finite set of odd prime labels,
$M_S=\prod_{\ell\in S}\ell$, and $k\ge0$.
Sample the same index $n$ uniformly over
$0\le n<2^kM_S$, evaluating the pair $(F_n,2^n-1)$.
For every binary Fibonacci pattern $b$ and every complex $z$,
\[
 \mathbb E\!\left[
 z^{C_k(n)}
 \boldsymbol{1}_{
   \boldsymbol{1}_{P_\ell\mid F_n}=b_\ell\ \text{for every }\ell\in S}\right]
 =
 G_k(z)\prod_{\ell\in S}
 \left(\frac1\ell\right)^{b_\ell}
 \left(1-\frac1\ell\right)^{1-b_\ell}.
\]
Consequently, for arbitrary complex marks $w_\ell$,
\[
 \mathbb E\!\left[
 z^{C_k(n)}
 \prod_{\ell\in S}w_\ell^{\boldsymbol{1}_{P_\ell\mid F_n}}\right]
 =G_k(z)\prod_{\ell\in S}(1+(w_\ell-1)/\ell).
\]
The entire nested Fermat block is independent of the Fibonacci
block. No independence is asserted within the Fermat block.

For $S_X$ the odd primes at most $X$, put
$\mu_X=\sum_{\ell\in S_X}1/\ell$ and let
\[
 T_{X,k}(n)=C_k(n)+
   \sum_{\ell\in S_X}\boldsymbol{1}_{P_\ell\mid F_n}.
\]
Compensation by the \emph{actual mean} gives
\[
 H_{X,k}(z)
 =e^{-(z-1)(\mu_X+1-2^{-k})}\,
   \mathbb E z^{T_{X,k}}.
\]
As $X,k\to\infty$, independently, these functions converge
locally uniformly on $|z|<2$ to
\[
 H(z)=\frac{e^{1-z}\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(z)}{2-z},
 \qquad
 \mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(z)=
 \prod_{\ell\ge3\ {\rm prime}}
   (1+(z-1)/\ell)e^{-(z-1)/\ell}.
\]
This limit extends meromorphically to the whole plane.
It has a simple pole at $2$, with residue
$-e^{-1}\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(2)\ne0$.
The exact compensation
\[
 (2-z)e^{z-1}H(z)=\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(z)
 \label{eq:fermat-fibonacci-pole-removal}
\]
extends to an entire function and recovers all the simple zeros
$1-\ell$, for odd primes $\ell$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The previous carrier theorems replace the events by
$2^{j+1}\mid n$ and $\ell\mid n$, respectively.
Since $2^k$ and $M_S$ are coprime, CRT identifies one uniform
scalar residue with independent uniform residues modulo $2^k$
and modulo $M_S$. The first coordinate determines the whole
Fermat count; the second determines the full Fibonacci pattern.
This proves the first formula. Summing over all binary patterns,
with the stated marks, gives the second formula and the mean
$\mu_X+1-2^{-k}$. Empty supports and $k=0$ are included.

The exact finite identity gives, for $z\ne2$,
\[
 G_k(z)-\frac1{2-z}
 =\frac{(1-z)(z/2)^k}{2-z}.
\]
For every fixed $0\le R<2$ this implies the uniform bound
\[
 \sup_{|z|\le R}\left|G_k(z)-\frac1{2-z}\right|
 \le \frac{1+R}{2-R}(R/2)^k.
\]
The dyadic mean correction tends uniformly on compact sets to
$e^{1-z}$. The previously proved canonical Bernoulli products
converge locally uniformly to $\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}$.
Their product therefore gives the stated two-parameter limit.

The displayed quotient supplies the meromorphic extension.
Every factor at $z=2$ is positive, and the logarithms of the
factors have an absolutely convergent sum, because their
absolute values are bounded by a constant times $\ell^{-2}$.
Thus $\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(2)>0$ and the residue is nonzero.
Multiplying by the stated entire compensator removes this pole.
Away from $2$ the removed factors are nonzero, so all odd-prime
profile zeros and their multiplicities are preserved.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{A shared physical prime does not mean a shared index event.}
The same prime $5$ occurs as $P_5=5$ and $R_1=5$, yet
\[
 5\mid F_n\Longleftrightarrow5\mid n,\qquad
 5\mid2^n-1\Longleftrightarrow4\mid n.
\]
These two events have probabilities $1/5$ and $1/4$ and joint
probability $1/20$ under uniform sampling modulo $20$.
Their independence comes from their coprime index clocks.
Likewise $P_3=2$ supplies the index-$3$ event, while $R_0=3$
supplies the index-$2$ event. Thus the arithmetic names of the
carrier primes are not the parameters of the probability law.

\paragraph{The RH information and the order of limits.}
For $k\ge1$ the event $C_k=0$ is exactly $n$ odd.
Writing $A(X)$ as in Theorem~\ref{thm:fibonacci-marked-nicolas},
the limiting density of avoiding every selected carrier in both
sampled sequences is therefore
\[
 B(X)=\frac12 A(X)=\prod_{p\le X}(1-1/p).
\]
The existing Nicolas criterion becomes
\[
 \mathrm{RH}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 B(X)<\frac{e^{-\gamma}}{\log\log(2M_X)}
 \quad\hbox{for all sufficiently large }X\ge3.
\]
The extra nested Fermat events beyond $R_0$ do not change this
vacancy event. This exact translation supplies no new independent
bound on the product or on zeta zeros.

All finite probability formulas above first use a complete period.
For fixed $X,k$, interval sampling over $1\le n\le N$, $N\ge1$,
has pattern error at most $2^kM_X/N$, by the same period-remainder
argument as before. If a fixed finite weight has absolute value
at most $W$, the corresponding expectation error is at most
$2^{k+1}M_XW/N$: the incomplete period and its predicted average
each have absolute contribution at most $2^kM_XW$ before
dividing by $N$.
Only after $N\to\infty$ at fixed cutoffs are $X,k$ allowed to grow.
The local uniform profile limit does not assert simultaneous
growing-cutoff sampling accuracy.

\paragraph{Formalization scope.}
The maintained candidate owners
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/FermatCarrier.lean\\
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/FermatCarrierProbability.lean
\end{quote}
check twenty-nine declarations: exact orders and integer power
tests, distinct selected carriers, actual dyadic sample sums and
their geometric recurrence, compensation at all $z$, the
exceptional value at $2$, and the exact mean.
Four further checked declarations in
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Helpers/FermatFibonacciProbability.lean
\end{quote}
prove coprimality of the sample periods, pointwise compatibility
of the actual scalar events with CRT, the generating-sum
factorization over arbitrary commutative semirings, and the
normalized real joint law. All thirty-three axiom audits contain
only the standard foundational axioms. The complex normalized
law follows by the same finite algebra; the interval estimate,
locally uniform limit, residue, and RH translation above are
ordinary proofs, with no separate Lean compilation claim.
The elementary order and geometric-law mechanisms are classical.
The purpose of this construction is
their common-index realization alongside the Fibonacci golden-root
clock and the exact preservation of the marked profile.

\subsection{All Fermat prime factors: a uniform logarithmic tail}
\label{sec:all-fermat-factors}

The preceding construction selected one factor $R_j$ at each Fermat
label. We now retain \emph{every distinct physical prime factor}.
For $T_j=2^{2^j}+1$ define
\[
 m_j=\omega(T_j),\qquad W_t=\sum_{0\le j<t}m_j,\qquad
 \mathcal U_k=\bigcup_{0\le j<k}\operatorname{PF}(T_j),
\]
where $\operatorname{PF}$ is the finite set of distinct prime
factors. The supported count is
\[
 C_k^{\rm all}(n)
 =\#\{r\in\mathcal U_k:r\mid2^n-1\}\qquad(n\ge0).
\]
At $n=0$ this count is $W_k$; it is not defined using
$\operatorname{PF}(0)$.

\begin{theorem}[The full weighted Fermat law]
\label{thm:full-fermat-law}
The supports $\operatorname{PF}(T_j)$ are pairwise disjoint,
$m_j\ge1$, and, for every $j,t\ge0$,
\[
 (j+1)m_j\le2^j,\qquad
 W_t<2^t,\qquad
 (t+1)W_t\le3\cdot2^t.
\]
Every index has the exact count
\[
 C_k^{\rm all}(n)=
 \sum_{j<k}m_j\boldsymbol{1}_{2^{j+1}\mid n}.
\]
Uniform sampling over $0\le n<2^k$ gives the distribution
\[
 \mathbb P(C_k^{\rm all}=W_t)=2^{-t-1}\quad(0\le t<k),
 \qquad
 \mathbb P(C_k^{\rm all}=W_k)=2^{-k},
\]
and hence
\[
 Q_k(z)=\mathbb E z^{C_k^{\rm all}}
       =\frac12\sum_{t<k}\frac{z^{W_t}}{2^t}
          +\frac{z^{W_k}}{2^k}.
\]
No independence among the factors inside one Fermat number
is used.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
A shared prime factor at two distinct labels would have two
different orders by Theorem~\ref{thm:fermat-dyadic-clock}.
The supports are therefore disjoint, and each is nonempty.

For a prime factor $r$ of $T_j$, the order $2^{j+1}$ is at most
the size $r$ of $\mathbb Z/r\mathbb Z$. Equality is impossible:
$r>2$ is an odd prime, whereas $2^{j+1}$ is even.
Thus $r\ge2^{j+1}+1$.
The product of the distinct prime factors divides $T_j$, so
\[
 2^{(j+1)m_j}
 <\prod_{r\in\operatorname{PF}(T_j)}r
 \le T_j=2^{2^j}+1.
\]
All quantities are integers. Therefore
$2^{(j+1)m_j}\le2^{2^j}$ and $(j+1)m_j\le2^j$.

For general natural weights satisfying this last inequality,
induction in $t$ gives the slightly stronger first bound
$W_t+1\le2^t$: use $m_t\le2^t$ and
$W_{t+1}=W_t+m_t$.
The second bound has the immediate bases $t=0,1$.
For its induction step with $t\ge1$,
\[
 (t+1)W_{t+1}
 =(t+1)(W_t+m_t)\le4\cdot2^t.
\]
Consequently
\[
 (t+2)W_{t+1}
 \le4\frac{t+2}{t+1}\,2^t
 \le6\cdot2^t=3\cdot2^{t+1},
\]
where $t\ge1$ gives $(t+2)/(t+1)\le3/2$.

Every factor in the $j$th block divides $2^n-1$ exactly when
$2^{j+1}\mid n$. Disjointness allows the full supported count
to be summed block by block, proving its identity.
For a nonzero residue with $2$-adic valuation $t<k$ the count
is $W_t$; the zero residue has count $W_k$.
The same dyadic residue counts as before prove the distribution.
The levels are distinct because all $m_j$ are positive.
The generating formula follows, including $k=0$.
\end{proof}

For positive indices the finite count stabilizes as $k$ grows.
Define the full observable, with an explicitly finite ambient set,
by
\[
 C^{\rm all}(n)=
 \#\{r\in\operatorname{PF}(2^n-1):
       r\mid T_j\text{ for some }j\ge0\}
 =W_{v_2(n)}\qquad(n\ge1).
\]
This is the component of the Mersenne-value prime factors whose
base-two order is $2^a$ for some $a\ge1$. Indeed, in the odd
prime field the half-order power then has order two, so it is
$-1$; the prime therefore divides $T_{a-1}$. The converse is
the proved Fermat order test. The observable is finite even
though all Fermat labels are allowed.

\begin{theorem}[A tail bound uniform in sample size and cutoff]
\label{thm:full-fermat-tail}
For every real $x\ge1$, every integer $N\ge1$, and every
cutoff $k\ge0$,
\begin{align*}
 \frac1N\#\{1\le n\le N:C_k^{\rm all}(n)\ge x\}
 &\le \frac{3}{x(1+\log_2 x)},\\
 \frac1N\#\{1\le n\le N:C^{\rm all}(n)\ge x\}
 &\le \frac{3}{x(1+\log_2 x)}.
\end{align*}
The same bound holds for the complete-period laws and their
limiting weighted geometric law.
For every $0<\theta<1$, all these laws have the uniform
fractional-moment bound
\[
 \mathbb E C^\theta
 \le\frac{1}{2(1-2^{\theta-1})}.
\]
With $H_0=0$ and $H_a=\sum_{j=1}^a1/j$ for $a\ge1$,
their truncated means satisfy
\[
 \mathbb E_{\rm period} C_k^{\rm all}
   =\sum_{j<k}\frac{m_j}{2^{j+1}}\le\frac12 H_k.
\]
For $L=\lfloor\log_2 N\rfloor$, positive-interval sampling has
the stronger cutoff-dependent bound
\[
 \frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N C_k^{\rm all}(n)
 \le\frac12 H_{\min(k,L)},\qquad
 \frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N C^{\rm all}(n)
 \le\frac12 H_L.
\]
Thus the full positive-interval mean is $O(\log\log N)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $W_t\ge t$ and $W_0=0$, for each $x\ge1$ there is a
least $t=t(x)\ge1$ with $W_t\ge x$.
If $k<t$, the truncated threshold event is empty.
If $k\ge t$, the nested clock gives the exact equivalence
\[
 C_k^{\rm all}(n)\ge x
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad2^t\mid n.
\]
The full observable has the same equivalence for every positive
$n$. Hence the positive-interval probability is exactly
$\lfloor N/2^t\rfloor/N$, at most $2^{-t}$.
There is no added period-remainder error, and no restriction
on how $k$ may grow with $N$.

The compiled cumulative inequalities give
\[
 x(t+1)\le W_t(t+1)\le3\cdot2^t,\qquad x\le W_t<2^t.
\]
In particular $\log_2 x<t$ and all denominators below are
positive. Therefore
\[
 \frac{\lfloor N/2^t\rfloor}{N}
 \le2^{-t}\le\frac{3}{x(t+1)}
 \le\frac{3}{x(1+\log_2 x)}.
\]
For the reference random variable $T$ with
$\mathbb P(T=s)=2^{-s-1}$, the limiting count is $W_T$ and
its threshold probability is exactly $2^{-t}$.
The complete-period law is $W_{\min(T,k)}$, so it obeys
the same upper bound.

For $U_N$ uniform on $1,\ldots,N$,
\[
 \mathbb P(v_2(U_N)\ge t)
 =\frac{\lfloor N/2^t\rfloor}{N}\le2^{-t}
 =\mathbb P(T\ge t).
\]
Thus $v_2(U_N)$ is stochastically dominated by $T$.
The cumulative weights are increasing, and truncation can
only decrease them. Every indicated fractional moment is
therefore at most that of $W_T$.
Since $W_s<2^s$,
\[
 \mathbb E W_T^\theta
 \le\frac12\sum_{s\ge0}2^{-(1-\theta)s}
 =\frac{1}{2(1-2^{\theta-1})}.
\]

Finally, linearity applied to the exact block indicators gives
the complete-period mean. The arithmetic bound
$m_j/2^{j+1}\le1/(2(j+1))$ proves its harmonic upper bound.
On $1,\ldots,N$, the $j$th indicator has probability
$\lfloor N/2^{j+1}\rfloor/N\le2^{-j-1}$ and is zero when
$j+1>L$. Summing only these active rows proves both interval
mean inequalities. No independent-factor approximation enters
any of these bounds.
\end{proof}

The theorem does not assert that the infinite reference mean
$\sum_{j\ge0}m_j2^{-j-1}$ is finite. Its finite partial sums
have the proved harmonic envelope; fractional moments below
one are uniformly finite without that extra assertion.

\paragraph{The infinite PGF records factor complexity.}
For $|z|\le1$ put
\[
 Q(z)=\sum_{t\ge0}2^{-t-1}z^{W_t}.
\]
The sum is absolutely convergent, and the finite reference
laws satisfy the explicit uniform estimate
\[
 \sup_{|z|\le1}|Q_k(z)-Q(z)|\le2^{1-k}.
\]
Indeed, the terminal atom of $Q_k$ and the omitted tail of
$Q$ each have total mass $2^{-k}$.

More generally, let
$\alpha=\limsup_{t\to\infty}W_t/t\in[1,\infty]$.
The radius of convergence of $Q$ is exactly
\[
 R_Q=2^{1/\alpha}\in[1,2],
 \qquad 1/\infty=0.
\]
Because the $W_t$ are strictly increasing, its nonzero Taylor
coefficients are precisely $a_{W_t}=2^{-t-1}$.
The Cauchy--Hadamard formula consequently gives
\[
 R_Q^{-1}
 =\limsup_{t\to\infty}2^{-(t+1)/W_t}
 =2^{-1/\alpha}.
\]
Thus the one-carrier pole at $2$ cannot simply be transferred
to the full factor count. Its analytic radius measures the
average growth of the actual Fermat factor counts.
No value of $\alpha$ beyond the stated range is assumed.

\paragraph{The Fibonacci connection is retained.}
For a finite odd-prime support $S$ and a common scalar sampled
modulo $2^kM_S$, the same CRT argument gives
\[
 \mathbb E\!\left[
 z^{C_k^{\rm all}(n)}
 \prod_{\ell\in S}w_\ell^{\boldsymbol{1}_{P_\ell\mid F_n}}\right]
 =Q_k(z)\prod_{\ell\in S}(1+(w_\ell-1)/\ell).
\]
The whole weighted dyadic block depends on the first CRT
coordinate, and the Fibonacci vector on the second; this is
the complete independence argument. With the Fibonacci
compensation alone, the limits on $|z|\le1$ are therefore
$\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(z)Q(z)$.
Adding independent noise to a mod-Poisson limit is classical
\cite[Example~2.2]{KN}; here the noise and its tail have an
explicit arithmetic realization. A neighborhood outside the
unit disc requires the actual radius condition above.
These sampling and factor-count results supply no new
independent estimate for the earlier zeta-zero exclusion bound.

\paragraph{Formalization scope.}
The maintained candidate modules
\begin{quote}\footnotesize\ttfamily
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/FermatFactorCount.lean\\
Mathlib/Probability/Distributions/WeightedDyadic.lean\\
Mathlib/NumberTheory/Primes/FermatAllFactorProbability.lean\\
Mathlib/Probability/Distributions/WeightedDyadicBound.lean
\end{quote}
check twenty-one declarations. They prove the physical
prime-factor lower and cardinal bounds, disjointness, the
arbitrary-weight generating law, its actual full-factor
transport, and both cumulative weight estimates.
Every axiom audit contains only standard foundational axioms.
The threshold/floor consumer, logarithmic tail, fractional
moments, interval means, infinite PGF and radius statements,
and the final weighted CRT specialization are the complete
ordinary proofs above, without a separate Lean compilation
claim for those combined analytic conclusions.


\subsection{Exact binary sampling and its sharp leading constant}
\label{sec:binary-sampling}

The weighted geometric law has a stronger finite-sampling statement
than the generic complete-period remainder estimate.  It does not
depend on the unknown sizes of the positive Fermat block weights.
We use total variation in the convention
$d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mu,\nu)=\frac12\sum_x|\mu(x)-\nu(x)|$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact binary defect and sharp sampling constant]
\label{thm:binary-sampling}
Let $w_j$ be positive integers, $W_t=\sum_{0\le j<t}w_j$,
$U_N$ uniform on $\{1,\ldots,N\}$ with $N\ge1$, and let $T$
have $\mathbb P(T=t)=2^{-t-1}$ for $t\ge0$.
For $k\ge0$ put
\[
 X_{N,k}=W_{\min(v_2(U_N),k)},\qquad X_k=W_{\min(T,k)}.
\]
Write
\begin{align*}
 r_t&=\frac{N\bmod 2^t}{2^t},\\
 b_t&=\left\lfloor\frac{N}{2^t}\right\rfloor
       -2\left\lfloor\frac{N}{2^{t+1}}\right\rfloor\in\{0,1\},\\
 h&=\min\{k,1+\lfloor\log_2N\rfloor\}.
\end{align*}
Then
\[
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}(X_{N,k},X_k)
 =\frac1{2N}\sum_{\substack{0\le t<k\\b_t=1}}(1-r_t)
 \le \frac{h}{6N}+\frac1{2N}.
\]
The same formula, with the finite sum over all nonzero bits of $N$,
and the bound with $h=1+\lfloor\log_2N\rfloor$ hold without truncation.
For the untruncated variables the leading constant is optimal:
\[
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \frac{N}{\log_2N}\,
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}(W_{v_2(U_N)},W_T)=\frac16.
\]
For nonnegative weights the displayed error remains an upper bound;
strict positivity is needed for the equality of transported defects.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Set $q_t=\lfloor N/2^t\rfloor$, so $N/2^t=q_t+r_t$,
$r_0=0$ and
\[
 q_t=2q_{t+1}+b_t,\qquad
 r_{t+1}=\frac{r_t+b_t}{2}.
\]
For $t<k$, the empirical mass at level $W_t$ is
$(q_t-q_{t+1})/N$.  Its difference from $2^{-t-1}$ is
\[
 \frac{q_t-q_{t+1}}N-\frac1{2^{t+1}}
   =\frac{b_t-r_t}{2N}.
\]
The terminal mass difference is
$q_k/N-2^{-k}=-r_k/N\le0$.
Since $0\le r_t<1$, the positive differences occur exactly at
the bits $b_t=1$.  The levels $W_t$ are distinct, so total variation,
the total positive difference between two probability laws,
is the stated sum.  All bits above $\lfloor\log_2N\rfloor$ vanish.
For the full law there are likewise only finitely many positive
differences, and the same calculation applies.  If levels merge
because some weights vanish, summing masses in each fiber can
only decrease total variation.

Here is the joint estimate on the retained digit sum.  Define
\[
 R(b,r)=b(1-r),\qquad B(r)=r-\frac{|r-\tfrac12|}{3}.
\]
For $0\le r\le1$ and $b\in\{0,1\}$, put $y=(r+b)/2$.
Then
\[
 R(b,r)\le \frac13+B(y)-B(r).
 \tag{*}
\]
This is a two-branch identity with a nonnegative slack.
For $b=0$ that slack is $0$ when $r\ge1/2$, and
$(1-2r)/3$ when $r\le1/2$.  For $b=1$ it is $0$
when $r\le1/2$, and $(2r-1)/3$ when $r\ge1/2$.
Thus all four cases prove $(*)$ directly.
We also have $B(0)=-1/6$ and $B(r)\le5/6$ on $[0,1]$.
Summing $(*)$ over the actual binary orbit makes all intermediate
compensator terms cancel:
\[
 \sum_{0\le t<h}b_t(1-r_t)
 \le \frac h3+B(r_h)-B(0)\le \frac h3+1.
\]
Division by $2N>0$ proves the claimed bound.

To prove sharpness symbolically for every $m\ge1$, take
\[
 N_m=\frac{4^m-1}{3}.
\]
Its binary expansion has ones exactly at $t=2i$,
$0\le i<m$, and
$r_{2i}=(1-4^{-i})/3$.  Therefore
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{0\le i<m}(1-r_{2i})
 &=\frac{2m}{3}+\frac49(1-4^{-m}),\\
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}(W_{v_2(U_{N_m})},W_T)
 &=\frac{m}{3N_m}+\frac{2(1-4^{-m})}{9N_m}.
\end{align*}
Since $\log_2N_m=2m-\log_2 3+\log_2(1-4^{-m})$,
the normalized limit along this sequence is $1/6$.
The universal upper bound gives the reverse limsup inequality.
For $k=0$ both laws are the point mass at $W_0=0$;
for $N=1$ and $k\ge1$ the exact defect is $1/2$.
These cases also verify the endpoint conventions.
\end{proof}

Taking $w_j=m_j=\omega(T_j)$ applies the theorem to the
actual all-Fermat-factor count, by the exact order and disjoint-support
theorems.  The result is independent of how many prime factors
any particular Fermat number has.  In particular, the complete
reference law approximates the actual positive-interval law in total
variation at the displayed rate, simultaneously for every cutoff.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform common-index sampling with odd clocks]
\label{thm:binary-odd-joint-sampling}
Let $M\ge1$ be odd, and let $A$ be uniform on
$\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z$, independently of $T$.
For every $N\ge1$ and integer $a\ge0$,
\[
 d_{\mathrm{TV}}\bigl((U_N\bmod M,v_2(U_N)),(A,T)\bigr)
 \le \frac{Ma}{2N}+2^{-a}.
\]
Consequently, if $N\ge M$, this distance is at most
\[
 \min\left\{1,\,
 \frac{M}{2N}\left(\log_2\frac NM+3\right)\right\}.
\]
Every deterministic joint observation of the two coordinates obeys
the same upper bound.  In particular, for any finite set $S$ of odd
prime labels, taking $M=M_S$ gives it for the full or truncated
weighted Fermat count together with
$(\boldsymbol1_{P_\ell\mid F_n})_{\ell\in S}$,
uniformly in the Fermat cutoff.
Thus $M_S=o(N)$ suffices for convergence of these growing finite
vectors to the independent reference law.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For each $0\le t<a$ and each residue $c\bmod M$,
the event $n\equiv c\bmod M$, $v_2(n)=t$ is exactly one
residue class modulo $M2^{t+1}$: combine $n\equiv c\bmod M$
and $n\equiv2^t\bmod2^{t+1}$ by CRT.
Its count on $1,\ldots,N$ differs from
$N/(M2^{t+1})$ by at most one.
The contribution of these $Ma$ cells to half the sum of
absolute mass differences is therefore at most $Ma/(2N)$.
On the remaining levels, the empirical total mass is
$\lfloor N/2^a\rfloor/N\le2^{-a}$, and the reference total
mass is $2^{-a}$.  Their contribution to half the absolute
difference is at most $2^{-a}$.  This proves the first estimate,
including $a=0$.
For $N\ge M$ choose $a=\lceil\log_2(N/M)\rceil$.
Then $2^{-a}\le M/N$ and $a\le\log_2(N/M)+1$.
Total variation is also at most one, giving the displayed minimum.
A deterministic map cannot increase total variation: sum the
signed masses over each fiber before applying the triangle
inequality.  For the Fibonacci labels use the odd modulus
$M=M_S$, and send $c$ to the vector
$(\boldsymbol1_{\ell\mid c})_{\ell\in S}$.
The maintained Fibonacci carrier theorem identifies this with
the actual vector $(\boldsymbol1_{P_\ell\mid F_n})_{\ell\in S}$.
Send the other coordinate to $W_{\min(t,k)}$, or to $W_t$
for the full count.  Finally, writing $q=N/M\to\infty$,
the bound $(\log_2q+3)/(2q)$ tends to zero.
\end{proof}

This common-index statement uses the Fibonacci clocks obtained from
Binet's quadratic identity and the golden-root quotient in
Section~\ref{sec:fibonacci-golden-carriers}.
It gives a growing-cutoff probability comparison for bounded tests.
For example, a bounded complex test of absolute value at most one has
expectation error at most twice the displayed total variation.
Tests outside that bounded class require their own integrability
estimates; the statement supplies no new zeta-zero exclusion.
Geometric approximation for valuation vectors is classical, for
example \cite[Proposition~1.9]{TaoCollatz}; the exact single-index
binary defect and its transport to these arithmetic counts are
the assertions proved here.  No literature priority is claimed.

\begin{corollary}[A simultaneous compensated Fibonacci--Fermat limit]
\label{cor:diagonal-fibonacci-fermat}
For real $X\ge3$ put
\begin{align*}
 S_X&=\{\ell\le X:\ell\text{ is an odd prime}\},\qquad s_X=|S_X|,\\
 M_X&=\prod_{\ell\in S_X}\ell,\qquad
 \lambda_X=\sum_{\ell\in S_X}\frac1\ell.
\end{align*}
Define the actual Fibonacci observation and compensated joint transform by
\begin{align*}
 B_X(n)&=\sum_{\ell\in S_X}\boldsymbol1_{P_\ell\mid F_n},\\
 \mathcal G_{N,X,k}(z,u)
 &=e^{-\lambda_X(z-1)}
   \frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N z^{B_X(n)}u^{C_k^{\mathrm{all}}(n)}.
\end{align*}
Let $\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}$ be the entire compensated Fibonacci
profile and $Q$ the full weighted Fermat PGF above.
For each $R\ge1$ there is a constant $C_R$ depending only on $R$
such that, for every $X\ge3$, $N\ge M_X$ and $k\ge0$,
\begin{align*}
 &\sup_{\substack{|z|\le R\\|u|\le1}}
 \left|\mathcal G_{N,X,k}(z,u)-\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(z)Q(u)\right|
 \le E_{N,X}(R)+C_R(X^{-1}+2^{-k}),\\
 &E_{N,X}(R)=R^{s_X}e^{(R+1)\lambda_X}
       \frac{M_X}{N}\left(\log_2\frac{N}{M_X}+3\right).
\end{align*}
In particular, fix $0<c<1$, and for all sufficiently large $N$ set
\[
 X(N)=\left\lfloor\frac{c\log N}{\log\log N}\right\rfloor.
\]
For every integer sequence $k(N)\to\infty$, the compensated transforms
converge to $\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(z)Q(u)$, uniformly on
$|z|\le R$, $|u|\le1$.  More precisely, for every $0<\eta<1-c$,
the uniform error is
\[
 O_{c,R,\eta}\left(
 N^{c-1+\eta}+\frac{\log\log N}{\log N}+2^{-k(N)}
 \right).
\]
No fixed-cutoff sampling limit or uniform first moment of the full
Fermat law is assumed.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In the independent reference law from
Theorem~\ref{thm:binary-odd-joint-sampling}, the Fibonacci coordinates
have probabilities $1/\ell$, and the other coordinate has PGF $Q_k(u)$.
Consequently its compensated joint transform is exactly
\[
 \mathcal F_X(z)Q_k(u),\qquad
 \mathcal F_X(z)=
 \prod_{\ell\in S_X}(1+(z-1)/\ell)e^{-(z-1)/\ell}.
\]
For the unnormalized test, $|z^{B_X}u^{C_k}|\le R^{s_X}$.
Also $|e^{-\lambda_X(z-1)}|\le e^{(R+1)\lambda_X}$.
The expectation bound $2\|f\|_\infty d_{\mathrm{TV}}$ and the
joint total-variation estimate therefore give
\[
 \sup_{\substack{|z|\le R\\|u|\le1}}
 |\mathcal G_{N,X,k}(z,u)-\mathcal F_X(z)Q_k(u)|
 \le E_{N,X}(R).
\]
This explicitly charges the growing complex test and its compensator.

The canonical product satisfies
\[
 \sup_{|z|\le R}|\mathcal F_X(z)-\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}(z)|
 \le C_R/X.
\]
For completeness, with $a=z-1$ a factor
$(1+a/\ell)e^{-a/\ell}$ differs from one by $O_R(\ell^{-2})$.
This follows by its Taylor expansion, uniformly for $|a|\le R+1$.
The tail sum is bounded by
$\sum_{n>X}n^{-2}=O(X^{-1})$.
The finite-product inequality
$|\prod_j(1+e_j)-1|\le\exp(\sum_j|e_j|)-1$
bounds the remaining tail product, while the initial products are
uniformly bounded on the compact disc.
This proves the displayed estimate first for large $X$ depending on
$R$; enlarging $C_R$ covers $3\le X$ in the bounded remaining range.
It uses the cross-multiplied tail factorization
$\mathcal F_{\mathrm{odd}}=\mathcal F_X\cdot\mathrm{tail}_X$,
so it remains valid at every zero of $\mathcal F_X$.
Since $|Q_k(u)|\le1$ and
$\sup_{|u|\le1}|Q_k(u)-Q(u)|\le2^{1-k}$, the first conclusion follows.

The diagonal claim needs only elementary bounds:
\[
 s_X\le X,\qquad \lambda_X\le1+\log X,\qquad M_X\le X^X.
\]
Indeed, the first and third count and bound the labels, and the second
bounds their reciprocal sum by the harmonic sum.
For the stated $X(N)$,
$X=o(\log N)$ and $X\log X=(c+o(1))\log N$.
Thus $M_X\le N^{c+o(1)}<N$ eventually,
$R^{s_X}e^{(R+1)\lambda_X}=N^{o(1)}$, and
$\log_2(N/M_X)+3=O(\log N)$.
It follows that $E_{N,X}(R)\le N^{c-1+o(1)}$,
hence it is $O_{c,R,\eta}(N^{c-1+\eta})$.
Finally $X(N)^{-1}=O_c(\log\log N/\log N)$.
Substitution proves the stated rate and simultaneous limit.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Formalization scope.}
The maintained candidate owner
\begin{quote}\footnotesize\ttfamily
Mathlib/Probability/Distributions/DyadicDiscrepancy.lean
\end{quote}
checks nine declarations: the common compensator and its endpoint
bounds, preservation of the unit interval, the one-step signed
inequality, its telescoped and normalized forms, and the exact
alternating reward sum.  Its axiom audit contains only the standard
foundational axioms.  The exact total-variation identification,
the sharp limsup, transport to the physical Fermat count, and the
joint odd-clock theorem and simultaneous compensated limit are the
complete ordinary proofs above, without a separate Lean compilation
claim for those assembled
probability conclusions.

\subsection{Rational residue-class laws: a stronger inverse theorem}

The preceding inverse theorem extends beyond a common leading coefficient.
Fix an integer $Q\ge1$. Suppose that outside finitely many primes the laws
have the form $\eta_1(p)=R_a(p)$ and $\eta_2(p)=S_a(p)$ for
$p\equiv a\pmod Q$, where $a$ runs over the units modulo $Q$.
Each $R_a,S_a$ is a rational function with rational coefficients.
Assume each is either identically zero or has an expansion
$\nu/x+\kappa/x^2+O(x^{-3})$ with rational $\nu>0$.
The actual probabilities are in $[0,1]$.

\begin{theorem}[Residue-rational profile rigidity]
Equality of the entire profiles implies $R_a=S_a$ as rational functions
for every unit class $a$. The complete laws then differ by a finite
permutation of prime coordinates.
More precisely, if one active $R_a$ differs from $S_a$, there is a unit
progression $r\pmod M$, refining $a\pmod Q$, such that for every
sufficiently large prime $p\equiv r\pmod M$ the value $R_a(p)$ is absent
from the entire second law.
Consequently the first profile has infinitely many zeros absent from
the second. Using the prime number theorem in a fixed progression,
their number in $[-T,0]$ is at least
$(\nu/\varphi(M)+o(1))T/\log T$.
If only the second law is active in a discrepant class, interchange
the two laws.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $R_a(x)=\nu/x+\kappa/x^2+O(x^{-3})$.
For each active target class $b$, write
$S_b(x)=\mu_b/x+\lambda_b/x^2+O(x^{-3})$.
If $R_a(p)=S_b(q)$ and $p$ is large, then $q$ is large and comparable
to $p$: finitely many small target primes have probabilities bounded
away from the positive values tending to zero under consideration.
Taking reciprocals gives
\[
 q=\frac{\mu_b}{\nu}p+
       \left(\frac{\lambda_b}{\mu_b}
                    -\frac{\mu_b\kappa}{\nu^2}\right)+O(p^{-1}).
\]
Both displayed coefficients are rational. The error belongs to a
fixed rational lattice because $p,q$ are integers, so it is eventually
zero. Thus every large equality satisfies an exact affine equation
$C_bq=A_bp+B_b$, with positive integers $A_b,C_b$ and integer $B_b$.

If $B_b=0$, primeness and sufficiently large $p,q$ force $q=p$ and
$A_b=C_b$. Then $b=a$, and $R_a(p)=S_a(p)$ has only finitely many
solutions unless the two rational functions are identical.
For each remaining equation, $B_b\ne0$. Choose a distinct auxiliary
prime $\ell_b$ not dividing $QA_bB_bC_b$, and impose
$p\equiv-B_bA_b^{-1}\pmod{\ell_b}$.
This is a nonzero residue. Together with $p\equiv a\pmod Q$, the
Chinese remainder theorem gives one unit class modulo
$M=Q\prod_b\ell_b$. Each affine equality on this class would make
$\ell_b\mid q$, impossible once the prime $q>\ell_b$.
This excludes every target class simultaneously; the finite
exceptional target primes were already removed.

Dirichlet's theorem supplies infinitely many primes in the chosen
unit progression. Their probabilities are eventually distinct,
since $R_a(p)^{-1}=p/\nu-\kappa/\nu^2+O(p^{-1})$ is strictly
increasing on large prime arguments. They therefore give distinct
exclusive zeros. Their cutoff is $p\le\nu T+O(1)$, which yields the
stated count from the fixed-progression prime number theorem.
Equal profiles cannot have such exclusive zeros. Hence all rational
functions agree, the labelled tails agree, and cancellation of the
common tail gives the finite-permutation conclusion.
\end{proof}

This is an ordinary proof; its finite affine-exclusion and
residue-rational classification have not yet been formalized in Lean.
It applies only after a family's rational local laws and exceptional
prime set have been established. It does not assert such a transfer
for every polynomial, marked family, or Dirichlet $L$-function.

\subsection{The completed signed-packet calculus and its open consumer}

For a finite $S\subset\{1,\ldots,N\}$ and complex coefficients $a,b$,
put $A_d=\sum_{n\in S,\ d\mid n}a_n$ and
$B_d=\sum_{n\in S,\ d\mid n}b_n$.
The maintained signed covariance identity is exactly
\[
 \sum_{\substack{n,m\in S\\(n,m)=1}}
      (a_n-cb_n)\overline{(a_m-cb_m)}
    =\sum_{d=1}^{N}\mu(d)|A_d-cB_d|^2.
\]
Indeed $\sum_{d\mid(n,m)}\mu(d)$ is the coprimality indicator, and
finite interchange of sums proves the identity. Each divisor row
retains the full expansion
\[
 |A_d-cB_d|^2=|A_d|^2
    -cB_d\overline{A_d}-\overline c A_d\overline{B_d}
    +|c|^2|B_d|^2.
\]
In particular, if $b$ is supported on primes larger than $D$, then
$B_d=0$ for every $2\le d\le D$. Such a prime-only compensator changes
none of these low rows, regardless of the complex coefficient $c$.
This rules out that specific compensation mechanism on those rows;
it is not an obstruction to other coupled compensators.

There is also an exact partition using every prime allowed by the
finite support, rather than a chosen finite set. For $v_1=0$, define,
for $1\le n\le N$ and prime $p\le N$,
\[
 v_p(n)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\mu(n)v_n\frac{\log p}{\log n},&p\mid n,\ p\ {\rm prime},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Only $n>1$ enters the first branch. Since $\mu(n)=0$ off the
squarefree numbers, $\sum_{p\le N}v_p(n)=\mu(n)v_n$ for
$1\le n\le N$. The coprime form of two blocks with the same prime
index vanishes exactly: both supported arguments are divisible by
that prime. Writing $\mathcal C(f,g)=\sum_{n,m\in S,\,(n,m)=1}f_n\overline{g_m}$,
this gives the exact identity
\[
 \mathcal C(\mu v,\mu v)=
 \sum_{\substack{p,q\le N\\p,q\ {\rm prime},\ p\ne q}}
            \mathcal C(v_p,v_q).
\]
The coefficient $v$ may itself retain a full compensation, so this
identity does not erase its cross terms. It does not estimate their
joint cancellation.

The reconciliation inventory
\path{paper/completed-research-inventory.json}
and the public theorem-audit inventory record the maintained owners
for these covariance identities, full-prime partitions, Gaussian
principal and Poisson inputs, Euler-continuation results, detector
adapters, and quantitative tail lemmas.
Their public export is
\path{PrimeFactorOscillations/Assembly/CompletedDetectorPackets.lean}.
A copied source is not certified by this list: the inventory separately
records the fresh compilation and axiom-audit status.
An earlier 20 October reconciliation checked 1,139 owned Lean modules and
4,843 selected theorem closures with standard foundations only; both the
public and canonical QRH statement comparisons passed. That reconciliation
identified 301 maintained owners and transferred all 132 proved private
graph nodes to maintained sources. A later 1,150-module promotion covered
the identity, finite-permutation and prime-coordinate inverse consumers.
Subsequent maintained owners add the full adaptive cutoff inverse and
exact optimal-distortion theorem, with separate build and axiom receipts.
The residue-rational classification and continuum-cardinality assertion
remain ordinary proofs. The signed arithmetic estimate for an improved
RH boundary remains open.

The unfinished analytic consumer remains explicitly open. In the
fixed-mask notation of that programme it asks, for each
$\varepsilon>0$ and each fixed admissible $G$, for constants
$C_{\varepsilon,G},D_{0,\varepsilon,G}$ such that
\[
 |I_{D,G}|\le C_{\varepsilon,G}D^{3/2+\varepsilon}
 \quad(D\ge D_{0,\varepsilon,G}).
\]
No uniformity for a growing $G$ is implicit. The identities above do
not prove this estimate. Detector precision, zero-signal preservation
and smaller separate remainders are recorded as structural results;
the certified unconditional boundary remains $7/8$.
Failed absolute-value reductions and restricted-range attempts retain
their failure records and are not counted as improvements of that boundary.
